# Side-by-Side Comparison: Captioning/Annotation Approaches

> Topic: Data processing for video generation models (including joint audio-video generation): data cleaning pipelines, data distribution, captioning/annotation methods

[← Back to Home](../index.md)

This page compares all entries side by side, field by field. ⚠️ marks entries where some information for this field is uncertain.

**Fields**: [Captioning model used (in-house VLM / open-source model, model scale)](#captioning-model-used-in-house-vlm--open-source-model-model-scale) · [Caption density and structuring level (short/long/dense description, structured fields such as camera motion, style tags)](#caption-density-and-structuring-level-shortlongdense-descriptions-structured-fields-such-as-camera-movement-style-tags) · [Joint audio-video caption structure (whether it covers both visual + auditory tracks simultaneously, whether split into separate fields, e.g. LTX-2's full soundscape description, Script-a-Video's factorized streams, Foley-Omni's three-field schema)](#joint-audio-video-caption-structure-whether-it-covers-both-visual-and-auditory-tracks-simultaneously-whether-it-is-split-into-separate-fields-eg-ltx-2s-full-soundscape-description-script-a-videos-factorized-streams-foley-omnis-three-fields) · [Dialogue transcription and speaker attribute annotation (ASR transcription + speaker identity/language/accent/emotion)](#dialogue-transcription-and-speaker-attribute-annotation-asr-transcription--speaker-identitylanguageaccentemotion) · [Geometric and structured annotation (camera parameters, depth, 3D point tracks, action annotation, explicit state)](#geometric-and-structured-annotation-camera-parameters-depth-3d-point-tracks-action-annotation-explicit-state) · [Synthetic data construction (controlled perturbation/editing to construct training pairs, e.g. InstructAV2AV)](#synthetic-data-construction-controlled-perturbation--edit-constructed-training-pairs-eg-instructav2av) · [Degree of human involvement (manual annotation, manual QC, model pre-screening + manual review)](#degree-of-human-involvement-manual-annotation-manual-quality-control-model-pre-screening--manual-review)

## Captioning model used (in-house VLM / open-source model, model scale)

`caption_model` · Level of detail: detailed

### [Allegro](../models/Allegro.md)

Two-tier labeling with a clear division of labor:
[Coarse-grained] Tag2Text (Huang et al., 2023): serves as stage 6 of the filtering funnel, generating tag-style descriptions for the middle frame of each video, providing preliminary semantic information; its output is used directly as the text-side input for the stage-7 CLIP similarity filter, and also serves as the basis for category distribution statistics (people / objects / landscapes). It was chosen for being lightweight and able to run at full scale on a 500M-clip corpus.
[Fine-grained] Aria (Li et al., 2024b, also developed in-house by Rhymes AI), a version fine-tuned on the video captioning task: Aria is an open-source, natively multimodal Mixture-of-Experts model with 25.3B total parameters, activating 3.9B per visual token (3.5B per text token), supporting up to 64K multimodal input tokens; officially it can generate a caption for a 256-frame video within 10 seconds — this throughput characteristic is precisely why it was chosen as the large-scale video annotator. The base model has been open-sourced under Apache 2.0 (rhymes-ai/Aria), but the weights of the video-caption fine-tuned version were not released separately.
[Text encoder] T5 (not mT5) was used during training, with a maximum of 512 tokens; the paper argues via qualitative comparison (Fig. 7–8) that T5 outperforms mT5 on this task.

### [Apollo](../models/Apollo.md)

Uses an annotation-model matrix organized as "subset-based routing + multiple models in parallel + unified fusion," all open-source or commercial API models, with an unnamed in-house model on the video side:
[Speech/singing transcription (three models in parallel)] Whisper-Large-v3 (OpenAI, multilingual ASR, ~1.55B), SenseVoice (Alibaba FunAudioLLM, multilingual speech understanding, supports Chinese/English/Cantonese/Japanese/Korean plus emotion/event recognition, ~234M), Qwen2.5-Omni (Alibaba's omni-modal large model, 7B/3B variants). This combination of using three models together is uncommon among comparable works, presumably for cross-validation or for dispatching by language/scenario.
[Audio captioning (two models in parallel)] Qwen2.5-Omni (open-source omni-modal) + Gemini 2.5-Pro (Google's closed-source flagship multimodal model). Calling Gemini 2.5-Pro at the scale of 81 million entries represents a significant cost investment, suggesting that some high-value subsets were routed to a stronger model.
[Video captioning] "a video expert model for detailed video labels" — unnamed, no scale given, presumably Kuaishou's internal in-house video-understanding model (paired with the Kling ecosystem).
[Speaker attribute extraction] Attributes such as gender and age are extracted by the aforementioned audio-model stack; no dedicated model is separately listed.
[Fusion] "All annotations are merged into unified dense captions" — the paper does not specify whether the fusion is performed via rule-based concatenation or LLM rewriting — this is a notable difference from MOVA (which explicitly uses GPT-OSS-120B for fusion and consistency verification).
[Downstream text encoding] Captions are fed into Qwen2.5-7B as the text encoder; there is also a dedicated 1024-dimensional TTS text encoder for the speech text to be synthesized; v1 additionally mentions Qwen3-8B Embedding.

### [CineDance / CineDance-1M](../models/CineDance.md) ⚠️

Uses open-source Qwen-series large models throughout, with no in-house captioning model; the inference framework credits vLLM:
[Narrative parsing backbone] Qwen3.5-27B — handles shot grouping and narrative boundary determination; the Tab. 4 ablation compares backbones of different Qwen scales, and the 27B version combined with a bottom-up strategy achieves the best F1 = 88.4%.
[Visual annotation model] Qwen3.5-35B-A3B — MoE architecture (about 35B total parameters, 3B activated), producing shot-level five-dimensional attributes, transition type, local character lists, active scenes, shot descriptions, and transition descriptions.
[Audio annotation model] Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B — an omni-modal MoE model (about 30B total / 3B activated), simultaneously handling sentence-level ASR extraction, shot-level audio prompt (music/ambient sound/sound effects) generation, and character timbre description, and also completing the binding of ASR sentences to character anchor tokens.
[Model-selection rationale] Different specialized models are used for vision and audio, and both choose an MoE sparse architecture (3B activated) to balance annotation throughput and quality at million-scale sequences; ASR and audio description are not delegated to a dedicated model such as Whisper but are unified under the Omni model, on the grounds that Tab. 5/7 show it far exceeds dedicated diarization tools such as Pyannote-3.1 (62.7%) and DiariZen (63.1%) on the speaker-attribution task.
[Also used for evaluation] WER/CER computation for CineBench uses Whisper-large-v3.
[Undisclosed] The original annotation prompts, per-model inference cost, and whether task-specific fine-tuning was applied to Qwen. [uncertain]

### [CogVideoX](../models/CogVideoX.md)

Split into two tracks, forming a "teacher generates → student distills" annotation system:
[Offline teacher pipeline (Dense Video Caption Data Generation, Fig. 7)]
1) Panda-70M's video captioning model → first generates a short caption;
2) CogVLM (the same image recaption model used in CogView3) → extracts 1 frame every 2 seconds and generates a dense image caption for each frame;
3) GPT-4 → summarizes the timestamped image_captions dictionary into the final video caption (Appendix G gives the full prompt, requiring content and changes to be described in chronological order, covering objects, scenery, animals, people, and camera movement, and explicitly forbidding boilerplate phrases such as "The video presents / depicts / showcases," "throughout the video," and line breaks);
4) For speed, 50K pieces of GPT-4 summary data were collected to fine-tune a LLaMA2 as a substitute summarization model for GPT-4, enabling large-scale production.
[Online student model] CogVLM2-Caption: built on CogVLM2-Video + Llama3, an end-to-end video understanding/annotation model fine-tuned on the dense-caption data produced by the pipeline above, roughly 12B in scale (a Llama3-8B-family base plus a visual side), used to further accelerate full-scale re-captioning. This model has been open-sourced (huggingface.co/zai-org/cogvlm2-llama3-caption) and is the only component of this work's data pipeline that can be directly reused externally. The paper also finds that chaining CogVLM2-Caption with CogVideoX enables video-to-video generation (Appendix I), which indirectly proves that its captions capture nearly all the detail of the original video.
[Inference side] There is also a caption upsampler: it uses a fine-tuned LLM (for text-to-video) or GPT-4V / CogVLM (for image-to-video) to rewrite the user's short prompt into a long description in the style of training captions; the paper explicitly states that the fine-tuned LLM outperforms zero-shot/few-shot approaches.

### [Cosmos-Predict2.5](../models/Cosmos-Predict25.md) ⚠️

Adopts a layered strategy of "medium-scale VLM for the general domain, larger VLM for specialized domains":
(1) General pretraining captions: Qwen2.5-VL-7B (the paper explicitly names the model and parameter count, citing Bai et al., 2025), generating a caption for each 5-second window; the prompt is engineered to elicit "factual, context-aware captions."
(2) Domain-specific captions: explicitly switches to "a larger VLM model (Bai et al., 2025)" — still in the Qwen2.5-VL family but a larger tier (not specified whether 32B or 72B), with a custom prompt configured for each domain. The team's judgment is that domain data volume is much smaller but requires higher description precision, which justifies the larger model.
(3) Captions for the multi-view driving model: the paper states "Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct," generated once every 150 frames at three length tiers (as written in the original; this differs from the "VL" phrasing used on the general side, possibly a typo or referring to an LLM performing a secondary rewrite of visual outputs).
(4) Domain classification model: the content-type classifier is an internally trained model; post-training domain partitioning uses a multi-head classifier trained on InternVideo2 embeddings.
(5) Text encoder (not a captioning model but related): Cosmos-Reason1 replaces the T5 used in Cosmos-Predict1; it is a decoder-only VLM dedicated to Physical AI. Rather than taking a single layer's output, it concatenates activations from multiple blocks and projects them to 1024 dimensions, to simultaneously capture local and global linguistic context.
[Uncertain: the exact parameter tier of the domain VLM; ambiguity in the description of the multi-view captioning model]

### [Data-Juicer 2.0](../models/Data-Juicer.md) ⚠️

Data-Juicer does not develop its own captioning model; instead it wraps a variety of captioning models as pluggable operators, making it one of the open-source frameworks with the richest set of video captioning operators in this survey. There are 6 video-captioning-related Mappers:
  · video_captioning_from_vlm_mapper — "uses a VLM that can accept video input to generate a video description." The most general interface, pluggable with any video VLM (Qwen2.5-VL, InternVL, Video-LLaVA, etc.); this is the operator that corresponds to the mainstream 2026 approach. Model scale is chosen by the user.
  · video_captioning_from_video_mapper — uses a HuggingFace video-to-text model to generate descriptions (e.g., the VideoBLIP family).
  · video_captioning_from_frames_mapper — extracts frames and uses an image-to-text model (BLIP/BLIP-2 family) to generate a description per frame, then aggregates them. Lowest cost but lacks temporal understanding.
  · video_captioning_from_audio_mapper — "generates a description for the video based on the audio track using Qwen-Audio." Auditory-side captioning, a key component for building joint audio-video annotation.
  · video_captioning_from_summarizer_mapper — "generates a video description by summarizing multiple already-generated pieces of text." This is DJ's most thoughtfully designed captioning operator: it aggregates outputs from the preceding routes (frame descriptions, audio descriptions, OCR text, content tags, ASR transcripts) and hands them to an LLM for summary fusion, producing a single comprehensive dense description. This "multi-source split description → LLM fusion" path is consistent with the multi-model fusion captioning philosophy used by teams like Movie Gen and Panda-70M.
  · video_captioning_from_human_tracks_mapper and video_captioning_face_attribute_emotion_mapper — person-centric targeted description (added in v1.5.4); the former generates descriptions based on person tracks, the latter generates facial-attribute and emotion descriptions for each tracked person.
[Accompanying tagging operators] video_tagging_from_frames_mapper (visual tags), video_tagging_from_audio_mapper (audio tags, AST), video_audio_ASR_mapper (speech recognition) — these do not produce natural-language descriptions but produce structured tags, serving as inputs to the summarizer operator.
[Actual usage] [uncertain] The official T2V case study did not enable any captioning operators — the three source datasets already come with their own captions (InternVid was generated with BLIP-2 + Tag2Text, Panda-70M with multi-model caption fusion + retrieval-based selection, MSR-VTT with manual annotation), and the case study only performed CLIP-alignment filtering without re-captioning. Thus, while DJ's captioning operator chain is complete, it lacks publicly validated results and recommended configurations on large-scale video-generation corpora. DJ has also not published recommended model choices or prompt templates for video captioning.

### [Foley-Omni](../models/Foley-Omni.md) ⚠️

[Primary annotation model] Gemini 2.5 Pro (Google's closed-source commercial multimodal large model). A distinguishing feature of this choice is its native support for joint video + audio input — this is a hard requirement for this task, since annotation must simultaneously rely on "what is seen" and "what is heard"; a pure-vision VLM (e.g., Qwen2.5-VL, InternVL) cannot handle audio-presence discrimination. Using a closed-source API means annotation cost is relatively high and not fully reproducible, but the paper does not disclose call volume or cost. Parameter scale is not disclosed.
[Auxiliary model — acoustic verification] Bandit (Watcharasupat et al., 2024), a model for cinematic audio source separation, naturally separates into speech / effects / music streams, which is exactly isomorphic to this paper's three-field schema — a very well-matched choice; using a general-purpose music source separation model (e.g., Demucs's vocals/drums/bass/other) would not map directly onto the three fields.
[Text encoder] UM-T5 encoder (a multilingual version of T5), used to encode the three-field structured text into a shared semantic space for DiT conditioning injection. All tasks share the same encoder, which is the implementation basis of the "unified interface" design.
[Visual feature model] CLIP (scene semantic features) + Synchformer (temporal synchronization features); the latter doubles as the synchrony scorer during the filtering stage — one model, two uses, with training and cleaning sharing the same synchrony representation, ensuring consistency between the filtering criterion and the model's learning objective.
[Uncertain] No independent ASR model is used for speech transcription (see below); no mention of any in-house captioner or distillation of Gemini outputs to reduce cost.

### [Goku](../models/Goku.md) ⚠️

★ Adopts a "dual complementary VLM + LLM fusion" three-model collaborative annotation scheme, a relatively advanced combination for its time:
(1) **InternVL2.0** — responsible for keyframe-level image captioning, with strengths in fine-grained content, objects, attributes, and scene description of static frames.
(2) **Tarsier2** — responsible for video-wide-level captioning. The paper specifically notes an inherent advantage of Tarsier2: it naturally describes **camera motion types** (e.g., zoom in, pan right), so no additional camera-motion annotation module is needed to obtain shot-language labels.
(3) **Qwen2** — serves as the LLM merger, combining the keyframe caption and the video caption into a single unified, non-redundant, non-contradictory final description. Quoting the paper: "we utilize Qwen2 to merge the keyframe and video captions."
[Design rationale] A single image VLM lacks temporal and motion information, while a single video VLM lacks granularity on static details; the two complement each other and are disambiguated and fused by the LLM, obtaining both spatial detail and temporal/shot semantics.
[Undisclosed] The specific parameter scale and version of each model (InternVL2.0 has multiple tiers from 1B to 76B, Tarsier2 has a 7B tier), whether domain fine-tuning was performed, annotation compute cost and throughput, and whether the image data (160M for T2I) used the same captioning scheme. [partially uncertain]

### [Hailuo / MiniMax Video](../models/Hailuo.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] (no disclosure at all). It is not stated whether an in-house VLM or an open-source model is used for video captioning, nor is any model scale given. Circumstantial background (not disclosure): MiniMax's in-house multimodal large models MiniMax-01 (VL version) and MiniMax-M3 (427B, Image-Text-to-Text) are capable of video captioning, and the company's degree of internal model self-sufficiency is high, suggesting the captions are likely produced by an in-house model, but there is no first-hand confirmation.

### [HunyuanVideo-Foley](../models/HunyuanVideo-Foley.md) ⚠️

The model configuration for the annotation stage is extremely minimal, in sharp contrast to other works that routinely coordinate three or four large models:
[The only captioning model: GenAU] Used to generate an audio caption for every 8-second clip; the paper describes it as providing "concise descriptions of the audio content." GenAU (Generative Audio Understanding, from the "Taming Data and Transformers for Audio Generation" line of work) is a dedicated audio captioning model, far smaller in scale than general-purpose multimodal large models. The paper does not disclose the specific version or parameter scale used. [uncertain]
[Key feature: only audio is labeled, not video] There is no video captioning step anywhere in the pipeline — no scene description is generated, no camera motion is described, and no visual tagging is performed. This is directly determined by the task shape: during training, video is fed in directly as visual features encoded via SigLIP-2, without needing a language intermediary; the role of the text condition is to supplement the desired sound characteristics, so only audio-side language description is needed. This makes the annotation cost of this work far lower than video-generation works (skipping the most expensive step, video captioning).
[Condition encoders (not annotation models, but they determine how captions are consumed)]
- Text: CLAP text encoder (Elizalde et al. 2023). Choosing CLAP over T5/umT5 is a deliberate decision — CLAP is an audio-text contrastive learning model whose text embedding space is naturally aligned with audio semantics, giving it stronger representational power for "text describing sound" than a general-purpose text encoder. The cost is that CLAP's text encoder has a relatively short context length (typically 77 tokens), unable to accommodate long dense descriptions, which directly caps the maximum caption length.
- Visual: SigLIP-2 visual encoder, extracting frame-level semantic features.
- Synchronization: Synchformer, extracting frame-level synchronization features, fed through an independent gated modulation pathway.
[No in-house annotation model] All models used are off-the-shelf open-source models; there is no in-house captioner or tagger of any kind.
[Total annotation model scale far smaller than peers] Compared with MOVA (MiMo-VL-7B + Qwen3-Omni×2 + GPT-OSS-120B) and UniVerse-1 (Qwen2.5-Omni + Whisper), this work uses only a single GenAU — an inevitable trade-off on annotation cost at the scale of 100,000 hours: the larger the scale, the lower the per-sample annotation budget must be.

### [HunyuanVideo](../models/HunyuanVideo.md) ⚠️

[Original version] An in-house Vision Language Model serves as the captioning model, used to generate JSON-structured captions; parameter scale, architecture, and training details are not disclosed. There is also a separate in-house camera-movement classifier. On the prompt side, the Hunyuan-Large LLM is used to rewrite user prompts.
[1.5] Upgraded to a "three-model division of labor" annotation system, the largest structural improvement relative to the original:
1. Image captioning model — follows the method of HunyuanImage-3.0;
2. Video captioning model — produces highly structured, multi-component descriptions;
3. Image-to-Video Instructional Captioning model — a newly added module that does not describe the whole-frame content but specifically describes "temporal evolution/change relative to the first frame," covering changes in both foreground subjects and background environment, shifting the text condition for I2V training from "descriptive" to "instructional," aligning it with the actual distribution of user input at I2V inference time (giving an image + saying how you want it to move);
plus a camera-motion recognition model (dual granularity: clip-level + temporal-level).
The names, parameter counts, and bases of the three captioning models are all undisclosed; only the training method is disclosed: OPA-DPO (a preference-optimization method targeting multimodal hallucination) is used for RL post-training to suppress hallucination. [uncertain]

### [InstructAV2AV](../models/InstructAV2AV.md) ⚠️

This work does not perform conventional video captioning (describing video content); instead it performs "editing instruction generation" (describing how to change the video), so the "captioning model" here corresponds to an instruction-generation model plus a suite of structured annotation tools.

[Instruction generation model] Qwen3-Omni (Xu et al., 2025b, Alibaba Tongyi's open-source omni-modal large model). The rationale for this choice is native support for joint video + audio + text input, enabling simultaneous understanding of the editable entities in the frame and the corresponding sounds on the audio track, thereby generating joint audio-video editing instructions. [uncertain] The specific scale tier used is not disclosed (Qwen3-Omni has multiple sizes); no prompt template is given; it is not stated how many instructions are generated per source; nor is it stated how instruction diversity is ensured (whether a template library, temperature sampling, or category-balanced sampling was used).

[Same model also serves as judge] Qwen3-Omni also handles the five-dimensional verification scoring of stage three (see model_as_data_judge). Having the same model generate and accept is a methodological risk in this pipeline.

[Accompanying structured annotation toolchain]
  · Grounded-SAM-2 (Ren et al., 2024) — open-vocabulary detection + segmentation + video tracking, producing instance-level masks that provide "editable object" anchors for instructions and editing regions for mask-guided synthesis.
  · TalkNet (Tao et al., 2021) — active speaker detection, annotating who is speaking and their spatio-temporal position.
  · ElevenLabs Scribe (2025) — ASR, extracting precise speech timestamps.
  · SAM-Audio (Shi et al., 2025) — "segment anything" for the audio side, separating the target entity's sound from the mixed audio track by semantics.
  · Semantic sound event labels — assigning a unique label (e.g., "dog barking") to each non-speech segment; the annotation tool used is not specified.

[Model-selection assessment] The entire pipeline uses only open-source or commercial off-the-shelf models, with no in-house captioner and no distillation or fine-tuning applied to Qwen3-Omni. The advantage is relatively high reproducibility (except for ElevenLabs, a commercial API) and low engineering cost; the disadvantage is that the capability ceiling of every stage is entirely bounded by the chosen off-the-shelf models, and the paper conducts no comparative experiments on tool selection.

### [2026 Other Joint Audio-Video Generation Works](../models/JAVG_2026_misc.md) ⚠️

This batch of works shows a clear evolution in captioning models from "specialized small models → general large models → multi-model division of labor":
[ALIVE — self-trained captioning model + two-round SFT + manual revision (self-built route)]
(1) The visual captioning model went through two rounds of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), trained on data first generated by an MLLM and then "manually revised caption data" — i.e., the classic self-built path of "large model generates → manual revision → SFT distillation into a dedicated captioning model," trading a one-time manual cost for low-cost large-scale inference.
(2) A second type of manual annotation: "decomposing sub-motion units to annotate the start and end timestamps of each complete small action" — decomposing actions into sub-motion units and manually annotating the start/end timestamps of each complete small action. This is extremely costly fine-grained temporal annotation, aimed at giving captions precise timeline information (serving the temporal narration in the Narration field and temporal alignment for UniTemp-RoPE).
(3) Audio information fusion: an MLLM processes the visual description to "incorporate the relevant audio information," achieving audio-visual convergence. The paper does not consistently name the base model of the main captioning model [uncertain]; Gemini 2.5 and Qwen3-Omni appear elsewhere in the pipeline.
[NAVA — three-model division of labor + dual-tier strategy (external large-model route)]
(1) Full dataset: Qwen3-VL generates video captions, Qwen3-Omni generates audio captions, and the two "are then fused by either direct concatenation or rewriting by Gemini-3-Flash." Note the either/or of "direct concatenation or rewriting," indicating a cost tiering: some data is only concatenated (zero cost), some is rewritten (with cost).
(2) High-quality subset: upgraded to Gemini-3-Pro, "to produce more accurate, structured, and temporally grounded audio-visual captions." "Temporally grounded" is the key term, echoing ALIVE's sub-motion timestamp annotation, indicating that the competitive focus of 2026 AV captioning has shifted to temporal precision.
(3) This "Flash for the full dataset + Pro for the premium subset" dual-tier strategy is the clearest caption cost-engineering practice in this batch.
[OmniCustom] Uses GLM-ASR to generate a transcript for each 5-second training audio clip; the attribute schema for the audio caption explicitly states "Following the OVI model approach," emphasizing speaker age, gender, accent, and vocal characteristics (pitch, prosody, emotion, speaking rate). The model used to generate these attribute annotations is not specified [uncertain]. Whether the visual caption is self-built is not stated [uncertain].
[StreamChar] Uses ASR timestamps ("ground-truth end indices derived from ASR timestamps") as supervision signal for the progress-aware pointer, but does not name the ASR tool [uncertain]; visual captioning relies on annotations already included in upstream datasets [uncertain].
[CCL / Baton] Neither describes any captioning model or annotation workflow at all [uncertain]. Although Baton uses Qwen3-8B as the VA-Planner backbone and regresses SigLip2 / WavTokenizer features, this is a model architecture choice, not a data annotation step; its training data's captions come from the built-in annotations of OpenHuman-Vid / AudioCaps / WavCaps, and how the self-collected internet portion is annotated is not stated [uncertain].
[Overall pattern] Larger data volumes lean toward building a proprietary captioning model (ALIVE, 11M samples → self-trained + manual revision); medium scale leans toward calling external large models (NAVA, 15M, but using a Qwen3/Gemini combination with tiered cost control); small scale directly reuses upstream annotations (Baton 1.5M, CCL 4M).

### [Joint Audio-Video Generation Baseline Collection](../models/JavisDiT_baselines.md) ⚠️

A complete evolution from "no caption" to "fully automated MLLM annotation":
[No-caption stage (MM-Diffusion / AV-DiT)] Both are unconditional generation models — generating audio-video pairs directly from Gaussian noise, accepting no text condition, so their training data requires no captions at all. The AV-DiT paper explicitly acknowledges in its limitations that it "primarily focuses on unconditional audio and video generation." This is the most fundamental paradigm difference between them and all subsequent T2AV models. MM-Diffusion's zero-shot conditional generation (V2A/A2V) is achieved via gradient guidance, which likewise involves no text captions.
[Reusing upstream captions (JavisDiT / JavisDiT++)] Does not do its own video captioning; directly uses the text captions that come with the TAVGBench dataset (TAVGBench's captions were generated by its original authors using automated methods); the 780K audio-side entries likewise use the audio text descriptions that come with datasets such as AudioCaps, Clotho, and WavCaps (of which AudioCaps and Clotho are manually annotated, and WavCaps is automatically annotated). The `text` and `audio_text` columns in data.md's CSV schema correspond to the video and audio text descriptions, respectively. The only place the JavisDiT team builds its own captions is the evaluation set JavisBench: an "advanced Qwen-series model" is used to generate captions and classify them into 19 scene categories; the specific version and scale of the model are not specified [uncertain].
[Fully automated MLLM annotation (Harmony, the most advanced in this collection)] Google Gemini is used to automatically annotate all 4 million audio-video clips, producing three types of content in one pass: ASR transcript text, descriptive video captions, and background-sound captions. Gemini's specific version (1.5 Pro / 2.0 / 2.5, etc.), prompt template, and output quality validation protocol are all undisclosed [uncertain]. Choosing Gemini over an open-source VLM indicates that its native long-video + audio joint understanding capability is what mattered — the same reasoning behind Ovi's use of an "audio-aware MLLM."
[UniAVGen] No mention of any captioning model or text annotation process [uncertain]; its task forms (audio-driven animation, video-to-audio dubbing, real-person video generation) inherently have low dependence on text captions.

### [Kling 3.0 / Kling Video 3.0 Omni](../models/Kling_30_Omni.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] The captioning model used by Kling 3.0 Omni is undisclosed; the official materials only mention a "video description enhancement" step driven by an in-house video-understanding/multimodal model. Publicly documented practices from the same team can serve as reference: Koala-36M generates seed captions with GPT-4V and then fine-tunes LLaVA as the final labeling model (a high-resolution visual encoder + 2×2 spatial pooling, trained on a mix of images and video); Kling-Foley generates audio-video descriptions via a three-stage pipeline of audio classification model + audio understanding large model + LLM fusion. Kuaishou also has its own in-house multimodal large model Kwai Keye-VL (arXiv:2507.01949), which is capable of undertaking large-scale video labeling. [Uncertain: model scale, whether it has since been switched to an in-house VLM]

### [LTX-2](../models/LTX-2.md) ⚠️

Uses Lightricks's in-house internal automatic captioner; not open-sourced, and model scale and base model are not disclosed.
[Previous generation, LTX-Video] Used an "internal automatic image and video captioner" to re-caption the entire training set, ensuring the text descriptions were accurate and relevant, improving alignment between visual content and text annotations.
[LTX-2 increment] Section 5.1 of the paper explicitly states that "a new video captioning system" was specially developed for LTX-2 training, able to describe both the visual and auditory tracks of a clip in exhaustive detail, "capturing every meaningful action, appearance, and sound." This system is positioned as "a comprehensive textual interface bridging the video, audio, and language domains, forming the descriptive foundation of LTX-2's multimodal training corpus."
[Undisclosed] The captioner's parameter count, base model, whether it is a multimodal LLM, how audio understanding is implemented (whether it is a combined pipeline of ASR + audio event detection + speaker separation), and annotation throughput and cost. Note that Gemma3-12B is the text encoder used during training/inference, not the captioner. [uncertain]

### [LongCat-Video](../models/LongCat-Video.md) ⚠️

Adopts a combination of "primary captioner + distillation-based enhancement + several specialized discriminative models," rather than a single model:
(1) Primary captioner: a fine-tuned LLaVA-Video model using in-house synthetic pairs, responsible for generating the base caption, covering both visual and temporal aspects;
(2) Temporal enhancement: annotations from Tarsier2 are introduced to participate in training/reinforcement; Tarsier2 is a video description model known for fine-grained temporal event description, used to compensate for the insufficient depiction of action progression in static descriptions;
(3) Structured attribute discrimination: Qwen2.5VL is responsible for shot scale, shot type, realism, animation style, and color tone; camera motion (pan/tilt/zoom) is handled by a separately trained lightweight classifier (rather than a VLM, presumably for cost and precision reasons);
(4) Category induction: an LLM (model not disclosed) names categories from clustering results of caption embeddings.
The parameter scale of each model is undisclosed (LLaVA-Video is commonly available in 7B/72B tiers, Qwen2.5VL commonly in 7B/72B tiers; the report does not specify which tier was used).
For Avatar 1.5, the captioner is switched to Qwen3-Omni (omni-modal, able to see both audio and video simultaneously). [Uncertain: the specific parameter scale of each captioning model]

### [MOVA](../models/MOVA.md)

Uses entirely open-source models, forming a three-model pipeline of "dual-modality division of labor annotation + large-model fusion," with no in-house captioner:
[Visual annotation] MiMo-VL-7B-RL (Xiaomi LLM-Core team, a 7B vision-language model, RL version); the instructions explicitly require focusing on video scene transitions.
[Audio annotation — dual-model strategy]
- Qwen3-Omni-Instruct: responsible for speech transcription (ASR), handling the speech component.
- Qwen3-Omni-Captioner: responsible for describing non-speech sounds and music, handling the non-speech component.
  The paper emphasizes that this dual-model division of labor "comprehensively covers linguistic content and acoustic characteristics, reduces information loss, and captures multiple facets of audio semantics."
[Fusion and consistency verification] GPT-OSS-120B (OpenAI's open-source 120B model), responsible for merging the video caption with the aggregated audio annotations (including both speech and non-speech) and performing cross-modal consistency checks.
[Inference-side prompt-enhancement model (not for training-data labeling)] Qwen3-VL extracts structured visual descriptions, and Gemini 2.5 Pro rewrites them via in-context learning into video-generation prompts consistent with the training data distribution.
[Compute] Annotation is executed on a mix of NVIDIA GPUs and Huawei Ascend NPUs.
[Scale comparison] The visual annotator is only 7B, the audio annotator is from the Qwen3-Omni series, and the fusion model is 120B — placing the largest model in the fusion/adjudication stage rather than the perception stage is a choice made under cost-effectiveness trade-offs.

### [Merged Entry: ① Mochi 1](../models/Mochi_MAGI_Motif.md) ⚠️

① Mochi 1: The labeling model is entirely undisclosed. All that is known is the text encoder used during training — a single T5-XXL (the official materials emphasize "a single T5-XXL language model rather than multiple pretrained models," i.e., deliberately avoiding multi-encoder fusion), with a text dimension of 1536 and a visual dimension of 3072; the visual stream's parameter count is about 4× that of the text stream. Whether a VLM was used for re-captioning, and the caption length and structure, are entirely unknown. [uncertain]
② MAGI-1: "an MLLM" is used to label the data, but the technical report never names the specific model or scale (it does not even state whether it is self-developed or a fine-tuned open-source model). There is one clear engineering parameter: since most mainstream MLLMs are designed for images, the team extracts a set of keyframes from each video to form an image sequence for input; empirical analysis found that "using 4 to 12 frames per video clip (depending on duration) achieves the best trade-off between description accuracy and computational efficiency." There is also one scale figure that belongs to the inference side rather than the labeling side: for lightweight deployment, the team distilled the large MLLM's enhanced prompts into a model of about 7B, with a training corpus of about 2 million entries (filtering out samples whose target text was too long, to control output length); human evaluation shows the distilled model's generation quality matches that of the large model while latency and compute cost drop substantially. [Partially uncertain: the labeling MLLM's model and scale]
③ Motif-Video 2B: explicitly named — all captions and tags come from Qwen3-VL-30B-A3B (a 30B-total/3B-activated MoE vision-language model). The video input is N frames uniformly sampled from the clip, while images are input directly. There is also an independent dedicated OCR model, PaddleOCR-VL (served via vLLM), for detecting text in frames. Regarding the text encoder: stages 1–3 use a sentence-level embedding model, and starting from stage 4 it switches to T5Gemma2 — the paper borrows PixArt-α's "class-conditional to text-conditional" curriculum idea, assuming a low-dimensional condition space can accelerate early convergence, switching to a stronger encoder once fine-grained compositional control is needed.

### [Movie Gen](../models/Movie_Gen.md)

Video side: uses LLaMa3-Video (Dubey et al., 2024) as the captioning model, fine-tuning 8B and 70B variants separately for the video captioning task, then using them to label all training clips. The scale ratio is explicit: 70% of the training set's captions come from the 8B model and 30% from the 70B model (balancing cost and quality). This is a native video captioning model (consuming video directly) rather than frame-by-frame image captioning concatenated together.
Auxiliary model: a self-trained 16-class camera-motion classifier, whose high-confidence predictions are prepended to the caption as a prefix.
Audio side: uses multiple models working together to generate synthetic captions — an audio quality prediction model (outputting a 1–10 score), an AED model (determining voice/singing presence and the posterior probability of music), a general-purpose audio captioning model (free-form description of sounds), and a music captioning model (supplementing mood and genre details). The paper specifically notes that the music captioning model is trained mainly on music samples and is prone to hallucination when there is no music, so both the AED music-probability signal and the music caption are retained together, and this combination is empirically found to give the best controllability.
In the post-training stage, captions are manually refined on top of model outputs.

### [NVIDIA NeMo Curator](../models/NeMo_Curator.md)

This field is the richest in information across version evolution, and a key reference point for model selection:
[Cosmos WFM production version (2025-01)] Uses an NVIDIA-internally fine-tuned VILA 13B as the captioner; deployed as an FP8-quantized TensorRT-LLM engine, achieving roughly a 10x speedup over unoptimized inference. Input consists of 8 frames uniformly sampled from the clip.
[NeMo Curator 25.09 / 26.02] The open-source version defaults to the Qwen-VL series (Qwen2.5-VL) as the captioning backend, and supports an optional Qwen-LM for a second-pass rewrite of the generated caption (caption enhancement / rewriting).
[26.04 addition] Nemotron Nano 12B V2 VLM is added as a captioning backend alongside Qwen-VL, offered in three precision variants: nemotron / nemotron-bf16 (default BF16, auto-downloaded), nemotron-fp8 (FP8 quantized, lower memory footprint), and nemotron-nvfp4 (NVFP4 quantization-aware distilled checkpoint).
[26.07 expansion] The captioning backend matrix is expanded to Qwen2.5-VL, Qwen3-VL, and multiple Nemotron variants (including the omni-modal Nemotron 3 Nano Omni), each optionally in BF16/FP8/NVFP4; the video preprocessing option is also removed in favor of a unified vLLM or HuggingFace preprocessing path.
[Engineering note] Captioning is explicitly identified by the official documentation as the rate-limiting stage of the entire pipeline — its throughput is significantly lower than other stages such as embedding generation, so Xenna's auto-scaling allocates the most workers to this stage. This observation is directly relevant reference value for anyone building their own video data pipeline.

### [OmniHuman Dataset + OHBench](../models/OmniHuman.md) ⚠️

Uses entirely off-the-shelf models, with no in-house captioner, forming a two-tier structure of "open-source omni-modal model as the workhorse + closed-source top-tier model as the semantic judge":
[Qwen3-Omni — the annotation inference core] Alibaba's open-source omni-modal large model (accepting video, audio, and text simultaneously), serving as the inference core for caption generation, responsible for the first-stage hierarchical feature extraction: global video context + a fine-grained attribute set (appearance, motion, expression, speech, music, sound effects). Choosing an omni-modal model over a purely visual VLM is a key decision in this work — because the content to be annotated itself spans both visual and auditory modalities (background-sound tags, music attributes, and speech emotion all require hearing the audio to annotate), a pure VLM cannot handle this. The parameter tier used is undisclosed. [uncertain]
[Gemini-3 / Gemini-3-pro — semantic judgment] Responsible for background plausibility and interaction assessment, the most difficult semantic/physical-level judgment in annotation, and also the scoring model for OHBench's subjective dimensions. Using a closed-source API model indicates the team judges open-source models to be not yet reliable enough on this dimension.
[FunASR-Nano — ASR] The lightweight tier of Alibaba DAMO Academy's FunASR series, responsible for speech transcription, producing text with speaker IDs and timestamps. The Nano tier was chosen as a cost consideration at million-scale volume.
[3D-Speaker — speaker separation] Identifies the M active speech intervals in the audio and the speaker index for each.
[SyncNet — audio-visual attribution] Determines which speech segment belongs to which person in the frame.
[ArcFace — identity embedding] Facial feature extraction, used for identity assignment and reference face selection.
[YOLOv11 + MOTRv2 — detection and tracking] Human instance detection (NMS refinement) and cross-frame association (query propagation).
[DWPose-L — pose] 134 full-skeleton keypoints (body + face + feet) plus dedicated hand detection and refinement.
[Comparison with similar works] The scale and specialization of the annotation model roster is among the highest in this survey: MOVA uses MiMo-VL-7B + Qwen3-Omni×2 + GPT-OSS-120B (four large models but no geometric pipeline); UniTalking uses Qwen3-VL + Whisper-V3 + Qwen3-Omni (three large models, zero geometric annotation); OmniHuman, by contrast, combines 2 large models with 7 specialized discriminative/geometric models, and its distinctive feature is connecting geometric perception (tracking, pose, identity) with semantic annotation (captioning) and having them cross-validate each other.
[Annotation prompts] All undisclosed. [uncertain]

### [Open-Sora Series](../models/Open-Sora.md)

[Open-Sora 1.x] The workhorse is **PLLaVA 13B** (the 13B version was chosen to balance speed and quality, configured with 2×2 spatial pooling, pooling_shape 4-12-12, input uniformly sampled to 4 frames per video). The documentation explicitly explains why GPT-4V was not used: "GPT-4V is better but 20 sec/sample is too slow for us" — a typical record of open-source projects trading off on annotation cost. There is also an LLaVA v1.6-Mistral-7B labeling script provided, and **LLaMA 3** is used to extract objects/tags from captions, with code in tools/caption/. Early versions also mention GPT-4V as an optional high-quality path.
[Open-Sora 2.0] Uses different models graded by training stage, forming a "labeling-quality pyramid" corresponding to the data pyramid: at the low-resolution (256px) stage, the massive dataset is labeled with the open-source **LLaVA-Video**; at the high-resolution (768px) stage, the curated 5M dataset is re-labeled with the stronger **Qwen 2.5 Max** (a closed-source API model), on the grounds of "obtaining more accurate captions with better semantic alignment." This "coarse labeling at the bottom + fine labeling at the top" tiered labeling is an important part of its low-cost strategy.
[Open-Sora Plan v1.3] The workhorse is **Qwen2-VL-7B-Instruct**; the Panda70M portion directly reuses its official public captions; the stock footage portion uses **ShareGPT4Video**'s annotations; the VIDAL portion retains its original OFA / mPLUG-Owl / ChatGPT multi-model refined captions. A separate **prompt refiner** was also trained: **LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct** as the base with LoRA fine-tuning (rank 64, batch 32, 1 epoch, 30 minutes on a single H100).

### [Ovi](../models/Ovi.md) ⚠️

Uses "an MLLM (multimodal large language model)" to unifiedly handle labeling for both audio-video corpora and pure-audio corpora, with the audio-video side and the pure-audio side explicitly using the same model ("the same MLLM as used in our audio-video data").
[Model identity and scale] The paper does not name it, does not state whether it is a commercial API (e.g., GPT-4o, Gemini) or an internal in-house model, and does not give a parameter scale [uncertain].
[Input form (key design)] Not purely visual input, but "7 uniformly sampled keyframes + the full audio track" fed in together — i.e., the captioning model itself must have audio understanding capability (an audio-capable MLLM), which is a core requirement distinguishing labeling for AV joint generation models from that for pure video models. The sparse 7-frame sampling implies that the visual-side description leans toward the event/semantic level rather than dense frame-by-frame description.
[Prompt engineering investment] The paper explicitly states it "conducted extensive experiments" to ensure captions cover all relevant visual and auditory events while respecting chronology, indicating the caption template went through many rounds of iteration.
[No independent ASR] Dialogue transcription is produced directly by this MLLM from the audio track; no mention of using an independent ASR model such as Whisper [uncertain].
[Inference-side counterpart] User prompts must follow the same format as the training captions; the repository provides GPT-generated example prompt CSVs (gpt_examples_t2v.csv / gpt_examples_i2v.csv), i.e., it recommends using an LLM to expand prompts according to the template.

### [Script-a-Video](../models/Script-a-Video.md) ⚠️

The labeling side involves two categories of models with a clear division of roles:
[Teacher model / data-labeling model] Gemini-2.5-Pro (Google, closed-source). Responsible for generating all MTSS labels for the 500K clips, and is the sole labeling source for the entire dataset. Parameter scale undisclosed (closed-source model).
[Student model / dedicated labeling model] Qwen3-Omni-Instruct (Alibaba Tongyi Qianwen's open-source omni-modal model). Supervised fine-tuning (the paper's original text says "supervised sine-tuning," presumably a typo for "supervised fine-tuning") was performed on the 500K MTSS data, yielding Qwen3-Omni-MTSS-FT. The paper does not disclose the specific parameter tier used (Qwen3-Omni has multiple scale variants). [uncertain]
[Quantified distillation effect] After fine-tuning on the MTSS data, the student model closes much of the gap to the teacher:
- Video-SALMONN-2 overall error rate: Qwen3-Omni native 0.5853 → zero-shot MTSS prompting 0.5156 → after MTSS fine-tuning 0.3913 (teacher Gemini-2.5-Pro native is 0.3959, i.e., after fine-tuning, the 8B/30B-tier open-source model already slightly outperforms Gemini-2.5-Pro's native caption on this metric);
- UGC-VideoCap composite score: 62.80 → 71.54 → 85.11 (teacher: 93.97);
- Daily-Omni: 0.1806 → 0.4117 → 0.5945 (teacher: 0.6825);
- WorldSense: 0.1569 → 0.3106 → 0.3875 (teacher: 0.4332).
[Comparison baselines (all captioning models)] AVoCaDO, ASID-Captioner-7B (7B parameters), Qwen3.5-Omni-Flash (closed-source), Gemini-2.5-Pro (closed-source).
[Key finding: MTSS is a zero-cost gain] The paper emphasizes that MTSS is effective for all tested models, including under fully zero-shot prompting with no fine-tuning at all — i.e., merely changing the output format requirement from "write a paragraph" to "fill in the MTSS four-stream structure" drops Gemini-2.5-Pro's overall error rate from 0.3959 to 0.2511, and Qwen3.5-Omni-Flash's from 0.5217 to 0.3655. This shows that the structured schema itself is an effective prompt-engineering technique that any team can reuse at zero cost.
[Generation-side text encoder] LTX-2's built-in Gemma-3 VLM encoder, responsible for parsing MTSS's JSON-like syntax and producing the semantic representation fed into the video/audio dual branches; multimodal input is fed in as interleaved image-text format.

### [Seedance 2.0 and Seedance 1.5 pro](../models/Seedance_20_Seedance_15_pro.md) ⚠️

Seedance 1.0: the captioning model uses Tarsier2 (an open-source model with strong video understanding) as the base, trained on manually curated high-quality annotation data, with the visual encoder frozen during training and the language model fully fine-tuned, trained on bilingual Chinese-English data to obtain bilingual capability; at inference time, a co-trained Prompt Engineering (PE) model rewrites user prompts into detailed video descriptions aligned in content and structure with the training captions. Seedance 1.5 pro states that its "advanced captioning system" provides rich, professional-level descriptions for both video and audio modalities, but does not specify the base model or parameter scale. Seedance 2.0: undisclosed. [Uncertain: the identity and scale of the captioning models for 1.5/2.0; presumably switched to the in-house Seed-VL series]

### [SkyReels Series](../models/SkyReels.md) ⚠️

[SkyReels-V2 → SkyCaptioner-V1 (open-sourced, the most valuable data-side output in this series)]
- Base: Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct (7B, a small model deployed to support labeling throughput at the hundred-million scale);
- Construction method: knowledge-distillation-style fusion — a general large MLLM (Qwen2.5-VL-72B-Instruct) first produces a general description, then three "sub-expert captioners" add film-professional dimensions, and the results of the two are fused and then distilled into the unified 7B model;
- Three sub-expert models: ① shot captioner (determining shot type/camera angle/camera position); ② expression captioner (facial expression and emotion intensity); ③ camera-motion captioner (6-DoF camera movement recognition, classification-based, trained on 93K high-confidence manually annotated samples plus 16K motion-axis-balanced synthetic samples, achieving 89% single-type motion accuracy on a 15K test set);
- Training configuration: about 2 million concept-balanced videos (curated from 10 million), global batch size 512, 32× A800;
- Effect: average accuracy on film-professional fields is 76.3%, with shot type 93.7%, camera angle 89.8%, camera position 83.1%, camera motion 85.3%, significantly outperforming larger models such as Qwen2.5-VL-72B and dedicated competitors.
[SkyReels-V4] Does not follow SkyCaptioner-V1's published approach, switching instead to a multi-model combination: visual-side captions are generated by an (unnamed) VLM at three tiers (short/long/structured); audio-side captions are generated uniformly by Qwen3-Omni; speech and singing content is transcribed by Whisper. In addition, a frozen MLLM is used as the text encoder (not a captioner) during training. V4 does not disclose the model or scale of the visual captioner. [uncertain]

### [Sora 2](../models/Sora_2.md) ⚠️

Entirely undisclosed. Sora 2 has published no information about a captioning model (in-house VLM name, scale, whether GPT-4o/GPT-5-series models are used for labeling). The prior generation can serve as reference: Sora 1's technical blog states that a "highly descriptive captioner model" was trained to generate highly descriptive text captions for training videos (following DALL·E 3's re-captioning approach), and at inference time GPT is used to expand the user's short prompt into a long, detailed caption. Sora 2 very likely continues and upgrades this approach, but there is no official confirmation and no model scale figures. [uncertain]

### [SpeakerVid-5M](../models/SpeakerVid-5M.md) ⚠️

Uses entirely open-source models, with no in-house captioner, forming a three-model division of labor — "one MLLM for visual description + one LLM for topic classification + one ASR model for transcription" — with no fusion step:
[Visual structured captioning] Qwen2.5-VL (Alibaba Tongyi Qianwen 2.5 vision-language model). The paper does not clearly state the parameter tier used (Qwen2.5-VL has 3B/7B/72B and other versions). [uncertain] The HuggingFace dataset card describes this annotation folder as "MLLM captions from qwen-vl."
[Motion intensity scoring] Also uses Qwen2.5-VL, but invoked independently via three persona prompts, a different call chain from caption generation.
[Topic classification] Qwen-3 (Alibaba Tongyi Qianwen 3 series LLM), performing category labeling of conversation topics.
[Speech transcription] Whisper (OpenAI), outputting the transcript text, confidence score, no-speech probability, compression ratio, and other diagnostic quantities.
[Pose annotation] DWpose, outputting facial, hand, and body keypoints.
[Identity and synchronization] ArcFace (face recognition), 3D-Speaker (voiceprint separation), SyncNet (lip-audio sync), YOLO (human detection and tracking), and Deep3DFaceRecon and UniSpeech (used for expression FID and timbre similarity on the VidChatBench evaluation side).
[Comparison with MOVA] MOVA uses four models in division of labor plus fusion — MiMo-VL-7B-RL + Qwen3-Omni-Instruct + Qwen3-Omni-Captioner + GPT-OSS-120B; SpeakerVid-5M's annotation model chain is lighter, and each annotation stream stays as an independent field with no LLM fusion — lower cost but also lower semantic integration.

### [Step-Video-T2V](../models/Step-Video-T2V.md) ⚠️

Uses an in-house Vision Language Model as the captioning model, generating a description for each video clip. The report does not disclose the VLM's name, parameter scale, base architecture, training data, or training method, nor does it state whether it was adapted from StepFun's own Step-1V multimodal model. No dedicated post-training for hallucination suppression was applied to the captioning model (no DPO/RLHF-type description); caption quality is instead backstopped by manual review during the SFT stage (humans "refine the caption") and by CLIP Score alignment filtering in stage 6.
The derivative model Step-Video-TI2V discloses one incremental step: an in-house video labeling model was fine-tuned specifically to strengthen description of "object motion dynamics" and "camera movement," with an example caption of the form "a flock of birds flying over a tree at sunset, camera pans left" — i.e., letting the caption explicitly carry camera-movement instructions, paired with I2V's controllable-camera-movement design. [uncertain]

### [UniTalking](../models/UniTalking.md) ⚠️

Uses entirely open-source models (mainly Alibaba's Qwen series), with no in-house captioner, with four models in a clear division of labor:
[Qwen3-VL] Responsible for purely visual video caption generation, producing both detailed and short granularities. The parameter tier used is not disclosed (Qwen3-VL has multiple scale variants). [uncertain]
[Whisper-V3] OpenAI's multilingual ASR model, responsible for speech transcription, providing the audio's textual content (dialogue lines).
[Qwen3-Omni-Captioner] The variant of the Qwen3-Omni series dedicated to audio description, responsible for generating the audio caption (describing acoustic content and environment).
[Qwen3-Omni] The omni-modal large model, taking both video and audio streams simultaneously, producing a single fused audio-visual unified description (the third caption format). This is the only one of the four models that performs cross-modal fusion. Scale tier not disclosed. [uncertain]
[IndexTTS2] Not a captioner, but part of the annotation/derived-data step; a zero-shot TTS model responsible for synthesizing reference-timbre audio.
[Text encoder] A frozen UMT5 encoder is used for text conditioning during both training and inference, with the text latent sequence length fixed at 512 (truncated if too long, zero-padded if too short).
[Design feature: two paths for labeling the same content] There is both a "label separately then concatenate" path (Qwen3-VL + Qwen3-Omni-Captioner/Whisper-V3) and a "unified model fusion labeling" path (Qwen3-Omni); the products of both paths are randomly sampled during training — a way of delegating "the choice of labeling strategy" to random sampling, letting the model adapt to both text distributions simultaneously; see caption_structure.
[Scale comparison with similar works] The total annotation model scale is noticeably smaller than MOVA's (MiMo-VL-7B + Qwen3-Omni×2 + GPT-OSS-120B) but larger than UniVerse-1's (QWen2.5-Omni + Whisper); and UniTalking's labeling is done offline as a one-time pass, unlike UniVerse-1, which generates labels online in real time during training.
[Annotation prompts] All undisclosed, with no caption examples given at all (Figure 4 shows a prompt, but it is only a test prompt used at inference time, not an original training caption). [uncertain]

### [UniVerse-1](../models/UniVerse-1.md) ⚠️

Uses entirely open-source models, with no in-house captioner, with far fewer models and far smaller scale than comparable works:
[Multimodal annotation] QWen2.5-Omni (Alibaba Tongyi Qianwen's omni-modal model), responsible for all three labeling streams — simultaneously outputting verified speech content, a descriptive video caption, and an ambient-sound caption. This is a single model producing multiple outputs, rather than MOVA's three-model division of labor. The paper does not disclose the specific parameter scale tier used (3B / 7B). [uncertain]
[Speech transcription] Whisper (OpenAI), performing ASR. In the offline funnel it is used to determine whether a clip contains speech (a routing gate); in online labeling it is used to transcribe the audio of the sampled window.
[No fusion model] There is no MOVA-style large-parameter LLM fusion and consistency-adjudication step (MOVA uses GPT-OSS-120B). The three labeling streams are produced in one pass, in parallel, by QWen2.5-Omni, remaining as independent fields.
[Text encoder] Text conditioning on both the training and inference sides uses umT5 (both base experts share the same text encoder family, which is also one of the preconditions for the SoE stitching to work).
[Evaluation-side labeling model] The Set2-V subset of Verse-Bench uses an LLM to generate captions plus Whisper for ASR, followed by manual verification.
[Scale comparison] UniVerse-1's total labeling model scale (QWen2.5-Omni + Whisper) is far smaller than MOVA's (MiMo-VL-7B + Qwen3-Omni×2 + GPT-OSS-120B), which is directly tied to its online-labeling architecture choice — online labeling requires the labeling model to be light enough to keep up with training throughput, and cannot bear the per-sample inference overhead of a 120B-tier model. This is a trade-off between "labeling timing" and "labeling model capacity."

### [Unison](../models/Unison.md) ⚠️

The paper does not disclose which model generated the training data's captions — this is the most significant gap in the labeling-method field.
[Confirmed models and their uses, strictly distinguishing training side from evaluation side]
- Gemini (specific version undisclosed): used only on the evaluation side. Generates ground-truth annotations for 1,000 held-out test samples (supporting T2AV/TI2AV evaluation), and serves as the LLM judge scoring Motion-Speech-SFX coherence in the user study. Not seen used for training data labeling. [uncertain]
- Whisper-large-v3: used only on the evaluation side. When computing WER, Mel-RoFormer is first used to separate vocals from the generated audio, then Whisper-large-v3 transcribes them for comparison against the target text. The paper does not state whether the training data's transcriptions were also produced by Whisper — but the model's text input explicitly includes a separate transcription-text channel, so training must have a source of transcription; the most likely scenario is either reusing upstream datasets' built-in transcripts or offline transcription with Whisper, and the paper confirms neither possibility. [uncertain]
- Mel-RoFormer: used on both training and evaluation sides. On the training side, it decouples mixed audio into speech and sound-effect ground-truth latents; on the evaluation side, it performs vocal separation before WER computation. It is the only tool model used across both training and evaluation.
[Inferred source of training captions] Unison's input includes both caption text and transcription text channels, and the SCG gating depends on the mean-pooled global semantic vector of both — so every training sample must have both a caption and a transcription. The most likely source is upstream datasets' built-in annotations: CelebV-Text is itself a "video + text description" paired dataset, OpenHumanVid also publishes accompanying captions, VGGSound comes with sound-event category labels, and WavCaps is an audio dataset with captions. If so, Unison did not build its own captioner, and caption density and style would exhibit significant heterogeneity across the five data sources (CelebV-Text's facial attribute descriptions vs. VGGSound's category labels vs. WavCaps's audio captions), and the paper gives no indication of how this heterogeneity is unified. This is an inference made in this entry, with no support from the original text. [uncertain]
[No in-house captioner] The paper mentions no self-developed or fine-tuned annotation model of any kind.

### [Veo 3 / Veo 3.1](../models/Veo_3_Veo_31.md) ⚠️

Uses "multiple Gemini models" to text-caption both audio and video clips — this is a core data-processing method repeatedly emphasized in the official technical report and Model Card. Using multiple models rather than a single model corresponds to a multi-granularity labeling need for "different levels of detail" (presumably: large models produce long dense descriptions, small models produce short descriptions to control cost). [uncertain] The specific Gemini version(s) used (1.5 / 2.0 / 2.5 series), parameter scale, whether an internal dedicated variant fine-tuned for video labeling is used, and labeling throughput and cost are all undisclosed.

### [Vidu S1](../models/Vidu_S1.md) ⚠️

Uses an "annotation model" to generate structured natural-language descriptions, and uses an omni model (omni-modal large model) during the quality-filtering stage. The paper does not disclose whether these models are in-house or open-source, nor their parameter scale. [uncertain]

### [Wan 2.5 / 2.6 / 2.7](../models/Wan.md) ⚠️

Wan 2.1 devotes an entire section (3.3 Dense Video Caption) to describing its in-house captioning model, with a level of detail that ranks among the most complete in comparable reports; 2.5+ is undisclosed.
[Motivation] Original web text descriptions are "too simplistic to convey detailed visual content"; citing the DALL-E 3 finding that training with highly descriptive generative captions can significantly improve a generative model's prompt-following ability, the in-house captioning model was built to re-label every image/video in the dataset.
[Architecture] LLaVA-style: a ViT visual encoder extracts image and video frame embeddings → a two-layer perceptron projection → fed into a Qwen LLM.
- Image side: uses LLaVA-style dynamic high resolution, splitting an image into up to 7 patches, with each patch's visual embedding adaptively pooled to a 12×12 grid to reduce compute.
- Video side: 3 FPS sampling, up to 129 frames; a slow-fast encoding strategy is used — every 4th frame retains its original resolution, while the embeddings of the remaining frames undergo global average pooling. The report gives a quantified benefit: under limited visual tokens, VideoMME (without subtitles) improves from 67.6% to 69.1%.
[Three-stage training] Stage 1 freezes the ViT and LLM, training only the MLP for visual-language space alignment (lr=1e-3); stage 2 trains all parameters; stage 3 trains end-to-end on a small amount of high-quality data (LLM and MLP lr=1e-5, ViT lr=1e-6).
[Training data] Open-source vision-language datasets (captions + visual QA, covering actions/counting/OCR) + pure-text instruction data (enabling the captioning model to produce captions of a specific style or content per user instruction) + a large volume of self-built data (see caption_structure).
[Results] Using a self-built automatic evaluation pipeline (based on CAPability), across 10 dimensions with 1,000 randomly sampled videos each, compared against Gemini 1.5 Pro by F1: the in-house model performs better on events (88.0 vs 52.6), camera angle (89.1 vs 52.6, the largest gain), camera motion, style, and object color; Gemini performs better on actions, OCR, scene, object category, and counting. Overall it is on par with Gemini 1.5 Pro.
[Parameter scale] The specific sizes of the ViT and Qwen LLM are not published. [uncertain]
[Other labeling models in the same family] Wan2.2-S2V labels directly with QwenVL2.5-72B; Wan 2.1's V2A audio captions use Qwen2-Audio; the visual-text branch uses Qwen2-VL.

### [Audio-Video Generation Benchmark Collection](../models/av_benchmarks.md) ⚠️

On the benchmark side, "labeling" mainly refers to prompt construction and QA-pair generation; the models used are as follows:
[VABench] Prompt generation uses a general-purpose LLM (specific model not named [uncertain]) with expert templates; the I2AV path uses a multimodal LLM to generate a unified audio-visual description from an image (model not named [uncertain]); the MLLM scorer at evaluation time is explicitly Qwen2.5-Omni 7B.
[AVBench] The evaluator is a self-trained model: Qwen2.5-Omni 7B (VT, AV dimensions, only the LLM part fine-tuned) and Qwen2-Audio 7B (AT dimension, LLM + connector fine-tuned); hard-negative construction uses LLM-driven perturbation (model not named [uncertain]).
[AV-SyncBench] Data quality control uses Gemini 3 Flash; semantic perturbation uses OpenVoice V2 (voice-timbre replacement) and a pretrained DDSP (instrument-timbre transfer); no caption generation is involved.
[PhyAVBench] An LLM is used for knowledge research, taxonomy construction, prompt-template generation, and preliminary quality screening (model not named [uncertain]); transcription for the speech task uses Whisper-Large V3 to compute WER.
[Omni-Judge] The judge model under test is Qwen3-Omni (30B total / 3B activated), comparing the instruct variant against the reasoning-enhanced variant.

### [Video Captioning Model Ecosystem](../models/caption_models.md) ⚠️

The core field of this entry. A complete ecosystem map organized by "who is used for labeling":

[First tier: open-source captioners trained specifically for video labeling (highest downstream reuse rate)]
· Tarsier2-7B (ByteDance, 2025-01): pretrained on 11M→40M video-text pairs, SFT for fine-grained temporal alignment, model-based sampling to construct preference pairs + DPO. DREAM-1K F1 exceeds GPT-4o by 2.8% and Gemini-1.5-Pro by 5.8%, with human evaluation gains of +8.6% / +24.9%; it is the first model to surpass 40% overall recall on DREAM-1K, and SOTA on 15 public benchmarks. Naturally describing camera motion type is its unique advantage. Downstream reuse: Seedance 1.0 (as the base, with frozen visual encoder + fully fine-tuned LLM + bilingual Chinese-English training), Goku (video-level caption, complementing InternVL2.0's keyframe caption, fused via Qwen2), LongCat-Video (temporal enhancement) — it is the single open-source captioner most widely adopted on the generation side.
· ShareCaptioner-Video (USTC + Shanghai AI Lab + CUHK, 2024-06): based on InternLM-XComposer2-4KHD, SFT-trained on 40K GPT-4V dense captions, producing 4.8M high-quality aesthetic video annotations. Its core method, DiffSW (differential sliding window) — converts "full frame set to caption" into a differential description task: a detailed caption is first generated for the first frame, then a sliding window of length 2 processes subsequent frames in order, with each step taking "previous frame + its differential caption + current frame" as input, and outputting four categories of change: camera motion, object motion, character action, and scene transition. Supports arbitrary duration, aspect ratio, and resolution, with an accompanying clip summarization capability (no need to reprocess frames). Open-Sora Plan v1.3's stock footage portion directly uses its annotations.
· CogVLM2-Caption (Zhipu, open-sourced 2024-09-19): built on CogVLM2-Video + Llama3, about 12B in scale, fine-tuned on dense captions produced by the Panda-70M→CogVLM→GPT-4→LLaMA2 teacher chain; it is the only reproducible link in CogVideoX's data pipeline.
· SkyCaptioner-V1 (Kunlun Wanwei, 2025-04): Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct base (a small model to support hundred-million-scale throughput), knowledge-distillation-style fusion — Qwen2.5-VL-72B produces a general description + three sub-expert captioners (shot type/camera angle/camera position, facial expression and emotion intensity, 6-DoF camera motion) → fused → distilled into 7B. Average accuracy on film-professional fields is 76.3%, significantly exceeding larger general-purpose models.
· AuroraCap (academic, 2024-10): token merging to compress visual tokens, three-stage training on 20 million image/video-text pairs, Flickr30k CIDEr 88.9 (vs GPT-4V 55.3, Gemini-1.5-Pro 82.2), with an accompanying VDC benchmark and VDCScore metric.

[Second tier: general-purpose VLMs used directly as labelers (lowest cost, most common)]
· The Qwen series is the de facto standard for 2025–2026: Qwen2-VL-7B-Instruct (Open-Sora Plan v1.3's workhorse), Qwen2.5-VL (LongCat-Video's structured attribute discrimination; SkyCaptioner's base), Qwen3-VL (UniTalking's visual caption; MOVA's inference-side prompt enhancement), Qwen3-VL-30B-A3B (the source of all captions and tags for Motif-Video 2B), Qwen3.5-27B / Qwen3.5-35B-A3B (CineDance's narrative parsing and visual annotation). Choosing an MoE sparse architecture (about 3B activated) to balance throughput and quality is a new trend in 2026.
· The LLaVA family: PLLaVA-13B (Open-Sora 1.x's workhorse, configured with 2×2 spatial pooling, 4 sampled frames; the documentation explicitly explains that GPT-4V was not used because "20 sec/sample is too slow"), LLaVA-Video (Open-Sora 2.0's low-resolution stage, LongCat-Video's primary captioner), LLaVA v1.6-Mistral-7B, and the LLaVA fine-tuned from GPT-4V seed captions used by Koala-36M.
· InternVL2.0 (Goku's keyframe caption), Aria 25.3B MoE / 3.9B activated (Allegro's fine-grained labeler, able to generate a caption for a 256-frame video within 10 seconds), MiMo-VL-7B-RL (MOVA's visual annotation), Tag2Text (Allegro's coarse-grained tagger).

[Third tier: omni-modal / joint audio-video captioners (an explosion starting Q4 2025)]
· AVoCaDO (Kuaishou Kling + CASIA et al., 2025-10): Qwen2.5-Omni-7B base (labeled 9B end-to-end on HF), AVoCaDO-SFT-107K → GRPO. Reward = ℛ_C (five-dimensional checklist coverage, judged by GPT-4.1) + ℛ_D (dialogue F1, speaker accuracy and content similarity, edit distance + dynamic programming to find the optimal aligned subsequence, threshold 0.6) + ℛ_L (length regularization, penalizing >4096 tokens). Apache-2.0.
· AVSCap-7B (Nanjing University NJU-LINK + Kuaishou Kling, 2026-07): same base, AVSCap-130K → GRPO (length control + speech preservation + AV consistency), AVSCapBench overall score 60.44, matching Gemini-3-Pro (60.97). The paper's key thesis: RL gains > scaling SFT data volume.
· video-SALMONN 2 / 2+ (Tsinghua EE + ByteDance, iterating from 2025-06 to 2026-02): LLaVA-OneVision + LoRA-added audio capability, 3B/7B/72B, MrDPO (multi-round DPO, periodically resetting the reference policy with a newly trained lightweight adapter), caption error rate reduced 28% relative to baseline, Apache-2.0.
· Qwen3-Omni-Captioner (Alibaba, 2025-09, audio-dedicated) and Qwen3.5-Omni (2026-03, API-only): the latter positions "script-level fine-grained description, automatic slicing, timestamp labeling, and character-audio relationship description" directly as a training-data-generation capability for video synthesis models, with Omni-Cloze 64.8 > Gemini-3.1 Pro's 57.2. Comes with Omni-Detective (an agentic Query-Observation loop annotation framework).
· Qwen2.5-Omni / Qwen3-Omni (widely used as audio-side or fusion-side labelers by UniVerse-1, UniTalking, MOVA, SkyReels-V4, InstructAV2AV, Apollo/Klear, LongCat Avatar 1.5).

[Fourth tier: closed-source APIs used as teachers or labelers for high-value subsets]
· GPT-4V / GPT-4 / GPT-4.1: ShareGPT4Video-40K, Koala-36M seed captions, MiraData's structured captions, CogVideoX's summarization, AVoCaDO's quality control.
· Gemini series (2.5-Pro / 3-Pro / 3-Flash): AVoCaDO's teacher, all of Script-a-Video's 500K MTSS annotations, Harmony's 4 million audio-video annotations, Foley-Omni's primary annotation (chosen for native support of joint video+audio input, which a purely visual VLM cannot handle for audio-presence discrimination), Apollo/Klear's high-value subset, Veo 3's "multiple Gemini models."
· Qwen 2.5 Max (Open-Sora 2.0's re-labeling of the high-resolution curated 5M dataset).

[Fifth tier: generation-side in-house and fully closed-source]
· OpenAI Sora: a "highly descriptive captioner model" (following DALL·E 3's re-captioning approach); Sora 2 discloses nothing.
· Meta Movie Gen: LLaMa3-Video, 8B and 70B variants fine-tuned separately for video captioning, with 70% of the training set's captions coming from the 8B model and 30% from the 70B model — the only work to publicly disclose a "large/small model mixing ratio" figure. Also a 16-class camera-motion classifier. Four models collaborate on the audio side (audio quality prediction, 1–10 score; AED for voice/singing presence and music posterior; general-purpose audio captioning; music captioning).
· Tencent Hunyuan: the original version uses a single in-house VLM producing JSON-structured captions; 1.5 upgrades to a three-model division of labor (image caption, video caption, image-to-video instructional caption) + a camera-motion recognition model, with OPA-DPO used to suppress hallucination.
· Lightricks LTX-2: a new captioning system developed specifically for joint audio-video generation, describing both the visual and auditory tracks in exhaustive detail.
· StepFun Step-Video: an in-house VLM; the TI2V version was specifically fine-tuned to strengthen descriptions of "object motion dynamics" and "camera movement."
· Kuaishou Kling 3.0 Omni: officially described only as having a "video description enhancement" step, with the model undisclosed [uncertain]; the same team's publicly documented practices are Koala-36M's LLaVA fine-tuning route and the in-house Kwai Keye-VL (arXiv:2507.01949).
· ByteDance Seedance 1.5 pro / 2.0: states it has an "advanced captioning system" providing professional-level descriptions for video and audio, with the base model unspecified [uncertain; presumably switched to the in-house Seed-VL series].

[Key selection patterns (cross-entry synthesis)]
(1) Parameter counts widely converge to 7B: ShareCaptioner, Tarsier2, SkyCaptioner-V1, AVoCaDO, AVSCap, and MiMo-VL are all in the 7B tier, because labeling at the hundred-million-sample scale requires controlling per-sample inference cost; placing large models (GPT-OSS-120B, Qwen2.5-72B) at the fusion/adjudication/teacher stage rather than the perception stage is a consistent industry-wide cost-effectiveness trade-off.
(2) Multi-model division of labor beats a single do-it-all model: Panda-70M's empirical result is the most compelling — a single best labeler covers only 30.8% of samples, while a combination of 8 covers 76.8%. Goku (image VLM + video VLM + LLM fusion), MOVA (visual + ASR + non-speech + 120B fusion), and SkyCaptioner (general + three sub-experts) are all implementations of this idea.
(3) Camera-motion recognition uses dedicated classifiers rather than VLMs: Movie Gen's 16-class classifier, LongCat's lightweight classifier, Hunyuan's independent classifier, SkyCaptioner's 6-DoF sub-expert (89% accuracy) — a consistent industry choice.
(4) Distillation for cost reduction is a standard move: GPT-4V/Gemini teacher → 7B student, done by nearly every work. Script-a-Video gives the most complete quantification of distillation gains (see data_ablation).
(5) The watershed of 2026 is "can it hear": a purely visual captioner cannot serve joint audio-video generation models; an omni captioner has become an essential component for AV generation models.

### [Geometric/Structured Annotation Dataset Collection](../models/geometric_datasets.md) ⚠️
