SceneScribe-1M: Qwen2.5-VL-72B (open-source VLM, 72B scale), leveraging its dynamic resolution handling and absolute time encoding capability to process long videos, with the same model simultaneously handling both quality filtering and caption generation; SpatialVID: a two-model cascade — Gemini-2.0-Flash (closed-source commercial API) performs visual parsing on 1fps-sampled frames to produce a draft, Qwen3-30B-A3B (open-source MoE, 30B total/3B activated) performs linguistic refinement and injects camera-pose priors to correct motion direction; WildWorld: uses a vision-language model to generate two-tier captions at both the action-sequence level and the sample level, with the specific model not specified in the paper [uncertain]; Action100M: three-model division of labor — Llama-3.2-Vision-11B handles frame-level (middle-frame) captioning, Perception-LM-3B (PLM-3B) handles video-clip-level captioning, and GPT-OSS-120B (a 120B reasoning model) handles evidence aggregation and structured field extraction, with total labeling compute of about 1.3 million V100 GPU-hours plus 300,000 H100/H200 GPU-hours.

### [Post-Training Data Strategy for Video Generation](../models/post_training_data.md) ⚠️

The anchor paper does not disclose the captioning model for SFT data [uncertain], but its Phase 3 prompt enhancement (PE) is in essence "training an LLM to rewrite prompts using GRPO," adopting the RePrompt (arXiv:2505.17540) paradigm, freezing the generation backbone and optimizing only the policy πθ, so it can be seamlessly plugged into any off-the-shelf T2I/T2V generator. PE's model scale, base model name, and training prompt corpus size are all undisclosed [uncertain].
[Cross-work post-training-stage caption/prompt model practices]
· NAVA's practice is the most notable: captions for the curated SFT subset (160K) are regenerated by the more expensive Gemini-3-Pro (the original pretraining captions were generated by Flash), i.e., a "curated subset + upgraded annotation quality" double purification — the SFT stage changes not only the data but also the labeler;
· Open-Sora 2.0: the curated 5M dataset in stage three is re-labeled with Qwen2.5-Max;
· Movie Gen: the SFT set's captions are manually rewritten (the final stage of a four-stage process);
· Step-Video-T2V: the manual review step of SFT also refines captions;
· MAGI-1's inference-side Prompt Enhancement small model (about 7B) is distilled from a corpus of about 2 million MLLM-generated enhanced prompts, filtering out samples whose target text was too long to control output length — the only publicly disclosed figure for PE training data scale;
· Open-Sora Plan's prompt refiner: 19,500 captions, LLaMA-3.1-8B LoRA (rank 64, batch 32, 1 epoch, 30 minutes on a single H100) — a lower-bound cost example for PE;
· MOVA uses an inference-side prompt rewriter (Qwen3-VL extracts structured visual description + Gemini 2.5 Pro rewrites it via in-context learning into a prompt consistent with the training distribution) in place of post-training instruction alignment, raising the human Arena ELO from 982.9 to 1025.3 — a quantified demonstration of PE's standalone value.
[Pattern] Re-captioning is a standard step at the SFT stage, not an optional one: labeling pretraining data cheaply in bulk, then re-labeling the SFT subset with the most expensive model or by hand, is the currently recognized cost-optimal configuration.

### [Merged Survey of Mainstream Video Pretraining Datasets: Panda-70M, InternVid, Koala…](../models/pretraining_datasets.md) ⚠️

**The choice of labeling model directly determines caption length, spanning a 60x range from 13.2 words to 824.2 words.**
[Panda-70M · multi-teacher + retrieval-based selection, the most architecturally complex yet producing the shortest output] The candidate pool has **31 labelers = 6 base models × weight variants × input-modality combinations** (V=visual/S=subtitle/M=metadata, i.e., title+description): Video-LLaMA (8 variants combining pretrain and finetune, **using only the visual branch, with the audio branch explicitly disabled**, Vicuna-7B), VideoChat (7B, 4 variants), VideoChat Text (4 variants, textualizing video into tags + dense description + overall description then dialoguing; ChatGPT-4 was swapped for LLaMA due to cost), Video-ChatGPT (4 variants), BLIP-2 (opt2.7b/opt6.7b/flant5xl, 3 variants, the image model randomly picking one frame from every 0.3N–0.7N frames), MiniGPT-4 (7B/13B, 8 variants). A user study on 1,000 clips performs **greedy set cover**: the single best model covers only 30.8%, all 31 together cover 84.7%, and the **greedily selected 8** cover 76.8% (Video-ChatGPT is in the pool but not selected). The visual-only prompt is "Please faithfully summarize the video (or image) in one sentence." The **selection step** uses fine-grained video-text retrieval: **UMT large = ViT-L/16 + BERT-large**, dual VTC+VTM losses + **hard negative mining (7 unselected captions weighted 1.0, other in-batch negatives weighted 0.01)**, 12 frames at 224×224, AdamW lr 2e-5, batch 32, 10 epochs, 8×A100-80G. Effect: fine-tuned UMT achieves **R@1 35.90%** vs. the pretrained UMT's 21.82%, **while human-to-human agreement is only 44.9%** (showing that "the best caption" is highly subjective). **Distilled student model**: the visual branch follows the Video-LLaMA design (8 frames → frozen ViT-G/14 (EVA-CLIP) + Q-Former → temporal fusion → 32×4096), the text branch uses a **text Q-Former** to produce a fixed 32×4096 using the video representation as query, concatenated to 64×4096 and fed into **Vicuna-7B-v0**; gradients from text back to visual are blocked, and metadata and subtitles are each independently dropped with probability 0.5; trained for **300K steps on 48× A100-80G** over the full Panda-70M dataset. The motivation is very practical: the complete pipeline requires **8+1=9** model inference passes per clip.
[InternVid · multi-scale, using different models at different scales — the most commonly misreported point] **The coarse scale uses BLIP-2 to describe the middle frame; the fine scale uses the lightweight Tag2Text to describe frames one by one at a low fps, then a pretrained language model synthesizes them into a full description.** **The fine scale is not BLIP-2.** The identity of this LLM is not made clear in the paper (the citations list both T5 and Vicuna without stating which was actually used); the `InternVid-18M-aes-vc2vicuna.jsonl` file on HF suggests the existence of a VideoChat2+Vicuna re-labeling pass, but that is evidence of a subsequent re-labeling, not of the original summarizer. **The original summarization prompt text, Tag2Text's fps, and the specific UMT variant used are all undisclosed.** [uncertain]
[Koala-36M · GPT-4V teacher → LLaVA student distillation] First uses **GPT-4V** on a seed set to produce captions following a structured schema, then fine-tunes an **LLaVA-based** labeler to run at full scale (**not Tarsier**). The fine-tuning lessons are worth reusing: (a) training the visual encoder (not freezing it) improves caption accuracy; (b) a high-resolution visual encoder helps capture detail; (c) to control token overhead, **2×2 average pooling is applied to the spatial dimension of visual tokens**; (d) **mixed image + video training**, which teaches both static/dynamic understanding and mitigates video data scarcity.
[MiraData · GPT-4V single-turn dialogue producing 5 fields + Panda-70M supplying the short caption] Of 6 fields, **5 are generated by GPT-4V in the same dialogue turn**, and 1 (short_caption) directly uses **Panda-70M's labeling model**. The input form is distinctive: **8 frames uniformly sampled and tiled into a single 2×4 grid image** (arranged chronologically left-to-right, top-to-bottom, separated by white borders), with the Panda-70M short caption injected into the prompt as a **hint**. The repository also has a `default_prompt_wo_hit` variant that removes the hint entirely, with the authors stating that "on a small sample the results are almost identical" — **which actually undermines the necessity of the Panda-70M step**.
[OpenVid-1M] **LLaVA-v1.6-34B** re-labels all videos, including supplementing captions for the originally caption-less CelebvHQ. **The original prompt text is undisclosed.** [uncertain]
[LVD-2M · three-stage hierarchical] (1) Videos longer than 30 seconds are first cut into **30-second segments**; (2) each segment has **6 frames uniformly sampled and tiled into a 2×3 grid single image** (method sourced from IG-VLM), fed into **LLaVA-v1.6-34B**, producing background, main character, main action, and camera viewpoint, with an **adaptive sampling interval (segment length / 6, capped at 5 seconds)**; (3) **Claude3-Haiku** refines and merges (two separate prompts handle "refining a single coarse caption" and "synthesizing the whole-segment description chronologically" respectively), motivated by the observation that "LLaVA-v1.6-34B tends to produce extra speculation and assumptions, leading to redundancy"; (4) the repository also contains a third track not mentioned in the paper, `rewritten_caption`, rewritten by **LLaMA-v3.1-70B** into a "concise user-input style." On 100 sampled examples, average word count: raw 215.4 → refined 81.8 → rewritten 41.3, **Claude3-Haiku compresses LLaVA's output by about 2.6x**.
[UltraVideo · fully open-source models, the largest scale] **Qwen2.5-VL-72B** produces 9 dimensions, then **Qwen3-4B** integrates them into a 10th, Summarized Description. The authors explicitly compare themselves against MiraData (GPT-4V + 2×4 grid) and Koala-36M (GPT-4V distilled into LLaVA), emphasizing their own use of open-source models. **Neither the prompt nor the frame-assembly method is disclosed** — GitHub issue #3 specifically asked about this and was closed with no maintainer response. [uncertain]
[Cross-cutting pattern] In the first half of 2024 (Panda-70M, InternVid), the field followed an engineering-heavy route of "multiple small models + fusion/selection"; from the second half of 2024 onward (MiraData, Koala-36M, OpenVid, LVD-2M), it uniformly shifted to "one strong VLM (GPT-4V/LLaVA-34B) labels directly + LLM refines"; in 2025 (UltraVideo) it shifted further to "large-parameter open-source VLM (72B) + small LLM aggregation." **The fusion complexity of teacher models is decreasing, while single-model capability is increasing.**
## Caption density and structuring level (short/long/dense descriptions, structured fields such as camera movement, style tags)

`caption_structure` · Detail level: detailed

### [Allegro](../models/Allegro.md) ⚠️

Belongs to the "long dense description + semi-structured field embedding" form:
· Coverage dimensions (as enumerated in the original paper): attributes of the subjects, interactions among the subjects, background description, environment, style, atmosphere, camera angle and motion, and temporal changes — i.e., covering both spatial and temporal information simultaneously.
· Composition method: video caption = static frame caption of the middle frame + dynamic caption of the entire video, concatenated together, balancing single-frame detail fidelity with whole-clip dynamic narration.
· Structured fields: camera motion is explicitly highlighted with a fixed sentence pattern — sentences of the form "Camera [MOTION_PATTERN]" are inserted into the caption, enabling the model to learn controllable camera-movement instruction response; the remaining dimensions are expressed in natural-language prose, not broken out into independent JSON fields.
· Dual granularity coexists: all samples retain both coarse-grained (Tag2Text global semantic tags) and fine-grained (Aria spatiotemporal detail) annotation sets simultaneously, the former mainly used for filtering and statistics, the latter for training.
· Caption length is constrained by the T5 encoder's 512-token cap.
· The caption length distribution and caption diversity rewriting (recaption / prompt upsampling) strategy are not disclosed. [Partially uncertain: length distribution and mixed training ratio of short/long captions]

### [Apollo](../models/Apollo.md) ⚠️

【Overall positioning】The paper positions its dataset as "the first large-scale audio–video dataset with dense captions," with dense and accurate being the two key attributes it claims for itself, but no actual example of the caption's form is given.
【Structuring level】The paper explicitly states the caption covers two levels: "including both meta information and detailed content." The meta-information layer includes structured fields such as speaker attributes (gender, age); the detailed-content layer is a natural-language description. This is a hybrid structure of "structured meta + natural-language body text."
【Conditional branching】Caption content varies with the audio subset: the speech/singing subset contains transcription text + speaker attributes + audio caption + video caption; the sound split contains only audio caption + video caption, without transcription or speaker attributes. That is, the caption schema is dynamically trimmed according to the data category.
【Final form】"merged into unified dense captions" — all annotations are ultimately merged into a unified dense caption, with no separate fields retained (consistent with MOVA's path of "branching at annotation time, fusing at training time").
【Model-side conditioning channels】Notably, at the model architecture level the input is four-way rather than one-way: video, video-related text, audio, and audio-related text — i.e., the video caption and audio caption remain two separate text conditions at the input-channel level, and are not merged into a single prompt. This is in tension with the "unified dense captions" statement; a possible explanation is that a unified caption is generated on the data side and then split back into two channels at training time, or the two statements describe different stages.
【Key omissions】Caption length distribution, word-count statistics, the original prompt text, complete caption examples, and whether structured fields such as camera movement/style tags are included — none of this is disclosed. [Uncertain]

### [CineDance / CineDance-1M](../models/CineDance.md)

The density and structuring level of the annotation is this dataset's biggest generational gap relative to existing work; the paper calls it "Configurable / Hierarchical Dual-Modal Annotation":
【Density】An average of 6,496.3 words of structured annotation per video (Tab.6), far exceeding datasets like MiraData that only have video-level captions, and it is the only one among comparable works to provide shot-level dense annotation.
【Two-layer structure】
  · Global layer: a global character list [⟨char₁⟩,…,⟨charₙ⟩] and a global scene list [⟨scene₁⟩,…,⟨sceneₘ⟩], defined as anchor tokens;
  · Shot-level layer: each shot's description explicitly references the above anchor tokens, achieving cross-shot identity and scene binding.
【Value of the anchor-token mechanism】This is the key design for solving multi-shot consistency — the generative model can thereby know that "⟨char₂⟩ in shot 3 and ⟨char₂⟩ in shot 17 are the same person"; on the evaluation side, CineBench also directly uses this token as the judgment anchor for identity continuity (ArcFace clustering) and scene continuity (DINOv2 cosine similarity).
【Shot-level structured fields】Five-dimensional shot attributes: scale, angle, movement, narrative function, and duration category; plus shot transition type, localized character list (characters appearing in this shot), active scene (the scene this shot is in), shot description, and transition description.
【The meaning of "configurable"】All fields and quality scores can be combined and filtered, allowing users to assemble task-specific subsets on demand (e.g., only those with camera-movement annotation, or only those with high aesthetic scores).
【Model-side usage format】During training it is organized as: a global header (character/scene definitions) + per-shot blocks [SHOT i | scene sᵢ | camera κᵢ] ⊕ transition description div ⊕ dialogue dia ⊕ {(speaker spkᵢ,ℓ, line speechᵢ,ℓ)}.

### [CogVideoX](../models/CogVideoX.md)

· Density: explicitly pursues dense long captions. The paper criticizes existing datasets such as Panda70M, COCO Caption, and WebVid for captions that are "usually very short and fail to describe the video comprehensively," and therefore builds its own pipeline to produce paragraph-level long descriptions. The comparative example in Appendix H is highly representative: for the same video, Panda-70M outputs "A crab is walking on the beach with a light bulb on its back." (one sentence), while CogVLM2-Caption outputs a paragraph of nearly 80 words, including subject appearance (dark glossy shell, reddish-brown legs), action, lighting changes (from a soft glow to a more pronounced illumination), setting (sandy terrain, tranquil sea backdrop), time (at night), and an atmosphere assessment (serene yet whimsical atmosphere).
· Structuring level: this is an "unstructured dense natural-language paragraph" — there is no Movie-Gen-style explicit field (no independent camera-motion label prefix, no FPS token, no enumerated style tags). But the GPT-4 summarization prompt forcibly constrains content coverage and writing style, forming a de facto implicit schema: it must cover five categories of elements — objects, scenery, animals, characters, camera movements; it must describe the content and its changes in chronological order; and it forbids fixed formulaic openings to avoid style collapse.
· Length constraint: the Text Length cap on the text-encoder (T5) side is 226 tokens, which is the actual ceiling on caption length.
· Train-inference distribution alignment: because users' actual input is far shorter than the training captions, a dedicated caption upsampler was designed to rewrite short prompts into long descriptions at inference time, consistent with DALL·E 3's approach.

### [Cosmos-Predict2.5](../models/Cosmos-Predict25.md) ⚠️

The structuring level is reflected in three dimensions: "temporal-granularity segmentation + three length tiers + domain-customized fields."
【Temporal granularity】Each clip is cut into 5-second windows and captioned window by window, rather than one sentence for the whole clip. The paper lists this as one of the core improvements over Cosmos-Predict1: "introduces finer content granularity by segmenting clips into shorter temporal windows," with the aim of obtaining "richer and more precise supervision signals."
【Three length tiers】Captions are uniformly produced in three lengths — short / medium / long (Captions are produced at multiple lengths) — serving both as training supervision signal and as conditioning prompt, allowing the model to accommodate both brief user input and detailed descriptions. The driving domain likewise has three tiers. The specific token-length ranges are not given.
【General-domain content constraints】Prompt engineering guides the model to describe three elements — the primary object, its motion, and key semantic details of the scene — while emphasizing factuality to suppress hallucination.
【Robotics-domain caption schema】The most strongly structured: the prompt mandates first enumerating the initial scene, then describing the robot's actions in chronological order, and explicitly labeling motion type (e.g., linear / rotational), involved parts (arm / wrist / gripper), camera motion, and fine-grained object attributes. It also normalizes the expression of viewpoint and embodiment (unifying camera-viewpoint naming across datasets). To reduce hallucination, the dataset's own metadata is injected into the prompt: GR00T injects task descriptions with human-rated success grades, Bridge injects step-level instructions, and AgiBot injects initial-scene descriptions. This practice of "injecting metadata into the prompt to constrain the VLM" is an effective paradigm for suppressing video-caption hallucination.
【Driving-domain caption schema】Six mandatory elements: ① various agents the ego vehicle must attend to (vehicles/pedestrians/cyclists) and traffic elements (traffic lights/signs); ② global environmental factors (weather, time of day, road surface conditions); ③ longitudinal and lateral meta-actions of the ego vehicle and surrounding vehicles; ④ speeds of the ego vehicle and surrounding vehicles; ⑤ dynamic behavior or state transitions of other objects; ⑥ interactions among key objects.
【Smart Spaces / Physics / Human Dynamics】Each has a customized prompt: Smart Spaces specifies that the video focuses on manufacturing environments such as factories/warehouses/industrial facilities/automobiles and adjusts the language style accordingly; Physics guides the VLM to accurately and thoroughly describe underlying physical processes and object interactions; Human Dynamics emphasizes detailed description of human motion and dynamics.
Not disclosed: the average token count of captions, and the mixing/sampling ratio of the three length tiers. [Uncertain: caption length values and the three-tier mixing ratio]

### [Data-Juicer 2.0](../models/Data-Juicer.md) ⚠️

【DJ's stance: provides assembly parts, does not prescribe a caption paradigm】DJ does not presuppose how long a caption should be or what fields it should contain; instead it provides a full spectrum of operators from "short tags" to "long dense descriptions," to be combined by the user as needed.
【The spectrum of caption density it can produce】
  · Sparsest: video_tagging_from_frames_mapper / video_tagging_from_audio_mapper produce a vocabulary of tags (e.g., AudioSet's 527 classes, RAM's open-vocabulary tags), not sentences.
  · Medium: video_captioning_from_frames_mapper / from_video_mapper produce conventional descriptions of one or a few sentences.
  · Densest: video_captioning_from_summarizer_mapper produces comprehensive long descriptions by fusing multi-source information; video_captioning_from_vlm_mapper can produce descriptions of arbitrary length and structure via prompt control.
【Support for structured fields】DJ's data model is "sample = multimodal fields + an arbitrary number of stats/tags fields," with each operator writing its output into an independent field rather than merging it into the caption text. This means structured information is naturally stored field-by-field: shot-level aesthetic score, motion score, OCR ratio, face ratio, content tags, speaker age/gender/emotion, camera intrinsics and pose, depth, full-body keypoints, etc. are all parallel structured fields that downstream training frameworks can concatenate into the conditioning text as needed, or use as independent conditioning channels. This "fields-first" design is, in terms of structuring level, actually higher than most model teams' pipelines that only produce a single block of long text.
[Uncertain] DJ does not provide an officially recommended video-generation caption template (e.g., whether it should include fixed fields such as camera movement, lighting, style, subject action), nor does any public case study report the average caption length, field composition, or comparative effects of different caption structures. This is a natural consequence of its "tool-neutral" positioning, but it also means users cannot obtain caption-design best-practice guidance from DJ.

### [Foley-Omni](../models/Foley-Omni.md) ⚠️

Highly structured, but with low per-field description density — a design orientation of "structure over density," clearly different from the text-to-video route of pursuing long dense captions.
【Format】A unified template `[WORDS] <spoken content>; [AUDIO] <sound event descriptions>; [MUSIC] <music specification>`, with three fields in a fixed order, explicitly labeled and separated; field content may be an empty string.
【Three layers of value in structuring】
  1. A composable conditioning interface: filling a single field = a single task (TTS/TTA/TTM), filling multiple fields = a complete score (V2ST); one template covers six task types, avoiding the need to design an independent conditioning pathway for each task.
  2. Explicit negative annotation: an empty field explicitly tells the model "this segment has no audio of this type," rather than making the model guess from absence. This directly helps suppress the model from gratuitously adding a musical score when it shouldn't (a common failure mode of V2A models).
  3. Verifiability: field-based structuring makes each annotation unit independently verifiable against acoustic evidence (the energy of the corresponding stem); a free-form whole-paragraph description cannot support this kind of fine-grained error correction.
【Relationship to contemporaneous work in the spectrum】In the paper's comparative context, this belongs to one branch of the 2026 trend of "factorized audio-video joint captioning": LTX-2 takes the route of a "unified description of the whole soundscape" (one piece of text describing the entire sound scene), Script-a-Video takes the route of factorized streams (decomposed into independent streams), and Foley-Omni is the most concise fixed-three-field scheme. The advantage of the three-field approach is an extremely simple schema, a natural correspondence with source-separation models, and ease of verification; the cost is limited expressiveness — it cannot describe the relative position, loudness layering, or temporal ordering of multiple sound sources within a field.
[Uncertain] No average length/word-count statistics for the captions are given; it is not stated whether the [WORDS] field is a verbatim transcript or a content summary (inferring from WER as an evaluation metric, it should be verbatim transcript text); it is not stated whether visual-side structured tags such as camera movement or visual style are included (judging from the template, they are not — visual information is carried entirely by CLIP/Synchformer features and does not enter the text condition).

### [Goku](../models/Goku.md) ⚠️

Belongs to the hybrid form of **dense long description (dense caption) + a small number of appended structured tags**:
【Main body】A natural-language dense description, generated by two paths — InternVL2.0 (keyframe detail: objects, attributes, scene, composition) and Tarsier2 (temporal dynamics, action evolution, camera movement such as zoom in / pan right) — then fused by Qwen2 into a single unified long text. Hence a single caption covers "visual content + temporal action + camera language" at once.
【Appended structured field】The most distinctive point is: **the motion score computed by RAFT is directly appended to the end of the caption**, turning motion intensity into an explicit conditioning variable that can be manually specified at inference time. This allows users to control the dynamic magnitude of the generated video by giving a motion score in the prompt, converting a statistic that was originally only used for filtering into a controllable generation interface — a typical example of "reusing a filtering signal as a conditioning signal."
【Not adopted】No hierarchical multi-granularity captions (short/medium/long multiple versions) are seen; no independent structured field system for style tags/lighting/image quality is seen; no description of caption dropout or a mixed short/long caption training strategy is seen. [Uncertain] (average caption length, whether a short-caption version is retained)

### [Hailuo / MiniMax Video](../models/Hailuo.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] (essentially no disclosure). The length, density, whether structured, and whether fields such as camera movement/style/lighting are included in captions are all undisclosed. Indirect evidence that can be inferred (speculative):
(1) The Hailuo series performs notably well at instruction-following on long, complex prompts (Hailuo 02 officially claims SOTA Instruction Following), which usually corresponds to long, dense training captions;
(2) video-01-director supports specifying concrete camera movements via bracketed instructions embedded in the prompt (a vocabulary of about 15 camera-movement terms such as [Push in], [Pan left], [Truck left]), which is the only confirmable trace of a "structured tag system" in the public API documentation — it strongly suggests that the training data contains an explicit, vocabulary-based camera-movement annotation field, and that this field is designed to be directly invocable by the user at inference time;
(3) Section 2.3's emphasis on "motion commands" hints that action-type tags are likewise structured.
The above is an inference of the training annotation schema deduced backward from the controllable interface; the actual organizational form of the caption remains unknown.

### [HunyuanVideo-Foley](../models/HunyuanVideo-Foley.md) ⚠️

【Density: an explicit low-density, short-description route】The paper characterizes its caption form with "concise descriptions," which is the opposite direction from the long, dense-caption pursuit common in the video-generation field.
【Why choose short captions — three constraints】
1) Encoder constraint: the CLAP text encoder has a limited context length (about 77 tokens), which physically cannot accommodate long descriptions;
2) Task constraint: the linguistic describability of audio content is inherently lower than that of vision — "a burst of hurried footsteps, accompanied by distant rain" already approaches the upper limit of information a human can give for an 8-second audio clip; lengthening it further would only introduce speculative content;
3) Cost constraint: at an annotation volume of about 45 million segments over 100,000 hours, the generation cost of long captions is unaffordable.
【The one structured field: the high-quality quality tag】This is the most ingenious point in this work's caption design, see Appendix A.1: for audio samples with a sampling rate above 16 kHz, a "high-quality" tag is appended to the caption; at inference time this tag is uniformly appended to all input captions. The mechanism lets the model learn during training the association "high-quality tag ↔ high sampling rate, rich high-frequency detail"; at inference, forcibly tagging pulls the generation distribution toward the high-quality end, thereby "enhanced high-frequency detail preservation."
This is a typical "quality conditioning" technique — similar ideas are seen in MOVA's explicit attribute marker "This video has no subtitles." and aesthetic-score markers in the image-generation field. Its value lies in not having to discard all low-to-medium-quality data entirely (which would lose data volume and diversity), but instead letting the model explicitly perceive the quality dimension and then selecting for it directionally at inference. This effectively defers the "filtering decision" to the inference stage, arriving at the same destination as UniVerse-1's LQLS loss-isolation strategy via a different route (both are "don't discard low-quality data, control how it influences instead"), but with a simpler implementation path — it only requires adding one word to the caption.
【Inconsistency in the threshold to note】The criterion for the quality tag is a sampling rate >16 kHz, whereas the admission threshold at tier 4 of the filtering funnel is an effective sampling rate >32 kHz. The two numbers differ, indicating that the tag is placed at a lower dividing line than the admission threshold — this hints that samples with sampling rates between 16–32 kHz may exist in the training data (i.e., the funnel threshold may not be strictly enforced on all data, or there may be other bypassing data sources). The paper does not clarify this apparent contradiction. [Uncertain]
【Not disclosed】The average caption length, vocabulary statistics, full examples, and generation prompt are all undisclosed; there are no visual-side structured fields such as camera movement/style/shot scale (because no video captioning is done). [Uncertain]

### [HunyuanVideo](../models/HunyuanVideo.md)

【Original version】Dense and strongly structured — one of the most complete caption schemas among 2024 open-source video models. Uses JSON format containing seven dimensional fields:
1. Short Description (a brief summary of the main content);
2. Dense Description (detailed scene elements, explicitly including narration of scene transitions and camera movement);
3. Background (setting/environment);
4. Style (documentary/cinematic/realistic/sci-fi, etc.);
5. Shot Type (shot scale and framing: aerial/close-up/medium shot/wide shot, etc.);
6. Lighting (lighting conditions);
7. Atmosphere (mood: warm/tense/mysterious, etc.).
There are also metadata-derived fields (source tags, quality tags). The camera-movement classifier's high-confidence predictions are also merged into the JSON caption, thereby giving the model camera-control capability.
During training, a "dropout mechanism + permutation and combination strategy" randomly drops/recombines fields from this JSON set to synthesize captions of varying length and sentence pattern, preventing the model from overfitting to a single caption template — this is critical, and directly determines why short prompts also work at inference time.
【1.5】Continues and refines the structure of "multi-level textual narration + a set of cinematic aesthetic attributes": the cinematic/aesthetic attributes explicitly enumerate shot type, shot angle, composition, lighting, style, color palette, and atmosphere — adding shot angle and color palette relative to the original version; camera movement is written into the caption in natural-language form, and distinguishes between clip-level (the dominant camera movement of the whole segment) and sequential-level (a camera-movement sequence that changes over time). 1.5 does not restate the dropout + permutation-and-combination strategy.

### [InstructAV2AV](../models/InstructAV2AV.md) ⚠️

This work's "text condition" is an editing instruction rather than a content description, and its structural characteristics differ fundamentally from the dense captions of generative models — it describes a difference (what to change) rather than a state (what it is).

【Disclosure at the paper level】Only stated as "diverse text instructions for comprehensive editing operations," covering four types of editing operations. [Uncertain] The paper gives no template, average length, word-count statistics, grammatical structure, or any example statistics for the instructions.

【The actual structure revealed by the Hugging Face dataset card (an important side finding, not mentioned in the paper)】Each CSV record contains four fields:
  · original_video — the source video path
  · target_video — the target video path
  · instruction — the forward editing instruction (source → target)
  · instruction_reverse — the reverse editing instruction (target → source)
【The significance of bidirectional instructions】The instruction_reverse field is a design that is not mentioned at all in the body of the paper but has considerable methodological value: for every synthesized pair (source, target), two training samples naturally result — "A plus a forward instruction yields B" and "B plus a reverse instruction yields A." This directly doubles the data volume, and the reverse sample's "source" is a synthesized product while its "target" is real footage — the opposite direction from the forward sample — which can mitigate the bias of the model only learning to mimic the data engine's output style (in forward samples, the target is entirely synthesized; if only trained on the forward direction, the model's output distribution would be anchored to Wan2.2-5B's synthetic distribution; adding reverse samples means half the samples have a target that is a real-video distribution). This is a clever, low-cost gain point in synthetic-data construction. It also partly explains the discrepancy between the 87K training pairs actually released by HF and the 79K claimed in the paper.
【Speech markers】Instructions may contain <S> and <E> markers to denote spoken content boundaries — i.e., the instruction embeds a piece of target dialogue text wrapped in special tokens, expressing "what to say" (precise content) and "how to change it" (editing intent) in layered fashion within the same instruction. This is a lightweight form of structuring: the instruction body is natural language, and the speech content is literal text wrapped in markers, preventing the model from mistakenly parsing the dialogue as editing intent.
【Comparison with visual-side structured tags】Does not include structured fields common in text-to-video, such as camera movement, style tags, or camera parameters — because the visual context of the editing task is provided directly by the source video itself (entering the model via Source Concatenation), with no need for text as an intermediary description. This is a natural simplification on the text-condition side of the editing paradigm relative to the generative paradigm.
[Uncertain] The instruction length distribution, the linguistic expression patterns of the four editing-operation types within instructions, whether an instruction template library exists, and the proportion of samples in which <S>/<E> markers appear are all undisclosed.

### [2026 Other Audio-Visual Joint Generation Works](../models/JAVG_2026_misc.md) ⚠️

【ALIVE — three-part structuring + inline temporal tags (the finest-grained in this batch)】
The caption schema is explicitly split into three fields:
- Subjects: contains "visual appearance + acoustic profiles" — i.e., each subject simultaneously carries a visual-appearance description and an acoustic profile. Binding "what this person looks like" and "what this person sounds like" under the same subject entry is a key innovation in caption design for AV models: it lets the model unify the visual and auditory aspects of identity during generation (this is also why the SubjectID correction stage needs a five-step pipeline to correct speaker attribution — if attribution is wrong, the acoustic profile gets hung on the wrong face).
- Visual: scene setting.
- Narration: a temporal sequence of actions, with two types of special tokens embedded inline:
  · <W>: wraps verbatim speech content
  · <I>: marks non-speech acoustic events
That is, the visual action narration, dialogue, and sound effects are interwoven in chronological order within the same Narration field. This is similar in idea to Ovi's <S>...<E> + <AUDCAP> scheme but more thorough — Ovi places the audio description uniformly at the end, while ALIVE also puts the non-speech events <I> inline on the timeline, giving stronger temporal expressiveness.
Combined with manually annotated start/end timestamps for sub-motion units, the temporal precision of the Narration has ground-truth support.
【NAVA — a structured dense caption with a unified template (four major sections)】
Explicitly adopts "structured dense captions rather than free-form short descriptions," with a unified template covering four sections:
- Global visual semantics: style, mood, subject, appearance, background, lighting
- Temporal dynamics: initial state, action sequences, motion details, ending state — this is an explicit "initial state → process → end state" three-part temporal skeleton
- Camera behavior: camera angle, shot scale, framing, composition
- Audio description: four categories — speech, SFX, music, ambient sound
In total about 18 explicit fields, the most complete caption schema among this batch in terms of fields. It is especially notable that "camera behavior" is broken out as its own section (4 fields), which is uncommon in AV generation work, indicating NAVA has a clear demand for camera controllability.
【OmniCustom】Audio captioning follows the Ovi system: speaker age, gender, accent + vocal characteristics (pitch, prosody, emotion, speaking rate), 7 attributes in total; plus segment-level transcription produced by GLM-ASR. The visual caption structure is not described [Uncertain]. This is a "structured audio side, undetailed visual side" lopsided design, consistent with its narrow task of identity + timbre customization.
【Baton — no text captions; uses continuous features as a "semantic blueprint" (the most unconventional in this batch)】
Baton's core claim is precisely that "text captions are not enough": existing methods rely on the coarse-grained embeddings of off-the-shelf text encoders, which "discards fine-grained semantics" and lack shared long-horizon planning. Its alternative is to have a VA-Planner (built on Qwen3-8B) produce planned tokens, and use dual semantic alignment towers (each with learnable queries doing cross-modal attention) to project the planned tokens into the continuous feature domain of pretrained perceptual encoders — aligning to the second-to-last-layer features of a frozen SigLip2 on the video side and the second-to-last-layer features of a frozen WavTokenizer on the audio side, supervised with L2 regression. The paper explicitly explains why discrete tokens are not used: "regressing continuous features preserves richer semantic structure."
This represents a new direction in the caption-structure spectrum: moving from "increasingly structured text captions" toward "bypassing text and directly using continuous perceptual features as the cross-modal semantic intermediary." If this route holds up, the focus of future AV data pipelines may shift from "how to write good captions" to "how to choose good perceptual encoders."
【CCL / StreamChar / ITS-JAVG】The caption structure is not described [Uncertain]. StreamChar uses a transcript (full dialogue text) rather than a descriptive caption; its orchestrator input is "transcript and historical context," which is a "script-driven" rather than "description-driven" conditioning form.

### [Audio-Visual Joint Generation Baseline Collection](../models/JavisDiT_baselines.md) ⚠️

【MM-Diffusion / AV-DiT】No caption (unconditional generation); this dimension does not apply.
【JavisDiT / JavisDiT++】Caption structure is inherited from upstream datasets, not self-built:
- Video side: a single natural-language description provided by TAVGBench (describing both audio and visual content simultaneously), of medium overall description density, with no structured fields.
- Audio side: the audio caption that each audio dataset carries natively, with widely varying style — AudioCaps/Clotho are human-written one-sentence audio descriptions, WavCaps are ChatGPT-assisted generated descriptions, and ESC50/UrbanSound8K/GTZAN etc. are essentially category labels rather than descriptive sentences. This "mixed multi-source caption style" is an inherent feature of its audio pretraining data; the paper does not perform style unification. [Uncertain]
- At the CSV-schema level there is indeed structured management: path, id, relpath, num_frames, height, width, aspect_ratio, fps, resolution, audio_path, audio_fps, text, audio_text — but these are metadata fields rather than structured tags within the caption itself.
- No explicit structured annotation such as camera movement, shot scale, lighting, or style. [Uncertain]
【Harmony (the most structured in this collection)】One Gemini annotation pass produces three separate text fields: (1) ASR transcription (transcript), (2) descriptive video caption, and (3) background-sound caption (audio caption / background sound caption). This is a clearly multi-field factorized structure, the opposite of Ovi's "single caption with embedded tags" route and closer to Script-a-Video's factorized streams. This three-field structure directly determines the conditioning setup of Harmony-Bench: the ambient-sound subset uses the audio/video caption as condition, the speech subset primarily uses the transcript as condition, and the complex-scene subset uses the "full multimodal prompt" — i.e., the three fields can be flexibly combined per task, a direct benefit of the factorized design. Prompt-template details and the average length of each field are not disclosed. [Uncertain]
【UniAVGen】Not described. [Uncertain]

### [Kling 3.0 / Kling Video 3.0 Omni](../models/Kling_30_Omni.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain, with a publicly disclosed paradigm from the same team] The caption structure of Kling 3.0 Omni is not disclosed, but the Kling-Omni report notes that pretraining text conditions span "from concise prompts to elaborate narratives" — i.e., the length/density of captions is deliberately diversified and mixed, to keep the model robust to both long and short prompts (this is also the data foundation underlying its ability to accept long vCoT instructions). The same team's Koala-36M publicly discloses a clear six-field structured caption schema: subject, subject actions, environment, visual language (style/lighting/composition), camera language (camera movement/angle/shot scale), and world knowledge, averaging 202 words in length (compared with Panda-70M's 13.2 words). Kling 3.0's strong camera-control and "intelligent storyboarding" capability depends heavily on this kind of structured dense caption containing camera-language fields.

### [LTX-2](../models/LTX-2.md)

Takes the route of "very long, dense, single coherent passage, purely factual" — a typical dense-caption paradigm.
【Density】LTX-Video paper Fig.14(a) gives the word-count distribution per caption, spanning roughly 0–175 words, concentrated in the tens-of-words range; LTX-2's captions are longer and denser because they must also cover the audio track. The official prompting guide recommends users write 4–8 descriptive sentences, kept as a single coherent paragraph — the inference-prompt format mirrors the training-caption format.
【Style constraints】The paper explicitly states its labeling guideline: "comprehensive yet factual, describing only what is seen and heard without emotional interpretation" — comprehensive but strictly factual, describing only what is seen and heard, with no emotional interpretation/subjective evaluation. This is a very clear caption-style specification.
【Structuring level】Does not use explicit JSON/key-value fields; instead melts structured information into a long natural-language description organized chronologically. LTX-Video's caption examples show a fixed set of covered semantic slots: subject appearance and detail → subject action/behavior → scene and background elements → camera position and movement (e.g., "camera follows … from behind, pans slightly" / "camera remains stationary") → lighting and color ("natural and slightly overcast lighting, casting soft shadows") → style/source tag (a fixed suffix such as "The scene is captured in real-life footage." or "The scene appears to be from a movie or TV show."). LTX-2 incorporates an audio slot on top of this.

### [LongCat-Video](../models/LongCat-Video.md) ⚠️

Relatively highly structured, adopting a synthetic caption-construction paradigm of "base description + attribute tags + random assembly":
(1) Base layer: a natural-language description produced by LLaVA-Video/Tarsier2, balancing visual content and temporal action;
(2) Structured tag layer, in two groups —
   · Cinematography: camera movement (pan/tilt/zoom), shot size, lens type;
   · Visual style: degree of realism, animation style, color tone;
(3) Augmentation layer: Chinese-English mutual translation (generating bilingual captions, paired with the umT5 bilingual text encoder), and generating concise summaries (forming long and short caption tiers, so the model accommodates both brief and detailed prompts);
(4) Assembly layer: "randomly selecting elements from cinematography and visual style categories and integrating them with augmented captions" — randomly drawing several elements from the cinematography and visual-style categories and splicing them into the augmented caption. This random assembly is a key design: it exposes the model during training to diverse prompt forms — "sometimes camera movement is mentioned, sometimes not" — so that at inference time it can respond to explicit camera/style instructions while also not forcibly imposing a certain camera language when the user doesn't mention one; this is the data foundation for camera-movement and visual-style instruction-following ability at the SFT stage.
Not disclosed: the average caption length, the token-count distribution, and the mixing ratio of long/short captions. [Uncertain: caption length distribution and long/short mixing ratio]

### [MOVA](../models/MOVA.md)

The caption structure is strictly structured JSON at the intermediate stage, and a long fused natural-language paragraph at the final stage — belonging to a two-stage design of "structured intermediate representation → natural-language final caption." The full original prompt text and complete example are given in Appendix A.5:
【Intermediate stage: structured JSON】
- The visual side outputs video_visual_report, containing two fields: visual_description (a detailed description of how visual elements evolve; outputs null when there is no visual change) and on_screen_text (a precise transcription of all visible text; outputs null when there is no text); plus a final_verification_audit self-audit block.
- The speech side outputs speech_transcription_report, containing speech_description (verbatim transcription; outputs null when there is no speech; unclear portions are marked with [inaudible]); plus a self-audit block.
- The non-speech side is free-text audio description (an extremely minimal prompt: "Please describe the audio you hear in detail.").
【Three laws of annotation (visual)】LAW OF VISUAL TRUTH (describe only visually verifiable elements, must not infer from audio or context), LAW OF VISUAL SILENCE (output null when there is no change), LAW OF VISUAL DYNAMICS (detect all transitions, precisely record motion trajectory, speed changes, and visual rhythm).
【Final stage: fused long caption】GPT-OSS-120B synthesizes a single fluent paragraph according to five rules; structurally it still implicitly contains a sequential information hierarchy: first narrate the visuals and anchor speaker context (describe actions, objects, scene, lighting, movement, and transitions in chronological order, without summarizing) → then embed the dialogue as quotations anchored to the visuals (using speech verbs that reflect tone, such as snaps/murmurs/laughs, and expressions that reflect pace, such as rushes/drawls/pauses) → finally introduce non-speech audio with phrases like "The audio includes…" or "In the background…" (limited to four categories: ambient sound, music, audio theme/sound source, and structural audio changes).
【Explicit style tags】No discrete tag system is used; style/camera-movement information is embedded in natural-language description form (only the inference-side prompt rewriter has an explicit four-category structure: visual style including color tone and lighting, cinematography including shot scale and composition, visual elements including subjects and spatial relationships, and OCR text).
【Length】The complete example given in the paper is a dense long caption of about 200 words; Figure 6b gives a prompt-length distribution chart (for the evaluation benchmark), but the length distribution of training captions is not quantified.
【Subtitle conditioning】In Phase 2, videos without burned-in subtitles have "This video has no subtitles." appended to the end of the prompt — i.e., an explicit controllable attribute marker exists within the caption.

### [Merged entry: ① Mochi 1](../models/Mochi_MAGI_Motif.md) ⚠️

① Mochi 1: Not disclosed. [Uncertain]
② MAGI-1 (two caption types coexist, its most distinctive data design):
【Highly Descriptive Caption】Following the conclusion of DALL-E 3 (Betker et al., 2023), it uses an MLLM to generate high-density descriptions. To address the extra temporal information videos have beyond images (action, camera movement, scene changes), a two-stage prompt structure is used: the first stage uses a set of targeted questions to guide the model through a structured analysis of predefined attributes; the attribute list (Table 3) has 8 items — Scene Count (identify the number of distinct scenes in the video), Camera Transitions (record perceptible transitions between shots), Camera Shot Type (specify the shot scale used), Camera Movement (describe the camera's movement), Main Subject Identification (determine who/what the video's core focus is), Subject Attributes (describe the subject's appearance), Subject Position (specify the subject's position in the frame), Subject Action (state what the subject is doing); only in the second stage is the final descriptive caption generated, which can absorb salient observations identified in the first-stage analysis. For image data, no attribute pre-analysis is performed — the caption is generated directly. Note that these attributes are "intermediate products used to guide analysis" rather than independently retained JSON fields; what ultimately reaches training is still natural-language prose.
【Auto-Regressive Caption】This design is unique to MAGI-1 and tightly coupled with its block-wise autoregressive architecture. Because the model generates block by block and can provide different text conditions for different parts of the video, the team provides a "second-by-second" fine-grained description for each segment: the caption for the 1st second is required to be a detailed description, while the caption for each subsequent second focuses on "the change relative to the previous second." The example in Table 4 clearly displays this incremental structure (e.g., "2nd second: the woman's head tilts slightly, her expression shifts from neutral to a faint smile, the lipstick is still in her hand, the camera remains static"). This kind of caption gives chunk-wise text control a grounded basis during training, and is also the data foundation that allows MAGI-1 to achieve multi-shot narrative at inference time using single-shot footage. The AR caption is used at staged proportions during training: 0% at stage-1, 10% each at stage-2 and stage-3 (Table 5).
【Inference-side patch】Because the model is trained on structured, highly descriptive captions while real user input ranges from extremely minimal to verbose, the team added a Prompt Enhancement strategy at inference time and distilled it into a roughly 7B small model (corpus of about 2 million entries), to bridge the mismatch between the training distribution and the user-input distribution.
③ Motif-Video 2B (three caption variants + a fixed JSON schema, directly targeting the training/inference distribution mismatch):
· Schema: requires every response to follow a fixed JSON format, containing both a free-text caption field and structured fields (style, subject, action, camera_move, quality, etc.); responses with invalid format are resampled.
· Dual prompts: the video prompt and the image prompt share the same JSON schema, differing only in the temporal fields. The video prompt treats the sampled frames as a single descriptive object and requests information in a fixed order — camera attributes (shot scale, angle, movement), subject, action, environment, lighting and color, and any on-screen text. The image prompt drops the temporal fields, requesting instead composition, framing, and a verbatim transcription of text.
· Anti-hallucination constraints: both prompts explicitly forbid three things — making claims not grounded in the image, frame-by-frame narration, and subjective commentary on quality/fluency/atmosphere; the goal is to reduce hallucinated labels and description drift.
· Three variants and sampling probability (the core design addressing the training/inference mismatch): each segment retains three caption variants derived from the same VLM response — caption_long (a detailed description of 150–250 words), caption_short (a single sentence of 15–25 words), and caption_truncated (only the first sentence of caption_long retained); during training these are sampled at fixed probabilities (p_long, p_short, p_truncated) = (0.5, 0.3, 0.2). The paper states its intent is to narrow the training-test mismatch between "long synthetic captions" and "the short prompts users actually give," and notes that the short/truncated variants also serve as a mild form of caption dropout, reducing overfitting to VLM-specific phrasing. The paper honestly labels this "a pragmatic recipe choice rather than an isolated claim of optimality."
Comparison point: MAGI-1 and Motif face the same problem (training with long dense captions vs. users giving short prompts), but their solutions go in opposite directions — MAGI-1 adds Prompt Enhancement on the inference side to lengthen short prompts, while Motif mixes in short captions on the training side to let the model directly adapt to short inputs. This is a very clean methodological comparison within this entry.

### [Movie Gen](../models/Movie_Gen.md)

Video-caption characteristics: dense and detailed (detailed generated captions), averaging about 100 words in length, with a unified style of "dense detail + consistent paragraph structure." Structured components include:
· Camera-movement prefix: the results of a 16-class camera-movement classification (zoom in / zoom out / push in / pull out / pan right / pan left / truck right / truck left / tilt up / tilt down / pedestal up / pedestal down / arc shot / tracking shot / static shot / handheld shot) are prepended to the caption when confidence is high, allowing users to explicitly specify camera movement at inference time.
· FPS token: the frame rate is added to the caption as a token, achieving control over the sampled frame rate from 16 to 32 FPS.
· Fields manually completed at the SFT stage: camera control, character expression, subject and background information, detailed motion description, lighting information; and 6 newly annotated shot-scale/framing categories (wide angle, close-up, aerial, low angle, over the shoulder, first person view), totaling 22 camera-related tags.
· Inference-side alignment: because users' actual prompts are typically under 10 words, differing greatly in length and style from training captions, an inference prompt rewrite was specially designed to rewrite short prompts into the training-caption style.
Audio captions use a highly templated structured four-part format (see joint_av_caption_schema).

### [NVIDIA NeMo Curator](../models/NeMo_Curator.md) ⚠️

【Density】Takes the dense long-description route. Measured statistics for the Cosmos WFM production version: an average of 559 characters / 97 words per caption.
【Temporal granularity】Uses "one caption per 256 frames" as the annotation granularity, i.e., a long clip is cut into multiple time segments each described separately, rather than one sentence for the whole clip — giving the caption a coarse-grained temporal correspondence.
【Sampling method】The VLM input is 8 uniformly sampled frames.
【Augmentation pipeline】The open-source version supports a two-stage process: the VLM first generates a base caption, which an LLM (Qwen-LM) then rewrites and augments, usable for style unification, adding detail, or generating multiple length variants.
【Downstream use】Captions are further encoded by a text-embedding model (as of 26.04, semantic deduplication defaults to the vLLM-backend google/embeddings-gemma-300m embedding model), and the text embeddings are written into the WebDataset together with the video embeddings.
【Structuring level】The official documentation does not disclose a caption template, slot definitions, or a structured-field specification (e.g., whether subject/action/scene/camera-movement/lighting must be covered), nor the full prompt text. What can be confirmed is that camera-movement types (pan/zoom/tilt) are produced as tags by an independent motion classifier, rather than relying on the caption text — i.e., structured tags and natural-language description are two parallel annotation systems. [Uncertain]

### [OmniHuman Dataset + OHBench](../models/OmniHuman.md) ⚠️

Adopts a structure of "hierarchical annotation + two-stage generation + placeholder anchoring," the most distinctive design in this work at the methodological level:
【A three-level annotation system (the paper's core organizing principle)】
- Video-level / global layer: scene type, shot scale, camera motion, background, lighting, background-sound tags, music attributes (genre/mood/relative volume);
- Frame-level / interaction layer: person-person interaction tags (including speaker ID and emotion), person-object interaction annotation, DWPose-L's 134 full-body skeleton keypoints, face-clarity score Cs = Var(ΔR), lip-sync quality assessment;
- Individual-level / attribute layer: person-ID assignment (face-embedding similarity > 0.55), body movement, appearance, expression state, speech transcription with emotion and timestamps, speech-quality annotation.
The three layers correspond respectively to the three major deficiencies the paper identifies in existing datasets (insufficient scene diversity / sparse interaction modeling / insufficient individual-attribute alignment); the structure maps one-to-one with the problem diagnosis, a clean point in the paper's narrative.
【Structured output form】Each video produces a set of per-subject structured records: {(ID_i, B_i, K_i, X_i, A_i, C_i)}, where ID is identity, B is bounding box, K is keypoints, X is attributes, A is audio-related, and C is hierarchical caption. This is a schema "organized by person" rather than "organized by video" — particularly important for multi-person scenes, since it naturally supports relational statements such as "person 2 is speaking, person 1 is listening."
【Two-stage caption generation】
- Stage one: Qwen3-Omni extracts fragmented attributes (a scattered set of attributes such as appearance, action, expression, speech, music, sound effects);
- Stage two: the fragmented attributes are synthesized into a coherent long-form narrative caption.
That is, "structure first, then naturalize into language" — compared with having the MLLM write a long caption directly in one pass, this approach decouples "what is seen" from "how it is described," significantly reducing attribute omission and fabrication in long-text generation.
【Placeholder anchoring mechanism】Fixed-position anchors are reserved within the caption, such as placeholders like [speech_m], which are replaced with actual content after generation (e.g., the real dialogue lines from ASR transcription). This design solves a concrete problem: MLLMs are extremely prone to hallucinating or paraphrasing when repeating speech content within a long caption; changing the approach so that "the MLLM is only responsible for leaving a slot in the correct position, and the real content is filled in by ASR" structurally eliminates dialogue hallucination. This is a very practical small design in this work for annotation reliability.
【Identity-alignment mechanism】Identity alignment based on reference face images: each person mentioned in the caption is bound to a specific reference face via an ⟨REF_i⟩ suffix marker — giving references like "the man on the left" or "the woman in red" in the caption a determinate visual anchor, which is critical for controllable generation in multi-person scenes. The reference face is selected as "the frame with the highest average confidence of face keypoints."
【Caption language】Provided in both English and Chinese versions.
【Not disclosed】Caption length statistics, word-count distribution, example original text, and the annotation prompt are all not given. [Uncertain]

### [Open-Sora Series](../models/Open-Sora.md)

Both projects take the route of **long, dense, structured captions**, and both write the structured field design explicitly into the prompt template, directly reusable:
【Open-Sora 2.0】Requires LLaVA-Video to cover **six aspects**: 1) main subjects; 2) subjects' actions; 3) background and environment; 4) lighting condition and atmosphere; 5) camera movement; 6) video style. Length statistics (Figure 3): **over 70% of video captions exceed 75 words in length**. In addition, the **motion score is directly appended to the end of the caption text**, allowing motion magnitude to be controlled via text at inference time.
【Open-Sora 1.x】Adopts a concatenated structure of "description + explicit conditioning tags": after the natural-language description generated by PLLaVA, three scalar/enumerated conditions — **aesthetic score, motion score, and camera motion** — are appended, of the form `aesthetic score: 5.5, motion score: 10, camera motion: pan left`. This "score condition" mechanism is the signature design of Open-Sora 1.2, allowing the same model to control image quality and camera movement via text at inference time, without an additional conditioning channel.
【Open-Sora Plan v1.3】The prompt requires the model to describe objects, scenes, characters, and camera movement **in chronological order**; post-processing uses a list of 28 common opening phrases to strip redundant prefixes such as "The video shows." Samples with an aesthetic score > 6.25 automatically get "A high-aesthetic scene" prepended.
【Explicit handling of the training-inference caption distribution mismatch (unique to Open-Sora Plan)】The authors note that training captions are dense and verbose while users' actual prompts are often under 10 words; to address this they specially built a **19,500-caption refiner training set**, covering four length/style categories: 11,000 short COCO user captions, 5,000 tag-style DiffusionDB captions, 3,000 medium-length JourneyDB LLM captions, and 500 super-long, hyperreal captions from official Sora/Vidu/Pika/Veo demos and GPT generation; ChatGPT was used to uniformly rewrite these into a target format of "subject description + action + scene description (+ optional camera language and atmosphere)," and LLaMA-3.1-8B was then LoRA fine-tuned as an inference-time prompt expander. This is the most systematic handling in this survey of "caption distribution mismatch," with the training data composition fully disclosed.

### [Ovi](../models/Ovi.md) ⚠️

Belongs to a design of "a single long caption with embedded structured tags," rather than multi-field JSON.
【Overall form】A single verbose (lengthy/dense) natural-language caption, with visual events narrated in chronological order, dialogue embedded inline via tags, and the audio description placed uniformly at the end.
【Tag system】
- Speech: <S> dialogue content <E> (start-of-speech / end-of-speech tags), which can appear multiple times in the caption, interleaved with visual events in temporal order, thereby implicitly encoding the temporal information of "who said what, when."
- Audio description: <AUDCAP> audio description <ENDAUDCAP>, placed at the end of the caption. Starting with Ovi 1.1, this format was simplified to a plain-text prefix "Audio: ..." placed at the end of the prompt.
【Density】The visual side is a verbose long description; since input is only 7 frames, the density leans toward event-level rather than frame-by-frame density.
【Structured fields】No explicit structured fields such as camera movement, shot scale, style tags, or lighting are seen [Uncertain]. The known structured dimensions are concentrated on the audio side (speaker attributes) and in the implicit temporal ordering.
【Single-prompt conditioning】The caption is not split into a video prompt and an audio prompt; instead the two are merged into a single string fed into one frozen T5 encoder, and the same embedding does cross-attention separately with the video tower and the audio tower. The paper's intuitive explanation is: visual context detail improves the specificity and diversity of the audio, while acoustic context detail guides facial and body movement. Ablation experiments (Section 5.5) confirm that this merged design outperforms a separate-encoding scheme.

### [Script-a-Video](../models/Script-a-Video.md)

MTSS is one of the most highly structured caption schemas in this survey, belonging to the "deeply structured + explicit relation graph" type, forming a paradigm-level contrast with the mainstream free-text long description:
【Overall form】A JSON-style four-stream structure rather than a natural-language paragraph. The four streams are Reference (reference stream), Shot (shot stream), Event (event stream), and Global (global stream).
【Stream 1: Reference Stream (entity bank, answers WHO and WHERE)】
- Positioning: a persistent Entity Bank, providing identity anchors for the entire script;
- Entity categories: four types — person / object / animal / scene;
- Filtering principle: only entities integral to the main plot are included; peripheral elements are demoted to scene descriptions in the Global stream;
- Fields: semantic_description (an overall-state description of the entity), timestamp (time of appearance), appearance_anchor (appearance anchor);
- Inside appearance_anchor: a general detail_description applicable to all entity types; for the person category, additionally expanded fine-grained attributes — clothing, accessories, hairstyles;
- Design benefit: all subsequent streams reference the persistent Reference ID rather than repeating the description, fundamentally guaranteeing absolute cross-shot identity consistency while eliminating text redundancy.
【Stream 2: Shot Stream (visual segmentation, answers WHAT-visual and HOW)】
- Each shot is anchored by a precise time_range;
- Visual-spatial layer: visual_description (an objective, chronological narration of the core action) + a camera field (professional cinematic language: movements, perspectives, scales);
- Relational layer: a references_in_shot array (mapping visible subjects in the frame to Reference IDs) + active_events (linking to auditory events concurrent within this shot);
- Temporal layer: intra-description timestamps embedded within the description text, anchoring micro-actions to the global timeline — the paper calls this achieving "surgical synchronization."
【Stream 3: Event Stream (audio events, answers WHAT-audio)】
- Admission principle: strict audio-visual coupling — only audio events with a direct visual counterpart or thematic relevance are included; sound effects must be produced by a visible subject in the frame;
- Event types: three — dialogue / sfx / music;
- Fields: type, time_range, and a content block containing speaker (relationally bound to a Reference ID), line (verbatim dialogue text), description (capturing emotional inflection, vocal technique, and other nuanced semantics);
- Concurrency handling: multiple simultaneous sound sources are split into parallel event entries rather than merged;
- Demotion channel: irrelevant background noise is filtered into the Global stream's global_audio;
- Alignment: precisely aligned with the visual track via micro-level timestamps.
【Stream 4: Global Stream (macro context)】
- scene_description (an overall description of the video's events), global_style (overall aesthetic style or genre), global_audio (ambient sound and background music that does not constitute an independent event).
【Two major design principles】
1) Stream Factorization: separating persistent information from time-varying information, reducing semantic redundancy, and supporting local updates;
2) Relational Grounding: identity linkage (centralized entity bank + ID references) + temporal linkage (shared anchors + intra-description timestamps) reweave the isolated streams back into a coherent script.
【Core claim: editability】The fatal flaw of monolithic captions is that local modifications inevitably trigger global rewrites ("local edits inevitably trigger global rewrites") — to change one camera movement or one sound effect, the entire passage must be rewritten to preserve narrative coherence. MTSS makes dependencies traceable and supports precise local updates — this is its structural advantage over long dense captions, and the basis of its "scalability" argument.
【Core claim: learnability】MTSS significantly narrows the performance gap between small and large models (Figure 1 explicitly presents this as its second major selling point); the paper explains this by noting that monolithic text requires the model to disentangle densely interwoven relations on its own, whereas MTSS has already pre-disambiguated WHO, WHERE, and WHEN, letting the downstream reasoning module focus on logical inference rather than identity resolution and temporal disambiguation.

### [Seedance 2.0 and Seedance 1.5 pro](../models/Seedance_20_Seedance_15_pro.md) ⚠️

Seedance 1.0 adopts a "dense caption" style, fusing two categories of features — dynamic and static: dynamic features describe in detail the actions and camera movement within a segment, highlighting changing elements, covering three categories — motion, changes in subject or scene, and camera movement; static features describe the characteristics of the core character or scene, covering categories such as appearance, aesthetics, and style. The team collected diverse data for these categories and performed high-quality manual annotation to train the caption model. In the Continue Training stage, two types of caption were designed for the I2V task: (1) the original long caption containing both dynamic and static detail; (2) a short caption with the static description corresponding to the first frame removed, focusing only on motion dynamics, to strengthen semantic alignment with the training objective. Seedance 1.5 pro is described as having "significantly improved the richness and professionalism of video description, and introduced audio description." [Uncertain: whether 1.5/2.0 add new structured-field schemas and a field list]

### [SkyReels Series](../models/SkyReels.md)

【SkyReels-V2】Takes the route of "structured fields + cinematic camera language," a notable difference from most vendors' "natural-language long description" route, and the methodological core of this series. The structured representation contains two major groups:
- Subject dimensions: subject type/category, subject appearance, action, expression, position;
- Camera dimensions (provided by a sub-expert): shot type (e.g., close-up/medium/wide), shot angle, shot position, camera motion (6DoF), environment, lighting.
The paper advocates a combined structured representation of "general description from the MLLM + fine-grained camera language from sub-expert models" as the data foundation for achieving shot-aware (camera-language controllable) generation; the "subject category" field is also reused as the basis for concept-balanced classification — the labeling schema directly serves data mixture design, a closed-loop design well worth learning from.
【SkyReels-V4】Changed to "three caption tiers in parallel":
- Short caption: a brief description;
- Long caption: a comprehensive description of environment, subject, lighting, atmosphere;
- Structured caption: follows a standardized description order, and uses special tokens to label five categories of elements — on-screen text, sound effects, dialogue, singing, and background music.
The coexistence of three tiers lets the model respond to both brief and dense prompts; on the inference side, a matching "prompt enhancement" module rewrites free-form user input into a form consistent with the training structured representation — the training-caption structure and the inference interface form a typical closed loop. The length distribution and mixing ratio of each caption tier are not disclosed.

### [Sora 2](../models/Sora_2.md) ⚠️

Completely undisclosed. No information on caption length distribution, density, or whether structured fields (camera movement, composition, style tags, etc.) are used. The predecessor Sora 1 emphasized "highly descriptive captions" to improve text fidelity and video quality, so it can be inferred that Sora 2 likely still follows the long-dense-caption route; and from capability claims such as "enhanced steerability," "expanded stylistic range," and "follows intricate instructions spanning multiple shots," it can be conjectured that the caption likely includes style tags, camera/movement descriptions, and multi-shot segmentation structure — but all of this is speculative. [Uncertain]

### [SpeakerVid-5M](../models/SpeakerVid-5M.md) ⚠️

Takes the route of "structured JSON fields" rather than long dense natural-language description, a notable divergence from generative-model datasets such as MOVA/LTX-2:
【Final form】Annotations are stored in JSON structure and released together with the dataset (the anno folder), with fields kept side by side rather than fused into a paragraph. Fields appearing in the paper's examples include:
  - Clarity
  - Camera Motion (camera-movement pattern)
  - Motion Intensity Level (1–5)
  - Entity List (list of entities in the frame)
  - Speech Presence (whether speech is present)
  - Observed Actions (detailed body-movement description)
  - Number of People
  - Upper-Body Only (upper-body/half-body vs. full-body)
  - Facing Direction (frontal/profile)
  - facial expression summary
  - topic category (dialogue topic category, annotated by Qwen-3)
【Density】Dominated by short field values; only Observed Actions is a relatively long natural-language description. The overall caption length is far shorter than MOVA's roughly 200-word dense fused paragraph. Figure 3 gives a caption word-cloud and word-frequency chart.
【Explicit style/camera-movement tags】Yes — Camera Motion and Upper-Body Only / Facing Direction are all explicit discretized structured fields, which is more convenient for conditioning control and data retrieval than pure natural-language captions.
【Design orientation】This schema is clearly optimized for "retrievable, filterable-by-condition subsets" (e.g., directly filtering for "full-body + frontal + high motion intensity + has speech" samples), rather than optimized to be "directly used as the diffusion model's text condition." Downstream models wanting to use it as a generation condition would generally need to rewrite it into a natural-language prompt themselves.
【Gaps】No average-length statistics for captions are given, and no manual quality-assessment or accuracy data for captions is provided. [Uncertain]

### [Step-Video-T2V](../models/Step-Video-T2V.md)

Adopts "three parallel captions" rather than a single dense description — the most noteworthy labeling-dimension design in this entry:
1. Short Caption: extremely minimal, focusing solely on the main subject and action;
2. Dense Caption: elaborates on key elements, emphasizing subject, event, environment, and visual presentation, and explicitly includes a description of camera movements;
3. Original Title: directly reuses the video's own original title text; the team's stated reason is to introduce "stylistic diversity" — i.e., preserving the distribution of genuinely human-written text with colloquial/clickbait/stylized coloring, preventing the model from only ever seeing the regular sentence patterns of VLM-generated text, thereby better adapting at inference time to real users' casual prompts. This shares the same purpose as HunyuanVideo's caption-field dropout + permutation-and-combination augmentation (aligning with the inference-time prompt distribution), but via a different path — Step-Video uses "introducing a real human text source," while HunyuanVideo uses "random recombination of structured fields."
【Structuring-level assessment】Compared with HunyuanVideo's seven-field JSON schema (with independent fields like Style, Shot Type, Lighting, Atmosphere), Step-Video-T2V's caption is natural-language long/short text rather than a strongly field-based schema; attributes such as style/lighting/atmosphere are implicit within the free text of the dense caption, not broken out into independent controllable fields.
