This limits the ability to do field-level conditioning control during training (its controllability is achieved mainly through the TI2V motion-score scalar).
The report does not state how the three caption types are mixed/sampled during training (ratio, random-switching strategy).

### [UniTalking](../models/UniTalking.md) ⚠️

Adopts a design of "three coexisting formats + random sampling during training," the most distinctive point in this work's annotation approach:
【Format a: detailed video caption + audio caption (concatenated)】A detailed video description generated by Qwen3-VL is concatenated with an audio description produced by Qwen3-Omni-Captioner/Whisper-V3.
【Format b: brief video caption + audio caption (concatenated)】Same as above, but the video description is replaced with a "short and concise" version.
【Format c: fused description】Video and audio streams are input to Qwen3-Omni simultaneously, directly producing a single unified audio-visual description without concatenation.
【Usage: random sampling】During training, one of the three prompt types is randomly sampled per sample as the text condition. This has multiple effects:
1) Caption length/density augmentation — the model sees both long and short descriptions, so it can respond to user prompts of varying detail at inference time, mitigating the classic problem of "training captions being too dense, causing short prompts to fail";
2) Caption structure augmentation — the model sees both a "concatenated (visual segment + auditory segment clearly delineated)" distribution and a "fused (audio-visual interleaved)" distribution, avoiding overfitting to a single caption style;
3) This amounts to a form of cheap prompt-robustness regularization, at no extra data cost.
This is a third route, different from both MOVA's "fuse into a single unified caption" and UniVerse-1's "three fields always kept separate": UniTalking is "keep both forms, and randomly switch during training."
【Structuring level】All three formats are internally free-text natural language, with no enumerated fields (no camera-movement tags, no shot-scale tags, no style tags, no emotion enumeration). The so-called "multi-level" refers to the levels of detail granularity and fusion form, not a hierarchy of field structure.
【Density and length】No length statistics are given, no caption examples are provided, and the annotation prompt is not disclosed. The specific length boundaries of "detailed" versus "short and concise" are not defined. [Uncertain]
【Indirect evidence of controllable attributes】The experiments section shows that the prompt "without any background music" can effectively control the presence of background sound in the generated result — indicating the captions do indeed contain descriptions of whether background music is present, and that the description is consistent enough to support controllable generation. But the paper does not make this kind of controllable attribute an explicit annotation specification.
【Temporality】It is not stated whether captions contain an ordering of events along a timeline, nor whether a caption corresponds to the whole segment or a sub-window. [Uncertain]

### [UniVerse-1](../models/UniVerse-1.md) ⚠️

Adopts three parallel structured annotation fields without fusion, a typical factorized schema:
【Three-way output】QWen2.5-Omni is instructed to output three mutually aligned, independent annotations:
1) verified speech content — verified speech content (Whisper ASR transcription verified/regularized by the multimodal model);
2) descriptive video caption — a descriptive caption of the visual footage;
3) ambient audio caption — the caption for ambient/non-speech audio.
【Structuring level】The division into three fields is itself structured, but the content within each field is free-text natural language, with no further enumerated fields (no camera-movement tags, no style tags, no shot-composition fields, no on-screen-text field).
【Density and length】The paper does not give caption length statistics, provides no complete caption example, and does not disclose the annotation prompt text. Density cannot be assessed. [Uncertain]
【Temporality】Captions correspond to the roughly 5-second window actually sampled during training, so at the temporal-granularity level they are "window-level" rather than "whole-clip-level" — this is precisely the core problem the online annotation is meant to solve. However, it is not stated whether fine-grained event ordering along the timeline (e.g., "first… then…") is done within a window.
【Relationship to the training condition】The three fields are fed into the model as parallel text conditions (via umT5 encoding), kept separate rather than merged into a single paragraph — a path different from MOVA's approach of "branching at annotation time, fusing into a single caption at training time": UniVerse-1 is "branched at annotation time, and also branched at training time."
【Controllable attributes】No explicit controllable marker is designed (such as MOVA's "This video has no subtitles.").

### [Unison](../models/Unison.md) ⚠️

The paper gives no caption length statistics, provides no caption examples, does not disclose the annotation prompt, and does not describe a structured-field design. Caption density and style are entirely blank. [Uncertain]
【The one confirmed structure that can be inferred from the model's input interface — dual text conditions】This is the only part of this field with textual support in the original paper. Unison's generation process is formalized as a function taking caption text and transcription text as parallel inputs — two independent text channels:
1) caption text (visual/scene description text);
2) transcription text (speech transcription text, i.e., "what the characters said").
Each is separately average-pooled into a global semantic vector, and both are jointly fed into the SCG to predict two gating coefficients. This constitutes a clear two-field structured schema: descriptive text and dialogue text are strictly separated, not merged into a single paragraph.
【The functional significance of this structure】The separation of caption and transcription is not merely an organizational form of annotation but directly carries functionality: the SCG judges whether the current scene is "narration-dominant" or "complex-soundscape" by comparing the relative strength of the two semantic vectors, and thereby dynamically adjusts how the speech stream and sound-effect stream influence each other. That is, the design of the text schema is coupled with the gating mechanism of the audio architecture: without distinguishing caption from transcription, the SCG would have no way to judge the scene type. This is a clear case in this entry of "annotation structure serving architectural design."
【Missing structured fields】No camera-movement tags, shot-composition fields, style tags, on-screen-text fields, emotion tags, or timeline event-ordering — none of this further structured design is seen.
【Heterogeneity risk】The native caption styles of the five data sources differ greatly (see the caption_model field for detail); the paper does not state whether unifying rewriting or format regularization was performed. [Uncertain]

### [Veo 3 / Veo 3.1](../models/Veo_3_Veo_31.md) ⚠️

Two points can be confirmed: (1) multi-granularity — "annotated with text captions at different levels of detail," i.e., the same piece of data is paired with multiple caption levels from short to long / coarse to fine, a standard practice for improving robustness to prompt length (both short and long prompts work); (2) synthetic captions used for concept expansion — "We generate synthetic captions to improve the variety and diversity of concepts associated with videos in the training data," i.e., rather than relying on original alt-text/titles, captions are rewritten and generated by Gemini, thereby covering conceptual dimensions such as cinematography technique, action, style, and scene context that the original metadata does not contain. This corroborates the model's described ability to precisely respond to "style, camera position, camera movement, and their combinations." [Uncertain] It is not disclosed whether a structured field schema exists (e.g., explicit slots for camera motion / shot size / lighting / style tags), the average caption length, the number of captions per data item, or the sampling ratio of multi-granularity captions during training.

### [Vidu S1](../models/Vidu_S1.md)

Dual-granularity + structured, customized for streaming interactive generation:
(1) full-clip caption: provides a coherent global semantic anchor for the entire video;
(2) speech-aware chunk-level caption: aligns the description with its corresponding time interval, providing a fine-grained, temporally localized conditioning signal for controllable interactive streaming generation — this is the key design adapting to the autoregressive streaming paradigm, allowing users to change subsequent content via voice instruction at any point during generation.
The structured fields cover three categories of attributes — visual, auditory, and dialogue — explicitly enumerated: subject appearance, actions, motion, emotion, scene context, camera language, cinematic properties, lighting, on-screen text, dialogue, sound effects, and background music — 12 categories in total.

### [Wan 2.5 / 2.6 / 2.7](../models/Wan.md) ⚠️

Takes the route of "dense caption + ten semantic dimension slots," with structured information melted into natural language rather than explicit JSON fields.
【Ten core dimensions】Inferred backward from the caption-evaluation system as the training targets: action, camera angle, camera motion, object category, object color, object counting, OCR text, scene, style, event. The team specifically notes that the MLLMs of the time (including GPT-4o and Gemini Pro) generally performed poorly at predicting camera angle and motion, so they specially manually annotated a camera-angle-and-motion dataset, used both directly to train the caption model and to train expert models for batch-expanding annotation in early training stages — the report explicitly states this improved the downstream video-generation model's camera-movement controllability.
【Wan2.2-S2V's explicit labeling specification (the closest to an official "caption template" statement)】Requires QwenVL2.5-72B to describe three aspects in detail: (1) camera angle — eye-level, high angle, low angle, wide shot, medium shot, close-up; (2) the character's appearance features (clothing, accessories) and actions, which must be decomposed into specific sub-actions; (3) the main features of the background environment, including architectural style, color scheme, greenery. It also explicitly requires the model to "avoid subjective evaluation and emotional interpretation, since these are of no benefit to the target generated video" — highly consistent with LTX-2's "comprehensive yet factual" style guideline, suggesting this is an industry-wide consensus labeling specification.
【Capability-oriented data for the caption model (reflecting structuring needs)】Celebrity/landmark/film-character recognition (thousands of identities, LLM collects names → TEAM CLIP-style model retrieval → keyword matching for dual-check denoising), object counting (numeric-keyword retrieval → LLM extracts (category, count) → Grounding DINO verification), OCR-enhanced captions (OCR extracts text first as a prior, then the model generates the description, currently Chinese and English only), camera angle and motion, fine-grained categories (animals/plants/vehicles, millions of images), spatial-relationship understanding (left/right/up/down, assisted by object-detection datasets), re-captioning (expanding existing short tags/short captions into dense descriptions), edit-instruction captions (describing the difference and transformation between two images), image-group descriptions (writing shared traits first, then describing each image individually), and manually annotated dense image/video captions (highest quality, used in the final training stage).
【Length and density】No word-count distribution for captions is given. Corroborating evidence can be found on the generation side: the prompt length cap grows progressively from 800 characters for wan2.2/wanx2.1 → 1,500 characters for wan2.5/2.6 → 5,000 characters for wan2.7-t2v, hinting that training captions are also continuously getting longer and denser.

### [Audio-Visual Generation Evaluation Benchmark Collection](../models/av_benchmarks.md) ⚠️

The structuring level of prompts/annotations differs significantly across benchmarks, with a direct mapping to caption design on the training side:
【VABench: the most thorough structural decoupling】The original prompt is structurally decoupled by an LLM into two independent streams — a "visual sub-prompt" and an "auditory sub-prompt" — from which fine-grained VQA (3–7 items) and AQA (3–7 items) question-answer pairs are generated respectively. That is, a single sample carries four categories of structured fields: the full prompt + the visual sub-prompt + the auditory sub-prompt + the per-modality QA pairs. This three-layer structure of "overall description → per-modality decoupling → verifiable QA" can be directly transferred as a hierarchical caption schema for training data.
【PhyAVBench: single-variable controlled prompts】337 paired prompt groups, with each pair differing in only a single physical variable, and deliberately avoiding subjective description and avoiding explicitly hinting at the expected acoustic outcome — this is an extremely structured "controlled-variable-style caption," essentially treating physical properties as an enumerable label dimension written into the text.
【AVBench】470 real-person-scene prompts at ≥720p, stratified and labeled by number of speakers and acoustic complexity; sampling uses attribute quotas, suggesting the prompts are already tagged with enumerable attributes (number of speakers, whether speech overlaps, background-noise level) [the specific tag system is not fully disclosed, uncertain].
【AV-SyncBench】Does not generate captions, but each sample carries structured perturbation metadata: perturbation type (global offset / local jitter / global speed change / voice-timbre substitution / instrument-timbre transfer) + perturbation-intensity tier + scene category + audio broad category. This "perturbation tag" structure is highly valuable as a reference for constructing negative samples for synchrony on the training side.
【Omni-Judge】Directly uses real user prompts from VidProM, with no structural rewriting — representing the natural distribution of real user captions (typically short, vague, lacking auditory description).

### [Video Caption Model Ecosystem](../models/caption_models.md)

【Length spectrum: from 13.2 words to 824.2 words, a 60x span】Panda-70M averages 13.2 words (a single-sentence summary, with the prompt "Please faithfully summarize the video in one sentence.") → Movie Gen captions average about 100 words → Koala-36M averages 202.3 words → AVoCaDO's optimal range is 2048–4096 tokens. The choice of captioning model directly determines caption length, which in turn determines the downstream T2V model's sensitivity range to prompt length.

【Four tiers of structuring level】
· Tier one · pure free-text single sentence: Panda-70M. Suitable for coarse alignment at large-scale pretraining, insufficient to support controllable generation.
· Tier two · long dense prose + embedded fixed sentence patterns: the mainstream approach. Allegro covers subject attributes, subject interactions, background, environment, style, atmosphere, camera angle and movement, and temporal changes, with camera movement explicitly inserted via a fixed sentence pattern "Camera [MOTION_PATTERN]" and the rest expressed in prose; Movie Gen prepends the high-confidence predictions of a 16-class camera-movement classifier to the caption; Step-Video-TI2V's example reads like "a flock of birds flying over a tree at sunset, camera pans left." This hybrid form of "prose body + structured fragment" is a compromise between controllability and naturalness.
· Tier three · explicit multi-field structuring: VDC defines five fields — camera / short / background / main object / detailed; Koala-36M has six categories of structured information; SkyCaptioner-V1 outputs professional cinematic fields such as shot type/shot angle/shot position/camera motion/facial expression and emotion intensity; Tencent Hunyuan outputs a JSON structured caption; LongCat-Video uses Qwen2.5VL to judge shot scale, shot type, degree of realism, animation style, and color tone; CineDance produces shot-level five-dimensional attributes + transition type + localized character list + active scene + shot description + transition description.
· Tier four · multi-granularity coexistence + random sampling: SkyReels-V4 generates three tiers — short/long/structured; Motif-Video 2B's three caption variants are sampled at probabilities 0.5/0.3/0.2 (the paper candidly admits this is "a pragmatic recipe choice rather than an isolated claim of optimality"); UniTalking retains the products of both "annotate separately then concatenate" and "unified-model fused annotation" paths, randomly sampling between them during training — handing the choice of annotation strategy over to random sampling, letting the model adapt to both text distributions simultaneously.

【DiffSW: the only structure designed for scalability】ShareCaptioner-Video's differential sliding window organizes the caption as a chained structure of "detailed first-frame description + stepwise differential descriptions," naturally carrying incremental temporal information, and supporting linear extension to arbitrary length as well as fast post-hoc summarization.

【Style constraints (a severely underrated engineering detail)】CogVideoX's Appendix G prompt explicitly forbids formulaic phrases like "The video presents / depicts / showcases" and "throughout the video," as well as line breaks; LTX-2's principle is "comprehensive yet factual" — describing only what is seen and heard, explicitly forbidding emotional interpretation (to prevent the caption from introducing uncontrollable subjective signals during T2V training). These two are the most valuable caption style-design lessons across the whole ecosystem.

【Alignment with inference-side prompt distribution (a downstream constraint on caption structure)】Open-Sora Plan explicitly demonstrates that the distribution mismatch between training captions (dense long text) and user prompts (<10 words) harms generation quality, and addresses it by LoRA fine-tuning LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct (rank 64, batch 32, 1 epoch, 30 minutes on a single H100) to train a prompt refiner; Seedance 1.0's PE model shares the same origin as its captioner to ensure structural alignment; MAGI-1 distills the large MLLM's enhanced prompts into a roughly 7B small model (a corpus of about 2 million entries) to reduce inference latency; Goku uses GPT-4o to expand GenEval's short prompts, achieving a score of 0.76, indirectly proving that model performance depends heavily on long prompts consistent with the dense training-time captions. **The choice of caption structure effectively locks in the need for a matching prompt rewriter on the inference side** — this is the most important systemic conclusion across this ecosystem.

【Text-encoder capacity limits】Allegro uses T5 (512-token cap; the paper qualitatively argues T5 is superior to mT5); Mochi 1 uses a single T5-XXL; Foley-Omni and UniVerse-1 use UM-T5/umT5; HunyuanVideo-Foley uses the CLAP text encoder (about 77 tokens, because CLAP's text-embedding space is naturally aligned with audio semantics); LTX-2 uses Gemma3-12B; Motif switches to T5Gemma2 starting from stage 4; Apollo/Klear use Qwen2.5-7B. The encoder's token cap directly truncates the usable upper bound of caption density.

### [Geometric/Structured Annotation Dataset Collection](../models/geometric_datasets.md)

SceneScribe-1M: a single comprehensive structured scene description, explicitly distinguishing three elements — scene settings, primary subjects or characters, and significant actions — a medium-density long caption; SpatialVID: a group of multi-style parallel structured captions, with the same clip producing four types of text — a motion-oriented description (averaging 50.3 words after refinement), a scene summary (averaging 28.6 words), an immersive narrative (averaging 89.7 words) — plus five categories of structured tags (weather/time of day/crowd density/lighting/scene type), achieving a "one video, multiple granularities and styles" density spectrum; Action100M: a Tree-of-Captions hierarchical structure, with each node having five structured fields — a brief action description (averaging 3.2 words), a detailed action description (averaging 27.8 words), actor identification, a brief caption (averaging 19.2 words), and a detailed caption (averaging 95.3 words) — covering a complete density gradient from 3 words to 95 words, with 21.3 billion words across the whole corpus; WildWorld: captions are split into two layers, action-sequence level and sample level, but captions are not the primary supervision signal — the core supervision comes from 119 columns of structured numerical annotation.

### [Video Generation Post-Training Data Strategy](../models/post_training_data.md) ⚠️

The anchor paper does not disclose the structure or density of SFT captions [Uncertain]. PE's reward design includes a "Structure Reward" item, enforcing format compliance and length constraints to ensure the prompt is valid and executable — this is the only information related to caption structure, indicating that its enhanced prompt has a clear structured template, though the template's content is not disclosed [Uncertain].
【Cross-comparison】The trend in caption structure at the post-training stage is toward "longer, denser, more structured": NAVA's SFT captions are generated by Gemini-3-Pro to be "more accurate, more structured, temporally grounded" audio-visual descriptions; HunyuanVideo 1.5's RLHF prompt set comes from a mix of LLM-generated prompts and training captions, indicating that the distribution difference between training captions and user prompts is explicitly modeled; MOVA's rewriter has the explicit goal of "bridging the gap between user input and the training-data distribution" — this actually reveals the core tension in SFT caption structure design: the denser the caption, the more beneficial for training, but the further it drifts from the real user-prompt distribution, so PE must be paired with it to realize the benefit. The anchor paper's treatment of PE as a standalone stage is precisely a systematic response to this tension.

### [Merged Survey of Mainstream Video Pretraining Datasets: Panda-70M, InternVid, Koala…](../models/pretraining_datasets.md) ⚠️

**Caption length and structuring level are the most intuitive difference among the seven datasets** (average word counts under the unified accounting of UltraVideo Table 1): Panda-70M **13.2 words/0 fields**; InternVid **17.6 words/0 fields**; LVD-2M **88.8 words/0 fields (but temporally dense)**; OpenVid-1M **126.5 words/0 fields** (HD subset 104.5 words); Koala-36M **202.1 words/6 fields (fused into one paragraph)**; MiraData **318.0 words/6 fields (independently addressable)**; UltraVideo **824.2 words/10 dimensions** (long 850.3 words).
【The four datasets with no structuring】Panda-70M: one sentence, 13.2 words; the distribution of the caption teacher sources is shown in the paper's Figure 11. InternVid: 17.6 words; about 50% between 10–20 words, about one-third under 10 words, about 11% over 20 words. OpenVid-1M: a single free-text caption from LLaVA; the paper only qualitatively states via a figure that it is "significantly longer than Panda-50M," **without giving a word count** (the 126.5-word figure is cited via the UltraVideo table). **LVD-2M is a unique intermediate form** — its structuring is expressed not through fields but through **temporal density**: each 30-second segment is described separately and then synthesized in chronological order, so its captions naturally describe "how events progress over time" rather than a static enumeration of the frame; word-count distribution: about 2% at 30–50 words, about 20% at 50–70 words, **about 38% at 70–90 words**, about 24.5% at 90–110 words, and about 13.5% at >110 words.
【The two datasets with six-field structuring — a strikingly consistent field design, directly reusable】
- **Koala-36M's six fields**: (1) subject, (2) subject actions, (3) environment, (4) **visual language** (style/composition/lighting), (5) **camera language** (camera movement/angle/focal length/shot scale), (6) **world knowledge**. The six fields are **each generated separately and then fused into one coherent passage**, ultimately published as a single caption field (measured caption character counts in the CSV: 123–3,320).
- **MiraData's six fields (the only one of the seven independently addressable)**: the CSV column names are exactly `short_caption`/`dense_caption`/`background_caption`/`main_object_caption`/`style_caption`/`camera_caption`. **Precisely because of this, it is the only one able to run four separate dimensions of text-image consistency evaluation in MiraBench — "camera alignment/subject alignment/background alignment/style alignment" — which the others cannot do.** Word-count composition: **dense caption about 90 words + four structured fields about 214 words = 318 words total** (**often misquoted as "dense caption 318 words," when it is actually the sum across all fields**). Measured mean per field across 100 official sample entries: short 19.0, dense 112.3, main object 84.5, camera 83.8, background 77.2, style 37.3. The five numbered items of the GPT-4V prompt correspond exactly to the five fields, and **the few-shot example directly uses the original text of Sora's official demo prompts** (a woman on a Tokyo neon street, a red-knit-helmeted astronaut on a motorcycle), and explicitly states "following DALL-E 3, have GPT-4V produce descriptions conducive to training text-to-video models," while also specifically requiring "do not describe frame by frame, do not use words like 'first frame.'"
【The ten-dimensional UltraVideo (the highest structuring level)】The first 9 are generated by Qwen2.5-VL-72B and the 10th is summarized by Qwen3-4B: (1) Brief Description (2) Detailed Description (3) Background (4) Theme Description (5) Style (6) **Shot Type** (7) **Camera Movement** (8) **Lighting** (9) **Video Atmosphere** (10) Summarized Description. **"Shot type," "lighting," and "atmosphere" are three dimensions unique to this dataset among the seven**, clearly oriented toward cinematic generation needs. The average word count per dimension **is only presented as distribution charts in Figure 4(d) and Figure A1, never as a table**; the character ranges given by the HF browser can serve as a proxy: Brief 34–526, Detailed 479–2,610, Summarized 586–6,060 characters.
【Two empirically demonstrated lessons from long captions】
1. **Text-encoder capacity must match**: MiraData explicitly switched to **Flan-T5-XXL (512-token cap)** because its 318-word caption would not fit into CLIP's 77 tokens; LVD-2M paid a price for this — its 88.7-word caption was truncated by the frozen CLIP text encoder, and the authors directly attribute the unremarkable improvement in I2V text matching (16%/70%/14%) **to the 77-token cap**.
2. **Distribution mismatch between training captions and user prompts**: LVD-2M points out that **VBench's prompts average only 7.6 words**, much closer to WebVid's 14.1 words than to its own 88.7 words, so the benchmark itself is **unfavorable** to datasets with long captions. UltraVideo's response is a **random caption-sampling strategy** — with 1/3 probability selecting one from {Brief, Detailed, Summarized}, and if Brief or Detailed is chosen, **randomly appending one more** from the remaining 7 structured categories as the final prompt, so the model adapts to both long and short prompts. **This is the only one of the seven datasets to design a training-time data-augmentation scheme specifically targeting the distribution mismatch, and is well worth reusing.**
## Joint audio-video caption structure (whether it covers both visual and auditory tracks simultaneously, whether it is split into separate fields, e.g. LTX-2's full-soundscape description, Script-a-Video's factorized streams, Foley-Omni's three fields)

`joint_av_caption_schema` · Level of detail: detailed

### [Allegro](../models/Allegro.md)

Not applicable. Allegro has no audio modality; captions cover only the visual track (including camera movement). There is no joint audio-video caption structure, independent auditory field, or full-soundscape description.

### [Apollo](../models/Apollo.md) ⚠️

Apollo adopts a "annotate by splitting according to audio type → produce each track independently → merge into a unified dense caption" scheme, but on the model input side it still retains dual visual/auditory text channels — a hybrid form:
【**Annotation track division**】Three types of annotation are produced in parallel — (1) speech transcripts (vocal subset only); (2) audio captions (all subsets); (3) video captions (all subsets). The speech and singing subsets additionally attach speaker attributes (gender, age) as meta fields.
【**Coordinates relative to similar schemes**】
- Compared to Foley-Omni's three fields being kept side by side long-term, Apollo performs merging on the data side (merged into unified dense captions).
- Compared to LTX-2's single full-soundscape description, Apollo retains the three-track source structure of "transcript / audio description / video description" and a conditional schema tailored by subset.
- Compared to Script-a-Video's factorized streams, Apollo's basis for splitting is **audio content type (vocal/non-vocal, single-speaker/multi-speaker/singing)** rather than narrative elements — this is the most distinctive aspect of its schema design: the schema itself varies with the audio category.
- Compared to MOVA's "three strictly mutually-exclusive-prompt tracks + a 120B LLM that merges and adjudicates cross-modal consistency," Apollo has not disclosed any mutual-exclusion constraints between tracks (e.g., prohibiting the visual annotator from referencing audio) or merging rules — the merging step is a black box.
【**Dual text channels on the model side**】Architecturally, audio-related text and video-related text are two independent inputs that are encoded separately before being fed into the MM-DiT. This means instructions can be issued separately to the audio and video sides at inference time (this is precisely the basis for the paper's emphasis on "instruction following in both joint and unimodal settings"), and it also supports the unification of T2A / T2V / T2AV multi-task training.
【**Missing**】The merging rules, field names, schema definition, and examples are all undisclosed. [Uncertain] (the track-splitting structure is confirmed, but schema details are missing)

### [CineDance / CineDance-1M](../models/CineDance.md)

The joint audio-video caption adopts a "dual-track parallel, field-split" design: the visual and auditory sides each form their own system but are aligned via shared anchor tokens and shot indices:
【**Visual track (produced by Qwen3.5-35B-A3B)**】Five-dimensional shot attributes (shot size / angle / camera movement / narrative function / duration category), transition type, local cast list, active scene, shot description, transition description.
【**Auditory track (produced by Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B, split into three fields)**】
  ① Sentence-level ASR transcription (sentence-level ASR segments) — at this stage speaker identity is deliberately not bound;
  ② Shot-level audio prompt — natural-language descriptions covering music, ambient sound, and effects, i.e. non-speech soundscape descriptions;
  ③ Character voice description — characterizes each character's vocal timbre.
【**Coupling points between the two tracks**】① Sharing global character anchor tokens ⟨charₖ⟩: ASR sentences are attached to specific character tokens in a subsequent binding step, thereby aligning with the shots in the visual track where that character appears; ② Sharing shot indices: the audio prompt is shot-level and corresponds one-to-one, shot by shot, with the visual description.
【**Compared to similar schemes**】More structured than LTX-2's "single-paragraph full-soundscape description"; belongs to the same lineage of thinking as Script-a-Video's factorized streams (split multi-stream scripts) and Foley-Omni's three-field design, but CineDance's distinguishing feature is introducing the anchor-token consistency mechanism into cross-shot long-sequence scenarios, and treating "speaker-to-character binding" as an independently evaluable subtask (95.4% accuracy).
【**Design motivation**】The three audio subtasks are invoked separately rather than output all at once; the paper explicitly states this is to reduce hallucination.

### [CogVideoX](../models/CogVideoX.md) ⚠️

Not applicable. CogVideoX's captions are purely visual descriptions that do not involve the auditory track at all; the training data contains no audio track, so there is no joint audio-video caption schema, nor any visual/auditory split fields.
Whether the cascaded CogSound side uses text captions as conditioning, and what its audio caption structure looks like, are both undisclosed [uncertain]; from the public description, CogSound is primarily conditioned on video features (GLM-4V understanding + frame-level feature cross-attention), and no explanation of the role of text conditioning is given.

### [Cosmos-Predict2.5](../models/Cosmos-Predict25.md)

Not applicable. The model has no audio modality; captions are entirely pure visual/semantic descriptions, containing no auditory-track fields at all, nor any schema splitting visual and auditory streams. The analogous "multi-stream decomposition" structure in this work manifests along the spatial dimension rather than the modality dimension: the seven cameras in the driving domain are each encoded independently, with a caption generated every 150 frames; and captions are split along the time dimension into 5-second time windows. There is no comparability with genuine joint audio-video caption paradigms such as LTX-2's full-soundscape description, Script-a-Video's factorized streams, or Foley-Omni's three fields.

### [Data-Juicer 2.0](../models/Data-Juicer.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] Data-Juicer does not define any joint audio-video caption schema — this is a notable gap relative to dedicated works such as LTX-2 (full-soundscape description), Script-a-Video (factorized streams), and Foley-Omni ([WORDS]/[AUDIO]/[MUSIC] three fields).
【**But it has the parts to assemble a joint schema from**】DJ's field-based data model allows users to construct a similar schema themselves:
  · Auditory-side content description: video_captioning_from_audio_mapper (Qwen-Audio generates a description from the audio track) → can serve as a "soundscape description" field.
  · Auditory-side tags: video_tagging_from_audio_mapper (AST, AudioSet 527 classes) → can approximately distinguish speech/music/ambient sound, serving as an audio category field.
  · Speech content: video_audio_ASR_mapper → can serve as a dialogue transcription field.
  · Speaker attributes: video_audio_detect_age_gender_mapper, video_audio_speech_emotion_mapper → can serve as speaker attribute fields.
  · Visual-side description: video_captioning_from_vlm_mapper / from_frames_mapper → visual description field.
  · Merging: video_captioning_from_summarizer_mapper can hand the above fields to an LLM to merge into a single unified description — this is in fact a feasible implementation of the "full soundscape + visual" unified-description route.
【**Key gaps**】There is no audio-track source-separation operator, so it cannot split the audio track into the three stems of speech/effects/music and describe/validate them field by field the way Foley-Omni does; there is no acoustic energy gating mechanism to correct VLM visual hallucinations (writing "music" just because an instrument is seen). These two points are key steps for constructing high-quality joint AV annotation, and DJ currently does not provide them.
【**Conclusion**】DJ provides most of the raw materials for building a joint AV caption, but does not provide a methodology for schema design or cross-modal cross-validation; users need to fill in roughly the remaining 30% of key steps themselves (source separation + energy verification + schema definition).

### [Foley-Omni](../models/Foley-Omni.md)

This is the most representative contribution of this work, and is what the note specifically flags as the "three fields."
【**Schema definition**】The joint audio-video annotation Ŝ = ([WORDS] speech content, [AUDIO] sound-effect event description, [MUSIC] music description). The three fields sit side by side, each independently nullable, jointly describing the complete auditory content of the same clip.
【**Whether it covers the visual track**】No. This is an important design choice: the schema only structures the "auditory" side; the visual side has no text description, and instead is injected into the model directly as dual CLIP (semantic) + Synchformer (temporal) features. The rationale is that video is conditioning rather than the generation target in this task, so there is no need for text as an intermediary representation; however, the annotation-generation process itself is "audio-visual joint" — Gemini watches the video frames and listens to the audio track simultaneously to produce these three auditory fields, so visual information participates implicitly in the annotation decision (e.g., using the picture to judge whether a sound belongs to an on-screen sound source).
【**Whether it is split into independent fields**】Yes, and this is exactly the core point. Compared with a "single-paragraph full-soundscape description," splitting brings three actionable benefits: (a) during training, different conditioning strength/dropout can be applied to different fields; (b) at inference time, users can fill in only certain fields for targeted control (e.g., wanting sound effects but not music); (c) during cleaning, each field can undergo independent acoustic validation and be nulled individually, whereas a whole-paragraph description can only be kept or discarded as a whole — a much coarser granularity.
【**Isomorphism with source separation**】The three fields ↔ Bandit's three stems ↔ the three generation tasks (TTS/TTA/TTM) form a strict one-to-one correspondence. This cross-stage structural consistency is the elegance of the design: the annotation schema, the validation tool, the task definitions, and the evaluation dimensions are all aligned on the same three-way classification.
【**Manifestation on the evaluation side**】V2ST-Bench is organized by field co-occurrence pattern: speech + sound effects (150), speech + music (120), all three present (30) — directly testing the model's joint generation capability when multiple fields are simultaneously non-empty.

### [Goku](../models/Goku.md)

Not applicable. Goku has no audio modality; captions cover only the visual track (picture content + action + camera movement + motion score). There is no auditory-track description, audio-video split fields, or full-soundscape description. It can serve as a baseline for "caption schema in the pure-visual era," forming a generational contrast with joint audio-video schemas such as LTX-2's full-soundscape description, Script-a-Video's factorized streams, and Foley-Omni's three fields: Goku's "keyframe + whole-clip" dual-path fusion idea is structurally of the same lineage as the later audio-video models' "visual stream + auditory stream" factorize-then-fuse idea, just with the dimension swapped to temporal granularity rather than modality.

### [Hailuo / MiniMax Video](../models/Hailuo.md)

Not applicable. The model does not generate audio; training captions do not involve auditory-track description, and there is no joint audio-video caption structure. There is no comparability with schemes such as LTX-2's full-soundscape description, Script-a-Video's factorized streams, Foley-Omni's three fields, or Vidu S1's dual-path audio-visual decoupling. If MiniMax releases an audio-capable video model in the future, this item will need to be re-researched.

### [HunyuanVideo-Foley](../models/HunyuanVideo-Foley.md)

HunyuanVideo-Foley does not have a "joint audio-video caption" in the traditional sense — this is the most important conclusion for this field, and the reason behind it carries methodological significance:
【**Only the audio track has a language description; the visual track does not**】The caption system is single-track — an audio description generated by GenAU plus a "high-quality" quality tag. The entire pipeline generates no picture-description text whatsoever.
【**Visual information enters the model as features rather than language**】This is the key design — visual semantics do not go through the "picture → text → text encoder" language intermediary; instead, SigLIP-2 encodes them directly into visual features, which enter joint self-attention together with the audio latent. That is, vision takes the "feature pathway" and text takes the "language pathway"; the two are fused inside the model rather than at the annotation stage.
【**The essential difference between this design and factorized schemas**】Both UniVerse-1's factorized streams (visual caption / speech content / ambient-audio caption, three fields side by side) and MOVA's fused single caption organize multimodal information within "language space"; HunyuanVideo-Foley instead organizes it within "representation space" — only information that cannot be inferred from visual features and must be explicitly specified by the user (desired sound characteristics, quality level) is expressed in language.
【**Why this makes sense**】For the V2A task, picture information is fully available (the video is right at hand at inference time); converting it to text and back to features is lossy and redundant. The real function of text conditioning is to supplement the parts that the picture cannot determine (e.g. the spatial sense of "coming from a distance," or a "metallic" timbre preference). Text's role in this model is therefore "supplementation and control" rather than "description," so the caption should naturally be brief rather than dense.
【**The modality-competition problem**】The paper explicitly states that a multimodal DiT needs to "resolve modal competition" — the visual conditioning carries a large amount of information and is strongly correlated with audio, making it prone to dominating generation and rendering text conditioning ineffective. The solution is to route vision through joint self-attention (deep fusion) and text through cross-attention (an independent pathway), structurally separating the two pathways to prevent text from being swamped by vision. This is a solution to the "joint schema" problem at the architecture level rather than the annotation level, and is where this work's real contribution along this dimension lies.
【**Limitation**】Since there is no visual caption, there is no way to perform cross-modal consistency checks of "whether the visual description and the audio description agree" — this function is transferred entirely to the ImageBind semantic-alignment detection in the cleaning stage (using embedding similarity rather than language comparison to determine consistency).

### [HunyuanVideo](../models/HunyuanVideo.md) ⚠️

Not applicable. Neither generation of the model has an audio modality; captions cover only the visual track, and there is no visual + auditory dual-track caption structure. It can serve as a reference baseline for "pure-visual structured captions," contrasting with AV schemes such as LTX-2's full-soundscape description, Script-a-Video's factorized streams, and Foley-Omni's three fields: HunyuanVideo's seven-field/multi-attribute schema already achieves a high degree of field-based structuring on the visual side, and the audio-track fields of AV models can be viewed as an orthogonal extension on top of it. [Uncertain]

### [InstructAV2AV](../models/InstructAV2AV.md)

This work's form of joint audio-video text conditioning differs markedly from the other works surveyed: it is not a "joint caption" but a "joint instruction" — the object being described is a cross-modal change operation, not cross-modal content.

【**Whether it covers both the audio and visual tracks simultaneously**】Yes, but as implicit, unified coverage rather than explicit splitting. A single instruction simultaneously implies both a visual change and an auditory change, and the two are bound by semantic causality — the single instruction "replace the dog with a cat" simultaneously requires the dog in the picture to become a cat and the dog barking on the audio track to become meowing. The instruction itself is not split into two fields, a "visual instruction" and an "audio instruction"; instead the model is left to infer, from a single natural-language instruction, what change each of the two modalities should undergo. This is the core setup of this work: the paper emphasizes that "given solely text instructions," joint editing can be completed — the user need not separately specify how audio and video should each change.

【**Lineage comparison with other schemas in the survey**】
  · LTX-2: unified full-soundscape description (one passage of text describing the entire soundscape) — descriptive type, single auditory track.
  · Script-a-Video: factorized streams (decomposed into independent visual and auditory streams) — descriptive type, dual-track split.
  · Foley-Omni: [WORDS]/[AUDIO]/[MUSIC] three fields — descriptive type, auditory split into three.
  · InstructAV2AV: a single natural-language edit instruction + <S>/<E> speech-content markers — instruction type, modalities fused, not split.
  It can be seen that this work sits at the low end of the "degree of structuring" axis (almost no field-based structuring), but this is not crude design — it is dictated by the nature of the task: if the edit instruction were forcibly split into "what to change visually / what to change aurally," it would instead undermine its core selling point (the user only needs to say one plain sentence) and introduce the risk of inconsistency between the two modalities' instructions. In the editing scenario, the benefits of structuring (separate controllability, independent verifiability) give way to ease of use and cross-modal consistency.

【**Traces of structuring still exist in two places**】
  1. The <S>...<E> markers — separating the literal content of the speech (the new line to be said) from the editing intent into layers; this is the only explicit field-based design.
  2. The bidirectional instruction / instruction_reverse fields — explicitly structuring the editing direction.

【**The visual side has no text description**】The content of the source video does not enter conditioning as text; instead it is injected directly into the diffusion process via Source Concatenation (concatenating the source latent with the noise latent). The model's "visual understanding" thus goes through the latent pathway rather than the text pathway, consistent with Foley-Omni's approach of carrying visual information via CLIP/Synchformer features — both are cases of "vision without a text intermediary."

【**The guarantee of jointness on the data-construction side**】Notably, the joint consistency of audio and video is guaranteed mainly not by the schema but by the generation order of the data engine: the new audio track is first synthesized according to the instruction, and then the audio features are used as frame-wise cross-attention conditioning to synthesize the new picture. That is, "jointness" is enforced within the causal chain of data generation rather than declared within the annotation structure. This is where this work's path to achieving jointness is distinctive relative to the others.
</content>

### [Other joint audio-video generation work, 2026](../models/JAVG_2026_misc.md) ⚠️

This batch of works diverges into four paradigms on joint audio-video captioning, making it the most information-rich cross-section along this dimension in 2026:
【**Paradigm one | Interleaved inline style (ALIVE)**】Three fields + two tags: Subjects [visual appearance + acoustic portrait] / Visual [scene] / Narration [action timeline + verbatim dialogue tagged <W> + non-speech acoustic events tagged <I>]. All three tracks (visual action, speech, sound effects) are inlined into the same temporal narrative, split by tags rather than by fields. Compared with Ovi's "<S>dialogue<E> inline + <AUDCAP> soundscape block appended at the end," ALIVE also pulls <I> (non-speech events) onto the timeline inline, making the temporal expression more thorough. Another distinctive point is that the Subjects field binds "visual appearance" and "acoustic portrait" under the same entity — this design elevates audio-video alignment from the "temporal level" to the "entity level," and is also the fundamental reason it must use a five-step SubjectID correction pipeline to guarantee correct attribution.
【**Paradigm two | Split-field parallel style (NAVA)**】Under a unified template, audio sits as an independent block alongside the visual block, and within the audio block is further subdivided into four categories: speech / SFX / music / ambient sound. The production method is "two models produce separately + a third model merges": Qwen3-VL produces the video caption, Qwen3-Omni produces the audio caption, and Gemini-3-Flash (or Pro) does the concatenation or rewriting-based merge. This "split first, then merge" production method corroborates the final "split-field parallel" structure, but it also brings a latent risk: when the two models generate independently, the audio and video descriptions may contradict each other, and the merging model must bear the responsibility of consistency checking (the paper does not state whether such a check exists [uncertain]).
【**Paradigm three | Attribute-based audio caption (OmniCustom)**】Explicitly constructs the audio caption "following the OVI model approach," emphasizing "speaker's age, gender, accent, and vocal characteristics (e.g., pitch, prosody, emotion, and speaking rate)" — i.e., the audio caption does not describe "what was heard" but rather "what the speaker is like," a speaker-attribute-based schema. Combined with GLM-ASR's segment-by-segment transcription, this forms two tracks: "attribute description + dialogue content." This schema directly serves its voice-customization task: controllable attributes = controllable timbre.
【**Paradigm four | Bypassing text captions (Baton)**】Does not construct a joint caption at all; instead it uses the planned tokens output by the VA-Planner as a semantic blueprint shared between audio and video, with the video side and audio side each aligned via dual semantic alignment towers to the continuous feature domains of SigLip2 and WavTokenizer respectively. This is a negation of the "joint caption" paradigm itself — its thesis is that text embeddings inherently lose fine-grained semantics, and using continuous perceptual features as the cross-modal intermediary carries more information. RS-RoPE (Relative Semantic RoPE) is responsible for injecting the blueprint into the denoising process.
【**No schema | CCL / StreamChar / ITS-JAVG**】CCL does not describe a joint caption design [uncertain]; StreamChar uses the transcript (dialogue lines) rather than a descriptive caption as its main conditioning, belonging to a "script-driven" paradigm; ITS-JAVG has no training data, but its validator AVHScore measures precisely the "semantic consistency between audio events and visual events" at inference time — equivalent to taking the audio-visual semantic binding relationship that a joint caption would want to encode, and instead checking it at the output end with a discriminative model.
【**Lineage positioning relative to existing works**】Placing this batch into the bigger picture: Ovi is "inline tags + a trailing soundscape block," LTX-2 is "unified full-soundscape description," Script-a-Video is "fully factorized independent streams," and Foley-Omni is "three-field split." ALIVE belongs to a strengthened version of the Ovi route (more thorough inlining + entity-level binding), NAVA belongs to the factorized route (block-parallel splitting), OmniCustom belongs to the dedicated attribute-based route, and Baton opens up a fourth path of "non-text intermediary." The state of play in the first half of 2026 is: two mainstream lines — inline temporal structuring and split-field structuring — running in parallel, alongside the emergence of explorations that bypass text altogether.

### [Collection of joint audio-video generation baselines](../models/JavisDiT_baselines.md) ⚠️

This collection's approaches to joint audio-video caption structure diverge markedly, conveniently covering three representative routes:
【**Route zero: no joint caption (MM-Diffusion / AV-DiT)**】Unconditional generation, no joint caption schema exists. Audio-picture alignment relies entirely on architectural mechanisms (random-shift attention / cross-modal adapter) and the native synchrony of the data itself, without any text intermediary.
【**Route one: a single mixed caption (JavisDiT / JavisDiT++)**】Uses TAVGBench's single passage of text, which describes visual and auditory content simultaneously but is not split into independent fields and contains no timestamps or speech tags. JavisDiT's key insight is precisely that "a single text conditioning input is insufficient to guarantee fine-grained spatiotemporal synchronization" — so rather than manipulating the caption structure, it additionally introduces HiST-Sypo (a Hierarchical Spatio-Temporal synchrony Prior estimator) on top of the text, first estimating a coarse-grained global prior and a fine-grained spatiotemporal prior from the prompt, and then using that prior to guide both denoising paths simultaneously. This can be understood as: JavisDiT moves "the spatiotemporal alignment information a joint caption ought to encode" from the text layer to an implicit prior layer. This is a third path, entirely different from Ovi (encoding timing via inline <S>dialogue<E> tags) and Foley-Omni (three-field splitting).
【**Route two: fully split three fields (Harmony, the most complete joint schema in this collection)**】Gemini annotation produces three independent text tracks:
(1) Visual track — a descriptive video caption;
(2) Speech track — ASR transcription text (transcript);
(3) Non-speech auditory track — background/ambient sound caption.
The three serve as independent conditioning fields rather than being concatenated into one sentence, so they can be combined by task as needed. Harmony-Bench's three-tier subsets directly validate the value of this design: the ambient-sound subset uses only video + audio captions, the speech subset mainly uses the transcript, and the complex-scene subset uses the full suite of multimodal prompts — showing that this schema supports "activating conditioning channels on demand." This may seem to contradict Ovi's ablation conclusion that "merging into a single T5 embedding outperforms separate CLAP/T5 encoding," but the two are actually addressing different problems: Ovi's separation is at the encoder level (different encoders cause the representation spaces to be disjoint), whereas Harmony's splitting is at the field level (possibly still sharing the same text encoder, umT5) [uncertain: whether Harmony's three fields share umT5 encoding before being concatenated].
【**UniAVGen**】The joint caption structure is not described [uncertain]; its conditioning form is mainly reference image + reference audio + transcript, and text caption is not the core conditioning channel.

### [Kling 3.0 / Kling Video 3.0 Omni](../models/Kling_30_Omni.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] Kling 3.0 Omni's joint audio-video caption schema is undisclosed. Native, simultaneous generation of audio and picture necessarily requires training samples to have both a visual and an auditory description track. The public paradigm given by the same team's Kling-Foley is "split then merge": first an audio classification model splits the audio track into sound effects/music/speech/singing; each category then has an audio-understanding LLM generate an audio description, while a video description is generated in parallel; finally an LLM merges the video description and audio description into a unified caption. Its open-source evaluation set, Kling-Audio-Eval, explicitly requires the video caption and audio caption to be annotated independently (to avoid cross-modal bias contaminating each other), and comes with sound-event tags (1919 tags across nine major sound scenes). This three-layer structure of "independent fields + event tags + merged caption" is very likely the prototype for Kling 3.0 Omni's joint caption, but there is no official confirmation.

### [LTX-2](../models/LTX-2.md)

The core contribution of this entry, and also one of the best examples of joint audio-video caption design in currently public materials.
【**Design principle**】A single caption exhaustively covers both the visual and auditory tracks at once (describing both the visual and auditory tracks of a clip in exhaustive detail), rather than being split into multiple independent fields — this is a different route from Script-a-Video's factorized streams and Foley-Omni's three fields: LTX-2 takes the route of a "fused, full-information long description."
【**Coverage on the auditory side (full soundscape)**】
- Music: genre, instrumentation, rhythm, mood
- Ambient sounds/atmosphere (ambient sounds, background): wind, rain, city noise floor, indoor room tone, crowd chatter
- Foley/sound effects: footsteps, clothing rustle, keyboard clacking, door creaks, glass clinking
- Dialogue: precise transcriptions, with speaker, language, and accent identification attached
【**Coverage on the visual side**】Camera motion, lighting, subject behavior, as well as appearance, scene, and style provenance.
【**Consistency design**】The official prompting guide is isomorphic to the training caption structure — user prompts likewise require a single paragraph, chronological order, and explicit description of ambience/foley/music/speech, with dialogue lines placed in quotation marks and language/accent optionally noted, using physical action cues rather than emotion labels to direct performance. This confirms the closed design loop of "the training caption is the inference interface."
【**Significance**】Audio is no longer an appendage to the visual caption but a description dimension on equal footing with vision; "audio information-content filtering (data side) + full-soundscape dual-track caption (annotation side)" together form the complete logic of LTX-2's data methodology: first ensure the sample's audio track carries information, then ensure the annotation fully exposes that information to the model.
【**Undisclosed**】The length ratio between visual and auditory content within the caption, the template or slot definitions for the auditory description, and whether there is a quality-verification step.

### [LongCat-Video](../models/LongCat-Video.md)

The base version has no audio modality; not applicable.
Although Avatar 1.5's caption scheme uses the omni-modal model Qwen3-Omni, its output consists of three visual/narrative-dimension fields — "Spatial Environment, Interpersonal Relationships, Plot Progression" — and requires descriptions to focus on objective physical manifestation rather than subjective inference. This is a narrative-context schema serving audio-driven character video, not a soundscape description covering the auditory track, and it differs from genuine joint audio-video caption paradigms such as LTX-2's full-soundscape description or Foley-Omni's three fields. In addition, Avatar 1.5's offline stage also generates a "temporal-span caption" (a caption split by time segment/span) used to describe local clips — this is fine-grained description along the time dimension rather than joint description along the modality dimension.

### [MOVA](../models/MOVA.md)

MOVA adopts a factorized-then-merged scheme of "first split into three independent tracks, then have an LLM merge them into a single unified caption" — a compromise between Script-a-Video-style factorized streams and LTX-2-style full-soundscape description:
【**Three independent tracks (splitting stage)**】
1) Visual track (MiMo-VL-7B-RL): visual description + on-screen text, strictly forbidden to reference audio.
2) Speech track (Qwen3-Omni-Instruct): verbatim dialogue transcription, strictly forbidden to include non-speech content or music.
3) Non-speech audio track (Qwen3-Omni-Captioner): descriptions of acoustic characteristics such as sound effects, music, ambient sound, recording quality, and speaker timbre and accent.
The three tracks are strictly isolated via mutually exclusive prompt constraints, preventing hallucination cross-talk between modalities.
【**Role reallocation at the merging stage (key design)**】GPT-OSS-120B's merging rules explicitly specify the "authoritative scope" of each of the three tracks:
- The **actual content** of dialogue is governed strictly by speech_description;
- **Dynamic speaker information** (total number of speakers, speaker switching, timbre characteristics such as high-pitched/hoarse) is governed by audio_description;
- The speaker's **visual anchoring** (who is speaking, where they are standing, what they are wearing) is governed by video_description.
  The three cross-reference each other, binding together the three layers of information: "who said what, what it sounds like, and who it looks like."
- Non-speech audio may appear only within four categories: ambient/background sound, music, audio subject/sound source, and structural audio changes (e.g., silence transitioning to a crescendo), and is strictly forbidden from mentioning any human voice or words.
- Rule 4 explicitly requires avoiding cross-segment repetition (e.g., the audio segment must not restate the speaker's visual position); rule 5 requires skipping empty fields, so that the final result reads like a coherent narrative written by a human rather than a rigid structure.
【**Final product**】A single, merged natural-language paragraph, with no independent fields retained. Thus at training time the model sees a unified caption rather than separated visual/audio conditioning.
【**Compared to similar approaches**】Relative to Foley-Omni's three fields kept side by side and LTX-2's single full-soundscape description, MOVA's characteristic is "splitting at annotation time, merging at training time," balancing annotation accuracy (splitting suppresses hallucination) with conditioning simplicity at training time (a single text condition).

### [Merged entry: ① Mochi 1](../models/Mochi_MAGI_Motif.md)

Not applicable. None of the three have an audio modality; captions cover only the visual track. Although Motif-Video 2B has a strict JSON schema (fields such as camera / subject / action / environment / lighting / on-screen text), all of them are visual fields containing no auditory-track description whatsoever; MAGI-1's 8 predefined attributes are likewise purely visual. There is no full-soundscape description, factorized audio/visual streams, or independent audio field.

### [Movie Gen](../models/Movie_Gen.md)

Movie Gen has no unified joint audio-video caption schema — vision and hearing belong to two separate independent data streams: Movie Gen Video's captions are purely visual and do not describe sound at all; Movie Gen Audio's captions are purely auditory and do not describe the picture (picture information is fed in directly as video-embedding conditioning, with text serving only as a supplement).
However, the audio caption itself is a structured, four-field template split into parts (Table 27), which can be viewed as a "factorized schema internal to the auditory track":
① Audio quality: "This audio has quality: 8.0." (a continuous 1–10 score given by an audio-quality prediction model, annotated in a manner modeled on the LAION aesthetic score)
② Presence of voice and music: "This audio does not contain speech. This audio does not contain vocal singing." + "This audio contains music with a 0.90 likelihood." (speech/singing use a binary output from AED posterior probabilities thresholded; music is kept as a continuous posterior probability because of ambiguity — e.g., a riser in film/TV can resemble both a sound effect and music)
③ Sound description: "This audio has a description: 'gentle waves lapping against the shore, and music plays in the background.'" (a free-form description from a general-purpose audio-captioning model)
④ Music-style description: "This audio has a music description (if applicable): 'A beautiful, romantic, and sentimental jazz piano solo.'" (the music-captioning model outputs mood and genre; this field is appended unconditionally regardless of whether the sample contains music, working together with the music probability from ② to achieve optimal controllability)
The paper emphasizes "prioritizing video alignment over text alignment," because text is merely supplementary and cannot cover every detail in the video, and text is invisible to the final viewer.

### [NVIDIA NeMo Curator](../models/NeMo_Curator.md)

None. This is the core gap in this toolchain with respect to joint audio-video generation scenarios.
Video captioning describes only the visual track and does not involve any auditory content (no description field for music/ambient sound/foley/dialogue); the audio pipeline produces only ASR transcription text, which is not associated with the video picture. There is no caption schema in the framework that covers both the visual and auditory tracks simultaneously — there is neither an LTX-2-style "fused full-soundscape long description" nor a Script-a-Video-style factorized streams or Foley-Omni-style three-field design.
Nemotron 3 Nano Omni, introduced in 26.07, is an omni-modal model with audio-video understanding capability, and in principle has the potential to produce joint captions, but in the official documentation its use is quality scoring for OCR synthetic data and as a video-captioning backend — no stage or example of joint audio-video annotation is seen. If this framework is used to serve a joint AV generation model, the joint-caption layer must be built entirely from scratch.

### [OmniHuman dataset + OHBench](../models/OmniHuman.md) ⚠️

OmniHuman's joint audio-visual caption adopts a hybrid scheme of "structured multi-field coexistence + final synthesis into a single narrative," and along the field dimension it is the broadest-coverage sample in this survey:
【**Auditory-side fields**】Background-sound tags, a music-attribute triple (genre/mood/relative volume), per-speaker speech transcription text, speech emotion, speech timestamps, speech-quality annotation, lip-sync quality assessment.
【**Visual-side fields**】Scene type, shot size, camera movement, background, lighting, person appearance, body movement, expression state, person-person interaction, person-object interaction, 134 keypoints, facial clarity.
【**Merging method**】In stage one, Qwen3-Omni (omni-modal, consuming both the visual and auditory streams simultaneously) extracts all of the above attributes — note that the extraction itself is already cross-modal, rather than a visual model annotating vision and an audio model annotating audio which are then concatenated; in stage two, all the fragments are synthesized into a single long-form narrative caption, with the visual and auditory content interwoven within the narrative rather than laid out side by side in separate sections.
【**Positioning compared with three existing routes**】
- UniVerse-1: "split at annotation, split at training too" (the three fields remain independent throughout);
- MOVA: "annotate separately, then use a 120B LLM to synthesize a single paragraph";
- UniTalking: "keep both the factorized and the fused finished products, randomly picking one of the two at training time";
- OmniHuman, by contrast, is "using an omni-modal model to extract structured fields cross-modally in one pass → synthesize a single narrative caption," and the structured fields themselves are also released together with the dataset (the sample_json contains four field categories side by side: captions / subjects / speech / audio). This means users can obtain both the fused natural-language caption and the raw structured fields to recombine themselves — more practical than schemes that release only the final caption.
【**The most distinctive point: music's "relative volume" field**】This explicitly quantifies the energy relationship between background music and the human voice, enabling downstream filtering of data by soundscape composition (e.g., training a dubbing model using only voice-dominant samples, or training full-soundscape generation using only music-rich samples). This level of granularity is not seen in comparable works.
【**Hallucination protection**】Achieved via a placeholder mechanism (speech content filled in from ASR) and consistency checks (subject count aligned with tracking results, transcription content aligned with ASR) — see model_as_data_judge for details. This is a reliability advantage of this schema relative to comparable works.
【**Limitation**】Whether the caption and the structured fields are fully consistent has not been comprehensively verified (only subject count and speech content were checked); although sound effects are listed among the extracted attributes in stage one, no corresponding independent annotation field description is seen, and the granularity at which they are presented in the final caption is unclear. [Uncertain]

### [Open-Sora series](../models/Open-Sora.md)

Not applicable. Neither project generates audio; captions cover only the visual track (subject/action/background/lighting/camera movement/style). There is no auditory-track description field, nor any LTX-2-style full-soundscape description or Foley-Omni-style three-field split design.

### [Ovi](../models/Ovi.md)

Ovi's joint caption schema is a hybrid structure of "interleaved inline + a trailing soundscape block," covering the visual, speech, and non-speech auditory tracks simultaneously:
(1) Visual track: natural language narrating visual events, unfolding in chronological order, forming the backbone of the caption.
(2) Speech track: inlined into the corresponding temporal position within the caption backbone using <S>...<E> tags, achieving "temporal interleaving of visual events and dialogue lines" — the key design for aligning semantics and timing (equivalent to weaving the script and the shot description together).
(3) Non-speech auditory track: wrapped at the end with <AUDCAP>...<ENDAUDCAP> (in version 1.1, "Audio: ..."), forming a complete soundscape description block. Its content adaptively switches depending on whether the clip contains speech:
   - For clips containing speech: it emphasizes the speaker's acoustic attributes — age, gender, accent, pitch, prosody, emotion, and speaking rate;
   - For clips without speech: it describes sound effects, background audio, and musical elements.
【**Positioning relative to other schemes**】Unlike LTX-2's unified full-soundscape description, Script-a-Video's fully factorized independent field streams, and Foley-Omni's three-field split, Ovi chooses "not to split into independent conditions" — all three tracks are packed into the same passage of text and the same T5 embedding, distinguished by tags rather than fields.
【**Ablation validation**】The only ablation in Section 5.5 targets exactly this point: the initial design used a CLAP encoder to encode the non-speech audio description and T5 to encode the speech transcription, attempting to decouple the T2A and TTS tasks; it was found that this separated setup limited the model's ability to generate coherent output — it could do sound effects alone or speech alone, but struggled to fuse the two into a unified, coherent audio track. After switching to a merged single T5 embedding, WER stayed roughly flat (0.033 → 0.035), while FD_PANNs dropped from 20.78 to 18.03, FD_VGG dropped from 7.13 to 5.02, IS rose from 8.34 to 11.20, and CLAP rose from 0.190 to 0.224. This provides direct quantitative evidence that "a joint AV caption should be encoded uniformly rather than split."
【**Alignment of pure-audio data**】Pure-audio corpora likewise use the same MLLM to produce two parts, "transcription + audio description" (with the transcription left blank if there is no speech), keeping the schema consistent with the audio-video side and ensuring the conditioning distribution does not drift across the two training stages.

### [Script-a-Video](../models/Script-a-Video.md)

This field is where Script-a-Video's most valuable contribution lies; its scheme sits at the deepest end of the structuring spectrum among comparable works:
【**Comparative positioning**】Arranged along the axis of "how audio-visual information is organized": LTX-2's single full-soundscape description (fused) → Foley-Omni's three fields side by side (shallow split) → UniVerse-1's three independent fields (split but unrelated) → MOVA's split-then-LLM-merged-into-a-single-paragraph (split then merged) → MTSS's four-stream decomposition + explicit relationship graph (split then explicitly reconnected). MTSS is the only scheme that, after splitting, reconnects the streams using "machine-readable reference relationships" rather than "natural-language narration."
【**Whether it covers both vision and hearing simultaneously**】Yes. The Shot stream carries the visual track, the Event stream carries the auditory track (three categories: dialogue/sound effects/music), the global_audio of the Global stream carries the ambient noise floor and background music, and the Reference stream is shared across modalities (referenced both by Shot's references_in_shot and by Event's speaker).
【**Whether it is split into independent fields**】Yes, and it is four streams rather than the more common two or three. The key difference is the addition of a Reference stream — this stream does not belong to any single modality but instead exists as a shared, cross-modal identity anchor; it is the structural innovation that distinguishes MTSS from other factorized schemes.
【**Two mechanisms of cross-modal connection**】
1) Identity linking: Event's speaker field and Shot's references_in_shot array simultaneously point to the same ID in the Reference stream. This means that "who is speaking" is explicitly expressed in the data structure as the "audio event's speaker pointer" and the "visual shot's visible-subject pointer" pointing to the same entity — the binding between speaker and on-screen character is structural and machine-verifiable, rather than relying on the implicit correspondence of natural-language description. This is the most direct means of resolving audio-video identity mismatches.
2) Temporal linking: Shot's active_events field links the shot to the audio events occurring concurrently; both sides share the same global timeline, and Shot's and Event's respective time_range fields can be directly used for interval arithmetic; finer-grained correspondence is handled by the intra-description / micro-level timestamps on both sides.
【**The admission filter for audio events is itself the alignment guarantee**】The strict audio-video coupling principle (a sound effect must be produced by a visible subject) essentially dissolves "audio-picture mismatch" samples at the annotation stage — a mismatched sound does not become an Event but is instead demoted to global_audio. This is an ingenious use of schema design in place of a filter: no additional audio-picture consistency discriminator model is needed, since the annotation rule itself accomplishes the splitting.
【**Direct downstream generation benefits (quantified)**】When replacing a monolithic prompt with the MTSS script, with the architecture entirely unchanged (LTX-2-AV → LTX-2-AV-MTSS): human-rated identity consistency 1.22 → 1.77 (+45%), human-rated audio-video alignment 1.18 → 1.85 (+56%), human-rated multi-shot controllability 1.00 → 1.71 (+71%); WER dropped from 0.84 to 0.13; Shot Boundary Deviation 3.79 → 3.27 frames. This comparison — "same architecture, same training paradigm, the sole variable being prompt structure" — is this work's most persuasive evidence: the paper explicitly states that the two baselines share exactly the same architecture and training paradigm, so any performance gap between them can be directly and entirely attributed to the MTSS paradigm itself.
【**Limitation**】The schema has no explicit fields for audio quality/loudness/acoustic environment (e.g., recording environment, reverberation, volume), nor structured language/accent tags; timbre description is only implicit within Event's free-text description.

### [Seedance 2.0 and Seedance 1.5 pro](../models/Seedance_20_Seedance_15_pro.md) ⚠️

Seedance 1.5 pro is the first version in this series to introduce audio-side description: its captioning system "provides rich, professional-grade descriptions for both the video and audio modalities," i.e., systematically introducing structured audio descriptions on top of traditional "video-caption" data, so that the model internalizes a richer joint audio-video semantic space. However, the report does not disclose the specific schema — it does not state whether it is a single full-soundscape description (LTX-2 style) or split into independent field streams (such as Script-a-Video's factorized streams or Foley-Omni's three fields). Judging from SeedVideoBench 1.5/2.0's audio-tag taxonomy (three main categories — vocal type / vocal attributes / non-speech audio, refined to 17 categories in 2.0) and Seedance 2.0's support for parallel output of three tracks — background music, ambient sound effects, and character voice — the training side very likely adopts a multi-field annotation structure split by audio track, but this is an inference rather than something the paper states explicitly. [Uncertain: the exact form of the schema and field names]

### [SkyReels series](../models/SkyReels.md)

Only SkyReels-V4 has this; it adopts a scheme of "a single structured caption + special-token partitioning," positioned between LTX-2's fused long description and Foley-Omni-style multiple fields:
【**Tokenized schema**】The structured caption uses five pairs of special tokens to explicitly partition and annotate:
- <text></text>: text appearing within the picture (in-video text, serving multilingual text-rendering capability);
- <sfx></sfx>: sound effects / foley;
- <dialogue></dialogue>: dialogue content (Whisper transcription results filled in directly);
- <singing></singing>: singing content (including lyric transcription);
- <bgm></bgm>: background music.
