【**Design points**】(1) Visual and auditory information coexist within the same text sequence, uniformly encoded by a shared, frozen MLLM text encoder that then drives both the video stream and the audio stream simultaneously — this is the key to achieving audio-video semantic alignment via "shared text encoder + token partitioning"; (2) the four auditory tokens correspond strictly one-to-one with the four-way audio classification (sound effects/music/speech/singing), and the classification result can directly determine which token gets filled in; (3) the visual token <text> sits alongside the auditory tokens, indicating that on-screen text is treated as "symbolic content requiring exact reproduction" on par with the audio track; (4) the special tokens allow precise control of a particular modality component at inference time (e.g., specifying only <sfx> while leaving <dialogue> empty to generate a dialogue-free scene).
【**Undisclosed**】Whether there are finer attribute slots inside the tokens (e.g., speaker, emotion, timbre), the length ratio between visual and auditory descriptions, and the method for caption quality verification.

### [Sora 2](../models/Sora_2.md) ⚠️

Entirely undisclosed. As a native audio-video model, Sora 2 must necessarily cover annotation of the auditory track (otherwise it could not respond to text instructions about dialogue/sound effects/music); in actual use, users can specify dialogue lines directly via quoted dialogue in the prompt, indicating that an explicit dialogue field or an equivalent mechanism exists in the caption schema. But OpenAI has not stated whether it uses a single fused caption or splits into independent visual/dialogue/sound-effect fields the way LTX-2 (full-soundscape description), Script-a-Video (factorized streams), and Foley-Omni (three fields) do. This is the largest information gap when comparing against comparable open-source works. [Uncertain]

### [SpeakerVid-5M](../models/SpeakerVid-5M.md)

SpeakerVid-5M adopts a schema of "multiple tracks kept side by side, without merging," making it the least-merged tier in this survey:
【**Visual track**】A structured JSON caption generated by Qwen2.5-VL (camera movement, entities, orientation, body composition, action, expression, etc.), which does not describe auditory content at all.
【**Speech track**】Whisper's ASR transcription text + confidence, stored in a separate asr folder.
【**Sync track**】Three SyncNet metrics (offset, confidence, embedding distance) saved as independent numerical annotations.
【**Structure track**】DWpose skeletal keypoint sequences (code open-sourced; data not uploaded due to size).
【**Quality track**】Face/hand clarity scores in the l_score folder, and clear/DOVER scores in merge_anno.
【**Key gap**】There is no non-speech audio description — no text description whatsoever of sound effects, ambient sound, background music, recording spatial sense, or speaker timbre/accent. This stands in sharp contrast to MOVA's Qwen3-Omni-Captioner non-speech track, LTX-2's full-soundscape description, and Foley-Omni's three-field schema. SpeakerVid-5M's audio-side annotation has only two categories — "what was said (transcription)" and "how well synced (SyncNet values)" — with no "what it sounds like."
【**There is also no merging step**】There is no cross-modal consistency check or unified-caption synthesis step comparable to GPT-OSS-120B in MOVA. Each track is released side by side as a folder, left for users to combine themselves.
【**Assessment**】This schema reflects SpeakerVid-5M's positioning — it is a "human-interaction structural dataset" rather than an "audio-video semantic dataset"; its value lies in precise speaker-picture-lip-shape correspondence and rich structured metadata, not in rich joint audio-video semantic description. Downstream users who need a joint caption must annotate it themselves.

### [Step-Video-T2V](../models/Step-Video-T2V.md) ⚠️

Not applicable. The model has no audio modality; captions cover only the visual track (subject, action, environment, visual presentation, camera movement). There is no visual + auditory dual-track caption structure, nor any soundscape description, speaker field, or sound-effect field. It can serve as a reference baseline for "pure-visual captions": within its three-way caption design (short/dense/original title), the idea of "keeping the original title to introduce a real human text-style distribution" could in principle be transferred orthogonally to an AV caption system (e.g., keeping the video's original title and human-written descriptions as a source of style diversity for soundscape captions), but the report itself contains no AV-related content. [Uncertain]

### [UniTalking](../models/UniTalking.md) ⚠️

UniTalking's joint audio-video caption schema is the only design in this survey's samples that provides both a "factorized" and a "fused" scheme simultaneously and lets the model randomly encounter either — it can be seen as a compromise answer to this problem:
【**Two routes coexist**】
- Factorized route (formats a, b): the visual description and the auditory description are generated separately by different dedicated models, then concatenated into one passage of text. Although physically concatenated into a single string before being fed to UMT5, the two portions of content are semantically distinct — close to Script-a-Video's factorized streams and Foley-Omni's multi-field-side-by-side idea;
- Fused route (format c): Qwen3-Omni consumes the video stream and audio stream simultaneously, producing a single unified description with vision and hearing interwoven. This is closer to LTX-2's single full-soundscape description and MOVA's idea of merging via a large LLM.
【**Positioning relative to the two comparison works**】UniVerse-1 is "split at annotation, split at training too" (the three fields remain independent and are fed to the model side by side throughout); MOVA is "split at annotation, merged at training" (annotated separately, then synthesized into a single paragraph by a 120B LLM); UniTalking is "keep both finished products, randomly picking one per sample at training time." The three form three distinct points in this design space.
【**Form of conditioning injection**】Regardless of which format, the result is ultimately flattened into a single text sequence (fixed length 512), encoded by a frozen UMT5, and fed into cross-attention; the model side does not distinguish which format the text came from, nor does it retain independent conditioning channels for the visual description versus the auditory description. This means factorization exists only at the annotation stage and the model architecture itself is not factorized — the conditioning channel that is genuinely factorized is a different one: the reference-timbre audio (c_audio) has its own independent KV projection layer, kept separate from the text conditioning and then summed together for fusion. Thus UniTalking's dimension of conditioning separation is "semantics (text) vs. timbre (reference audio)," not "vision vs. hearing."
【**Attribution of transcription content**】Whisper-V3's ASR transcription is classified under the audio caption side, i.e. dialogue content and acoustic-environment description share the same passage of text and are not listed as a separate field (contrast with UniVerse-1, which lists "verified speech content" as its own separate field). This requires the model, during training, to separate "what is to be said" from "what it sounds like" on its own from the mixed text. [Uncertain]
【**Missing mechanisms**】There is no consistency check among the three caption types; no cross-modal hallucination protection; no caption quality scoring or filtering; no verification of alignment between the caption and the audio-video content. Nor was there any ablation on "which caption format is more effective."

### [UniVerse-1](../models/UniVerse-1.md)

UniVerse-1 adopts a pure factorized-streams scheme, making it one of the samples in this survey with the most "thoroughly separated" joint audio-video caption structure:
【**Three tracks kept side by side, never merged**】The visual track (video caption), the speech track (verified speech content), and the non-speech audio track (ambient audio caption) are all produced in a single pass by Qwen2.5-Omni and retained as three independent fields all the way through to the training stage, without ever being merged.
【**Design orientation**】Compared with LTX-2's "single full-soundscape description" and MOVA's "split-then-merged-by-a-120B-LLM-into-a-single-natural-language-paragraph," UniVerse-1's scheme is closer to Script-a-Video's factorized streams and Foley-Omni's three fields side by side. Its advantage is that the conditioning for the three modalities can be controlled independently at inference time (the user can specify dialogue lines, picture, and ambient sound separately); its disadvantage is the lack of a cross-modal consistency-check step — the three annotation tracks are produced simultaneously by the same model and in principle share context, but the paper does not state whether there is a mechanism to prevent or detect semantic conflicts among the three.
【**Explicit split between speech and non-speech**】Splitting "what was said" and "what other sounds there are" into two fields is a natural choice aligned with its data mixture (speech only 15.4%, non-speech 84.6%): for the large number of samples, the speech field is empty and the ambient field dominates; for the small number of speech samples, both fields are populated.
【**Correspondence with generation tasks**】The three-field structure directly supports Verse-Bench's four categories of evaluation tasks (joint generation, audio-to-video, video-to-audio, TTS) — because the conditioning is separated, any subset of the channels can be masked out at will to construct different task forms.
【**Key differentiator: online alignment**】This scheme's real uniqueness lies not in the field division itself, but in the fact that the annotation for these three tracks is generated on the fly, at training time, for the actual sampled window — so the three annotation tracks share the same time window with each other and with the audio-video latents fed into the model, fundamentally eliminating the temporal mismatch that offline annotation produces. This is an alignment guarantee for the "joint caption schema" along the temporal dimension, not merely along the field dimension.

### [Unison](../models/Unison.md) ⚠️

Unison's joint audio-video annotation schema is a two-field factorized structure, but its real distinctiveness lies not at the annotation layer but in "the one-to-one correspondence between the annotation schema and the audio-generation streams":
【**Annotation layer: caption + transcription, two fields side by side**】The visual/scene description and the speech transcription are input as two independent text-conditioning streams that are never merged throughout. Compared with UniVerse-1's three fields (video caption / verified speech content / ambient audio caption), it is missing one field — Unison has no independent "ambient sound description" field; ambient-sound information is implicit within the caption text. Unlike MOVA's approach of "split annotation then merged by a 120B LLM into a single paragraph," Unison splits at annotation and uses the splits separately.
【**Generation layer: speech stream + sfx stream, dual streams side by side**】The audio latent is physically split into two streams, speech and sound effects, each with its own independent ground-truth latent (produced by Mel-RoFormer separation) and its own independent flow-matching loss.
【**The correspondence between the two layers — this work's structural distinguishing feature**】The transcription text naturally corresponds to the speech stream (what is said), and the caption text naturally corresponds to the sfx stream and the visual stream (what is in the picture, and hence what sound there should be). SCG works precisely by exploiting this correspondence: it uses the transcription's global semantic vector to modulate the speech stream's gating of the sfx stream, and uses the caption's global semantic vector to modulate the sfx stream's gating of the speech stream. Thus, in Unison, "the field division of the annotation schema" and "the stream division of audio generation" are isomorphic to each other — a decoupling more thorough than a plain factorized caption: not only is the annotation split, but the generation targets and supervision signals are split as well.
【**Positioning relative to comparable schemes**】
- LTX-2 "single full-soundscape description": annotation not split, generation not split;
- MOVA: annotation split then merged into a single caption, generation not split;
- UniVerse-1: annotation split three ways, generation not split (a single audio latent);
- Foley-Omni/Script-a-Video: annotation split into multiple fields, generation varies case by case;
- Unison: annotation split two ways + generation split into two streams + a one-to-one correspondence between the two + dynamic gated rebalancing. This is the highest tier of decoupling thoroughness in this survey.
【**Cost and limitation**】The lack of an independent ambient-sound description field means users cannot specify ambient-sound content separately the way UniVerse-1 allows; ambient sound can only be controlled indirectly via the scene caption. Also, neither annotation stream undergoes cross-modal consistency checking — if there is a semantic conflict between the caption and the transcription (e.g., the caption describes a quiet library while the transcription is shouting), there is no detection mechanism. The paper does not discuss this. [Uncertain]
【**Ablation support**】Table 2 shows that removing SGHS (Semantics-Guided Harmony Strategy, i.e. canceling the dual-stream decoupling) causes PQ to drop from 6.34 to 6.12, the largest decrease among all audio-side ablations, which the paper attributes to "spectral interference"; removing SCG (i.e. keeping the dual streams but canceling the semantic gating) drops PQ to 6.21 and LSE-C from 3.30 to 3.22. These two results together demonstrate the independent contributions of "decoupling" and "text-semantics-based rebalancing" respectively.

### [Veo 3 / Veo 3.1](../models/Veo_3_Veo_31.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] The official statement is: "Audio and video clips were annotated with text captions at different levels of detail," meaning both audio and video are textually annotated, indicating that captions cover both the auditory and visual tracks; the model can respond separately to quoted dialogue, sound-effect descriptions, and ambient-sound descriptions within a prompt, which likewise indicates that these three categories of auditory information are explicitly described in the training captions. But officially it has never been stated how these are organized: whether they are fused into a single "full-soundscape" description as with LTX-2, split into factorized streams as with Script-a-Video, or split into three independent fields as with Foley-Omni; whether the audio and video captions share the same passage of text, and whether they are injected separately into different cross-attention branches — none of this information is public.

### [Vidu S1](../models/Vidu_S1.md)

A typical "joint audio-video caption but dual-path decoupled" scheme: the caption covers both the visual track and the auditory track simultaneously (the auditory side contains three field categories: dialogue, sound effects, background music). To improve annotation fidelity and reduce cross-modal hallucination, it adopts a dual-path strategy that decouples the two modalities — visual attributes are inferred exclusively from video frames, and auditory attributes are inferred exclusively from the audio track; the two are never cross-referenced with each other, and are only merged into a structured description afterward. The paper states that this structured annotation scheme improves the quality and consistency of the multimodal representation, and makes annotation more reliable and generation more controllable. This belongs to the same lineage of thinking as LTX-2's full-soundscape description and Foley-Omni's three fields, but Vidu S1's distinguishing feature is layering on speech-aware temporal chunk-wise alignment.

### [Wan 2.5 / 2.6 / 2.7](../models/Wan.md) ⚠️

Wan 2.1's V2A module gives an explicit three-part joint audio-video caption structure, the most valuable first-hand design in this entry for reference; but from 2.5 onward, once it shifted toward speech and lip-sync, the schema is entirely undisclosed.
【**Wan 2.1 V2A three-component structured caption**】As the report states, the final structured caption integrates three parts — (1) dense video description; (2) ambient sound characterization; (3) background music analysis, the last of which is characterized by four attributes: style, rhythm, melody, and instrumentation.
【**Example (an actual template from Fig. 32 of the paper)**】"The video description: a horse is running. This audio contains ambient sound: the sound of clip-clop. This audio does not contain music." — this shows it to be a natural-language string concatenated from fixed sentence-pattern templates, and it explicitly supports a negative "does not contain music" statement, enabling the model to learn that "whether music is present" is itself a controllable condition.
【**Generation method**】The visual side continues to use an in-house dense video caption model; the audio side has Qwen2-Audio generate an audio-specific caption, categorized into ambient sound and music. This is a split design of "the visual captioner and the audio captioner each produce their output, then concatenated according to a fixed schema" — different from LTX-2's route of "a single captioner producing a fused full-soundscape long description," and closer to Foley-Omni's three-field idea.
【**Random masking during training**】The implementation details explicitly state that, during training, the ambient-sound caption and the music caption are randomly omitted with a preset probability. The purpose is to force the model to establish robust cross-modal associations from visual cues alone, while retaining controllability when text is present. This practice of "randomly dropping out caption components" is the most worthwhile training-side companion design to learn from within this schema.
【**Gaps**】The length ratio among the three types of text, the specific attribute vocabulary for the audio caption, and whether there is a quality-verification step are all undisclosed; how the schema was extended after speech was introduced in 2.5/2.6/2.7 (whether a dialogue-transcription field or a speaker field was added) is entirely unknown. [Uncertain]

### [Collection of audio-video generation evaluation benchmarks](../models/av_benchmarks.md)

This field is the most direct reverse guidance the evaluation ecosystem offers to the training-side caption schema:
【**VABench's factorized dual-stream scheme**】It explicitly has an LLM decouple a unified audio-visual description into two independent field streams, a visual sub-prompt and an auditory sub-prompt, and separately generates VQA/AQA question-answer pairs for cross-validation. This belongs to the same design philosophy as the training-side Script-a-Video's factorized streams and Foley-Omni's three-field scheme. The I2AV route's approach is particularly noteworthy: within the description a multimodal LLM generates from a static image, the visual portion is required to be objectively observable while the audio portion is required to be commonsense-inferred audio, after which humans review the plausibility of the auditory inference — this provides an actionable paradigm for "how to fill in an audio caption for a video/image with no audio track."
【**VABench's observability constraint**】The human-verification step explicitly checks "element observability" and "satisfaction of physical/commonsense constraints," i.e., the auditory description in the caption must be reasonably inferable from the picture and must not be fabricated out of thin air. This constraint can directly serve as a quality-control standard for audio captions in training data.
【**AVBench's three-way consistency split**】It splits the audio-visual-text relationship into three independent consistency dimensions — AT (audio-text), VT (video-text), AV (audio-video) — each with its own dedicated evaluator trained — indicating that joint-caption quality should be measured separately along these three edges rather than merged into a single score.
【**AV-SyncBench's orthogonal split**】It further splits the AV edge into two orthogonal sub-dimensions, "temporal alignment" and "semantic matching," meaning a complete joint AV caption quality check requires at least 4 edges: AT, VT, AV-temporal, AV-semantic.
【**PhyAVBench**】Audio attributes within the prompt are expressed implicitly in the form of physical causality (describing physical conditions rather than directly describing the sound) — another schema idea: letting the model derive the acoustic outcome from physical conditions rather than giving a sound label directly.
【**Omni-Judge**】Among its 9 evaluation dimensions is tri-modal coherence across audio-video-text, making it the only benchmark that explicitly defines a caption-quality metric for "tri-modal jointness."

### [Video caption model ecosystem](../models/caption_models.md)

Joint audio-video caption schema design is the most active point of innovation in this ecosystem in 2026, and four identifiable paradigms have already taken shape:

【**Paradigm one · Full-soundscape fused style (a single caption narrating both vision and hearing at once)**】LTX-2's in-house captioner is the benchmark: the visual side covers camera motion, lighting, and subject behavior; the audio side covers music and ambient sounds, plus precise dialogue transcription with the three attributes speaker/language/accent attached; the design principle is "comprehensive yet factual," explicitly forbidding emotional interpretation. AVoCaDO belongs to the same category, outputting free-form long text (not JSON), with its structure expressed via the five-dimensional breakdown of the reward checklist: Static Entity Description, Dynamic Action & Interaction, Auditory Elements (speech/music/ambient sound and narrative sound effects), Spatio-temporal & Cinematography, and **Cross-modal Narrative Logic** (vision and hearing mutually explaining/supplementing/guiding each other — its core innovation dimension).

【**Paradigm two · Factorized streams (split into independent fields, each forming its own track)**】Script-a-Video's MTSS (Multi-Track Structured Script) is the representative example, with 500K annotations generated by Gemini-2.5-Pro and then distilled into Qwen3-Omni-MTSS-FT. UniVerse-1 uses a single Qwen2.5-Omni to output, side by side in one pass, three independent fields — "verified speech content / descriptive video caption / ambient-sound caption" — without any merging. UniTalking produces four formats (a detailed version + a brief version of the visual caption from Qwen3-VL, a Whisper-V3 transcription, an audio caption from Qwen3-Omni-Captioner, and a fused unified description from Qwen3-Omni), sampled randomly during training.

【**Paradigm three · Fixed three-field schema (aligned with the natural categories of audio source separation)**】Foley-Omni's three-field design (speech / effects / music) is fully isomorphic to the output structure of Bandit (cinematic audio source separation) — using a general-purpose music-separation model instead (e.g. Demucs's vocals/drums/bass/other) would not correspond directly. This is the best example of schema design working in concert with a validation tool. MOVA's dual-modality division of labor (Qwen3-Omni-Instruct handling speech, Qwen3-Omni-Captioner handling non-speech and music, and GPT-OSS-120B merging and performing a cross-modal consistency check) is structurally equivalent to a three-field-then-merge approach.

【**Paradigm four · Script-level structuring (with timestamps and character binding)**】Qwen3.5-Omni provides "script-level fine-grained description, including automatic slicing, timestamp labeling, and description of the relationship between characters and audio," and is currently the only general-purpose model that natively supports this form. CineDance goes to the finest granularity: shot-level five-dimensional attributes + transition type + local cast list + sentence-level ASR + shot-level audio prompt (music/ambient sound/sound effects) + character voice description, and completes the binding from ASR sentences to character anchor tokens.

【**Formalization of quality criteria (AVSCap's contribution)**】A high-quality omni-modal caption must satisfy three criteria simultaneously: Acoustic Completeness (covering the three categories Speech / SFX / Music), Visual Completeness (environment, people, action, object interaction, camera motion, OCR), and Audio-Visual Synergy (explicit binding and temporal alignment of audio-visual events). This is currently the most actionable acceptance standard for a schema.

【**Implicit requirements of the input form**】Ovi's approach reveals a key constraint: the annotation input is "7 uniformly sampled keyframes + the full audio track," meaning the captioning model itself must have audio-understanding capability, and the sparse sampling of 7 frames means the visual description leans toward event-level/semantic-level rather than dense frame-by-frame — joint AV annotation is inherently an asymmetric form of "coarse vision, fine audio."

### [Collection of geometric/structured-annotation datasets](../models/geometric_datasets.md)

Not applicable. All four datasets are purely visual, with no audio-track description and no dual visual/auditory-stream caption schema. Compared with joint audio-video paradigms such as LTX-2's full-soundscape description, Script-a-Video's factorized streams, and Foley-Omni's three fields, this batch of datasets represents another, orthogonal direction — placing the second category of supervision beyond the caption on the geometric and state dimension (camera, depth, 3D trajectory, skeleton, world state, action ID) rather than the auditory dimension.

### [Post-training data strategy for video generation](../models/post_training_data.md) ⚠️

The anchor paper does not touch on joint audio-video caption at all [uncertain] — its four reward models are all along the visual dimension, and PE's three rewards (text-video alignment, video aesthetics, structural reward) likewise have no audio term. For models with AV generation capability, the paper only references, at the distillation stage, OmniForcing's asymmetric block-causal alignment and audio sink token — this belongs to architecture rather than the annotation schema.
【**Cross-cutting AV caption practices on the post-training side**】
· NAVA's SFT subset is re-annotated with Gemini-3-Pro to produce exactly "more accurate, structured, temporally grounded audio-visual captions" — a clear case of upgrading joint AV annotation at the post-training stage;
· The cinematic split of Movie Gen's audio SFT set is automatically screened by a cinematic-feel classifier + AED (audio event detection) and then manually selected; the annotation system distinguishes on-screen (diegetic) sound from off-screen ambient/theme music;
· JavisDiT++'s preference ranking uses ImageBind scoring separately along the two alignment chains of text-audio and text-video, which is equivalent to splitting the caption into two evaluation tracks, visual and auditory;
· Post-training of ecosystem captioners is already highly mature: AVoCaDO's GRPO reward includes five-dimensional checklist coverage (judged by GPT-4.1), dialogue F1 (speaker accuracy + content DP-alignment edit-distance similarity, threshold 0.6), and a length regularizer (penalty above 4096 tokens); AVSCap's GRPO mixed reward = length control + speech fidelity + audio-visual consistency, and it puts forward the key conclusion that "RL gains > scaling up SFT data volume"; Tencent Hunyuan 1.5's caption model uses OPA-DPO to suppress multimodal hallucination. These are mature paradigms for post-training on the caption side, but have not yet propagated back into the video generation model's own AV preference-reward design.

### [Merged survey of mainstream video pretraining datasets: Panda-70M, InternVid, Koala...](../models/pretraining_datasets.md)

Not applicable. The captions of all seven datasets **cover only the visual track**, containing no auditory-track description whatsoever and no field design that splits vision from hearing. Even UltraVideo, with the most fields (10 dimensions), and MiraData (6 independent fields) have dimensions that are entirely visual (subject/background/style/shot size/camera movement/lighting/atmosphere, etc.), none touching on soundscape, sound effects, speech, or music. Consequently there is no counterpart here to LTX-2-style full-soundscape description, Script-a-Video-style factorized streams, or Foley-Omni-style three-field structure. **This is the most fundamental capability gap between the seven datasets surveyed here and the data ecosystems of joint audio-video generation models: existing mainstream video pretraining datasets cannot provide any auditory-side supervision signal for AV models, and the joint audio-video data for AV models must be built entirely from scratch.**
## Dialogue transcription and speaker attribute annotation (ASR transcription + speaker identity/language/accent/emotion)

`dialogue_transcription_attributes` · Detail level: brief

### [Allegro](../models/Allegro.md)

Not applicable. No audio processing pipeline; no ASR transcription, speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation performed.

### [Apollo](../models/Apollo.md) ⚠️

This is the relatively most substantively disclosed part of Apollo's data system, and also a relatively differentiated investment compared with peer work:
【Transcription】Speech transcription is performed on the vocal subset (containing singing, single-speaker speech, and multi-speaker speech), using three models in parallel: Whisper-Large-v3, SenseVoice, Qwen2.5-Omni. The three are complementary in capability (Whisper for general multilingual coverage, SenseVoice for Chinese/dialects and emotional events, Qwen2.5-Omni for full-modality contextual understanding), presumably used for cross-validation or scenario-based dispatch, but the arbitration rule is not disclosed.
【Speaker attributes】Speaker attributes are explicitly extracted, exemplified as gender and age: "For speech and singing, we extract speaker attributes (e.g., gender, age)." The "e.g." indicates the attribute set is not limited to these two, but the full list is not given. Compared with MOVA's approach of describing timbre/accent/speech rate in natural language, Apollo's gender and age are closer to discrete structured fields.
【Speaker-count dimension】Expressed through data partitioning rather than an annotation field — single-speaker speech and multi-speaker speech are two separate subsets, from which the model can distinguish single-person/multi-person scenarios. However, whether speaker diarization and speaker labels ([S01]/[S02]) were applied within the multi-speaker subset is not stated in the paper; this is exactly the multi-speaker scenario bottleneck that MOVA explicitly points out.
【Accent/emotion】No mention of accent annotation; no mention of emotion annotation (despite SenseVoice natively supporting emotion recognition).
【Correspondence with evaluation】Evaluation metrics include WER (word error rate) and SyncNet Confidence (lip-sync confidence), corresponding to verifying the generatability of transcribed content and lip-shape alignment. Table 3 shows that full-task training brought WER down from 0.044 to 0.028.
[Uncertain] (complete list of the attribute set, diarization handling, and multi-ASR arbitration rule are all missing)

### [CineDance / CineDance-1M](../models/CineDance.md) ⚠️

Dialogue processing is the most fine-grained part of this dataset along the AV dimension:
【ASR transcription】Performed by Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B at sentence level, rather than as streaming output over the whole film; the segmentation granularity is aligned to sentence boundaries.
【Speaker-identity binding (core contribution)】Designed as a step independent of and subsequent to ASR, binding each ASR sentence to a global character anchor token ⟨charₖ⟩. A windowed scheme is adopted: non-speech intervals are filtered out first, and window segmentation simultaneously ensures shots are not cut mid-way and sentences are not truncated.
【Quantitative results (Tab.5 / Tab.7)】On a benchmark of 100 manually annotated clips:
  · Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B + windowing: 95.4% (whole-film input only 67.2%, sliding-window prompt 83.1%, whole-film 56.4% — the figures differ slightly across different tables);
  · Gemini family models: 82.8% ~ 87.4%;
  · DiariZen: 63.1%;
  · Pyannote-3.1: 62.7%.
  The conclusion is that Omni large models + windowing engineering significantly outperform traditional dedicated speaker-diarization tools.
【Timbre attribute】Provides a character voice description field describing the character's vocal characteristics.
【Missing dimensions】No explicit language label, accent label, or emotion label fields are seen, and speaker gender/age or other structured attributes are not annotated. [Uncertain]

### [CogVideoX](../models/CogVideoX.md) ⚠️

None. There is no ASR transcription, no speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation in the CogVideoX data pipeline, and the model does not generate speech either. On the contrary, the team actively lists "Lecture Type (dominated by continuous person speech, with minimal effective motion)" as a negative label and removes the entire category using a classifier with 0.99 accuracy — effectively excluding dialogue-dense footage at the data level. Whether the CogSound side processes speech is not disclosed [Uncertain].

### [Cosmos-Predict2.5](../models/Cosmos-Predict25.md)

Not applicable. There is no audio input, so no ASR transcription or speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation of any kind exists. Person-related annotation instead follows a purely visual-geometric route: the Human Dynamics domain uses YOLOX for human detection and RTMPose for full-body keypoint and facial landmark estimation, used to filter frame composition (person-frame ratio, cap on number of people, single-person area ratio), rather than for speech or expression-semantic annotation.

### [Data-Juicer 2.0](../models/Data-Juicer.md) ⚠️

【Transcription capability】video_audio_ASR_mapper — performs speech recognition from the video's audio track; the documentation describes it as "generating video tags from the audio stream based on Audio Spectrogram Transformer" (this operator's documented description overlaps with tagging-type operators; in practice it takes on both ASR and audio-event annotation duties). It can produce speech-transcription text as an independent field.
【Speaker attributes — a group of capabilities DJ covers relatively well】
  · video_audio_detect_age_gender_mapper — "detects age and gender from the video's audio signal based on a pretrained wav2vec2 model." Produces the speaker's age bracket and gender attributes, which is relatively rare among open-source data-processing frameworks.
  · video_audio_speech_emotion_mapper — speech emotion recognition, producing an emotion-label field.
  · video_active_speaker_detect_mapper — "detects the active speaker in a video by analyzing visual face tracks and the audio signal." This is the key operator that binds the "speaker" from the audio track to a specific person visible in the frame, used in multi-speaker scenarios to determine "who is speaking right now."
  · video_captioning_face_attribute_emotion_mapper — generates a natural-language description of facial attributes and emotion for each tracked person; this is visual-side emotion evidence that can be cross-validated against speech emotion.
  · video_human_tracks_face_demographic_mapper — demographic attributes of person tracks.
  · video_human_tracks_extraction_mapper — extracts face and human-body bounding-box tracks, a common prerequisite for the above person-related operators.
【Attribute-coverage comparison】age ✓, gender ✓, emotion ✓ (both audio and visual channels), speaker identity (track-level ID) ✓, speaker-to-frame binding ✓; language ✗, accent ✗.
【Corroborating application】HumanVBench (CVPR 2026, same team) explicitly built on Data-Juicer, using 20+ SOTA operators to construct a "Human-Centric Video Annotation Pipeline," with a benchmark covering four dimensions — emotion perception, person identification, behavior analysis, and speech-visual alignment. This is a complete real-world case of the above operator chain being applied in building an actual dataset, and indirectly indicates this operator group already has production-level usability.
[Uncertain] The specific choice of the underlying ASR model and its multilingual support range are not disclosed; no transcription quality assessment or confidence-based filtering mechanism is provided.

### [Foley-Omni](../models/Foley-Omni.md) ⚠️

Dialogue transcription is carried in the [WORDS] field, but the implementation path is unconventional — instead of using a standalone ASR, Gemini 2.5 Pro produces the speech-content text directly within multimodal annotation. The benefit is that transcription, the existence determination, and the descriptions of the other two fields are all completed within a single call, making both cost and workflow simpler; the trade-off is that transcription accuracy is inferior to a dedicated ASR (such as Whisper), and the paper does not perform any transcription-quality evaluation.
【Quality safeguard】The [WORDS] field is likewise constrained by the −35 dB energy gate on the Bandit speech stem — if the separated speech track's energy does not meet the threshold, it indicates that although a person's mouth is moving on screen, no actually audible speech exists (or the speech is drowned out), and the field is left empty. This effectively prevents ASR/VLM hallucinated transcription in places where there is no speech.
【Speaker-attribute annotation】[Uncertain] The paper does not describe any structured annotation of speaker identity ID, gender, age, language, accent, or emotion. Within the three-field schema, [WORDS] carries only speech-content text, not a description of speaking style/timbre. This means the model's timbre-control capability relies mainly on visual conditioning (inferring timbre from the speaker's face) rather than textual attribute annotation — consistent with the task setting of VisualTTS, but also limiting explicit controllability over speech style.
【Indirect evidence】Evaluation uses WER as a core metric (V2ST-Bench 7.59, GRID 15.3, versus Ground Truth 8.03), indicating that the [WORDS] field is a verbatim transcription rather than a content summary — otherwise WER could not be computed.

### [Goku](../models/Goku.md)

Not applicable / not addressed. There is no ASR transcription step in the data pipeline, and no annotation of speaker identity, language, accent, or emotion attributes. On the contrary, Goku **actively removes** on-screen text (including subtitles) — clips are discarded if OCR text area exceeds 1%~2% — indicating its data orientation deliberately avoids dialogue and subtitle scenes.

### [Hailuo / MiniMax Video](../models/Hailuo.md) ⚠️

Not applicable + [Uncertain]. There is no audio-generation task, hence no need for ASR transcription or speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation. The official party has not disclosed whether original-video dialogue information is retained and used in the training data (e.g., as a semantic cue to assist captioning); presumed not used.

### [HunyuanVideo-Foley](../models/HunyuanVideo-Foley.md) ⚠️

This work does not involve dialogue transcription or speaker-attribute annotation, and this is an explicit design boundary rather than an oversight:
【No ASR】There is no speech-recognition step anywhere in the pipeline — no Whisper or any ASR model is used, dialogue content is not transcribed, and no speech text is produced.
【The speech-detection stage serves only classification, not transcription】The speech-music detection model at Level 7 of the pipeline performs only the determination of "whether there is someone speaking in this audio segment"; its output is a category label rather than text content, used for category-distribution management.
【No speaker attributes】Speaker identity, gender, age, timbre, accent, emotion, and speaking rate are not annotated; no speaker diarization is performed; no face detection is performed (there is no RetinaFace or any face-related model anywhere in the pipeline).
【No lip-sync detection】SyncNet is not used, LSE-C/LSE-D are not computed — this is the most notable process difference from speaker-video-generation works such as UniVerse-1 and MOVA.
【Task boundary】The model's target output is Foley sound effects and ambient sound; it does not generate dialogue. The ideal behavior is: given a frame with someone speaking, generate the scene's ambient sound and action sound, leaving dialogue to a dedicated TTS/dubbing step. This matches the division of labor in real film post-production among "Foley artists, sound-effects engineers, and voice actors" — this model targets the Foley-artist role specifically, not dubbing.
【Open question about actual behavior】The paper does not evaluate the model's behavior on frames containing people speaking — whether it would generate ambiguous speech-like sounds (babble), or whether it correctly maintains a vocal gap; no data is given on either. This is an important product concern in real-world application (fake generated speech would seriously undermine usability), but neither the paper nor its evaluation benchmarks cover it. [Uncertain]

### [HunyuanVideo](../models/HunyuanVideo.md) ⚠️

Not applicable. No audio-track processing, no ASR transcription, no speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation. Related capability within the Tencent ecosystem resides in separate models such as HunyuanVideo-Avatar (audio-driven digital human), outside the scope of this entry. [Uncertain]

### [InstructAV2AV](../models/InstructAV2AV.md) ⚠️

Dialogue processing is the most complex of the four task types in this work (corresponding to the HF clone_id / clone_voice / clone_id_voice subsets); the processing chain is complete, but attribute-annotation disclosure is insufficient.

【Transcription step】ElevenLabs Scribe (2025) is used for ASR, with the explicit purpose being "extract precise speech timestamps" — note the emphasis is on precise timestamps rather than text content alone. Timestamp precision is a hard requirement for this task: the edited new dialogue line must replace the original speech within its time window, and the new lip motion must be aligned frame-by-frame with the new speech; inaccurate time boundaries would directly break lip-sync.

【Speaker localization and on-screen presence determination】TalkNet (Tao et al., 2021) performs active speaker detection, locating "the person who is currently speaking" in the frame. Combined with the transcription result, a hard admission condition is enforced: a clip is retained only when the speech is temporally aligned with a visible on-screen speaker (clips retain speech only when temporally aligned with a visible on-screen speaker). This simultaneously excludes voice-over, narration, commentary, post-production dubbing, and scenes where the speaker is not on screen — equivalent to enforcing, at the data level, that "every speech sample comes with built-in lip-sync supervision." This is a more thorough approach than after-the-fact filtering using SyncNet scores — the latter filters synchronized samples out of a mixed pool, while the former only admits audio-visual co-sourced material from the source.

【Decoupled annotation of speech synthesis and identity/timbre】The naming of the three HF subsets reveals an implicit attribute-dimension system:
  · clone_id — change the visual identity, keep the original timbre;
  · clone_voice — change the timbre, keep the original visual identity;
  · clone_id_voice — change both visual identity and timbre simultaneously.
  This means that, during data construction, "visual identity" and "auditory timbre" are treated as two orthogonal factors that can be manipulated independently, with controlled samples constructed separately for the three combinations. This is a capability unique to synthetic data: in reality it is impossible to capture genuine footage of "the same video with the face changed but the voice unchanged"; only controlled synthesis can provide this kind of decoupled supervision, letting the model learn to control identity and timbre separately. This is the most persuasive example in this work of "synthetic data dimensional representativeness."
  Speech synthesis is performed by ElevenLabs (a platform known for its voice-cloning capability); presumably clone_voice-type samples are generated using its voice-cloning function, and identity-preserving speech-modification samples are generated using its capability to alter content while preserving the original timbre — but the paper does not describe the specific configuration.

[Uncertain] A large amount of attribute-annotation information is missing: no structured annotation fields are seen for speaker identity ID, gender, age, language, accent, or emotion; it is not stated whether the ASR transcription text is retained as a data field (from the <S>/<E> markers in the instructions, the target dialogue text does enter the instruction, but whether the source dialogue transcription is retained is unknown); the ElevenLabs invocation configuration is not described (voice-cloning parameters, speech-rate control, emotion setting); no quantitative evaluation of transcription quality or synthesized-speech quality is performed. On the evaluation side, Sync-C / Sync-D are used to measure lip-sync, but WER is not used to measure speech-content accuracy — meaning the most direct fidelity metric for speech editing, "does the model actually say the line the instruction requires," is missing from the evaluation.

### [2026 Other Audio-Video Joint Generation Works](../models/JAVG_2026_misc.md) ⚠️

【OmniCustom — the most complete (dialogue transcription + seven speaker attributes)】
(1) Transcription: "we used GLM-ASR to generate transcriptions for each 5s training audio clip" — GLM-ASR is used to generate transcriptions for each 5-second training audio clip. GLM-ASR is a Zhipu-family speech-recognition model with relatively strong Chinese-language capability.
(2) Speaker attributes (following the Ovi scheme): age, gender, accent + four vocal characteristics (pitch, prosody, emotion, speaking rate), 7 items in total.
(3) Implicit timbre annotation: its "same-timbre-different-content" pairing construction (a 4-second reference audio at the front + a 5-second training audio at the back, both from the same video, same speaker) essentially provides a form of timbre supervision without explicit voiceprint annotation — timbre stays constant within the same pair while content differs, naturally forming a contrastive-learning signal for timbre decoupling. This is much cheaper than explicitly annotating voiceprint IDs, and is a clever data-construction trick.
(4) The evaluation set controls the gender ratio at 1:1 and includes 30 real people outside the training set to verify zero-shot timbre transfer.
【ALIVE — speaker-attribution correction is its unique contribution】
(1) Transcription annotation: the <W> tag wraps verbatim speech content, indicating precise ASR transcription is present; the <I> tag marks non-speech acoustic events.
(2) Acoustic profile: within the Subjects field, each subject carries an acoustic profile, i.e., the speaker's timbre attributes are bound to the visual appearance.
(3) SubjectID correction five-step pipeline (targeting the "who is speaking" mis-attribution error in multi-person scenes; the only work in this batch to systematically address this problem):
  ① TS-TalkNet performs target-speaker active-speaker detection, generating a face-voice correspondence matrix
  ② Qwen3-omni parses narration timestamps
  ③ majority voting establishes a "sentence → TrackID (face-track ID)" mapping
  ④ CLIP + ArcFace embeddings perform visual-semantic matching
  ⑤ Text is rewritten with the corrected labels (text rewriting with corrected labels)
  Videos that do not pass the high-confidence threshold are filtered out directly, yielding a "simplified" but high-quality initial dataset. This pipeline solves a problem that a pure-video model would never encounter at all: attaching dialogue lines to the wrong face in a multi-person frame would cause the model to learn an incorrect "face-voice" binding, directly breaking lip-sync and timbre consistency in multi-person dialogue scenes. The threshold value is not disclosed [Uncertain].
【StreamChar】Uses ASR timestamps as supervision: "ground-truth end indices derived from ASR timestamps" is used for the smooth L1 loss of the progress-aware pointer — i.e., the progress information of "how many characters into the line has been spoken" is used as an explicit supervision signal, forming the data basis for transcript fidelity. The ASR tool used is not named [Uncertain]; speaker-attribute annotation is not described [Uncertain].
【NAVA】Timbre-in-Context Conditioning encodes a "reference utterance" through a timbre encoder (implemented as a ReDimNet speaker embedder), associating the reference timbre cue with the corresponding speech spans — i.e., timbre is not a global condition but is bound per speech segment, supporting distinct timbres for multiple speakers. However, the paper does not state the annotation protocol (how speech-span boundaries are determined, whether diarization is used) [Uncertain]; the ASR method is not detailed [Uncertain]. Its five-class YAMNet labels distinguish single-speaker / multi-speaker speech, which can be considered a coarse-grained speaker-count annotation.
【CCL】Reports the WER metric, indicating it evaluated speech intelligibility, implying a transcription-vs-generation comparison exists, but the data-side transcription process is not described [Uncertain].
【Baton】Not described [Uncertain].

### [Audio-Video Joint Generation Baselines Collection](../models/JavisDiT_baselines.md) ⚠️

【MM-Diffusion / AV-DiT】Training data contains no human speech at all (only natural ambient sound and instrumental music); no transcription, no speaker-attribute annotation — this dimension does not apply.
【JavisDiT / JavisDiT++】The reverse operation — not only does it not transcribe, it actively uses FunASR to detect and remove videos containing human speech, purging speech from the audio-video training data. This is a deliberate capability trade-off: giving up lip-sync and dialogue generation in exchange for focus on ambient sound/sound-effect event alignment. FunASR here plays the role of a "speech detector" rather than a "transcriber." Although the audio pretraining stage does include speech-type data, no transcription annotation is performed.
【Harmony (has complete transcription)】Gemini automated annotation explicitly produces ASR transcription text (transcripts), which is fed into the model as a speech-conditioning channel; the speech data comes from Emilia (a TTS corpus that already carries its own transcription), OpenHumanVid, and SpeakerVid (two-person interaction data, which naturally contains multi-speaker scenes). Regarding speaker attributes, Harmony has a mechanism independent of transcription — Stage 2 "Timbre Disentanglement Finetuning," which uses cross-utterance mismatched reference/target pairs of the same speaker (for ambient sound, non-overlapping segments of the same clip are used instead), training the model to extract timbre features from 1–3 seconds of reference audio without leaking content. This actually decouples timbre through "the data-pairing method" rather than "attribute annotation," which is lighter-weight than explicitly annotating age/gender/accent. However, explicit attribute labels such as age, gender, accent, and emotion are not seen [Uncertain], and speaker diarization and speaker ID are also not mentioned [Uncertain].
【UniAVGen】On the evaluation side, Whisper is used to compute WER, indicating transcription exists in the evaluation stage; whether ASR transcription annotation is performed on the training-data side is not stated [Uncertain]. The model explicitly optimizes two metrics — "timbre consistency" and "emotion consistency" — implying that conditioning channels for timbre and emotion exist during training (very likely implemented via reference audio rather than textual attribute labels), but the specific annotation method is not disclosed [Uncertain].

### [Kling 3.0 / Kling Video 3.0 Omni](../models/Kling_30_Omni.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] The specific scheme for ASR transcription and speaker-attribute annotation is not disclosed. Inferring backward from capability: the model supports lip-sync for 5 languages + accents, co-reference of multiple characters (3+) with distinct timbres, and "uploading reference material to bind a specific subject's visual features and timbre to it" — this requires the training data to have sentence-level dialogue transcription, timestamp alignment, speaker diarization and speaker identity/timbre ID, language and accent labels, emotion labels, and other structured annotations. KlingAvatar 2.0 explicitly constructs multi-speaker dialogue data from podcasts, interviews, and multi-character TV dramas, and builds an automated annotation pipeline for multi-character video (YOLO person detection + DWPose keypoints + SAM2 segmentation and temporal tracking, cross-checked against frame-level detection results), used to bind "who is speaking" to the audio track — this is public evidence of speaker-to-frame correspondence annotation. [Uncertain: the ASR model used, the emotion-label taxonomy]

### [LTX-2](../models/LTX-2.md)

Explicitly implemented, and an explicit component of the caption schema: the caption includes "precise transcriptions of dialogue," annotated together with three attributes — speaker identity (speaker), language, and accent. These three attribute annotations directly support the model's multilingual lip-sync, speaker-differentiation, and accent-controllability capabilities (the paper states the model can synthesize speech "not only synchronized with lip movement, but also natural in rhythm, accent, and emotional tone").
A noteworthy contrast: emotion is not used as an annotation field — the annotation guidelines explicitly require "no emotional interpretation," only objective description of what is seen and heard; emotional expression is expected to be learned indirectly through dialogue content, physiological action cues, and timbre description. The official prompting guide advises users to break dialogue lines into short sentences, insert acting directions between sentences, and use physiological cues rather than emotion labels.
Not disclosed: the specific ASR model used, the speaker diarization/clustering method, the language and accent recognition model, transcription word-error rate, or quality-control measures. In multi-speaker scenes the model still exhibits the problem of dialogue lines being mis-attributed to the wrong character (a limitation stated in the paper), implying that speaker-to-dialogue-line binding annotation or modeling is still not strong enough.

### [LongCat-Video](../models/LongCat-Video.md) ⚠️

Not applicable for the base version.
Avatar 1.5 involves speaker processing, but does not adopt an ASR-transcription route — the report does not specify any transcription model, nor does it perform verbatim text annotation. Its speaker-related processing focuses on "who is speaking" rather than "what was said": it uses two active-speaker-detection models, TalkNet and UniTalk; in multi-person scenes, YOLOv6 detection + ByteTrack are used for person tracking to associate identity, and time intervals with concurrent speaker activity are explicitly excluded (excluding intervals with concurrent speaker activity), avoiding audio-visual attribution ambiguity. On speaker-attribute annotation, an emotion dimension is done: 6 emotion classes are defined, filtered using EmotiEffLib with confidence s>0.7, and samples containing synthetic content, more than two subjects, identity switches, or an overly small subject-to-frame area ratio are uniformly labeled null. Language/accent attributes are not annotated. [Uncertain: whether there is an undisclosed ASR step]

### [MOVA](../models/MOVA.md)

Dialogue transcription and speaker-attribute annotation are the core of MOVA's data system:
【Transcription】Qwen3-Omni-Instruct performs ASR, outputting verbatim transcription. Three laws constrain it: LAW OF LANGUAGE FIDELITY (preserve the original language, translation strictly forbidden); LAW OF SPEECH DYNAMICS (create a new event entry whenever a new speaker / language / intonation begins); LAW OF SILENCE (when there is no speech, speech_description outputs null). Unclear segments are marked [inaudible].
【Speaker attributes】Covered in natural-language form through Qwen3-Omni-Captioner's audio descriptions: number of speakers (e.g., "two distinct voices" / "a group of overlapping speakers"), speaker-switch points, timbre characteristics (e.g., high-pitched / gruff / mature male), accent (in the example, "a General American accent"), tone and speech rate (calm/authoritative/professional; measured/declarative; rhythmic evenly paced cadence), and recording environment (studio-clean, slight reverb). These attributes are not enumerated fields but descriptive text, bound to a specific person on screen during the fusion stage.
【Speaker-identity binding】The fusion prompt requires that speech be embedded in quoted form and anchored to a visual subject (e.g., "the teenager in the corner," "the gray-haired woman"), achieving a three-way binding of "visual identity — timbre — dialogue content."
【Corresponding facility on the evaluation side】MOSS Transcribe Diarize (a transcription + speaker-diarization model released by the same team in 2026, arXiv:2601.01554) is used to perform speaker diarization (explicit speaker labels [S01], [S02]) and ASR on the generated results, and to compute cpCER (concatenated minimum permutation CER), to evaluate whether speaker identity and dialogue content are correctly reflected. MOVA-720p achieves the best cpCER of 0.149.
【Known deficiency】The paper's Limitations section explicitly states that diarization errors and imperfect active-speaker labels propagate into the training data, causing lip-audio mismatch and temporal drift in multi-speaker scenes; directions for improvement include stronger active-speaker detection, cross-modal speaker tracking, and better noisy-clip filtering.

### [Merged entry: ① Mochi 1](../models/Mochi_MAGI_Motif.md)

Not applicable. None of the three has an audio-processing pipeline; no ASR transcription, no speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation. Note that Motif-Video 2B's caption schema does have "verbatim text transcription" and "on-screen text" fields, and MAGI-1 also has a dedicated subtitle-detection logic, but these both refer to visual text on screen (OCR), unrelated to speech transcription; furthermore, in Motif's pipeline, the consequence of detecting a persistent text region is that the clip is cropped or the whole segment removed, rather than retained as annotation.

### [Movie Gen](../models/Movie_Gen.md)

None. Movie Gen Audio does not perform ASR transcription and does not annotate speaker identity, language, accent, or emotion — because the model deliberately does not generate speech: diegetic speech is difficult to generate without a dialogue script and when the generated video has artifacts; non-diegetic speech (narration) is considered fillable by TTS given a script. All speech processing is limited to: using an AED model (the speech/singing subclass of the AudioSet ontology) to determine whether a sample contains human voice or singing, whose output, after binarization with a preset posterior threshold, is written into the caption as a control label; and using voice as one of three major audio types participating in the CAVTP threshold determination during data bucketing. The cinematic split used for fine-tuning directly excludes clips containing vocals altogether. The paper explicitly acknowledges in its limitations that the model "currently does not support human voice generation due to a design choice."

### [NVIDIA NeMo Curator](../models/NeMo_Curator.md) ⚠️

The video pipeline has no dialogue transcription (does not process the audio track).
The audio pipeline has partial capability but is disconnected from the video: (1) ASR transcription — uses the NVIDIA NeMo ASR model family to generate transcription text; (2) transcription-quality assessment — uses WER (word error rate) and CER (character error rate) as quality metrics and filters accordingly (WER-filtering), the clearest quality-control measure this framework has on the speech-data side; (3) speaker diarization — from version 26.02, supports streaming Sortformer for speaker diarization and VAD (voice activity detection), with a long-form audio cutting tutorial; (4) 26.07 adds a new audio-augmentation stage: tagging, the SQUIM objective-quality metric, bandwidth estimation, punctuation preparation, and optional secondary ASR scoring.
【Missing】Speaker-identity attribute annotation, language identification, accent annotation, and emotion annotation have no built-in stage; the default thresholds for WER/CER are not given in the documentation. These capabilities are necessary for building multilingual lip-sync data and would need to be built in-house. [Uncertain]

### [OmniHuman dataset + OHBench](../models/OmniHuman.md) ⚠️

Dialogue and speaker attributes form the most heavily annotated part of this dataset, and also the core increment relative to predecessors like OpenHumanVid:
【Transcription】FunASR-Nano performs ASR, producing speech text along with speaker ID and timestamps. The transcription result serves two purposes: as annotation content itself, and as ground truth for verifying MLLM captions (via edit-distance verification).
【Speaker diarization】3D-Speaker performs diarization, identifying M active speech intervals and each one's speaker index. This is a prerequisite for two-person scene annotation.
【Speaker-to-frame attribution (the key step of this work)】SyncNet is used to resolve audio-visual attribution, assigning each audio segment to the visual ID track with the highest response via greedy matching; ArcFace face embeddings are then used to complete identity confirmation (embedding similarity > 0.55 is judged as the same person, with a 5-frame tolerance for tracking loss). This step binds "speaker m in the audio" to "person i on screen," a necessary condition for usable two-person dialogue data — without it, the model cannot know whose mouth should move. Table 1 shows that, previously, the only comparable dataset with speaker annotation was SpeakerVid-5M.
【Speaker-attribute annotation】
- Emotion: annotated per speaker and per speech segment, appearing both at the individual level (speech transcription accompanied by emotion) and at the interaction level (person-to-person interaction labels carry speaker ID and emotion);
- Language: the speech field of sample_json carries a language label;
- Timing: start/end timestamps of speech segments;
- Speech quality: a separately listed speech-quality annotation within individual-level annotation;
- Lip-sync quality: lip-sync quality assessment within frame-level annotation.
【Not annotated】No annotation of speaker gender, age, or ethnicity is seen; no text description of timbre/voiceprint is seen (unlike MOVA's detailed timbre descriptions); accent is not annotated; speech rate and volume are not seen annotated per speaker (music has relative volume, speech does not). [Uncertain]
【Multi-speaker support】Fully supported, and one of the dataset's design priorities: 128 two-person evaluation videos, two-person interaction labels, a two-person ID-consistency evaluation metric (IC*, using ArcFace cosine similarity), and a listener-realism metric (LR, evaluating the accuracy of audio-visual attribution and the naturalness of the listener's expression). This "listener" dimension is especially rare — most speaker datasets only care about the person who is speaking, while OmniHuman explicitly evaluates whether the non-speaking person behaves naturally, which directly addresses the most common failure mode in two-person dialogue generation (both people's mouths moving at once).
【Evaluation-side metrics】SenseVoice computes WER for pronunciation accuracy; DNSMOS's OVRL score is used for speech authenticity; SyncNet is used for lip-sync.

### [Open-Sora series](../models/Open-Sora.md)

Not applicable. The data pipeline does not process the audio track at all; no ASR transcription, no speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation.

### [Ovi](../models/Ovi.md) ⚠️

【Transcription】An audio-understanding MLLM produces dialogue text directly from the full audio track, written into <S>...<E> tags and interleaved chronologically within the visual description; pure-audio data likewise produces transcription, and the field is left empty when there is no speech. No mention of using a standalone ASR (Whisper, etc.) or of performing transcription-quality verification/WER filtering [Uncertain].
【Speaker attributes】For clips containing speech, the audio description is explicitly required to emphasize speaker-related acoustic attributes: age, gender, accent, pitch, prosody, emotion, speaking rate. This attribute set allows the model, at inference time, to directly control timbre and emotion via prompt; Ovi 1.1 further strengthens this into "prompt-based emotion instruction tags."
【Speaker identity/diarization】No mention of speaker diarization, speaker-ID annotation, or multi-speaker separation annotation; no mention of speaker embeddings or reference-timbre conditioning either (the README's Todo list still shows "Reference voice condition" as unimplemented), so in multi-speaker dialogue scenes, explicit supervision for "which line belongs to which person" is lacking. [Uncertain]
【Language annotation】Accent is annotated, but whether language is separately annotated is not stated. [Uncertain]

### [Script-a-Video](../models/Script-a-Video.md) ⚠️

Dialogue transcription has an explicit structured carrier in MTSS, but speaker-attribute annotation is relatively thin:
【Transcription】Within the Event stream, events of type dialogue have a "line" field recording verbatim dialogue text. The paper does not state whether these lines were produced by ASR transcription or transcribed directly by Gemini-2.5-Pro listening to the audio — given that the entire annotation set is produced by Gemini-2.5-Pro in one pass, the latter seems more likely, i.e., no standalone Whisper-type ASR step. [Uncertain]
【Speaker identity】Implemented through the "speaker" field, whose value is an entity ID from the Reference stream (e.g., PERSON_1). This is a relational binding rather than an attribute description — the speaker identity points directly to an entry in the entity library carrying complete appearance anchors (clothing/accessories/hairstyle), so "voice" and "appearance" are hard-bound at the data-structure level. This design is especially important for training audio-video joint generation models to identify "who is speaking."
【Speaker attributes】No independent structured field is set. Gender, age, timbre, language, accent, and speech rate all have no corresponding field. The closest thing is the Event's "description" field, which the paper describes as used to capture "nuanced semantics like emotional shifts or vocal techniques" — i.e., emotion and vocal manner are recorded in free text, not as enumerated labels, and thus not directly usable for ratio statistics or conditional control.
【Multi-speaker handling】Naturally supported through a concurrent-event splitting mechanism: multiple simultaneous audio sources are split into parallel event entries, each with its own speaker, time_range, and line, so multi-person dialogue scenes can be fully represented without being mixed together. This is a clear advantage over approaches that "describe an entire soundscape in one passage."
【Verification of transcription accuracy on the generation side】WER is computed by transcribing the generated audio with Whisper-large-v3 and comparing against ground-truth text using jiwer (CJK text segmented with jieba). Results: LTX-2-AV baseline single-shot 1.64 / multi-shot 0.84 → LTX-2-AV-MTSS single-shot 0.78 / multi-shot 0.13 → Ours(Full) multi-shot 0.19. The paper states that the explicit audio-event descriptions provided by the Event stream turn speech and sound-effect generation from "near-random" into "semantically accurate."
【Audio-quality metric】Audio Quality is evaluated with UTMOS (a lightweight speech-quality MOS predictor): baseline 4.12-4.18, MTSS version 4.60, full method 4.68.

### [Seedance 2.0 and Seedance 1.5 pro](../models/Seedance_20_Seedance_15_pro.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] The report does not explicitly state the ASR transcription and speaker-attribute annotation process. Indirect evidence strongly supports its existence: Seedance 1.5 pro has precise multilingual and dialect lip-sync, and can capture the distinctive speech prosody and emotional tension of each language; SeedVideoBench 1.5's "vocal attribute" label system explicitly includes three categories — timbre, accent, emotional tone; SeedVideoBench 2.0 covers 17 categories including Chinese dialects/accents, multi-person dialogue, minority languages, and voice-over. These capabilities must be built on a data foundation of dialogue transcription + speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation, but the paper does not describe the specific implementation (whether an in-house ASR was used, whether speaker diarization was used, etc.).

### [SkyReels series](../models/SkyReels.md) ⚠️

【Transcription】SkyReels-V4 uses Whisper to transcribe content for "speech" and "singing" among its four audio categories, and the transcribed text is filled into the <dialogue> and <singing> tokens of the structured caption; sound-effect and music categories are not transcribed, and instead receive a descriptive caption generated by Qwen3-Omni.
【Speaker attributes】The paper does not mention speaker diarization, speaker-identity annotation, emotion annotation, accent annotation, or timbre description fields — a clear gap compared with LTX-2 (which explicitly annotates speaker/language/accent). How dialogue-line-to-character binding is handled in multi-speaker scenes is not stated.
【Language】Multilingual capability is achieved mainly by supplementing with synthetic TTS data (Chinese/English/Japanese/Korean/German/French, etc.), rather than by annotating and balancing language on real data. Whisper is capable of language identification, but the paper does not state whether language is stored as an explicit field. [Uncertain]

### [Sora 2](../models/Sora_2.md) ⚠️

Not disclosed on the training-data side. Indirect evidence: the safety stack explicitly feeds "audio transcripts" as an independent channel into the safety classifier, indicating that OpenAI possesses and routinely uses ASR-transcription capability for video audio tracks; using this capability for training-data annotation would be a natural extension. There is no disclosure whatsoever regarding speaker identity/language/accent/emotion attribute annotation. The product-side cameo feature requires users to record a one-time video+audio sample to complete identity verification and bind their likeness and voice, indicating a "speaker identity–timbre–likeness" binding representation exists, but this belongs to a personalization/conditioning-injection mechanism rather than a training-data annotation schema. [Uncertain]

### [SpeakerVid-5M](../models/SpeakerVid-5M.md) ⚠️

This is SpeakerVid-5M's strongest dimension, and also its core asset distinguishing it from other video datasets:
【Transcription】Whisper performs ASR, outputting transcription text per clip, retaining three diagnostic quantities used for filtering: transcription confidence (< −1.5 removed, the HQ subset requires > −1), no-speech probability (> 0.8 removed), compression ratio (> 2.5 removed, used to identify repetitive/degenerate or hallucinated output). The pipeline also detects "language mismatch" and filters accordingly.
【Speaker diarization and identity annotation】Three mechanisms are chained together, forming this dataset's technical backbone:
  1) 3D-Speaker performs voiceprint diarization, selecting the two main speakers by speaking duration/frequency (together accounting for over 80% of total speaking time), discarding all other speakers — this "only the two leads" decision directly defines the dataset's dyadic property.
  2) SyncNet confidence performs audio-visual binding, associating the audio-side speaker ID with the visual bounding box detected by YOLO, answering "which person in the frame does this voice belong to."
  3) ArcFace performs cross-clip identity correction, computing face cosine similarity to verify consistency; outlier samples are reassigned to a different ID if they are more similar to another ID.
  The final result is 83K unique speaker IDs in the single-person branch and 16K in the dialogue branch, and IDs are traceable across clips over the whole dataset.
【Speaking/listening state determination (unique to this dataset)】Two sets of rules:
  - Co-present scenes: if only one person is speaking, and the SyncNet-score difference between the two people exceeds a preset threshold, the one with the lower score is judged to be "listening."
  - Non-co-present scenes: if the ASR result is valid, transcription confidence is above threshold, but that person's video has a SyncNet score below a preset threshold, they are judged to be a listener.
  This set of rules directly generates the listening branch, a rare design that explicitly models "who is listening" as a data label.
【Multi-turn dialogue context】The multi-turn branch aggregates prior turns' ASR transcriptions within a time window [x−T, x], forming multi-turn dialogue-context annotation; adjacent clips with a time gap smaller than δt are judged to be a continuous conversation that can be concatenated.
【Unannotated attributes】Language labels, accent, emotion category, speech rate, timbre description — attributes that MOVA naturalizes in its audio captions — have no corresponding field in SpeakerVid-5M (emotion information is reflected only indirectly through the visual-side facial expression summary). [Uncertain]

### [Step-Video-T2V](../models/Step-Video-T2V.md) ⚠️

Not applicable. The data pipeline does not process the audio track; no ASR transcription, no speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation. Within the StepFun ecosystem, such capability resides in the separate Step-Audio series of speech models (which support speech generation with attributes such as emotion, dialect, language, and singing voice), but has no overlap whatsoever with Step-Video-T2V's training data. [Uncertain]

### [UniTalking](../models/UniTalking.md) ⚠️

【Transcription】Whisper-V3 is used for ASR, producing the text content of speech. The transcription result serves as a component of the audio caption participating in text-conditioning construction. The paper does not state: whether the original language is preserved (or uniformly translated to English), whether punctuation and numeral normalization is done, whether low-confidence transcription samples are filtered, whether transcription-failure clips are handled, or whether timestamps (word-level timestamp) are retained. [Uncertain]
【Speaker-attribute annotation: explicit annotation is entirely absent】No annotation of speaker identity, gender, age, ethnicity, emotion, speech rate, or volume; no speaker diarization; no speaker clustering or identity de-duplication. Even the total number of unique speakers in the dataset is not reported.
【How timbre is handled: not annotated, converted into an audio condition instead】This is the key divergence between UniTalking and the MOVA route. MOVA uses natural language to describe timbre, accent, tone, and recording environment in detail, making speaker attributes explicit as text; UniTalking instead does no text description at all, and injects timbre as an independent audio-conditioning channel (c_audio) directly — a frozen MMAudio VAE encodes 3–5 seconds of reference audio (fixed latent length of 257), passed through a dedicated KV-projection layer for cross-attention. This is "representing audio with audio" rather than "describing audio with text," sidestepping the inherent difficulty of precisely describing timbre in language.
【Data cost paid for this】Because the source data itself does not contain a "reference audio" pairing signal, the team had to construct it manually (see synthetic_data_synthesis) — the most costly and highest-risk step in the entire pipeline.
【Measured level of timbre capability】TR2AV timbre similarity: English 0.703, Chinese 0.662. This is mid-to-lower tier compared with professional TTS — lower than Qwen3-Omni (0.773/0.772) and MiniMax (0.756/0.780), and roughly on par with ElevenLabs (0.613/0.677). The paper candidly admits it is "hard to surpass the performance of SOTA large audio models," claiming only to have achieved "a certain degree of voice-cloning capability."
【Multi-speaker】Not supported. Future Work explicitly acknowledges that the framework does not yet support multi-person reference generation (similar to Sora2's Cameo feature). The training data also shows no construction of multi-speaker dialogue samples.
【Evaluation metrics】WER (Seed-TTS test-en, 0.038) measures textual fidelity, Speaker Similarity measures voice cloning, Sync-C/Sync-D measure lip-sync. No cpCER-type multi-speaker metric.

### [UniVerse-1](../models/UniVerse-1.md) ⚠️

【Transcription】Whisper is used for ASR, producing verbatim speech content; QWen2.5-Omni then outputs "verified speech content," i.e., the multimodal model performs a layer of verification/normalization on the ASR result. The paper does not state the specific verification rules, whether unrecognizable segments are handled, or whether the original language is preserved without translation. [Uncertain]
【Speaker-attribute annotation】Essentially absent. The paper does not annotate speaker identity, gender, age, timbre, accent, emotion, speech rate, or any other speaker attribute, and does not perform speaker diarization.
【An architecture-level decision about speaker modeling】More critically, UniVerse-1 actively removes the speaker encoder originally present in Ace-step, with the explicit purpose of "generalizing the model beyond speaker-specific generation." This means timbre is not controlled by an explicit speaker embedding, but is instead implicitly determined by the reference image and text conditions — the model card describes its capability as "generating speech with a timbre matching the character's appearance" (character-matched voice timbre).
