This is a technical route entirely different from MOVA (which describes timbre, accent, tone, and recording environment in detail via natural language, and binds it to an on-screen person during fusion): UniVerse-1 relies on architectural simplification + implicit visual-condition modeling, while MOVA relies on explicit annotation.
【Multi-speaker scenario】The paper does not discuss multi-speaker handling, speaker switching, or dialogue-turn processing; the training data's roughly 5-second single-shot window also largely does not carry multi-turn dialogue.
【Evaluation】WER (word error rate; UniVerse-1's is 0.18) is used to measure the intelligibility and textual fidelity of generated speech, and LSE-C (1.34) is used to measure lip-sync — but there is no MOVA-style cpCER (speaker-differentiated concatenated minimum permutation character error rate), since speaker-identity evaluation is not involved.

### [Unison](../models/Unison.md) ⚠️

【A transcription channel does exist】The model input explicitly includes transcription text (speech transcription text) as an independent conditioning channel alongside caption text, and its average-pooled semantic vector participates in the SCG gating computation. Therefore every training sample containing speech must be paired with transcription text.
【Source of the transcription not disclosed】The paper does not state where the training data's transcription comes from — whether it inherits transcriptions already attached to upstream datasets, or is generated offline with an ASR such as Whisper. Whisper-large-v3 appears in the paper only in the evaluation stage (computing WER of generated speech); it is not stated to be used for training-data transcription. [Uncertain]
【Speaker-attribute annotation: entirely absent】None of speaker identity, gender, age, timbre, accent, emotion, or speech rate is annotated; no speaker diarization is performed; no description of multi-speaker scene handling is given. This is a significant gap between Unison and MOVA (which describes timbre/accent/tone/recording environment in detail via natural language).
【Multi-speaker awareness implied by face-count detection】The lip-filtering operator explicitly "detects the number and location of faces," indicating the pipeline is aware of how many faces are in the frame. This leaves an interface for multi-speaker handling, but the paper does not state the strategy when multiple faces are detected — whether to discard, select a main speaker, or verify and retain each one individually. This is a valuable but unelaborated design detail. [Uncertain]
【Timbre-control mechanism not stated】How the model decides the timbre of the generated speech (whether implicitly determined by the reference image, whether a speaker embedding is used, whether it is random) is entirely unstated. By contrast, UniVerse-1 explicitly removes the speaker encoder to pursue generalization, and MOVA explicitly specifies timbre via text description; Unison is silent on this dimension. [Uncertain]
【Evaluation】WER is one of Unison's strongest points: 0.22 under the TI2AV setting (best in class, better than LTX-2's 0.25 and MOVA's 0.29), and 0.09 under the T2AV setting (also best, better than LTX-2's 0.11). This advantage can be directly attributed to its decoupled dual-stream speech/sound-effect design — the speech stream is not interfered with by sound effects, naturally improving transcription intelligibility. However, since there is no cpCER-type metric, performance in multi-speaker scenes cannot be assessed.

### [Veo 3 / Veo 3.1](../models/Veo_3_Veo_31.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] No ASR transcription process or speaker-attribute annotation system is disclosed at all. Inferred clue: the model can precisely generate corresponding dialogue matching the quoted text in the prompt while maintaining lip-sync, indicating the training data must contain verbatim dialogue transcription (very likely completed via Gemini's audio-understanding capability or Google's internal ASR), with the transcription aligned to the video timeline. However, the official party explicitly states, when assessing deepfake risk, that the model "has poor controllability with respect to voice," and does not offer fine-grained control over accent or timbre — strongly suggesting that attributes such as speaker identity, timbre, accent, and emotion are not systematically annotated as independent conditioning fields.

### [Vidu S1](../models/Vidu_S1.md) ⚠️

Has a relatively complete speaker-attribute annotation chain: speech components are extracted from the raw audio → VAD (voice activity detection) + ASD (active speaker detection) annotate the timestamps of each speech segment and its associated speaker → based on the correspondence between the speaker and the on-screen subject, each segment is labeled as one of three categories: onscreen / offscreen / overlap. The caption includes a dialogue field. However, the paper does not clearly state whether complete ASR text transcription is performed, nor does it annotate the speaker's language, accent, age, or other attributes [Partially uncertain]. The evaluation benchmark Vidu-StreamBench is mentioned as covering diverse speaker attributes and emotions.

### [Wan 2.5 / 2.6 / 2.7](../models/Wan.md) ⚠️

There is a marked capability-disclosure gap here, one of the biggest information gaps in this entry.
【Wan 2.1】Explicitly excludes speech: the V2A training set "systematically remove[s] videos … containing speech/vocal music," so no ASR transcription or speaker-attribute annotation step of any kind exists; the report explicitly acknowledges that this results in the model being unable to generate laughter, crying, or speaking sounds.
【Wan2.2-S2V】Processes speaker video, but the paper's data chapter only performs "active speaker detection" (Light-ASD) and face-visibility filtering; it does not mention dialogue ASR transcription or speaker identity/language/accent/emotion annotation; its dense-caption spec (camera angle/appearance-action/background environment) also contains no auditory fields.
【Wan 2.5/2.6/2.7】In terms of capability, these already support precise lip-sync, Chinese/English dialogue recitation and singing, audio-driven lip movement and performance, with 2.6 further supporting "role-play" (uploading a personal video to replicate one's likeness and voice) — the latter essentially requires speaker modeling at the timbre/voiceprint level. On the data side, these capabilities necessarily correspond to a complete pipeline of ASR transcription + speaker diarization + voiceprint/timbre annotation, but Alibaba has never disclosed any related information: no ASR model selection, no transcription-quality metrics, no speaker-attribute field definitions, no language/accent annotation.
Contrasted with LTX-2, which explicitly writes "precise transcription + speaker + language + accent" into its caption schema, the Wan series is a complete black box on this dimension. [Uncertain]

### [Audio-Video Generation Evaluation Benchmark Collection](../models/av_benchmarks.md) ⚠️

【AVBench has the most complete coverage】Among its 10 dimensions, three directly target dialogue: Speech Content Accuracy (implying an ASR-transcription comparison), Speech Realism, and Lip Sync Consistency; hard-negative construction covers speaker identity mismatches and emotional polarity reversals — indicating its data annotation includes speaker-identity and emotion labels. The Hard subset annotates speaker count (3–4 people) and speech-overlap attributes.
【PhyAVBench】Its speech task uses Whisper-Large V3 to compute WER as a content-accuracy metric.
【VABench】Its lip-sync metric borrows the LatentSync method to compute alignment confidence, applied only to the "vocal-linguistic" subset in which a talking head is detected.
【AV-SyncBench】For vocal scenes, OpenVoice V2 is used for timbre substitution to construct semantic negative samples, implying controllable-editing capability for speaker timbre/identity attributes; scene labels distinguish four categories: single-person speech, dialogue, group vocalization, and singing.
【Omni-Judge】No separate dialogue dimension is listed [Uncertain].
None of the five discloses accent or language label annotation. [Uncertain]

### [Video Captioning Model Ecosystem](../models/caption_models.md)

【Three routes for ASR model choice】
· Plugging in a dedicated ASR (most common): Whisper-large-v3 (OpenAI, ~1.55B) is the de facto standard, widely used by SkyReels-V4, UniTalking, Unison (evaluation side), Apollo/Klear, UniVerse-1, and CineDance (evaluating WER/CER); Alibaba's SenseVoice (~234M, Chinese/English/Cantonese/Japanese/Korean + emotion/event recognition) is used alongside Whisper and Qwen2.5-Omni by Apollo/Klear; ElevenLabs Scribe is used by InstructAV2AV for precise speech timestamps.
· Native transcription from Omni models (2026 trend): Ovi explicitly does not use a standalone ASR — dialogue transcription is produced directly by the annotating MLLM from the audio track; UniVerse-1 uses Qwen2.5-Omni to simultaneously output verified speech content; CineDance uses Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B to jointly handle three subtasks — sentence-level ASR, shot-level audio prompting, and character-timbre description — the rationale being that it far outperforms dedicated diarization tools on the speaker-attribution task (Pyannote-3.1 only 62.7%, DiariZen 63.1%).
· Forcing transcription capability out via RL (AVoCaDO's original contribution): rather than using a standalone ASR module, the GRPO stage uses a dialogue reward ℛ_D to force the model to learn to transcribe — dialogue is extracted as (speaker, spoken content) structured pairs, with ℛ_D being the F1 of speaker-recognition accuracy and content similarity, where content similarity uses edit distance + dynamic programming to find the optimal aligned subsequence, with a threshold of 0.6. This is currently the most practically useful publicly known scheme for optimizing dialogue accuracy.

【The completeness spectrum of speaker-attribute annotation】
· Most complete: LTX-2 annotates three attributes — speaker / language / accent — the data foundation for its multilingual lip-sync capability, and also the only scheme in the whole ecosystem that explicitly annotates accent.
· Medium: CineDance's character-timbre description + binding of ASR sentences to character anchor tokens (the windowed scheme raises Qwen3-Omni from 67.2% to 95.4%, versus the Gemini family's 82.8%–87.4%); Apollo/Klear extracts attributes such as gender and age via its audio-model system; InstructAV2AV uses TalkNet for active speaker detection, annotating who is speaking and their spatiotemporal position.
· Simplest: AVoCaDO does only (speaker, content) pairs, with no language/accent field.

【Methodological comparison of speaker diarization (CineDance Tab.5, the only quantitative comparison in this ecosystem)】On a 100-clip benchmark, Qwen3-Omni-30B + sliding-window prompt reaches 83.1%, while whole-film input reaches only 56.4% — indicating that the windowed prompt design has a far greater impact on speaker diarization than the choice of model itself.

【Downstream impact of speech quality】AVSCapBench shows that Speech is the strongest dimension for all AV captioners (Gemini-3 family 79.8, AVoCaDO 70.42, AVSCap 69.45), but bare Qwen2.5-Omni scores only 13.92 — speech-transcription capability must be obtained through dedicated training.

### [Geometric/Structured Annotation Dataset Collection](../models/geometric_datasets.md)

Essentially not applicable. SceneScribe-1M, SpatialVID, and WildWorld have no dialogue transcription or speaker-attribute annotation. Action100M is the only relevant one: narration ASR transcription inherited from HowTo100M is available for 72% of the videos (covering 10.6 years of content), but the paper explicitly states its annotation paradigm is "pure visual action supervision that does not depend on ASR," with ASR used only as a reference/auxiliary cue, and it contains no attribute fields for speaker identity, language, accent, or emotion.

### [Post-training Data Strategy for Video Generation](../models/post_training_data.md) ⚠️

The anchor paper does not touch on this at all [Uncertain]. Cross-sectionally: dialogue and speaker-attribute annotation are nearly blank in the post-training stage — the cinematic split used for Movie Gen's audio SFT explicitly excludes clips containing human voice; Seedance 1.5 pro claims its reward covers audio fidelity but does not state whether this includes dialogue accuracy or speaker consistency [Uncertain]; whether Kling 3.0 Omni's preference annotation includes lip-sync degree and timbre consistency is not disclosed [Uncertain].
The transferable experience from within the ecosystem comes from captioner post-training: AVoCaDO's dialogue F1 reward (speaker accuracy + content edit-distance DP-aligned similarity, threshold 0.6) is currently the only design that formalizes "dialogue transcription accuracy" as an optimizable reward; in theory it could be directly transferred as a dialogue-dimension reward for AV generation models, but as of the time of this survey no generation-side work has done so.

### [Survey of Mainstream Video Pretraining Dataset Combinations: Panda-70M, InternVid, Koala…](../models/pretraining_datasets.md)

Not applicable. None of the seven has published ASR transcription; none annotates speaker identity/language/accent/emotion. Two related-but-not-actually-dialogue-annotation situations need clarification: (1) **Panda-70M used English subtitles (including YouTube auto-generated subtitles) but only as text-side input to the teacher model** (video2dataset configuration writesubtitles:True, subtitleslangs:['en'], writeautomaticsub:True); the subtitles land in each clip's JSON metadata and are not published as a dataset field, and contain no speaker attributes; (2) **InternVid internally collected ASR subtitles in 11 languages** (the paper's Figure 16 shows English/Chinese/Korean/German examples), but these were neither used to generate captions nor released with the dataset — a case of a multilingual resource that was collected but discarded. In addition, for privacy reasons, Panda-70M **uses NLTK to replace all person names in captions with "person"** — effectively actively erasing speaker-identity information.
</content>
## Geometric and Structured Annotation (Camera Parameters, Depth, 3D Point Tracks, Action Annotation, Explicit State)

`geometric_structured_annotation` · Detail level: brief

### [Allegro](../models/Allegro.md)

Only lightweight semantic camera-motion annotation is present: captions use the explicit sentence pattern "Camera [MOTION_PATTERN]" to label the camera movement style (a semantic description generated by a fine-tuned Aria, not numerical parameters).
No camera intrinsic/extrinsic parameter estimation, depth maps, 3D point tracks, action/skeleton annotation, or explicit object-state annotation was performed. On the filtering side, motion information is reflected only as UniMatch's scalar optical-flow magnitude ([1.0, 100]), and is not retained as a dense optical-flow condition or structured field.

### [Apollo](../models/Apollo.md)

The paper uses no geometric or structured visual annotation whatsoever: no camera parameters (intrinsics/extrinsics, trajectories), no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no skeleton/keypoint/action annotation, no explicit physical-state annotation, no bounding boxes or segmentation masks. The only annotation that comes close to being structured is the speaker-attribute meta field on the audio side (gender, age). Positional information is handled entirely implicitly inside the model, in the form of positional encoding via MixD-RoPE (3 dimensions for video + 1 for audio), rather than being introduced as a data annotation. Although "camera stability" appears among the filtering dimensions, it is a scalar quality score used for screening and does not constitute structured annotation of camera motion.

### [CineDance / CineDance-1M](../models/CineDance.md)

[Existing structured annotation] The shot-level five-dimensional attributes contain fairly rich cinematographic-syntax information: scale (shot size, e.g., close-up/medium/long shot), angle (camera angle), movement (camera movement style, e.g., dolly/pan/tilt), narrative function, and duration category; plus shot transition type (e.g., hard cut/dissolve). During training, camera parameters are injected as an independent conditioning term in the form [SHOT i | scene sᵢ | camera κᵢ].
[Structuring of scenes and identities] A global scene table ⟨sceneₘ⟩ and a global character table ⟨charₙ⟩ form an explicit state-annotation system that lets identity/scene continuity across shots be explicitly modeled and evaluated.
[Missing dimensions] No numerical camera extrinsics/intrinsics (camera pose, focal length, etc.), depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields, human pose, or action skeletons are provided as geometric annotation. Camera information is a linguistic, qualitative description (κᵢ) rather than numerical parameters.
[Geometric substitute on the evaluation side] CineBench uses ArcFace (face clustering) and DINOv2 (cosine similarity of visual features) for consistency judgment, which is representation-level rather than geometry-level.

### [CogVideoX](../models/CogVideoX.md)

Essentially no explicit geometric or structured annotation: the paper uses no camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields (optical flow is used only as a scalar score for filtering, not retained as annotation), segmentation masks, or action-category labels.
The only structured element is implicit linguistic annotation: the GPT-4 summarization prompt explicitly requires captions to include "camera movements," so camera-motion information is embedded in the caption as natural language rather than as an independent classification field or label taxonomy (contrast with Movie Gen's 16-class camera-motion classifier plus prefix concatenation).
On the model side, "structure" is reflected mainly in positional encoding rather than data annotation: 3D RoPE explicitly models positional relationships across the text-time-space dimensions, and Multi-Resolution Frame Pack carries the shape information of each sample within a batch.

### [Cosmos-Predict2.5](../models/Cosmos-Predict25.md)

Geometric and structured annotation is a distinguishing feature that sets this work apart from general-purpose video generation models, since it targets Physical AI and robotics/driving simulation:
[Human keypoints] In the Human Dynamics domain, YOLOX (Ge et al., 2021) is used for human detection, and RTMPose (Jiang et al., 2023) for full-body keypoint and facial landmark estimation; these geometric quantities are used as hard admission criteria for data: person-present frame fraction >40%, visible person count ≤8 in any frame, and at least one person occupying ≥3% of the frame area.
[Multi-camera calibration and view structure] Driving data comes from 7 synchronized cameras (front-wide, front-tele, front-left/left, front-right/right, rear, rear-left, rear-right — naming varies slightly across sections), at 30 FPS, 20 seconds; a 7-dimensional per-view learnable embedding is concatenated along the latent channel dimension for each view, and a 3D-factorized RoPE is constructed separately for each view — that is, view identity is explicitly encoded structured information.
[Driving structured control signal, the "world scenario map"] The control input for Cosmos-Transfer2.5-2B/auto/multiview is constructed by projecting HD high-precision map elements and dynamic objects onto the seven camera views, including lane markings, road markings, poles, road boundaries, traffic lights (including light state), and traffic signs, and can express complex road topology including interchanges; dynamic objects are represented as 3D bounding-box cuboids, color-coded by coarse-grained category ontology (truck / vehicle / pedestrian, etc.), with shading used to distinguish front from back faces. This is the heaviest structured-annotation system in the entire paper.
[Robotics-domain action and metadata] The action-conditioned variant uses action as a conditioning input; on the caption side, motion type (linear/rotational), the parts involved (arm/wrist/gripper), and camera motion are mandatorily annotated; structured metadata such as GR00T's manual success ratings and Bridge's step-level instructions are also injected. The policy-learning experiments additionally include action chunks of end-effector pose and gripper commands (8-step horizon, sampled at 10 FPS) and joint states.
[Segmentation and detection (for downstream augmentation)] In robot policy data augmentation, Grounding DINO + SAMv2 are used to detect and segment robot pixels, enabling zoned control such that "edge control applies to the whole image while blur control applies only to robot pixels"; Cosmos-Transfer2.5's general-purpose ControlNet supports four control modalities: edge, blur, segmentation, and depth.
[Not covered] The general pretraining corpus is not annotated with depth maps, 3D point tracks, or camera extrinsic trajectories (these appear only in the ControlNet's control signals and in driving-specific data).

### [Data-Juicer 2.0](../models/Data-Juicer.md) ⚠️

This is the direction in which Data-Juicer has invested most heavily in 2026, and also its most distinctive angle relative to other data-processing frameworks — driven by Embodied AI / VLA requirements, three consecutive versions (v1.4.5, v1.5.0, v1.5.3) added a large number of geometric and structured annotation operators. For video generation, this batch of operators corresponds precisely to "a second annotation paradigm beyond text captions," as seen in SceneScribe-1M, SpatialVID, and similar works.
[Camera parameter annotation — three implementations provided]
  · video_camera_calibration_deepcalib_mapper — computes intrinsics and field of view (FOV) for a static camera based on DeepCalib.
  · video_camera_calibration_droidcalib_mapper — extracts camera intrinsics from video based on DroidCalib.
  · video_camera_calibration_moge_mapper — computes intrinsics and FOV based on MoGe-2.
  · video_undistort_mapper — undistorts the raw video using the previously obtained intrinsics and distortion coefficients.
[Camera pose/trajectory]
  · video_camera_pose_megasam_mapper — "extracts camera pose by combining MegaSaM with MoGe-2." MegaSaM is a 2025 dynamic-scene SLAM method that can estimate camera trajectories on in-the-wild video containing moving objects — this is exactly the core capability needed for "camera motion" conditioning annotation in video generation.
[Depth]
  · video_depth_estimation_mapper — performs depth estimation on video, producing a dense depth-map sequence.
[Body and hand — action-annotation chain]
  · video_whole_body_pose_estimation_mapper — extracts 2D whole-body pose estimation of body, hand, foot, and facial keypoints.
  · video_hand_reconstruction_hawor_mapper — hand reconstruction based on HaWoR + MoGe-2.
  · video_hand_reconstruction_mapper — hand localization and reconstruction based on the WiLoR model.
  · video_hand_motion_smooth_mapper — smooths hand motion in world coordinates and removes outliers.
  · video_hand_action_compute_mapper — "computes 7-DoF actions and 8-dimensional states from hand reconstruction results and camera pose." This is unambiguously "explicit state" annotation — converting video into a robot-usable action-state sequence, the standard format for VLA training data.
  · video_atomic_action_segment_mapper — segments the unified hand trajectory into atomic action clips, i.e., action-level temporal segmentation annotation.
  · video_trajectory_overlay_mapper — "samples frames and overlays hand trajectories to prepare VLM-readable frames." Visually overlaying geometric trajectories onto the frame before feeding it to a VLM is a bridging technique between geometric and semantic annotation.
  · video_clip_reassembly_mapper — reassembles hand-action results from overlapping clips back onto the long-video timeline.
[Object level]
  · video_object_segmenting_mapper — text-guided semantic segmentation based on YOLOE + SAM2, producing an object mask sequence for the entire video.
[Assessment] This chain of operators makes DJ the most complete open-source framework in terms of geometric annotation capability currently available: camera intrinsics, distortion, pose trajectories, depth, whole-body/hand pose, 7-DoF actions, 8-dimensional state, atomic action segmentation, and object segmentation are all present, and it makes heavy use of the latest 2025–2026 models (MegaSaM, MoGe-2, HaWoR, WiLoR, SAM2, YOLOE). Although designed originally for embodied intelligence, it can be directly reused for controllable video generation that requires camera control, depth conditioning, or action conditioning.
[Uncertain] No dedicated operator for 3D point tracks (long-horizon point trajectories in the style of CoTracker/SpatialTracker) is seen; the throughput, cost, and real-world effectiveness of this batch of operators on large-scale video-generation corpora are all undisclosed.

### [Foley-Omni](../models/Foley-Omni.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] The paper does not involve any geometric or 3D structured annotation: no camera parameters (intrinsics/extrinsics, trajectories), no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no human pose or action annotation, no explicit state annotation.
The closest thing to "structured visual information" is the temporal synchronization features extracted by Synchformer and the scene-semantic features from CLIP, but these are implicit neural-network representations rather than interpretable geometric annotation. Motion score is the only explicit quantification of motion, and is used solely for filtering-threshold decisions, not retained as training annotation.
This absence has little impact on the task at hand: audio generation does not require precise 3D geometric understanding, and audio-visual alignment relies mainly on 2D temporal event cues. However, for finer-grained spatial audio generation (e.g., binaural/surround sound, sound-source direction changing with camera motion), the lack of camera and 3D annotation would become a bottleneck — this work generates mono/stereo mixed audio tracks and does not involve spatial audio.

### [Goku](../models/Goku.md)

Structured annotation is very lightweight, comprising only two items:
(1) **Camera motion** — not obtained through geometric estimation, but implicitly described in natural-language form by Tarsier2 when generating video captions (zoom in, pan right, etc.), constituting a "linguistic camera label" rather than a parametric camera trajectory.
(2) **Motion intensity** — the scalar motion score obtained from RAFT optical flow, used both as a filtering threshold and as a numerical condition in the caption.
[Not covered] Camera intrinsic/extrinsic calibration, depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields as conditioning input, human pose/skeleton, object boxes and trajectories, segmentation masks, explicit state annotation, etc. Goku's conditioning dimensions consist only of "text + (in the I2V setting) first-frame image + motion score," a level of structure notably lower than works around 2026 that emphasize geometric controllability.

### [Hailuo / MiniMax Video](../models/Hailuo.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] (No explicit disclosure, only strong indirect clues.) No mention of camera parameter calibration, depth maps, 3D point tracks, pose/skeleton keypoints, or explicit physical-state annotation. Indirect clues:
(1) video-01-director's camera-movement vocabulary (roughly 15 combinable camera-motion instructions) indicates the existence of at least discrete camera-motion label annotation, but this is a semantic label rather than continuous camera extrinsics;
(2) Hailuo 02/2.3's emphasis on physical plausibility and complex human motion may correspond to pose- or kinematics-level annotation/filtering, but there is no explanation whatsoever;
(3) S2V-01's Subject-Reference capability requires data support for character identity consistency, hinting at the existence of face/identity-embedding-level annotation and paired data construction, likewise undisclosed.

### [HunyuanVideo-Foley](../models/HunyuanVideo-Foley.md) ⚠️

The paper uses no geometric or structured annotation: no camera parameters, no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no skeleton/pose estimation, no object detection boxes, no optical-flow fields, no action annotation, no physical-state annotation.
[The only "structured" information involving temporal structure: Synchformer frame-level synchronization features] This is not annotation but rather an internal conditioning feature of the model — Synchformer extracts frame-level synchronization-related representations from the video frame sequence and injects them into the DiT via adaLN and gated modulation pathways. It encodes temporal-structure information about "what changed on screen and when," functionally serving as a "visual event timeline" and forming the core basis on which the model achieves precise audio-visual synchronization. However, it exists as a dense feature rather than discrete annotation — it is not interpretable, not editable, and is not persisted as a dataset field.
[Why the V2A task does not need geometric annotation] The value of geometric annotation (camera parameters, depth, point trajectories) in video generation lies in controlling spatial structure and motion consistency. For sound-effect generation, the geometric information that would theoretically be valuable is "sound-source distance" (which determines volume and reverb ratio) and "spatial direction" (which determines stereo imaging), but this model outputs mono/stereo audio and does not perform spatial-audio modeling, so this kind of information is not exploited.
[An unexplored direction] Introducing depth information to judge sound-source distance, or introducing object detection to localize the sounding body, could in principle improve the spatial realism of sound effects and accuracy in multi-source scenes. The paper does not explore this direction, which can be regarded as room for future work. [Uncertain: this is analysis, not paper content]

### [HunyuanVideo](../models/HunyuanVideo.md)

A limited but clearly defined item: camera-movement annotation.
[Original version] A camera-movement classifier was trained that can predict 14 camera-movement types: zoom in, zoom out, pan up, pan down, pan left, pan right, tilt up, tilt down, tilt left, tilt right, around left, around right, static shot, handheld shot. Only high-confidence predictions are written into the JSON caption.
[1.5] The camera-movement recognition model is no longer restricted to a fixed 14-class enumeration (the report says it recognizes "multiple camera-movement types" but does not enumerate them); it now outputs at both clip-level and temporal-level granularity, and is transcribed into natural language folded into the caption.
Neither generation uses explicit camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, explicit physical state, or action-skeleton annotation — camera motion is expressed as discrete labels/natural language rather than geometric parameters. This differs from routes such as Seedance and Movie Gen that introduce stronger geometric supervision.

### [InstructAV2AV](../models/InstructAV2AV.md) ⚠️

This work uses two kinds of structured visual annotation, but both are instrumental intermediate products of the editing task and are not retained as training annotation.
[Instance-level segmentation masks] Grounded-SAM-2 (Ren et al., 2024) produces instance-level masks, the most important structured annotation in this pipeline. It serves a dual purpose: (1) providing anchors for editable objects when Qwen3-Omni generates instructions ("which entities in the frame can be referenced by an instruction"); (2) constraining the editing region for the mask-guided video editing model, structurally guaranteeing that pixels outside the mask are not altered — this is a hard mechanistic guarantee of "content preservation" at the data level, far more reliable than relying on the model to voluntarily refrain from changing the background. Note that masks are used only during the data-synthesis stage; the final InstructAV2AV model is mask-free (the user only needs to give a text instruction, no mask is required) — that is, the information carried by the masks is implicitly distilled into the model through the data. This design of "using masks at construction time, not using masks at inference time" is a clear point of value in this work.
[Point trajectories] CoTracker3 performs gridded point tracking, producing point trajectories used to compute average motion magnitude. The trajectories themselves are used only as a filtering criterion and are not retained as annotation.
[Spatio-temporal speaker localization] TalkNet outputs the spatio-temporal location of the active speaker, used for audio-visual attribution judgment, likewise for filtering purposes.
[Uncertain] No annotation at the 3D or camera level is involved: no camera intrinsics/extrinsics, no camera trajectories, no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no human/facial keypoints or pose annotation, no explicit object-state annotation.
[Direct link between this absence and a stated limitation of the paper] The paper acknowledges that the model "struggles to preserve 3D spatial consistency or object permanence during extensive camera movements." This failure mode is directly related to the data side's lack of camera and depth annotation, and to the absence of any tiered control over camera motion — the data neither tells the model how the camera is moving, nor applies mixture-ratio control or a graded curriculum over samples with different camera-motion intensities; the model can only learn implicitly from pixels, and naturally fails under large motion. Introducing camera-parameter annotation or bucketing by camera-motion intensity in future work would be a clear path for improvement.

### [2026 Other Audio-Video Joint Generation](../models/JAVG_2026_misc.md) ⚠️

This batch of works is generally weak on geometric annotation; the sole highlight lies in face/identity-related structured annotation (which matters more to AV models than camera parameters):
[ALIVE — structured annotation of face trajectories and identity (the only work in this batch with substantive structured annotation)]
(1) Face trajectory ID (TrackID): the SubjectID correction pipeline builds a "sentence → TrackID" mapping, indicating that face-trajectory-level structured annotation exists in the data (each face is tracked over time and assigned an ID).
(2) Face-voice correspondence matrix: the face-voice correspondence matrix produced by TS-TalkNet is an explicit structured cross-modal association annotation.
(3) Identity embeddings: ArcFace face embeddings + CLIP visual embeddings, used for cross-clip identity matching. The character-driven pipeline gives explicit matching thresholds: face similarity >0.35, CLIP similarity >0.7, absolute proximity to 0.9 — the only publicly disclosed set of identity-matching thresholds in this batch.
(4) Sync score: TS-TalkNet's sync score is used to select identity anchors ("1.5-second sub-clip with maximum sync score").
(5) Manual sub-motion-unit timestamp annotation: decomposing actions into sub-motion units and annotating their start/end times — a form of temporal structured annotation (action boundaries); not geometric, but explicitly structured.
(6) Not covered: camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, pose keypoints, segmentation masks [all uncertain].
[NAVA] The caption template includes a "camera behavior" section (camera angle, shot scale, framing, composition) — this is a linguistic description of the camera rather than numerical camera parameters, constituting "weakly structured" annotation. No depth, 3D tracks, pose, etc. [uncertain]. Speaker embeddings (ReDimNet) can be regarded as structured annotation on the acoustic side.
[OmniCustom] The reference image is obtained by "randomly sampling and cropping a frame containing a face" — implying face-detection annotation, but used only for cropping and not retained as a structured field [uncertain]. No geometric annotation.
[StreamChar] Uses a reference frame and a motion frame as conditioning — the motion frame is an implicit representation of motion state, not explicit geometric annotation. ASR timestamps constitute temporal structured annotation.
[Baton] Planned tokens are essentially "continuous semantic features," which can be regarded as a non-human-readable implicit structured intermediate representation — this is an alternative approach to traditional structured annotation: rather than annotating human-defined structure (camera, depth, action), it lets the model learn its own semantic structure. The penultimate-layer features of SigLip2 and WavTokenizer are the carriers of this "structure."
[CCL / ITS-JAVG] None [uncertain].
[Overall assessment] The focus of "structured annotation" in AV generation models is clearly different from that of pure video-generation models: the latter focus on camera parameters, depth, and 3D trajectories; the former focus on face-trajectory IDs, face-voice correspondence, speaker embeddings, and speech timestamps. ALIVE's face-voice correspondence matrix and TrackID system are the most typical embodiment of this difference.

### [Audio-Video Joint Generation Baseline Collection](../models/JavisDiT_baselines.md) ⚠️

This collection is almost entirely blank; only UniAVGen has one item of face-related structured information.
[UniAVGen (the only one)] Contains a Face-Aware Modulation module, which requires face-region information in order to apply modulation to specific regions; the data side must therefore include a face-detection/face-region annotation step, but the paper does not describe which face detector is used, whether it is a bounding box or a mask, or whether it is computed offline [uncertain]. The use of the EMTD benchmark (an audio-driven facial-animation benchmark) for evaluation also corroborates its face-related processing.
[MM-Diffusion] No geometric annotation whatsoever. Worth noting: the AIST++ dataset itself natively comes with 3D human pose and camera-parameter annotation (AIST++'s original design goal was 3D dance-motion generation), but MM-Diffusion makes no use of this annotation at all, using only RGB video and audio — i.e., "the dataset has geometric annotation, but the work does not use it."
[AV-DiT] No geometric annotation whatsoever.
[JavisDiT / JavisDiT++] No camera parameters, no depth, no 3D point tracks, no pose keypoints, no action labels, no segmentation masks. The only thing approaching structured information is the optical-flow score (flow score, scalar motion intensity) and metadata such as resolution/frame count in the CSV. Although JavisDiT's HiST-Sypo prior is named a "spatio-temporal prior," it is an implicit vector estimated from text rather than explicit geometric annotation.
[Harmony] No geometric annotation [uncertain].
[Common assessment] The technical route across this collection is generally "letting cross-modal attention mechanisms learn alignment spontaneously," rather than "constraining alignment via explicit geometric priors" — this is consistent with Ovi's claim of "requiring no face bbox, no face mask," indicating that the audio-visual joint generation community as a whole leans toward data-driven rather than geometric-prior-driven approaches. UniAVGen's Face-Aware Modulation is one of the few counterexamples to this trend, at the cost of confining the model's capability to real-person speaking scenarios.

### [Kling 3.0 / Kling Video 3.0 Omni](../models/Kling_30_Omni.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] The Kling-Omni report does not disclose geometric annotation such as camera parameters, depth, or 3D point tracks. Indirect evidence: Kling 3.0 emphasizes "3D spatiotemporal joint attention," able to model physical interactions such as inertia, weight transfer, and collision detection, and Kling has historically had strong camera-control capability, hinting that the training data contains structured labels for camera motion/shot type (Koala-36M's "shot language" field is precisely this kind of textualized camera-motion annotation). Human-body structured annotation has clear evidence in KlingAvatar 2.0: DWPose keypoints, SAM2 instance segmentation, and temporal tracking. [Uncertain: whether there is explicit camera-extrinsics estimation, depth-map, or point-tracking annotation]

### [LTX-2](../models/LTX-2.md) ⚠️

Essentially none. Neither paper mentions structured geometric annotation such as camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields, explicit physical state, or action-category labels. The only relevant item is natural-language camera description — captions record camera movement in text form (tracking shot, pan, fixed position, etc.), which is weakly structured. Additional clue: the official release includes camera-motion-control LoRA and pose-control LoRA, indicating the existence of purpose-built control datasets, but how they were constructed (whether explicit camera-trajectory or pose-estimation annotation was used) is undisclosed. [Uncertain]

### [LongCat-Video](../models/LongCat-Video.md) ⚠️

Structured annotation concentrates at the level of "shot language" rather than the geometric level: camera-motion categories (pan/tilt/zoom, determined by a dedicated classifier) and shot scale/type (Qwen2.5VL) are annotated, but these are discrete semantic labels, not continuous camera extrinsics/intrinsics (no camera-pose trajectories, no camera-pose numerical values). On the motion side, optical-flow statistics are annotated, an aggregate quantity of the dense 2D motion field.
The report does not involve geometric annotation such as depth maps, 3D point tracks, point clouds, or action skeletons (pose/keypoints, base version).
Because of character-driving needs, Avatar 1.5 performs face detection and facial-landmark extraction to verify facial visibility, plus ByteTrack-based person-tracking boxes, and applies a mask-area constraint (a limit on the person-mask area fraction) when sampling training data — these are 2D structured annotations, still not 3D geometry. [Uncertain: whether there is undisclosed depth/pose annotation]

### [MOVA](../models/MOVA.md) ⚠️

The paper uses no geometric or structured annotation: no camera parameters, no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no skeleton/action annotation, no explicit physical-state annotation. The closest thing to structured annotation is the on_screen_text field in captions (precise transcription of on-screen text) and the four categories of structured visual description extracted by the inference-side prompt rewriter (visual style/cinematography/visual elements/OCR text), but the latter serves prompt augmentation at inference time and is not part of the training-data annotation schema. Camera-movement information appears only as natural language in captions (e.g., "The camera alternates between…", "camera panning, zooming, and rotation"), with no parametric representation. [Uncertain]

### [Merged entry: ① Mochi 1](../models/Mochi_MAGI_Motif.md) ⚠️

① Mochi 1: No disclosure. On the architecture side, a learnable RoPE positional encoding extended to three dimensions is used, but this belongs to model design rather than data annotation. [Uncertain]
② MAGI-1: Only semantic-level camera structured annotation, no numerical geometric annotation. The caption attribute table includes five structure/geometry-related attributes: Camera Shot Type, Camera Movement, Camera Transitions, Subject Position (the subject's position within the frame), and Subject Action, but all are conveyed via captions in natural-language descriptive form; no camera intrinsics/extrinsics estimation, depth maps, 3D point tracks, or skeletal-action annotation is performed. The geometry-related quantities on the filtering side are RAFT optical flow's scalar statistics (three motion intensities: overall/foreground/background) plus optical-flow divergence and optical-flow consistency; these are used only for discrimination and are not retained as conditioning.
③ Motif-Video 2B: Likewise semantic-level rather than numerical-level. The camera_move field among the VLM's structured labels, together with the three camera attributes ("shot type, angle, motion") requested by the video prompt, constitute camera descriptions usable for conditioning, but remain in text form; no camera-parameter regression, depth, 3D tracking, or explicit state annotation is performed. Geometry-related numerical processing appears only in preprocessing (cropdetect's content rectangle, IoU clustering of OCR detection boxes, the 512-dimensional SSCD descriptor) and filtering (UniMatch optical-flow score).
None of the three adopts the dense geometric-annotation route seen in some 2025–2026 works (e.g., introducing camera-trajectory parameters, point tracking, or depth conditioning).

### [Movie Gen](../models/Movie_Gen.md)

Dominated by semantic camera annotation, with no explicit numerical geometric annotation:
· Camera motion: a self-trained classifier predicts 16 classes of camera motion (dolly/pan/tilt/pedestal, arc, tracking, static, handheld), with high-confidence predictions prefixed into the caption; during the SFT stage, 6 additional classes of shot type and camera angle (wide, close-up, aerial, low-angle, over-the-shoulder, first-person view) are manually annotated. This is the most central structured-annotation system in the entire paper.
· Action/behavior: a taxonomy of 600 human verbs and expressions is built, used for zero-shot text→video retrieval and text k-NN retrieval, indirectly constituting action-category annotation.
· Face/identity: PT2V data uses a face detector (sampling frames per second), ArcFace identity similarity (adjacent frames with similarity >0.5 are judged as the same person throughout the clip), and face-region cropping and segmentation (to prevent the model from attending to non-critical areas such as the background); synthesized reference images with ArcFace similarity <0.7 are discarded.
· Objects: SFT uses Detic object detection to judge whether the subject is too small.
· Motion intensity: FFmpeg motion score and motion vector serve as scalar motion quantities; evaluation prompts were also manually tagged with high/medium/low motion-level labels.
No explicit geometric annotation such as camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, or optical-flow fields is used [confirmed as not adopted].

### [NVIDIA NeMo Curator](../models/NeMo_Curator.md) ⚠️

Structured-annotation capability is limited but present in two places:
[1. Camera-motion labels] A motion classifier (ViT + optical-flow sequence) labels camera-motion type — pan, zoom, tilt — while filtering. This is an explicit structured label directly usable for camera-motion-control training, and a rare non-text annotation output from this pipeline.
[2. Motion scores] Two numerical metrics, motion_score_global_mean and motion_score_per_patch_min_256, are retained alongside each clip and can serve as a continuous conditioning signal for motion intensity.
[3. Embeddings] Both Cosmos-Embed1 video embeddings and caption text embeddings are stored in Parquet and packaged into the WebDataset, usable for retrieval, clustering, and conditional modeling.
[Missing] There is no built-in stage for camera intrinsics/extrinsics estimation, depth maps, 3D point tracks, persisted optical-flow fields, object boxes/segmentation masks, action-category labels, or explicit physical state. Compared with the Cosmos platform as a whole (which also has components such as Cosmos Transfer involving depth/segmentation conditioning), geometric annotation on the curation side is a weak link. [Uncertain]

### [OmniHuman Dataset + OHBench](../models/OmniHuman.md) ⚠️

Geometric and structured annotation is the dimension in which OmniHuman stands out most relative to every other entry in this survey — most AV-generation datasets score zero on this dimension, whereas OmniHuman builds a complete geometric pipeline:
[Human detection and tracking] YOLOv11 detects human instances → NMS refinement → MOTRv2 models cross-frame association via query propagation, outputting per-person identity trajectories and bounding boxes (B_i). The tracking-loss tolerance span is 5 frames (beyond which the trajectory is considered broken).
[2D skeleton: 134 keypoints] DWPose-L extracts 134 full-skeletal keypoints across body, face, and feet, together with dedicated hand detection and optimization — separately optimizing the hands is a pragmatic detail, since general-purpose pose estimators are typically least accurate at the hands, and hand movement is exactly the key element of human-object interaction and gesture expression. The 134 points correspond to the COCO-WholeBody scheme (17 body + 6 feet + 68 face + 42 hands).
[3D parametric human body: SMPL / MANO] The tracking_npz in the HuggingFace release explicitly contains "per-frame SMPL/MANO body & hand tracking" — that is, the dataset also provides per-frame parameters for a 3D parametric body model and hand model. This is an important asset that exists in the release but is not emphasized in the paper's main text, providing direct support for downstream applications such as pose-controllable generation, 3D consistency constraints, and motion retargeting. Note that the paper's main text mentions only DWPose's 2D keypoints; how the SMPL/MANO parameters were obtained (which estimator was used, how they were fused with the 2D keypoints) is entirely unstated. [Uncertain]
[Identity embeddings] ArcFace facial features, used for identity assignment (similarity > 0.55) and identity consistency across frames/samples. The rule for selecting the reference face is "the frame with the highest average confidence for facial keypoints."
[Face clarity] Cs = Var(ΔR), the variance of the Laplacian response, retained as a queryable annotation field.
[Shot-language parameters] Shot scale and camera motion are retained as video-level annotation fields; although these are labels rather than continuous parameters, they already suffice to support shot-level controllable generation and diagnostics.
[Person-object interaction annotation] Frame-level annotation includes person-object interaction annotations and object annotation (the Object Anno. column in Table 1), the only one of its kind among all the comparison datasets. On the evaluation side this corresponds to two metrics: Object Consistency and Contact Naturalness (comprising spatial accuracy, temporal synchronization, and force realism).
[Not provided] Camera intrinsics/extrinsics (no camera calibration or pose estimation performed), depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields (UniMatch's optical flow is used only for filtering, not retained as annotation), expression coefficients (3DMM/FLAME blendshapes — SMPL is present but no facial-expression parameterization is seen). [Uncertain]

### [Open-Sora Series](../models/Open-Sora.md)

Annotation depth is fairly shallow, with structured information only at the 2D-motion level, no 3D geometric annotation:
- Present: **camera-motion labels** — Open-Sora 1.x classifies camera motion (pan left, zoom in, etc.) based on UniMatch optical flow, labeling only high-confidence clips and writing them into the caption, with the module separately open-sourced under tools; Open-Sora 2.0 and Open-Sora Plan instead treat camera motion as a descriptive field of the caption, generated by a VLM in natural language, not as an independent structured field.
- Present: **scalar quality conditions** — aesthetic score, motion score (VMAF/optical-flow/LPIPS), OCR text-region count, and DOVER technical score are all stored as structured columns in the meta CSV, some of which are concatenated into the caption as controllable conditions.
- Absent: camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields themselves as conditioning input, explicit object-state/action labels, segmentation masks, etc.
- Open-Sora Plan v1.3 trained an independent **Structure Controller** that extracts three types of structural signals from the data — **canny edges, depth maps, and sketches** — as control conditions (20k steps, 8 NPU cards); this is the only place involving depth-information annotation, but its purpose is ControlNet-style conditional control rather than being part of the annotation schema for the main training data.

### [Ovi](../models/Ovi.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain]. Ovi has no geometric/structured annotation step at all: no camera intrinsics/extrinsics, no camera-motion labels, no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no pose/keypoints, no action-classification labels, no segmentation masks, no explicit state annotation.
The two closest items are: (1) the motion score produced by RAFT (scalar motion intensity, not structured geometric information); (2) the face presence/count output by an internal face-detection model (used only for data-composition mixture control, not injected into the model as a condition).
The paper particularly emphasizes that its method "requires no face bounding box, no face mask" or other heuristic geometric priors (contrast with HunyuanVideo-Avatar, which uses masks to restrict audio features to act on the facial region), treating independence from geometric annotation as a design advantage — lip sync is learned entirely spontaneously from the data and cross-modal attention; the attention visualizations in Section 5.1 show speech tokens automatically focusing on the mouth, drum sounds focusing on the drum, and animal calls focusing on the corresponding body part.

### [Script-a-Video](../models/Script-a-Video.md)

MTSS contains no numerical geometric annotation: no camera intrinsics/extrinsics, no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no optical-flow fields, no skeleton/pose keypoints, no bounding-box coordinates, no segmentation masks.
However, this work goes quite deep on the dimension of "non-numerical structured annotation," which is the substantive content of this entry:
[Semanticized annotation of shot language] The camera field of the Shot stream records camera movements, perspectives, and shot scales using professional cinematic language. This structures camera information in the form of "director's language" rather than a "parameter matrix" — sacrificing precision in exchange for controllability that can be directly understood and generated by an LLM. For generative models with text as the conditioning interface, this is a more practical form than numerical camera parameters.
[Entity-level structured appearance anchoring] The appearance_anchor of the Reference stream expands person-type entities into fine-grained attribute slots such as clothing, accessories, and hairstyle. This is an identity representation of "substituting attribute slots for pixel-level annotation."
[Structuring of the timeline] This is MTSS's most rigorous dimension of structure — three layers of temporal annotation: shot-level time_range, event-level time_range, and intra-description / micro-level timestamps embedded inside the description text. The three layers share a single global timeline, forming an explicit temporal structure over which interval arithmetic can be performed. The paper states its goal is to achieve sub-frame audiovisual coordination.
[Relational graph structure] Three types of references — references_in_shot (many-to-many edges from shot → entity), active_events (edges from shot → event), and speaker (edges from event → entity) — together form an explicit entity-shot-event tripartite graph. This is the part of the entire schema that comes closest to a "knowledge graph," and is the substantive referent of the term "Relational Grounding."
[Action annotation] Exists as free text within visual_description, required to narrate the core action "objectively and in temporal order," and anchored by intra-description timestamps, but with no action-category label taxonomy.
[Explicit state] The semantic_description of the Reference stream records the overall state of an entity, a weakened form of explicit state annotation.

### [Seedance 2.0 and Seedance 1.5 pro](../models/Seedance_20_Seedance_15_pro.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] No disclosure of geometric annotation such as camera parameters, depth, or 3D point tracks. The known structured annotation stays at the text-caption level: Seedance 1.0's captions explicitly include a camera-movements category as one of the dynamic features; Seedance 1.5 pro has autonomous camera-movement scheduling ability (continuous long takes, dolly zoom/Hitchcock zoom, cinematic transitions, and professional color grading); Seedance 2.0 has basic directorial and cinematographic reasoning ability, able to autonomously plan shot sequences. These suggest that camera movement enters training as language labels rather than numerical camera parameters.

### [SkyReels Series](../models/SkyReels.md) ⚠️

[SkyReels-V2] Clear structured annotation exists at the "cinematographic geometry" level, but as classification labels rather than continuous parameters: shot type, camera angle, camera position, and camera motion (classification of 6-degree-of-freedom camera movement, output by a dedicated motion-recognition captioner, backed by 93,000 manually annotated samples plus 16,000 synthetic samples). These fields enter the caption as discrete labels, supporting the controllable camera-movement capability of the SkyReels-V2-Camera-Director variant. No continuous geometric annotation such as numerical camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, or optical-flow fields is used.
[SkyReels-V4] The focus of structured annotation shifts toward "masks and conditions" rather than geometry: to unify the inpainting/editing framework, training data needs conditioning channels such as region masks, reference images, reference video clips, and reference audio (in Stage 6, image reference and video reference each account for 20%); a visual segmentation model is used to construct the masks. The paper does not mention annotation such as camera parameters, depth, point trajectories, or explicit physical state. [Uncertain]

### [Sora 2](../models/Sora_2.md) ⚠️

Entirely undisclosed. No mention of camera parameters, depth maps, 3D point tracks, action annotation, or explicit physical-state annotation. Third-party technical commentary claims the training data carries "physical annotations" covering gravity, momentum, buoyancy, material deformation, and collision dynamics; if true, this would constitute explicit structured state annotation, but this claim is not an official OpenAI statement and cannot be verified. OpenAI officially claims only, at the capability level, that the model "understands real-world physics," and positions Sora 2 as a step toward a world simulator. [Uncertain]

### [SpeakerVid-5M](../models/SpeakerVid-5M.md)

Structured annotation is a strength of this dataset, at a density far higher than most generative video datasets:
[Human pose] DWpose outputs complete keypoint (skeletal) sequences for face, hands, and body. This is core geometric annotation prepared for downstream gesture-generation and body-motion-driving tasks. Note: because the data volume is too large, the skeleton sequences themselves are not uploaded to HuggingFace — only the computation code is open-sourced, and users must run it themselves.
[Spatial boxes] Per-frame spatio-temporal bounding boxes obtained from YOLO human detection and tracking, stored in merge_anno, and also the basis for spatial cropping of clips.
[Facial identity features] ArcFace facial features and cross-clip cosine similarity, used to verify speaker-ID consistency.
[Discretized annotation of framing and orientation] The caption fields Upper-Body Only (half-body/full-body), Facing Direction (front/side), and Number of People explicitly discretize body composition, and are the differentiating annotation items in Table 1 relative to head-focused datasets such as CelebV-HQ.
[Camera motion] The caption's Camera Motion field annotates the camera-motion pattern in text form, but this is a natural-language/categorical description, not a parametric camera pose (no extrinsics matrix, no trajectory coordinates).
[Motion intensity] A 1–5-point Motion Intensity Level (scored by an ensemble of three Qwen2.5-VL personas).
[Blur and quality scores] Laplacian-variance blur scores, clarity scores, and DOVER scores for face/hands.
[Numericalization of audio-visual sync] SyncNet's triplet of offset, confidence, and embedding distance is saved per clip.
[Not provided] Camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields, 3D human meshes (SMPL, etc.), explicit physical-state annotation. On the evaluation side, Deep3DFaceRecon is used to compute expression FID, but that is a VidChatBench evaluation tool, not training-data annotation.

### [Step-Video-T2V](../models/Step-Video-T2V.md)

Structured annotation is relatively sparse, consisting mainly of two kinds of scalars and one kind of text:
[Motion scalars] Three numerical labels derived from Farneback optical flow — Motion_Mean / Motion_Max / Motion_Min (used for filtering in pretraining; upgraded in Step-Video-TI2V into an explicit controllable condition injected into the model, enabling controllable motion magnitude);
[Clustering scalars] Cluster_Cnt and Center_Sim from VideoCLIP embeddings;
[Camera motion] Written into the dense caption as natural language (e.g., "camera pans left"), rather than as a discrete label enumeration — this differs from HunyuanVideo's approach of training a 14-class camera-motion classifier that outputs discrete labels; Step-Video-TI2V achieves "controllable camera motion" by fine-tuning the caption model to strengthen camera-motion description, still following the natural-language route.
[Entirely missing] No geometric structured annotation such as camera intrinsics/extrinsics estimation, depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields themselves as a supervision signal, action skeletons, or explicit physical state is used. This contrasts with routes such as Movie Gen and Seedance that introduce stronger geometric/structural supervision, and is also one of the shortcomings the team acknowledges in the "Challenges and Future" section of the report (insufficient capability for complex physics and causal modeling).

### [UniTalking](../models/UniTalking.md) ⚠️

The paper uses no geometric or structured annotation: no camera parameters, no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no optical-flow fields, no facial landmarks, no expression coefficients (3DMM/FLAME/blendshape), no head pose, no explicit physical-state annotation. Information such as camera movement, shot scale, and composition is likewise neither parameterized nor labeled, and may only be scattered as natural language within Qwen3-VL's video captions, but there is no caption example available to confirm this. [Uncertain]
[Only two models in the pipeline touch spatial structure, and neither of their outputs is retained]
- LightASD: active-speaker detection, which internally needs to localize and track the face region, but its output in the pipeline is used only as a boolean criterion (whether the speaker is in frame); the face box and trajectory are not retained as annotation;
- LipSync: lip-sync evaluation, which likewise needs to localize the mouth region, but outputs only a single alignment score used for filtering; the mouth-region information is not retained.
That is, facial spatial information is discarded immediately after being used in the filtering stage, and never converted into a condition usable for training.
[A piece of ready-made structured annotation that was abandoned] Its upstream data source, OpenHumanVid, natively comes with human skeleton sequences as a motion condition — a ready-made, high-value piece of structured annotation. UniTalking makes no use of it whatsoever (the paper does not mention the skeleton at all); its conditioning system consists only of text, identity image, and reference audio. For the talking-head generation task, the skeleton could in principle have improved controllability over upper-body and hand motion, and abandoning it is an unexplained trade-off. [Uncertain]
[Alternative structural modeling on the architecture side rather than the data side] UniTalking's handling of spatio-temporal structure is reflected in anisotropic RoPE: the spatial dimensions (h, w) of audio tokens use RoPE at a single fixed position, degenerating the spatial dimension so as to force the model's modeling capacity to concentrate on the time axis — this injects structural priors via positional encoding rather than via annotation.

### [UniVerse-1](../models/UniVerse-1.md) ⚠️

The paper uses no geometric or structured annotation: no camera parameters, no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no skeleton/pose/action annotation, no explicit physical-state annotation, no optical-flow fields.
The only model touching spatial structure is RetinaFace face detection, but its output is used only as a boolean criterion within the funnel (whether a face is present in the frame, to decide whether the clip can enter the speech subset and undergo SyncNet verification); the detection-box coordinates and facial landmarks are not retained as annotation or used as a training condition.
Information such as camera movement, shot scale, and composition is likewise neither parameterized nor labeled, and may only be scattered as natural language within video captions generated by QWen2.5-Omni, but the paper does not publish caption examples, so this cannot be confirmed. [Uncertain]

### [Unison](../models/Unison.md) ⚠️

The paper uses no geometric or structured annotation: no camera parameters, no depth maps, no 3D point tracks, no skeleton/pose/keypoint annotation, no optical-flow fields, no physical-state annotation, no action-category labels.
[The only element touching spatial structure is the face-detection box] The lip-filtering operator detects the number and location (bounding boxes) of faces, but these detection boxes are used to constrain the operating region of SyncNet (applied exclusively within these bounding boxes) — an internal intermediate product of the pipeline, not retained as annotation and not fed into the model as a training condition. The detector model is undisclosed. [Uncertain]
[Notably, how "motion" is modeled] Although the title centers on the concept of motion, Unison performs no explicit geometric parameterization of motion whatsoever (no skeleton, no keypoint trajectories, no hand pose) — this contrasts with one of its data sources, OpenHumanVid, which does provide skeleton annotation, indicating that Unison does not exploit this feature of that dataset. The correspondence between motion and audio relies entirely on implicit learning: architecturally via frame-level cross-attention over a three-frame window, and in training via bidirectional cross-modal forcing. The paper's qualitative results (note onsets in piano performance corresponding to finger movements, moments of impact corresponding to impact sounds) show that this implicit route is effective within short time windows, but the lack of explicit geometric supervision also means the physical correctness of the correspondence cannot be verified or controlled.
[Camera-movement/shot-scale information] Neither parameterized nor labeled; may be scattered as natural language within the caption text, but this cannot be confirmed due to the absence of caption examples. [Uncertain]

### [Veo 3 / Veo 3.1](../models/Veo_3_Veo_31.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] No geometric or structured annotation of any kind is disclosed. No mention of camera parameters (intrinsics/extrinsics/pose), depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical flow, action labels, or explicit physical-state annotation. Two points can be inferred: (1) Veo 3.1 offers a clear product capability of "camera controls (precise framing and camera-movement control)," hinting at some form of camera-movement annotation in the training data (most likely still achieved via natural-language camera-movement descriptions in captions, rather than explicit camera parameters); (2) DeepMind's own paper "Video models are zero-shot learners and reasoners" (arXiv:2509.20328) tests Veo 3 across 62 vision tasks and finds it zero-shot capable of segmentation, edge detection, keypoint localization, super-resolution, deblurring, denoising, etc., and proposes the concept of "chain-of-frames" — the authors emphasize that the model was never explicitly trained on these tasks, which instead suggests that these geometric/structural abilities emerge from large-scale natural video rather than coming from explicit geometric annotation.

### [Vidu S1](../models/Vidu_S1.md) ⚠️

No explicit geometric annotation. Captions contain semi-structured text labels such as "camera language," "cinematic properties," and "lighting"; the omni model additionally outputs semantic labels such as shot and scene, but the paper does not mention geometric or explicit-state annotation such as camera-parameter estimation, depth maps, 3D point tracks, or pose/skeleton keypoints. [Uncertain]

### [Wan 2.5 / 2.6 / 2.7](../models/Wan.md) ⚠️

The Wan family invests more heavily in structured annotation than most peers, and most of it serves "controllable generation" rather than pure quality checking.
[Pose] Wan2.2-S2V: VitPose tracks each character's 2D pose and converts it to DWPose format, explicitly serving two purposes — (1) as an optional multimodal control signal fed into the generation model, achieving precise temporal alignment with body motion; (2) as a fine-grained filtering criterion (removing samples where the person's spatio-temporal footprint is too small).
[Camera] Wan 2.1: a batch of videos was manually annotated with camera angle and camera motion, used along two paths — one directly for training the final-stage caption model, the other for training an expert model to bulk-annotate more videos (for use in early training stages). This is a typical example of the Wan family's leveraged annotation pattern of "manually label a seed set → expert model expands the labels → full coverage," and the report explicitly states this improves the generation model's camera-motion controllability. Wan 2.1 additionally has a dedicated camera-motion-controllability extension module (Section 5.5).
[Face] Personalization data: 1 FPS face detection, ArcFace inter-frame similarity, face segmentation (removing background interference), and facial-keypoint detection (used for canvas alignment during training).
[Text-image consistency / temporal consistency metrics] SigLIP feature cosine similarity is used as a data-filtering metric for three types of tasks — I2V data requires the similarity between the first-frame feature and the mean of the remaining frames' features to exceed a threshold (the threshold value is undisclosed); video-continuation data requires the SigLIP feature similarity between the preceding 1.5-second segment and the following 3.5-second segment to meet a standard; first/last-frame transition data instead increases the proportion of samples with large first/last differences.
[Optical flow] Wan-Dancer uses SEA-RAFT to generate optical-flow masks incorporated into the loss function; S2V uses UniMatch optical flow to compute a motion score.
[Spatial relationships and counting] Left/right/up/down spatial-relationship data is constructed with the help of object-detection datasets; Grounding DINO performs count verification.
[Not seen] No camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, or explicit physical-state annotation. [Uncertain]

### [Audio-Video Generation Evaluation Benchmark Collection](../models/av_benchmarks.md) ⚠️

[PhyAVBench is the richest] Its 41 fine-grained test points essentially form a structured physical-property annotation system: material hardness/damping, density, surface texture, object size/shape/thickness, impact velocity, contact area/sharpness, rotational speed, tightness, tension, flow rate, viscosity, spatial volume, surface sound absorption, propagation medium, sound-source distance, direction (horizontal/vertical localization), etc., all being enumerable explicit state annotation. In addition, the Observer Physics dimension implicitly involves camera/listener position and distance parameters, and the Binaural Effect test points involve binaural spatial-direction annotation.
[VABench stereo subset] 116 prompts explicitly specify left/right channel spatial direction, equivalent to structured annotation of sound-source direction; on the evaluation side, spatial geometric quantities such as Mid/Side energy ratio, phase coherence, and inter-channel consistency are computed.
[AV-SyncBench] Precise timing parameters of the annotated perturbations (offset: five levels from 50–500ms; jitter: three levels from 30–700ms; speed change: ten levels from 0.8×–1.25×) constitute structured annotation on the timeline.
[AVBench] Hard negatives include precise annotation of time offsets of 0.2–3.0s.
None of the five involves visual geometric annotation such as camera parameters, depth maps, or 3D point tracks [uncertain].

### [Video Caption Model Ecosystem](../models/caption_models.md) ⚠️

The captioner ecosystem adopts a consistent strategy for geometric/structured annotation — "bolt on a dedicated model, splice the result into the caption" — rather than requiring the VLM to output geometric quantities directly:
[Camera parameters and camera motion (most mature)] Movie Gen's 16-class camera-motion classifier (high-confidence predictions prefixed into the caption); SkyCaptioner-V1's 6-DoF camera-motion sub-expert (classification-based, 93,000 manually annotated samples + 16,000 motion-axis-balanced synthetic samples, achieving 89% single-type motion accuracy and 85.3% overall camera-motion field accuracy on a 15,000-sample test set); Tencent HunyuanVideo's independent camera-movement classifier (version 1.5 outputs at clip-level plus temporal-level dual granularity); LongCat-Video's lightweight pan/tilt/zoom classifier; Allegro embedding camera motion into captions with the fixed sentence pattern "Camera [MOTION_PATTERN]." Tarsier2 is among the few general-purpose captioners that naturally describe camera-motion type.
[Instance-level spatial annotation] InstructAV2AV uses Grounded-SAM-2 (open-vocabulary detection + segmentation + video tracking) to produce instance-level masks, serving as anchors for "editable objects" and as the editing region for mask-guided synthesis — the case in this ecosystem with the deepest involvement of geometric annotation.
[OCR / text detection] Motif-Video 2B pairs with PaddleOCR-VL (served via vLLM) for on-frame text detection; AVSCap's Visual Completeness criterion explicitly includes OCR.
[Action/temporal annotation] ALIVE performs "decomposing sub-motion units" — decomposing actions into sub-motion units and manually annotating the start/end timestamp of each complete small action, the most costly fine-grained temporal annotation in this ecosystem; Tarsier2's SFT stage performs fine-grained temporal alignment; CineDance's shot grouping and narrative-boundary determination (F1 88.4%).
[Depth / 3D point tracks] [Uncertain] No mainstream captioner-ecosystem work is seen outputting depth maps or 3D point tracks as part of the caption — this kind of annotation appears in world-model/robotics video data (such as the Cosmos family) but is not part of the standard configuration of the T2V captioner ecosystem.
[Explicit state annotation] CineDance's local character table plus active scenes is the publicly disclosed design that comes closest to "explicit state."

### [Geometric/Structured Annotation Dataset Collection](../models/geometric_datasets.md)

The core field of this entry. SceneScribe-1M (the geometric "three-piece set" plus text): ① Camera parameters — estimated by MegaSaM, which is built on the DROID-SLAM camera-tracking backbone and injects depth priors from Depth Anything and UniDepth during bundle adjustment; the paper compared DPVO, Fast3r, MonST3R, and VGGT before selecting MegaSaM, on the grounds that it is more robust when feature points are sparse and more effective for dynamic scenes and low camera parallax; ② Dense depth maps — MegaSaM's bundle-adjustment layer outputs temporally consistent continuous video depth; ③ 3D point tracks — TAPIP3D, which projects 2D video features into 3D world space to compensate for camera motion, then iteratively refines them to obtain long-horizon-consistent 3D point tracks, which can also be projected back onto the image plane to remain compatible with 2D tracking tasks; ④ Text description. SpatialVID: per-frame camera pose + intrinsics, depth maps (MegaSaM's depth module replaced with UniDepth v2 + Depth Anything v2), SAM2-generated dynamic-object masks and per-frame dynamic ratio, three trajectory metrics (MoveDist/RotAngle/TrajTurns), and serialized motion instructions — derived from the relative translation and rotation between adjacent frames and mapped to a controlled cinematography-term vocabulary (dolly in, pan left, truck right, etc.), discretizing continuous pose into an instruction sequence consumable by text models — this is the dataset's most distinctive design. WildWorld (engine ground truth, the highest precision): per-frame annotation across 119 columns, including ① Character and monster skeletons — 3D keypoints and joint-tree structure; ② World state — entity position, rotation, velocity, animation ID, gameplay attributes such as health/stamina; ③ Camera intrinsics and extrinsics; ④ Losslessly encoded depth maps; ⑤ Discrete action labels — on the character side, 5,960 unique triplets (weapon type, bank ID, motion ID) and 455 unique character actions; on the monster side, 2,132 unique pairs and 527 motion IDs, covering movement, attack, dodge, defense, item use, and transition actions. Action100M: structured annotation leaning semantic rather than geometric — temporal localization boundaries + open-vocabulary action labels + actor identification + a hierarchical caption tree, constituting "structured action annotation" rather than 3D geometric annotation.

### [Video Generation Post-Training Data Strategy](../models/post_training_data.md) ⚠️

The anchor paper does not address this [uncertain]. Cross-comparison yields only two examples: Cosmos-Predict 2.5's domain-specialized post-training uses a "world scenario map" (projected from HD maps and 3D boxes) as the control signal for the Cosmos-Transfer2.5 driving ControlNet, the clearest case of using geometric structured annotation at the post-training stage; LTX-2 released a series of control LoRAs for camera motion, pose control, and lip dubbing, which clearly require purpose-built geometric/pose paired datasets, but the construction method is entirely undisclosed [uncertain]. LongCat-Video incorporates a dedicated "camera motion and visual style" dataset during the SFT stage to strengthen instruction following, but it is not stated whether this includes explicit camera-parameter annotation [uncertain]. LongCat Avatar 1.5 introduces a first-frame hand-visibility check in GRPO to prioritize sampling of hand-containing samples, a distinctive practice of using a structured detector for rollout sampling scheduling.

### [Combined Survey of Mainstream Video Pretraining Datasets: Panda-70M, InternVid, Koala…](../models/pretraining_datasets.md)

**All seven datasets' structured annotation stays at the 2D semantic level; none provides 3D geometric information**:
[What exists]
