- **Camera motion**: six of the datasets treat camera movement as a descriptive field within the caption (Koala-36M's "shot language" includes movement/angle/focal length/shot scale; MiraData has an independent `camera_caption` column; UltraVideo has two independent dimensions, Camera Movement and Shot Type; LVD-2M's captions include camera perspective; OpenVid/InternVid touch on it sporadically within free text). **But all of these are natural-language descriptions — not one of them provides a structured enumeration of camera-motion labels or camera-trajectory parameters.**
- **Scalar quality scores (the only genuinely structured part)**: **Koala-36M is the most complete** — its CSV directly publishes three columns of scores usable for conditional control: `clarity_score` (0–1), `aesthetic_score` (2.28–6.56), `motion_score` (0.01–267), plus a fused score `video_training_suitability_score` (2.50–4.95). **Panda-70M** publishes `matching_score` (a UMT image-text matching score), `desirable_filtering` (a 6-class enumeration), and `shot_boundary_detection` (a list of shot intervals output by TransNetV2, where a length of 1 means a single shot). **LVD-2M** publishes `total score` (average optical-flow score), `scene_cut`, `span`, `video_time`, and `dataset_src`. **InternVid** publishes a UMT-SIM score and an aesthetic score. The CSVs of MiraData/OpenVid/UltraVideo consist mainly of caption fields; quality scores are not published alongside the data.
- **Video category labels**: only **LVD-2M** classifies videos into 8 categories using BART (with exact percentages given); **UltraVideo** has a scheme of 7 topics and 108 subcategories (but the mapping relationship is unpublished — an inquiry in issue #5 went unanswered).
[What is completely absent] Camera intrinsics/extrinsics, depth maps, 3D point tracks, optical-flow fields themselves as a condition, explicit object-state/action labels, segmentation masks, human pose/keypoints — **all seven datasets score zero on every one of these**. The only thing that comes close is MiraData, which on the evaluation side (MiraBench) uses **GVGC 3D reconstruction** to compute MAE/RMSE as a "3D consistency" metric and uses **CoTracker** to compute Tracking Strength, but that is an **evaluation metric, not data annotation**, and is not published alongside the dataset.
**Conclusion**: these seven datasets are purely "video-text pair" resources, unable to directly support camera-controllable generation, depth-controllable generation, or 3D-consistency training — if downstream use requires these capabilities, additional annotation must be added independently.
## Synthetic data construction (controlled perturbation / edit-constructed training pairs, e.g. InstructAV2AV)

`synthetic_data_synthesis` · Detail level: brief

### [Allegro](../models/Allegro.md)

The paper does not use synthetic data construction for training pairs, and does not perform controlled perturbation/edit construction (such as InstructAV2AV-style paired data synthesis). All data comes from real videos that have been segmented and filtered. The only "generative" step is that captions are generated by a model (Tag2Text / Aria), which counts as synthetic annotation rather than synthetic video.

### [Apollo](../models/Apollo.md)

The paper does not mention any synthetic data construction: no controlled perturbation/edit-constructed training pairs, no InstructAV2AV-style instruction-editing data pairs, no TTS-dubbed synthetic pseudo audio-video pairs, no feedback loop of model-generated data (self-distillation / rejection sampling). All 81 million training samples come from real videos with native audio tracks; the pipeline's role is filtering and annotation rather than generation. The only "synthetic" component is that all captions are produced by an automatic model (synthetic annotation, not synthetic content). The multi-task capability (T2A / T2V / T2AV / I2V / I2AV) is not obtained by constructing synthetic data pairs of different forms, but by dynamically constructing training targets on the same batch of real data through **random modality masking** — this is a "task synthesis at training time" idea rather than "synthetic samples on the data side," and it is a key design that makes Apollo relatively economical in data engineering effort.

### [CineDance / CineDance-1M](../models/CineDance.md)

None. CineDance-1M consists entirely of real filmed footage, and the pipeline contains no controlled perturbation, edit construction, paired-sample synthesis (such as InstructAV2AV-style before/after edit pairs), or data-augmentation-style synthesis steps.
[Closest operation] The only "construction" is regrouping atomic shots into narrative sequences, which is a re-grouping of real footage rather than synthesis; and the DARC curriculum in CineDance model training (continuous noising of reference frames ρ(rₖ; ηᵥ(u)) = ηᵥ(u)·rₖ + (1−ηᵥ(u))·εₖ, random index switching, reference-frame dropping with probability p_drop(u)), which is an input perturbation strategy at training time rather than offline synthetic data construction.

### [CogVideoX](../models/CogVideoX.md) ⚠️

· Visual data: entirely real captured video; no synthetic video was constructed, and no controlled perturbation or edit-constructed training pairs were used.
· Text data: captions are entirely synthetic text, produced by a multi-stage model synthesis chain — Panda-70M short captions + CogVLM frame-by-frame image captions → GPT-4 summarization (50,000 entries) → distilled into LLaMA2 → further distilled into CogVLM2-Caption. This is the only systematic synthetic data construction in this work, and it counts as "synthetic annotation" rather than "synthetic samples."
· Inference-side synthesis: the caption upsampler uses an LLM to synthesize long prompts so that the inference input distribution moves closer to the training caption distribution — essentially also a synthetic-data alignment technique.
· Details of training-pair construction for the image-to-video (I2V) model are given in Appendix D; the paper body does not describe whether synthetic first frames were used [uncertain].

### [Cosmos-Predict2.5](../models/Cosmos-Predict25.md)

This work displays a "reject synthesis on the training-data side, produce synthesis on the downstream-application side" two-faced attitude, which is one of its distinctive features:
[Pretraining side: actively excludes synthetic content] The filtering pipeline's final stage explicitly removes video games, synthetic visual patterns, animations, and cartoons, on the grounds of maintaining alignment with the real physical-world distribution. That is, at the training corpus level, synthetic/non-physical content is treated as contamination.
[Controlled rewriting of robot data] In the robotics domain, videos with overly slow motion have their playback speed increased to normalize motion pacing — a controlled temporal perturbation applied to real data.
[Downstream: the model as a synthetic-data factory] This is one of the paper's core application claims — using Cosmos-Transfer2.5-2B to apply visual augmentation to real robot demonstrations in order to expand the policy training set. The specific procedure: 100 human teleoperation demonstrations (a bimanual pick-and-place task of grasping an apple and placing it in a bowl) → a VLM generates a detailed caption for a sample video → Cosmos-Transfer2.5 is used repeatedly to generate and check outputs in order to refine the caption → the variable components are parameterized into slot templates ([TABLE], [COLOR_APPLE], [COLOR_BOWL], [SENTENCE_LIGHT], [SENTENCE_BACKGROUND]) → an LLM generates candidate variants for each slot → 5 synthetic variants are generated per original demonstration (500 in total), replacing only the image observations while keeping actions and joint states unchanged. Control parameters: edge threshold medium, blur threshold very low, CFG scale 3.
[Results] Across 10 real-robot test scenarios with 3 trials each: the base policy trained on only the 100 real demonstrations succeeded 1/30; the baseline policy using traditional image augmentation (brightness/contrast/saturation/hue perturbation, gamma correction, salt-and-pepper noise, histogram equalization, random blur/sharpening) succeeded 5/30; the proposed policy augmented with Cosmos-Transfer2.5 succeeded 24/30, and was significantly stronger on object swaps, tablecloth changes, added spotlights, added distractors, changed background cabinets, opened drawers, and combined hard cases. The paper uses this to argue that traditional pixel-level augmentation cannot perform semantic edits (changing object color, environment appearance, lighting), whereas generative augmentation can.
[VLA synthetic data] Section 6.5 separately discusses using Cosmos-Predict2.5 to generate synthetic data for training Vision-Language-Action models.

### [Data-Juicer 2.0](../models/Data-Juicer.md) ⚠️

[Scale of synthesis capability] Of the 229 operators, about 50 are dedicated to data synthesis and augmentation, one of DJ's four major capability areas (analysis, synthesis, annotation, post-training). This proportion is notably higher than in comparable frameworks that focus mainly on cleaning.
[Synthesis/construction operators on the visual side]
  · video_ffmpeg_wrapped_mapper — wraps FFmpeg video filters and can apply arbitrary video transformations (cropping, color grading, noise addition, speed change, transitions, etc.), a general-purpose tool for constructing controlled-perturbation samples.
  · audio_ffmpeg_wrapped_mapper — the audio-side counterpart.
  · audio_add_gaussian_noise_mapper — Gaussian-noise augmentation for audio.
  · video_resize_resolution_mapper / video_resize_aspect_ratio_mapper — spec rewriting, usable for constructing multi-resolution training pairs.
  · video_remove_watermark_mapper — watermark removal, which can be paired with the original watermarked sample to form a "watermark removal" edit training pair.
  · video_object_segmenting_mapper — produces object masks, a prerequisite for constructing object-level edit/replacement training pairs.
[Synthesis recipes in DJ-Cookbook] DJ maintains a "video data synthesis" (video-data-synthesis) YAML recipe, a contrastive-learning data synthesis recipe (corresponding to the CVPR 2025 ImgDiff work, arXiv:2408.04594, which improves VLLMs via contrastive data synthesis), character-driven dialogue synthesis, and a curriculum-learning recipe based on data difficulty.
[Related research] The same team's ImgDiff (CVPR 2025) and MindGYM (NeurIPS 2025, question synthesis) are both methodology works on "using large models to synthesize training data," and their capabilities have already been folded back into DJ operators.
[Uncertain] DJ does not provide an InstructAV2AV-like capability for constructing audio-video editing instruction pairs, nor does it offer a ready-made recipe for "constructing controlled-perturbation positive/negative sample pairs" aimed at video generation (e.g., constructing motion-intensity contrast pairs, or audio-visual misalignment negative-sample pairs). The specific content and actual output scale of the video synthesis recipe are not disclosed.

### [Foley-Omni](../models/Foley-Omni.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] The paper does not describe any synthetic data construction step. The pipeline contains no controlled perturbation, audio-track substitution, time-shift-constructed negative samples, or InstructAV2AV-like editing instruction pair construction.
The only thing that involves "audio decomposition and recombination" is Bandit source separation, but its purpose is annotation verification (energy gating) rather than constructing training samples — the paper does not mention using the separated single-class stems as synthetic training data for TTA/TTM (this would be a natural data-augmentation idea: separating pure sound-effect tracks from V2ST data could expand the V2A training set, but the paper neither adopts nor mentions this).
All data consists of real captured audio-video pairs plus model-generated text annotations, following a "real data + model annotation" paradigm, with no generative data synthesis.

### [Goku](../models/Goku.md) ⚠️

No synthetic data construction is used in the training data. The paper has no controlled perturbation, edit-constructed training pairs, rendering-engine synthesis, data-augmentation-generated new samples, or any other synthetic data pipeline.
The only two places that touch on "synthesis/rewriting" are both unrelated to training data:
(1) the "augmentation and oversampling" applied to underrepresented categories during the distribution-balancing stage — here "augmentation" presumably refers to traditional data augmentation/resampling; the paper does not state whether it includes generative expansion;
(2) during evaluation, GPT-4o is used to expand GenEval's short prompts ("we expand the original short prompts in GenEval with ChatGPT-4o, preserving their semantics while enhancing descriptive detail"), an evaluation-time prompt-rewriting technique used to bridge the distribution gap between the dense captions seen during training and the short prompts seen during evaluation — this itself is important evidence that "the style of training captions shapes the form of inference prompts." [Partially uncertain] (the specific meaning of "augmentation" in the balancing stage).

### [Hailuo / MiniMax Video](../models/Hailuo.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] (no disclosure at all). There is no mention of using synthetic data, controlled perturbation, or edit-constructed training pairs (such as InstructAV2AV-style paired construction). S2V-01's subject-reference task, in principle, typically requires "same person across different clips" paired data, which could plausibly be constructed automatically via identity clustering rather than manual collection — but this is a methodological speculation with no primary-source evidence.

### [HunyuanVideo-Foley](../models/HunyuanVideo-Foley.md)

The paper does not construct any synthetic data:
- No controlled-perturbation-constructed training pairs (no positive/negative sample pairs constructed via sound-effect substitution, time shift, volume perturbation, etc.);
- No edit-style data construction (no InstructAV2AV-style audio editing instruction pairs);
- No manual mix-and-synthesize approach (no manual pairing of clean sound-effect library assets with video footage to synthesize training samples — a viable but unadopted route in the Foley field);
- No self-distillation using data produced by TTS or an audio-generation model itself;
- No negative-sample construction for contrastive learning.
All training data consists of real captured native audio-video clips, and native synchronized audio tracks are mandatory.
[The only "synthetic" component] All captions are automatically generated by the GenAU model — this is synthetic annotation, not synthetic content.
[The only design close to "construction": the high-quality tag] By manually appending a quality tag to captions and forcing its use at inference time, the work constructs a conditioning mechanism where "the training distribution and inference distribution are deliberately offset" — during training, tags faithfully reflect sample quality, while at inference the high-quality tag is always used, steering generation toward the high-quality end of the distribution. This is not data synthesis, but it is an artificial construction of the data condition, and is methodologically related to the conditional manipulation used in classifier-free guidance.
[A notable unadopted route] The Foley field has a classic data-construction route: using clean, single sound effects from a professional sound-effect library plus video footage, manually aligned to synthesize "perfectly aligned, perfectly clean" training pairs. This work follows the real-data route entirely — the benefit is realistic acoustic scenes and natural multi-source mixing relationships, at the cost of not being able to obtain a fully clean supervision signal (real audio tracks always contain some irrelevant background sound mixed in). The paper does not discuss this trade-off.

### [HunyuanVideo](../models/HunyuanVideo.md) ⚠️

Neither generation uses synthetic data construction for training pairs; the reports contain no description of controlled-perturbation/edit-style paired data (such as InstructAV2AV-type approaches). The only thing with a "construction" character is the T2V DPO stage in 1.5: for the same prompt, the model itself samples N candidate videos, which are paired into non-repetitive preference pairs, then judged win/lose by human GSB annotation — this is model self-sampling to construct preference data, not training-corpus synthesis. In addition, the original version's caption dropout + permutation/combination can be regarded as data augmentation on the text side. [Uncertain]

### [InstructAV2AV](../models/InstructAV2AV.md) ⚠️

This is InstructAV2AV's most representative dimension in this survey, and the reason the note lists it as the "representative case for the synthetic-data dimension." In this work's training data, every single target is a synthetic product — not something filmed for real, but something manufactured under control.

[Why synthesis is necessary: the task's fundamental dilemma] Instruction-guided editing requires (source, instruction, target) triplet supervision, where source and target must be two videos of "the same scene differing only in the target region." Such a pairing simply does not exist in reality — you cannot film the same scene twice with only a dog turned into a cat and every other pixel identical. The paper explicitly states this as its starting point: "Given the scarcity of such source-to-target paired resources in the audio-visual domain." Hence data synthesis is not an efficiency measure but the precondition for the task to be viable at all.

[Three layers of controlled design in the synthesis mechanism]
  1. Spatial control (visual side): Grounded-SAM-2 produces instance masks; the mask-guided editing model (based on Wan2.2-5B, trained with a source-conditioned flow-matching objective) synthesizes new content only within the mask region, while pixels outside the mask come from the source video. This construction hard-guarantees that "the non-target region is strictly unchanged," making source-target form a pixel-level clean differential pair.
  2. Sound-source control (audio side): SAM-Audio separates the target entity's sound from the original mixed audio track; ElevenLabs synthesizes the new target sound, which is then seamlessly mixed back with the retained original background sound. This likewise guarantees that "the non-target sound is strictly unchanged," with ambient/background sound being identical on both sides.
  3. Cross-modal synchronization control: the video synthesis model receives the already-synthesized target audio features as a condition via frame-wise cross-attention, forcing the new imagery to align with the new audio track frame by frame. The synthesis order is "audio first, then video" — once the speech's duration and rhythm are fixed, the lip movements must follow it.
  Together, these three layers of control produce something real data can never provide: precise, factor-level differential supervision.

[Decoupled construction of orthogonal factors] The five subsets — add_and_remove / clone_id / clone_voice / clone_id_voice / general_editing — embody independent manipulation and combinatorial enumeration of the factors "visual identity," "auditory timbre," and "entity presence or absence." In particular, the pair clone_id (change the face, not the voice) and clone_voice (change the voice, not the face) forms a strict control-group contrast — from which the model can learn that identity and timbre are two separable attributes. This kind of decoupled supervision is the core advantage of synthetic data over real data, and is the most valuable construction idea to borrow from this work.

[Data gain from bidirectional pairing] The HF dataset includes an instruction_reverse field: the same (source, target) pair can simultaneously serve as a forward training sample (real → synthetic) and a reverse training sample (synthetic → real). The generation target of the reverse sample is the real video, which can mitigate the problem of the model's output distribution being anchored to the synthetic distribution.

[Inherent risks of the synthesis route (not discussed in the paper)]
  · Inherited capability ceiling: the quality upper bound of the target is determined by Wan2.2-5B and ElevenLabs; the editing effect InstructAV2AV learns cannot, in principle, exceed its data engine. The limitations the paper acknowledges (physical realism, lighting consistency, 3D consistency) are explicitly phrased as things that "inherit from the foundational generation model" — precisely a manifestation of this risk.
  · Self-reinforcing distributional bias: the training set and the evaluation set (1K) come from the same engine, so the model may score highly by "mimicking the engine's style" rather than by genuinely being able to edit. The paper partially addresses this concern with an external, zero-shot AvED-Bench evaluation (FVD 227.82 vs. AVI-Edit 372.37).
  · Shared failure modes: systematic flaws in the data engine (e.g., a certain category of object always synthesizing poorly) get passed through completely unchanged to the downstream model, and cannot be caught by automated verification — because the Qwen3-Omni used for verification and the Qwen3-Omni used for generation are the same model.
[Uncertain] The data engine's per-attempt synthesis success rate, the average number of pairs produced per source, and the compute cost and total time for synthesis are not disclosed.

### [Other 2026 joint audio-video generation works](../models/JAVG_2026_misc.md) ⚠️

[ALIVE — the only work in this batch that systematically constructs synthetic/augmented data, and whose method is worth examining closely]
The character-driven data pipeline splits into two tracks:
(1) Cross-pair Pipeline (cross-clip pairing, using real data to construct "same identity, different scene" training pairs):
  - Extract N 3–10 second clips from a 10–30 minute long video
  - Select the "1.5-second sub-clip with maximum sync score" as the identity anchor — using the sync score rather than image-quality score to select the anchor is a deliberate choice: a high sync score means the person is clearly speaking at that moment and facing the camera directly, the moment when identity features are most fully exposed
  - Use CLIP + ArcFace embeddings for multi-subject feature matching
  - A valid cross-clip pair must simultaneously satisfy: face similarity >0.35, CLIP similarity >0.7, absolute proximity to 0.9
  This track is "mining same-identity pairs from real long videos," not generating synthetic pixels, but it is a typical case of training pair construction.
(2) In-pair Augmentation (within-clip augmentation, genuine synthetic data construction):
  - pose/expression perturbation
  - background augmentation (background enhancement/replacement)
  - semantic editing via Qwen-Image-Edit
  The purpose is explicit: "to decouple identity from appearance" — i.e., to have the model learn that "this person is still the same person after changing pose, background, or clothing," rather than memorizing background and clothing as part of identity. Using a large image-editing model to perform controlled perturbation to construct positive-sample pairs is a very typical 2026-era approach (methodologically related to InstructAV2AV-type ideas, but ALIVE uses it for identity decoupling rather than instruction editing).
(3) Synthesis on the prompt side: during training, the cross-attention signal is dropped out with probability 0.3, a form of conditional dropout augmentation; on the inference side, FAISS is used to build a dense vector index, extract sound events from the user prompt and retrieve nearest neighbors with a similarity threshold τ>0.85, performing retrieval-augmented prompt rewriting — this is synthesis at inference time rather than training time.
[OmniCustom — pair construction rather than pixel synthesis] Its splitting strategy of "first 4 seconds as reference audio / last 5 seconds as training audio-video" is a zero-cost form of pair construction: "each reference-training pair shares the same timbre but contains distinct speech content." This directly provides supervision for timbre decoupling: the model must learn to extract only the timbre from the reference audio without copying its content. The reference image is likewise randomly sampled and cropped from frames containing a face, another instance of pair construction. There is no pixel-level synthesis or editing.
[StreamChar — the distillation data is itself self-generated data] In the two-stage distillation, Stage II is "rollout consistency" (fine-tuning the student under online chunk rollout), which is essentially using the student model's own generated rollout sequences as training data — this is on-policy self-generated data, different from traditional synthetic data construction but belonging to the same broad category of "feeding model-produced data back into training." Stage I runs 600 steps to compress the step count down to a 4-step generator; Stage II runs 400 steps, with student lr 2e-6 and fake score network lr 4e-7. The distillation data source reuses the same audio-video dataset, with no additional construction.
[NAVA / CCL / Baton] No synthetic data construction is described [uncertain]. In Baton's three-stage training, the Stage 2 → Stage 3 transition involves "switching from clean ground-truth features to planner-predicted features," a curriculum-style input perturbation (replacing clean labels with the model's own imperfect predictions), methodologically close to scheduled sampling; it can be seen as a form of synthesis on the training-input side, but it does not produce new data samples.
[ITS-JAVG] Its Best-of-N and EvoSearch generate large numbers of candidates at inference time and then select the best; if the selected results were fed back into training, this would constitute a synthetic data pipeline — but the paper is explicitly training-free and does not make this extension. This is an obvious follow-up direction (using ITS's multi-verifier selection to produce high-quality synthetic SFT data) that the paper does not touch on.

### [Joint audio-video generation baseline collection](../models/JavisDiT_baselines.md) ⚠️

Two works in this collection use synthetic/bootstrapped data construction, and both are the most valuable parts of this collection to draw on for the data side:
[JavisDiT — controlled construction of asynchronous negative samples (ST-Prior training)] To train the HiST-Sypo spatiotemporal synchronization prior estimator, "synchronized" and "unsynchronized" audio-video pairs need to be distinguished. Beyond the 610,000 synchronized triplets, the authors additionally "synthesized asynchronous negative samples," paired with contrastive learning to train the prior estimator. This is a typical case of "constructing training pairs via controlled perturbation" — manufacturing negative examples by time-shifting the audio track, substituting it, or mismatching it across samples. The specific negative-sample construction method (how many frames of time shift, whether cross-sample random pairing is used, the ratio of positive to negative samples) is given in Appendix C.2.4, "Negative Sample Construction," of the paper, but this section is truncated in the publicly available HTML version, so the details could not be obtained [uncertain]. This design forms a sharp contrast with Ovi's "filtering out unsynchronized data via strict thresholds": Ovi discards unsynchronized data, whereas JavisDiT actively manufactures unsynchronized data to use as negative examples.
[JavisDiT++ — model self-generated preference data (AV-DPO)] A reference model generates N=3 audio-video candidates for each prompt in a pool of 30,000 prompts, which together with 1 ground-truth sample form a candidate group of "1 ground truth + 3 generated," then six reward models score and rank them, constructing about 25,000 preference pairs. This is entirely model-bootstrapped synthetic data. One quantitative finding worth recording: about 30% of the winning samples in the final preference data come from model generation rather than ground truth — i.e., in nearly three out of ten cases the model can generate a sample better than the real data, from which the authors conclude that the baseline model already has fairly strong generation capability. This ratio is a useful reference signal for judging "when it is time to move to DPO/RLHF."
[Harmony — construction at the data-pairing level] Stage-two timbre-decoupling fine-tuning uses "reference-target pairs mismatched across utterances": for speech data, different utterances from the same speaker are taken as reference and target; for ambient-sound data, non-overlapping segments of the same clip are taken as reference and target. This does not generate new data, but constructs a training signal by re-pairing existing data — a lightweight synthetic pairing strategy.
[MM-Diffusion / AV-DiT / UniAVGen] No synthetic data construction is used [uncertain].

### [Kling 3.0 / Kling Video 3.0 Omni](../models/Kling_30_Omni.md) ⚠️

Yes, and it is explicitly disclosed by the official source as a highlighted point. The Kling-Omni report states that data construction follows a dual track of "large-scale real-world data collection + task-oriented synthetic data construction"; the synthetic side is implemented by an "expert-model-driven synthesis pipeline," using in-house image-editing models and video-understanding models to construct training pairs for image/video editing tasks and multi-image reference tasks; it also proposes an "automatic reverse synthesis strategy" to construct reference-to-video training samples — that is, reference images/conditions are reverse-extracted from finished footage and then paired as (condition, target video) training pairs. This directly supports the Omni Edit capability (localized targeted editing without regenerating the whole segment) and the multi-image + timbre reference-locking capability. [Uncertain: the proportion of synthetic data in the total volume; whether there is controlled-perturbation-style synthesis on the audio side, such as InstructAV2AV-type audio-video editing pair construction]

### [LTX-2](../models/LTX-2.md) ⚠️

The technical report does not mention using any synthetic data or controlled-perturbation/edit-constructed training pairs (no InstructAV2AV-like editing pair construction). The training data all comes from real video (publicly available + licensed footage) plus image datasets. The only place involving "model-generated content" is in the LTX-Video paper, where a Sora-generated video is used as a demonstration example of the captioner's effectiveness — this is a demonstration, not training data. Whether distilled/self-generated data (e.g., for training a distilled version of the model) is used in undisclosed processes cannot be determined. [Uncertain]

### [LongCat-Video](../models/LongCat-Video.md) ⚠️

Limited use. One clear instance is in training the primary captioner: LLaVA-Video is fine-tuned "using in-house synthetic pairs" (internally synthesized image/video-text pairs), i.e., synthetic data is used to train the annotator itself, not directly as training samples for the generative model.
No controlled-perturbation/edit-constructed training pairs are seen on the video generation side (such as InstructAV2AV-style paired edit data construction). The training-data construction for the Video-Continuation task is a self-supervised conditional split — using the first several frames of a clip as condition frames and the subsequent frames as the prediction target, with the number of condition frames distinguishing three task types (T2V has 0 condition frames, I2V has 1 reference frame, VC has multiple preceding frames) — this automatically derives a supervision signal from real video rather than synthetically generating new content.
In addition, the caption's "randomly assembled cinematic-language and style tags" can be regarded as a form of synthetic augmentation on the prompt side. [Uncertain: whether synthetic video data bootstrapped by a generative model was used]

### [MOVA](../models/MOVA.md) ⚠️

The paper does not construct any synthetic training pairs: no controlled perturbation, no edit-constructed paired data (such as InstructAV2AV-style edit pairs), and no feeding generative-model-produced video back into training. All training data is real video. The only "synthetic" components are: (1) the in-house TTS-synthesized speech data used in audio-tower pretraining; (2) all captions being automatically generated by an MLLM/LLM (synthetic annotation, not synthetic content); (3) evaluation-benchmark prompts uniformly rewritten and consolidated by GPT-5, and rewritten prompts generated by Gemini 2.5 Pro in the inference workflow. [Uncertain]

### [Merged entry: ① Mochi 1](../models/Mochi_MAGI_Motif.md) ⚠️

① Mochi 1: No disclosure. [Uncertain]
② MAGI-1: All data comes from real footage that has been segmented and filtered; no controlled-perturbation/edit training pairs are constructed. The only "synthetic" step is on the text side — captions are generated by an MLLM (synthetic annotation), plus about 2 million synthetic prompt-rewriting entries used to distill the small Prompt Enhancement model.
③ Motif-Video 2B: The only one in this group that explicitly uses synthetic data on the visual side. The original pool is split into four branches — Image Real / Image Synthetic / Video Real / Video Synthetic — meaning both the image and video sides have a synthetic branch. The key scheduling constraint is: "Synthetic video is injected only at 720p, where its controlled quality is most compatible with the admission criteria" — that is, synthetic data is treated as a supplement for the high-quality stage rather than filler for pretraining. However, the paper does not state which model generates the synthetic video, what proportion it makes up, or how self-distillation-style distributional collapse is avoided, and it does not conduct a synthetic-vs-real ablation. It likewise does not construct InstructAV2AV-style paired edit data. [Partially uncertain: the generation method, proportion, and impact of the synthetic data]

### [Movie Gen](../models/Movie_Gen.md)

Extensive and systematic use of synthetic data, but concentrated on two capabilities — personalization and editing — while the T2V backbone still uses real data:
· Personalization (PT2V) cross-paired synthesis: training directly on paired data would let the model learn a copy-paste shortcut (the generated person always replicates the reference image's expression, head pose, and looking straight at the camera). Beyond collecting O(10)K real cross-pairs (clips from the same scene at different camera angles within the pretraining data), a pretrained personalized image-generation model (He et al., 2024b) is used to generate a reference image for the first frame of each video in the paired data, varying expression, head pose, lighting, etc. through diverse prompts; generated images with identity drift are removed using an ArcFace similarity threshold <0.7, ultimately yielding O(1)M synthetic cross-paired samples, used specifically to improve naturalness in the third stage of pretraining.
· Video editing (Movie Gen Edit) three-stage synthesis: the paper's core claim is that SOTA video-editing capability can be obtained "without relying on any supervised video editing data." Stage one is multi-task training, alternating between treating image editing as single-frame video editing and text-to-video generation. Stage two introduces two synthetic multi-frame editing tasks — Animated Frame Editing (applying random affine transformations to image-editing sample pairs to generate "animated" multi-frame editing pairs) and Generative Instruction-Guided Video Segmentation — and is fine-tuned jointly with text-to-video multi-task training for about a thousand steps. Stage three transfers the backtranslation idea from NLP to video editing: the stage-two model is used to generate edited videos, which after filtering are used to construct (noisy generated edited video x̂, backward editing instruction c_instruct-bwd, clean real video x) triplets, training the model to recover the clean real video from the noisy edited result — thereby training on multi-frame, high-quality real output videos. Ablations show backtranslation clearly outperforms standard fine-tuning.

### [NVIDIA NeMo Curator](../models/NeMo_Curator.md)

The curation framework itself does not perform synthetic video data construction, but the related capabilities are evolving:
[Video side] There is no stage for controlled-perturbation/edit-constructed training pairs (no InstructAV2AV-like editing pair construction). Cosmos WFM's training data contains about 4% "synthetically rendered" video, but that is externally supplied material, not something generated by the pipeline.
[Text/multimodal side] Since 26.04, NeMo Data Designer (a synthetic data design tool) has been integrated; in 26.07 a Nemotron OCR synthetic data pipeline was added, which can generate OCR training records from existing datasets and optionally score quality with Nemotron Nano Omni; 26.07 also introduced the Nemotron-CLIMB data-mixture optimization workflow (embedding–clustering–ratio search over documents, selecting a final mixture from 64 candidate ratios).
It is clear that NVIDIA is bringing "synthetic data generation" and "automatic data-mixture optimization" into the Curator roadmap, but currently this only covers text and OCR — the video modality has not yet followed.

### [OmniHuman Dataset + OHBench](../models/OmniHuman.md)

[None] The dataset consists entirely of real captured YouTube video, without using any synthetic or controlled-edit-constructed training pairs: no generated/edited samples on the video side, no TTS-synthesized content on the audio side (contrast with UniTalking, which uses IndexTTS2 to synthesize 6.9 million reference audio clips), and no controlled-perturbation-constructed editing instruction pairs (not an InstructAV2AV-style route).
[The only "construction"-like operation: derived tracks produced by source separation] Demucs four-source separation splits the original mixed audio track into a vocal track and a background track, producing two derived signals that do not exist independently in the raw data. Strictly speaking this is signal-processing decomposition rather than data synthesis, but functionally it does construct new possible training pairings — for example, "vocal track + video" can train a dubbing model, "background track + video" can train ambient-sound generation, and "video → vocal + background" can train full-soundscape generation. This is a low-cost, zero-hallucination-risk way to gain data, and unlike the TTS-synthesis route it introduces no training-inference domain gap.
[Frame cropping also causes a slight distribution shift] The frame-cropping operation that removes subtitles/watermarks changes the composition and aspect ratio of some samples — this is preprocessing, not synthesis.
[Judgment on design orientation] This dataset positions itself as targeting "complex, real-world physical scenes," deliberately pursuing a realistic distribution, so not introducing synthetic data is self-consistent with its stated aim — synthetic data would introduce distributional shift, which would run counter to its stated diagnosis of the problem of "insufficient scene diversity."

### [Open-Sora series](../models/Open-Sora.md)

Neither project **uses model-synthesized video data**; all training video comes from real, publicly collected datasets. The "constructed data" that does exist is limited to two categories, neither of which is video synthesis:
1. **Mask-constructed multi-task training pairs (Open-Sora Plan v1.3 Image Controller)**: on the same batch of real video, **different frame-masking patterns** are used to construct four task types — T2V, I2V (first-frame condition), Transition (first-and-last-frame condition), and Continuation (video continuation). Stage one has 50M samples split into 7 progressive sub-steps, with the frame-retention rate progressively lowered from 50% → 25% → 12.5%, and the task mixture gradually shifting toward I2V (40%) and Transition (40%); stage two's 15M high-quality data continues this mixture. This is a typical way of deriving multi-task supervision signals from a single batch of real data using masking, and does not involve a generative model.
2. **Synthesis at the caption level (Open-Sora Plan prompt refiner)**: ChatGPT is used to rewrite 19,500 real-sourced captions into a uniformly formatted "short prompt → long prompt" pairing — this is synthesis on the text side.
In addition, Open-Sora 2.0 builds a text-to-image-to-video pipeline on the inference side (first text-to-image with FLUX, then image-to-video), but this is an inference strategy, not training data synthesis.

### [Ovi](../models/Ovi.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain]. The paper does not describe any synthetic data construction step: no controlled-perturbation-constructed training pairs, no edit-style paired data (such as InstructAV2AV-type approaches), no feeding TTS-synthesized speech back into training, no construction of audio-visual-mismatch negative samples, and no data augmentation via audio-track substitution/shifting. All audio-video training samples are real captured native paired data.
The only "artificial construction" is at the data-format level: the audio-only fine-tuning data is padded to exactly 5.04 seconds to match the 121-frame video duration; and on the inference side, using GPT to expand the user prompt according to the training caption template is recommended (this is synthesis at inference time, not training time).

### [Script-a-Video](../models/Script-a-Video.md)

The paper does not construct any synthetic data: no controlled-perturbation-constructed training pairs, no edit-style data augmentation (no InstructAV2AV-style audio-video editing instruction pairs), no TTS-synthesized speech, no sound-effect overlay/mixing, no self-distillation using data self-produced by a generative model. All training data consists of real captured film/lifestyle-category audio-video clips.
[The only "synthetic" component is annotation, not content] All MTSS annotations for the 500K dataset are automatically generated by Gemini-2.5-Pro, which counts as synthetic annotation. This forms a standard model-distillation chain: annotation by a strong closed-source model → SFT of an open-source model → obtaining a cheaper dedicated annotator.
[The design closest to data construction] MTSS itself can be regarded as a form of "data restructuring at the representation level" — the same video, represented either as a monolithic caption or as an MTSS script, naturally forms a pair of contrastive data, and the paper uses exactly this contrast (LTX-2-AV vs. LTX-2-AV-MTSS, same architecture and same training, with the sole variable being the prompt structure) to make its core argument. But this is an evaluation design, not data synthesis.
[Potential synthetic value of editability (not practiced in the paper)] MTSS's locally editable characteristic is theoretically very well suited to constructing controlled-perturbation pairs — changing only the description of one sound effect in the Event stream, or only the camera field of a certain shot, would yield a set of training pairs with controlled semantic difference, without needing to rewrite the whole text. The paper points out this structural advantage but does not use it for synthetic data construction — it remains an unexplored application direction for this schema.

### [Seedance 2.0 and Seedance 1.5 pro](../models/Seedance_20_Seedance_15_pro.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] The synthetic data construction pipeline is not disclosed. The only relevant point is in the RLHF stage: Seedance 1.0 explicitly incorporates "synthetic videos generated by different stages of our model" as a source of data pairs for human preference annotation; experiments show that multi-source visual material can improve the reward model's domain capacity, extend the preference upper bound, and enhance generalization. This is preference-data synthesis rather than controlled-perturbation-style edit-training-pair construction. Seedance 2.0 has video editing and continuation capabilities (targeted modification of a specified segment/character/action/plot, forward/backward timeline continuation); it is reasonable to infer that its training requires paired before/after editing data, but the construction method is not disclosed.

### [SkyReels series](../models/SkyReels.md)

The SkyReels series makes heavy use of synthetic data in both generations, and for different purposes — this is one of the more information-dense entries here:
[SkyReels-V2] Synthetic data serves motion quality: (1) annotation side — 16,000 "motion-axis-balanced" synthetic samples are generated to fill in motion directions/types that are scarce in real data, used to train the motion-recognition captioner; (2) reinforcement-learning side — negative samples are automatically constructed by applying "controlled distortions" to real videos, forming DPO training data together with manually annotated preference pairs; the distorted samples cover the three variant forms V2V, I2V, and T2V, forming a "semi-automatic preference data production pipeline" — a typical practice of "using controlled perturbation to manufacture training pairs."
[SkyReels-V4] Synthetic data fills three gaps in real data: (1) multilingual on-screen text — synthesizing images/videos containing Chinese/English/Japanese/Korean/German/French text, addressing the shortage of multilingual glyph-rendering data; (2) multilingual speech — TTS-synthesized speech fills out language coverage; (3) inpainting/editing paired data — the paper explicitly states that "paired training data is inherently unavailable in real-world datasets," so a complex pipeline combining "a visual segmentation model + an image/video editing model + controllable generation techniques" is used to construct "before/after editing" paired samples, which is the data precondition for V4's unified generation-inpainting-editing capability. The specific segmentation/editing model versions used, the volume of constructed samples, and the quality-verification method are not disclosed.

### [Sora 2](../models/Sora_2.md) ⚠️

Completely undisclosed. In the System Card, the word "generate" in "information that our users or human trainers and researchers provide or generate" may cover content generated by human trainers, but whether this includes model-synthesized data, or whether training pairs are constructed via controlled perturbation/editing (such as InstructAV2AV-style editing pairs), there is no information at all. The safety-evaluation stage uses a "helpful-only version of the video model" to batch-generate adversarial sample outputs for building an automated evaluation set — this is a clear case of model-generated data being used for evaluation/safety alignment, but it does not count as main training-data synthesis. [Uncertain]

### [SpeakerVid-5M](../models/SpeakerVid-5M.md) ⚠️

None. SpeakerVid-5M is obtained entirely from real YouTube video through segmentation, cropping, filtering, and annotation, and contains no model-generated or controlled-perturbation-constructed synthetic samples, nor any "before/after editing"-style training pairs (contrast with InstructAV2AV's controlled-perturbation construction).
[The only "construction"-like component is data recombination, not data synthesis] The organization of the four branches has clear constructive character: the dialogue branch pairs independent clips of two speakers from the same time period; the listening branch uses the SyncNet score difference to identify the listener, thereby deriving listening samples from what was originally a speaking scene; the multi-turn branch constructs multi-turn context via time-window aggregation and δt-interval concatenation. These are all re-pairing/re-sequencing of real footage, not pixel- or audio-level generation, editing, or perturbation.
[Synthetic component on the annotation side] All captions, topic categories, and motion-intensity scores are automatically generated by an MLLM/LLM — this counts as "synthetic annotation" rather than "synthetic content."
[Data augmentation] The paper describes no augmentation strategy at training time or data-construction time (such as temporal jitter, color perturbation, mixup, etc.). [Uncertain]

### [Step-Video-T2V](../models/Step-Video-T2V.md) ⚠️

No synthetic data construction is used on the training-corpus side (no controlled-perturbation/edit-constructed training pairs, such as InstructAV2AV-type approaches). Only two post-training steps have a "construction" character, and both are model self-sampling rather than corpus synthesis:
· Video-DPO preference data: for each prompt, Step-Video-T2V generates multiple videos using different random seeds, forming a candidate set for human preference ranking — these are model-self-generated preference pairs;
· Distillation data: to train the accelerated Step-Video-T2V-Turbo, about 95,000 (95k) samples are sampled to form the distillation dataset.
In addition, there is light synthesis on the prompt side: besides randomly sampling from the training data, the DPO prompt set also invites human annotators to "synthesize" new prompts following carefully designed guidelines, in order to widen the prompt distribution coverage. [Uncertain]

### [UniTalking](../models/UniTalking.md) ⚠️

UniTalking constructs a clearly defined category of synthetic training data, and at no small scale — this is the most engineering-distinctive and most worth-examining part of its data pipeline:
[Motivation for construction] For the model to support the TR2AV (text + reference timbre → audio-video) task, it needs "reference audio + target audio-video" paired training data. But in the source dataset, each video only has its own audio track — there is no "another audio segment from the same speaker" available as a reference. The paper states directly: "our source dataset lacks corresponding reference audio." It must therefore be synthesized.
[Construction method] IndexTTS2 (a zero-shot, emotion-controllable, duration-controllable autoregressive TTS model) is used. For each video:
1) Input one: the video's original audio track, used as the target timbre reference;
2) Input two: a randomly generated text prompt — constructed as "randomly sampling sequences of words and numbers," i.e., a semantically meaningless random word string;
3) Output: a synthesized speech segment whose timbre matches the original video's speaker but whose content is entirely unrelated;
4) Duration constraint: each synthesized reference audio clip is strictly controlled to between 3 and 5 seconds;
5) Data augmentation: this process is repeated 3 times per video, generating 3 different reference audio clips. Across 2.3 million videos, this synthesizes roughly 6.9 million reference audio clips in total.
[Design-intent analysis: why random word strings rather than meaningful text] This is the most critical and most clever part of this design. If meaningful text were used to synthesize the reference audio, the model might learn to extract semantic content from the reference audio and leak it into the output — a shortcut-learning failure mode where "the content of the reference audio contaminates the generated content." Using semantically meaningless random word strings means the only stably usable information in the reference audio is the timbre itself, thereby forcing "decoupling of timbre and content" at the data level. This is a typical case of "eliminating shortcuts through data construction," more direct than decoupling via the loss function or architecture.
[Design-intent analysis: why TTS synthesis rather than splitting the original audio track] A more naive approach would be to cut out another segment from the same video's audio track as the reference. But then the reference audio and the target audio come from the same recording, sharing the recording environment, background noise, and even some content, making it very easy for the model to "cheat" via acoustic-fingerprint matching rather than genuine timbre understanding. Re-synthesizing via TTS cuts off this side-channel information, retaining only the timbre features modeled and reconstructed by the TTS model.
[Potential risk] The reference timbre comes from IndexTTS2's reconstruction rather than a real recording, so its timbre-fidelity ceiling is exactly IndexTTS2's cloning-capability ceiling; at the same time, the acoustic statistical characteristics unique to synthesized speech (TTS artifacts) become an inherent distribution of the reference condition, causing a training-inference mismatch — at inference time the user provides a real recording, which has a domain gap from the TTS-synthesized audio seen during training. This may well be one of the reasons TR2AV's timbre similarity is only 0.66–0.70, trailing dedicated TTS methods. The paper does not discuss this domain-gap issue, nor does it run a "synthetic reference vs. real reference" ablation. [Uncertain]
[No synthesis on the video side] No synthetic or edit-constructed video data is used; there are no controlled-perturbation training pairs (not an InstructAV2AV-style approach).
[Another instance of broadly defined synthesis] The nature of the "in-house TTS data" used in the stage-one audio pretraining is unclear — if it is TTS training corpus then it is real recordings, but if it is TTS-generated corpus then it is synthetic data; the paper does not specify. [Uncertain]

### [UniVerse-1](../models/UniVerse-1.md)

The paper does not construct any synthetic data: no controlled-perturbation-constructed training pairs, no edit-style data augmentation (such as InstructAV2AV-style audio-video editing instruction pairs), no TTS-synthesized speech, no sound-effect overlay/mix synthesis, and no self-distillation using data self-produced by a generative model. All training data consists of real captured native audio-video clips (with native synchronized audio tracks mandatory).
[The two designs closest to "construction," but neither counts as data synthesis]
1) Random time-window sampling during online annotation — the same source clip is sampled into different 5-second windows at different epochs/steps and paired with different on-the-fly annotations, which objectively serves as data augmentation, but what is augmented is "window position + annotation," not synthesized new content;
2) Independent Noise Sampling Strategy (INSS) — diffusion noise is sampled independently for the video and audio modalities to avoid spurious cross-modal correlation from a shared PRNG state. This is a noise-construction technique on the training side and does not involve content synthesis. Ablation shows its impact is significant (removing INSS worsens FD from 1.25 to 1.43, KL from 2.70 to 3.51, CLAP drops from 0.16 to 0.11, and LSE-C drops from 1.34 to 0.99), indicating that cross-modal noise decorrelation is critical for both audio quality and lip sync.

### [Unison](../models/Unison.md) ⚠️

The paper does not construct any synthetic data: no controlled-perturbation-constructed training pairs, no edit-style data augmentation (such as InstructAV2AV-style audio-video editing instruction pairs), no TTS-synthesized speech, no sound-effect overlay/mix synthesis, no self-distillation using data self-produced by a generative model, and no video re-rendering or face-swap-type construction. All training data consists of real captured native audio-video and audio.
[The two operations closest to "construction," but neither counts as data synthesis]
1) Mel-RoFormer source separation — decomposes the real mixed audio track into two streams, speech and sound effects, as the ground truth for dual-stream supervision. This is decomposition of real data rather than synthesis; the two resulting signals are both components of real audio and contain no generated content. But it does create a form of supervision signal that does not exist in the original data — the original data only has a mixed audio track, and the "pure speech track" and "pure sound-effects track" are products of the separation model, so the quality ceiling of these two ground-truth streams is bounded by Mel-RoFormer's separation capability, and separation residue (e.g., sound-effect leakage into the speech track) becomes training noise. The paper does not discuss the effect of separation error on dual-stream supervision, nor does it give a quantitative assessment of separation quality — this is a potential weak point of the dual-stream architecture. [Uncertain]
2) Asynchronous noise sampling for cross-modal forcing — timesteps are sampled independently for video and audio, constructing a training configuration where "one modality is clean and the other is noisy," with the student modality dynamically designated. This is a noise-scheduling construction on the training side and does not involve content synthesis, but it does artificially manufacture a "noise mismatch" training scenario that does not exist in the original data, functionally similar to a form of data augmentation. Ablation demonstrates its significant value: removing CMFS worsens DS from 0.08 to 0.19 (the worst across all ablations), drops LSE-C from 3.30 to 3.02, and lowers VA from 4.02 to 3.91.
[A phenomenon worth recording] Removing CMFS harms not only alignment but also video quality (VA 4.02→3.91); the paper interprets this as "the video branch benefits from audio-stream guidance to reinforce visual coherence" — i.e., even with the video backbone frozen, the audio signal can still improve the coherence of the visual output in reverse, via the fusion module. This is direct evidence of the value of cross-modal joint training.

### [Veo 3 / Veo 3.1](../models/Veo_3_Veo_31.md) ⚠️

The confirmed synthesis is limited to the text side: synthetic captions are generated by Gemini, used to expand the diversity of concepts associated with videos. [Uncertain] No synthetic data construction on the visual/audio side is disclosed; there is no mention of generating training pairs through controlled perturbation, edit pairs, or instruction-style audio-video editing pairs (such as InstructAV2AV-style construction). Veo 3.1 has editing capabilities such as Insert/Remove object insertion and removal, first-last frame transitions, outpainting, and style reference; such capabilities typically require paired before/after editing training samples, so a synthetic construction pipeline very likely exists, but the official materials give no explanation at all.

### [Vidu S1](../models/Vidu_S1.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain]. The paper does not mention using synthetic data or constructing training pairs via controlled perturbation/editing. The "artificial supervision" on the training side is embodied in the training strategy rather than data construction: stage two uses a mixed Teacher Forcing and Diffusion Forcing strategy for causal adaptation, and stage three uses DMD (Distribution Matching Distillation) + PCM (Phased Consistency Models) regularization for few-step distillation, with a bidirectional teacher model providing distribution-matching supervision for the student.

### [Wan 2.5 / 2.6 / 2.7](../models/Wan.md) ⚠️

Wan 2.1 explicitly uses synthetic data and gives two specific cases of considerable scale; however, there is no InstructAV2AV-style "controlled-perturbation-constructed audio-video editing training pair."
1) Visual-text synthesis (hundreds-of-millions scale): Chinese characters are rendered on a plain white background, synthesizing "hundreds of millions" of text-containing images. This is used together with real-world text-containing images (recognized in Chinese and English via multiple OCR models and then sent to Qwen2-VL to generate descriptions containing precise textual content) as two branches mixed during the pretraining stage. The report states that this combination lets the model generate rare characters in video with accurate glyph shapes and high realism, giving it a significant lead in visual-text generation.
2) Facial-diversity synthesis (O(1)M scale): O(1)M clips are randomly drawn from O(10)M personalization videos, and Instant-ID is used to synthesize a diverse face for each; a text-template library of 100+ prompts is built (styles such as anime, line drawing, cinematic look, Minecraft, etc.); each time, one prompt is randomly drawn together with a random human pose estimated from other videos, used as Instant-ID input; after generation, ArcFace similarity is used to filter out low-quality samples. The report states this substantially increases the diversity of the personalization dataset in style, pose, lighting, and occlusion.
3) Reverse constraint: a synthetic-image detection expert classifier actively removes AI-generated images of unknown provenance, because as little as <10% contamination significantly degrades performance — i.e., "in-house controllable synthetic data is usable, but wild AI-generated images must be purged." This distinction is a very clear line in Wan's data philosophy.
Whether 2.5/2.6/2.7 use synthetic audio-video pairs (such as controlled dubbing-mismatch-constructed negative samples) is unknown. [Uncertain]

### [Audio-video generation evaluation benchmark collection](../models/av_benchmarks.md) ⚠️

This field covers the core methodology of AVBench and AV-SyncBench, which is highly valuable for constructing controlled data pairs on the training side:
[AVBench's hard negative synthesis] 30K real clips are drawn from OpenHumanVid as positive examples, and through LLM-driven perturbation + algorithmic mismatching, expanded per dimension to 100K pairs each, for a total of 300K supervision samples. Five hard-negative types are explicitly enumerated: ① temporal shift (0.2–3.0 seconds); ② acoustic corruption (pitch shift, speed change); ③ speaker-identity mismatch; ④ emotional-polarity reversal; ⑤ state transitions. This recipe of "real positive examples + five types of controllable degraded negatives" can be directly reused for training an evaluator or a data filter.
[AV-SyncBench's dual-axis perturbation synthesis] Three perturbations on the temporal axis: global offset (50–500 ms, five levels), local jitter (30–700 ms, three severity levels), global speed change (0.8×–1.25×, ten levels); two perturbations on the semantic axis: voice-timbre substitution (OpenVoice V2) and instrument-timbre transfer (pretrained DDSP); the key design is that the semantic perturbations are forced to preserve temporal invariance — the edited audio and the original audio are exactly identical in timing, with only the semantic attribute changed, thereby achieving strict decoupling of the two axes. This is a textbook example of "orthogonal perturbation constructing orthogonal evaluation dimensions."
[PhyAVBench's single-variable pairing] 337 groups of paired prompts, each group differing in only a single physical variable while all other conditions remain unchanged — this is controlled-variable synthesis on the text side; the corresponding real videos are actual footage rather than synthesized.
[VABench] Prompts are LLM-synthesized, but the audio-visual content is entirely generated by the model under test, with no manual degradation construction [uncertain whether it includes negative-sample design].
[Omni-Judge] Does not construct synthetic data.

### [Video caption model ecosystem](../models/caption_models.md)

[Synthetic caption data (the most common form of synthesis in this ecosystem)]
· Teacher distillation is itself synthesis: captions generated by GPT-4V/Gemini all count as synthetic annotation — this applies to ShareGPT4Video-40K, the Koala-36M seed set, AVoCaDO-SFT-107K, and Script-a-Video's 500K MTSS.
· Two-stage synthesis (AVoCaDO): Gemini-2.5-Pro first generates a visual caption and an audio caption separately, then feeds both captions plus the original video back into the same model to synthesize a temporally coherent multimodal caption — this "decompose first, then synthesize" two-stage design significantly outperforms one-shot joint generation.
· Three parallel syntheses (AVSCap-130K): each video produces three separate annotations — visual / audio / synergistic — for use with different training objectives.
· Bootstrapped synthesis: video-SALMONN 2 uses its own produced high-quality caption corpus for SFT of subsequent models.
· Preference-data synthesis: Tarsier2 uses model-based sampling to automatically construct preference pairs for DPO; AVoCaDO/AVSCap's GRPO reward itself synthesizes preference signal online.
[Synthetic training samples (controlled-perturbation/edit-constructed training pairs)]
· SkyCaptioner-V1's camera-motion sub-expert uses 16,000 "motion-axis-balanced synthetic data" entries to fill in long-tail camera-movement types — this is controlled synthesis to correct the annotator's own class imbalance, and is the clearest synthetic-data use case in this ecosystem.
· InstructAV2AV uses Qwen3-Omni to generate editing instructions plus Grounded-SAM-2's mask-guided synthesis to construct "source-instruction-target" triplets (InsAVE-80K) — an instance of a captioner being used for synthetic data construction rather than video description.
· Movie Gen's cross-paired synthetic data (PT2V): slightly lowers ArcFace identity similarity, but gains significantly more diverse head poses and more natural expressions in exchange.
[Methodological hazards of synthetic data] InstructAV2AV uses the same Qwen3-Omni to both generate the instructions and perform five-dimensional verification scoring; the paper itself acknowledges that "generation and acceptance sharing the same source is a methodological hazard of this pipeline"; AVSCap is both the benchmark's author and its top scorer, so the risk of same-source overfitting needs cross-validation against third-party benchmarks.

### [Geometric / structured annotation dataset collection](../models/geometric_datasets.md)

WildWorld is a representative case of purely synthetic data construction: all 108 million frames are produced by real-time rendering from a game engine, using a self-built capture platform (OBS Studio + Reshade split-screen sub-window + multi-source timestamp synchronization) to record RGB and depth simultaneously; actions and states can be actively controlled to construct controlled-perturbation samples — changing the action ID in the same scene generates a paired counterfactual sequence, a form of supervision that real video simply cannot provide. The other three are all automatic annotations of real video and do not construct synthetic training pairs: SceneScribe-1M and SpatialVID generate pseudo ground-truth (pseudo-GT) annotations via geometric reconstruction models, which counts as "model annotation" rather than "data synthesis"; Action100M expands supervision via VLM pseudo-labeling (with a comparative ablation against direct pseudo-labeling by PLM-3B), likewise not involving video synthesis.

### [Post-training data strategy for video generation](../models/post_training_data.md)

There are two entirely different paths for "synthetic data" in the post-training stage, and this entry needs to distinguish them strictly:
[Path one: controlled distortion constructing negative samples (automated preference pairs)] SkyReels-V2's "semi-automatic data production pipeline" is the most complete publicly documented case — the automated side generates corrupted samples by applying controlled distortion to real video, which naturally forms preference pairs with the real video, covering the three variants V2V, I2V, and T2V, complementing the 30,000 manually annotated sample pairs. The advantage of this path is zero annotation cost and controllable negative samples; the disadvantage is that the distortion types may not match the model's actual real-world failure modes.
[Path two: model self-sampling constructing candidate groups (online/offline preference pairs)] The anchor paper's GRPO belongs to the online form of this path: given a prompt c, N trajectories are sampled from the reference policy to form a group, and within-group normalized advantage A_i = (R_i − mean) / std is used for relative comparison, requiring no pre-annotated preference pairs. Cosmos-Predict 2.5 is likewise an online form (8 rollouts × 20 diffusion steps per condition, within-group normalization, 256 training steps, batch 32). The offline form is represented by Step-Video-T2V (generating multiple videos per prompt with different random seeds, then human annotation), Kling 3.0 Omni (multiple variants under the same MVL condition + human comparison), and JavisDiT++ (3 generations + 1 ground truth per prompt, 4 candidates total).
[Synthetic data in the anchor paper's reward-model training] Stage 2 explicitly "leverages unlabeled data produced by models of different capability levels and different RL iterations" to train the reward model — i.e., intermediate products from the model's own evolution process are used as extra training material for the RM. This is an extremely low-cost, distribution-matched source of synthetic data, and is the same kind of approach as Seedance 1.0's "preference data covering synthetic videos generated at different stages of the model."
[Self-generated data on the distillation side] In the anchor paper's Phase 4 Self-Forcing distillation, each frame performs autoregressive rollout based on previously self-generated output (with KV cache), and the DMD loss is applied at the video level — the training data is the student model's own rollout, which is purely self-generated data. StreamChar's two-stage distillation Stage II (400 steps, student lr 2e-6, fake score network lr 4e-7) likewise uses online chunk rollout.

### [Merged survey of mainstream video pretraining datasets: Panda-70M, InternVid, Koala…](../models/pretraining_datasets.md)

**All seven do not use model-synthesized video data**; the training video is 100% real, either captured from the web or sourced from stock-footage sites. The "constructed data" that does exist is limited to the text side and the annotation side:
- **Koala-36M (the only self-supervised data construction, and the most worth reusing)**: to train the Color-Struct SVM transition detector, **rules are used to construct positive and negative sample pairs — frame pairs within the same video are negative examples (no transition), while frame pairs from different video sources are positive examples (a transition)**. This treats "splicing different videos together" as a synthetic proxy for a transition, at extremely low construction cost and requiring no manual annotation at all.
- **LVD-2M (multi-track rewriting at the caption level)**: the same video retains three caption tracks — LLaVA-v1.6-34B's `raw_caption` (215.4 words), Claude3-Haiku's refined `refined_caption` (81.8 words), and LLaMA-v3.1-70B's rewritten `rewritten_caption` (41.3 words). **This effectively synthesizes long/medium/short prompt distributions for the same video**, directly usable to mitigate training-inference prompt mismatch, and is the only dataset among the seven that provides multi-granularity prompt pairing (and the third track is not mentioned in the paper — it exists only in the released data).
- **MiraData**: when GPT-4V performs annotation, Panda-70M's short captions are injected as a hint — this is cascaded generation on the text side, not data synthesis.
- **UltraVideo**: Qwen3-4B consolidates 9 sub-captions into a 10th summary description, which is text-side synthesis; separately, an LLM **generates retrieval keywords** from 108 topics for subsequent manual search, which is a data-recall strategy rather than data synthesis.
- **Panda-70M**: teacher captions are selected via UMT and then used to distill a student model — this is knowledge distillation, not data synthesis.
- **InternVid / OpenVid-1M**: no data construction step of any kind.
**Common gap**: none of the seven **has controlled-perturbation/edit-constructed training pairs** (such as InstructAV2AV-style before/after editing pairs), nor any data rendered by a synthetic engine (Unity/UE/Blender) — MiraData's "3D engine-rendered scenes" category consists of **rendered footage collected from others on YouTube**, not an in-house rendering pipeline.
## Degree of human involvement (manual annotation, manual quality control, model pre-screening + manual review)

`human_in_loop` · Detail level: brief

### [Allegro](../models/Allegro.md)

The data pipeline is fully automated, with no manual annotation or manual quality-control step: from scene splitting, through the 7-level filtering, to caption generation, everything is done by tools and models — the paper does not mention any manual review, spot-checking, or annotation team.
Human involvement appears only in the evaluation stage: the user study uses 46 prompts covering diverse scenarios and six evaluation dimensions (video–text relevance, appearance distortion, appearance aesthetics, naturalness of motion, motion magnitude, overall quality), with each video pair scored by 2 annotators, totaling 5,448 ratings. In addition, the VBench quantitative evaluation uses 946 prompts to generate 4,730 videos.

### [Apollo](../models/Apollo.md) ⚠️

The degree of human involvement is extremely low and disclosed only vaguely; the sole trace of manual work in the entire pipeline appears at the tail end of training rather than in the data pipeline:
【Data pipeline】Fully automated. The paper explicitly positions it as "a novel automated data-construction pipeline," and all 81M samples are "automatically annotated samples." How the filtering thresholds were determined (whether, as in MOVA, humans spot-checked the retained samples under different cutoffs to calibrate them) is not stated; whether the annotation results underwent manual spot-check verification is not stated; no manual annotation, manual quality control, or "model pre-screening + manual review" step is mentioned anywhere.
【Training tail end】Stage III uses "the manually-curated, high-quality dataset" for the final fine-tuning — this is the only mention of human involvement in the entire paper, but the size of this dataset, the manual curation criteria, the number of people involved, and the annotation guidelines are all undisclosed.
【Conclusion】Apollo's positioning is a two-stage design of "automated pipeline for scale, manual curation for the final refinement," with human effort concentrated on the last mile — but every detail of that last mile is a black box. [Uncertain]

### [CineDance / CineDance-1M](../models/CineDance.md) ⚠️

Human involvement is positioned as "threshold calibration + quality spot-checking + benchmark construction," and does not participate in large-scale annotation production (annotation is done entirely by large models):
【① Threshold calibration】Through a human reference set, the empirical minimum duration for narrative completeness was measured at 18.4 seconds, and the anti-fragmentation soft threshold was set at 20 seconds accordingly — a typical case of human priors directly determining a pipeline parameter.
【② Manual artifact audit】500 clips were randomly sampled, and three independent annotators separately inspected them for residual artifacts (burned-in subtitles, logos, watermarks, TV-network overlays, title cards, end-credit text, screen recordings, transition effects, freeze frames, etc.); disagreements were adjudicated through joint review, yielding a 2.8% non-compliance rate, contrasted against Koala-36M's 37.4%.
【③ Manually-annotated benchmark】A small benchmark of 100 manually annotated clips was built to evaluate ASR-to-Character binding accuracy (95.4%) against various diarization baselines.
【④ CineBench human evaluation】Each video is scored by 10 independent evaluators using a 5-point Likert scale (1 = unusable, 5 = excellent), double-blind with randomized presentation order, and Spearman rank correlation is then used to verify the consistency between automatic metrics and human judgment.
【Pattern summary】This follows the modern paradigm of "full annotation by a large model + small-sample manual verification and alignment," with manual cost concentrated on the verification side rather than the production side. The recruitment method, total headcount, and compensation of the human annotators are undisclosed. [Uncertain]

### [CogVideoX](../models/CogVideoX.md) ⚠️

Human involvement is concentrated at a single point — "seed annotation for training the filter" — and the overall approach follows the classic paradigm of "small-scale manual annotation → train a model → fully automatic large-scale screening":
· Filter seed annotation: humans manually sampled and labeled 20,000 videos with positive/negative quality labels (covering 6 categories of negative labels), of which a random 10% was held out as a test set to report classifier accuracy. This is the only clearly defined manual annotation step in the entire pipeline.
· The negative-label taxonomy itself was designed by humans (the paper lists 6 category definitions and gives example videos for each in Figure 16).
· No human involvement in the labeling stage: captions are all generated automatically by the model chain, with no mention of manual refinement or review (contrast with Movie Gen, where annotators rewrite captions one by one during the SFT stage).
· Whether the selection of the high-quality fine-tuning subset (20%) was based on automatic scores or manual screening is unstated [uncertain]; from the phrasing it is inferred to be automatic threshold-based screening.
· There is heavy manual involvement on the evaluation side: 100 carefully designed prompts, with human reviewers scoring sub-items on a 0–1 scale and an overall score on a 0–5 scale, with the rule that "if instructions are not followed, the overall score cannot exceed 2"; evaluation dimensions are Sensory Quality, Instruction Following, Physics Simulation, cover-image quality, etc., each with detailed three-tier (1 / 0.5 / 0) scoring rubrics (Appendix J). The acknowledgments specifically thank the data annotators.

### [Cosmos-Predict2.5](../models/Cosmos-Predict25.md) ⚠️

The data-cleaning stage is fully automated; human involvement is concentrated in evaluation and post-training model selection, following a "humans stay out of the data flow but inside the decision loop" pattern.
【Data side: almost no human involvement】The seven-stage pipeline is entirely driven by models and rules, with no description of manual annotation or manual review. The only indirect trace of human involvement is that the robotics data retains human annotations already present in the original source dataset — GR00T's "human-labeled success ratings" are injected into the caption prompt; and the 100 human teleoperation demonstrations used for policy learning were collected by humans.
【Manual screening on the post-training data side】The paper's introduction explicitly states they "manually curate specialized post-training data tailored for Physical AI" — but does not describe the rules, workload, or team size behind this manual curation.
