【Evaluation and model-selection side: extensive human involvement】(1) Each SFT domain has a domain-specific test set with a human preference study, tallied as SFT Win / Base Win / Tie; (2) Model merging: quality assessment is first performed on "a small, hand-picked set of challenging examples" to select the best model from 20+ candidate merges, then verified on a larger evaluation set using human preference voting; (3) Human voting comparisons are done before and after RL; (4) Comparisons against Wan2.1/2.2 also use human win ratios, with annotators making pairwise comparisons along four criteria: realism, visual quality, temporal consistency, and alignment with conditioning inputs.
The number of annotators, the number of evaluations per sample, and consistency metrics are all undisclosed. [Uncertain: scale and protocol of the human-evaluation annotator pool]

### [Data-Juicer 2.0](../models/Data-Juicer.md) ⚠️

Data-Juicer's design philosophy is to "minimize human involvement as much as possible, replacing human judgment with model feedback," confining the human role to two areas.
【Human-involvement point one: final arbitration of recipe design and threshold decisions】DJ does not preset any default thresholds; all operator hyperparameters are externalized to YAML. But the core contribution of Sandbox is precisely to transform this step from "humans setting thresholds by experience" into "automatic ranking via feedback from small-scale reference-model training + benchmark evaluation" — humans only need to specify the candidate operator set and the evaluation benchmark, while which segment of the distribution is retained is decided by experimental data. This is the only work in this survey that systematizes and automates the threshold-determination process.
【Human-involvement point two: interactive operations and visual review】The interface layer provides multi-level human-machine interaction channels: a low-level Python API (for engineers to program against), RESTful endpoints (for service-based invocation), a visual editor (Alibaba Cloud PAI Designer component, drag-and-drop configuration of data-processing pipelines), and natural-language conversational instructions based on an AgentScope agent (describing data-processing needs in one sentence). The "Q&A Copilot" introduced in v1.4.6 further lowers the barrier to use. DJ also provides data-analysis reports and a tracker (Tracer, with Ray-mode support since v1.4.6) that lets humans see exactly which samples each operator actually removed — this is supporting infrastructure for manual sample-based quality control, but the quality control itself is carried out by the humans themselves.
【Large-scale crowd participation】The DJ 2.0 paper mentions that it has supported data filtering/synthesis competitions involving 3,000+ teams, a crowdsourced form of recipe exploration, but not sample-by-sample manual annotation.
【No manual annotation step】All of DJ's annotation capability is provided by model operators; it contains no manual annotation workflow, annotation-platform integration, or annotation quality-spot-check mechanism.
[Uncertain] The official T2V case study does not disclose whether there is a manual review step; DJ also provides no annotation-consistency testing or human-vs-model annotation comparison quality-assurance tools.

### [Foley-Omni](../models/Foley-Omni.md) ⚠️

The degree of human involvement is low and concentrated in two places — threshold calibration and evaluation-set gatekeeping — with training-data annotation being fully automatic.
【Threshold calibration】The −35 dB RMS energy gate for acoustic post-hoc verification was determined through "manual inspection of a small validation subset." This is a typical "human calibrates, machine executes" pattern — humans only participate in choosing the hyperparameter, not in sample-by-sample judgment. The paper does not state the basis for determining the other six thresholds in Table 7 (480p, 1 Mbps, [0.1, 3.2], 0.6, 0.3, 0.2); it is presumed these likewise involve some element of empirical manual tuning.
【Manual review of the evaluation set】The 300 clips of the V2ST-Bench underwent manual review, with the review dimensions explicitly stated as three: audiovisual consistency, annotation accuracy, and suitability for mixed soundtrack evaluation. This falls under the "model pre-screening + manual review" pattern, but is applied only to the 300 evaluation samples — an extremely small scale.
【Subjective evaluation】Three subjective opinion scores — A-MOS / S-MOS / T-MOS — are used, requiring human listening tests. [Uncertain] The number of evaluators, recruitment method, scoring guidelines, and consistency checks are not stated.
【Training data】Approximately 2.0M training annotations are all produced automatically by Gemini + Bandit, with no manual review step, and no accuracy report from sample spot-checks is mentioned either.

### [Goku](../models/Goku.md) ⚠️

The paper **does not mention any manual annotation, manual quality control, or manual review step**; the entire five-stage pipeline is presented as fully automatic, driven purely by models and thresholds (PySceneDetect + DINOv2 + aesthetics model + OCR + RAFT + classification model + VLM/LLM labeling).
Possible but undisclosed points of human involvement include: the design and training-annotation of the internal video-classification model's taxonomy of 9 major categories / 86 subcategories, the criteria for judging "high quality" among the 60 million internal high-quality images, and the process for determining the various thresholds (4.3/4.5, 0.85/0.90, 0.02/0.01, motion-score upper/lower bounds) — the selection of these thresholds almost certainly involved manual trial-and-error and spot-checking, but the paper does not elaborate. In addition, the paper's subjective quality comparisons (Figure 5, etc.) involve human judgment, but this belongs to evaluation rather than the data pipeline. [Uncertain] (the actual extent of human effort)

### [Hailuo / MiniMax Video](../models/Hailuo.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain] (no disclosure at all). It is not stated whether there is manual annotation, manual quality control, or a "model pre-screening + manual review" step on the data side, nor is the size of any annotation team disclosed. There is a clear human-feedback channel on the product side: the Hailuo AI (海螺AI) and Hailuo AI web/app provide user ratings and reporting entry points for generated results, and the Media Agent supports manual adjustment, which in principle could accumulate as preference data — but the vendor does not state whether this feedback flows back into training.

### [HunyuanVideo-Foley](../models/HunyuanVideo-Foley.md) ⚠️

The degree of human involvement is close to zero — the paper's abstract explicitly characterizes the pipeline as being built "through automated annotation":
【Training data side is fully automatic】The seven-level cleaning funnel (audio-track detection → scene splitting → silence-ratio check → bandwidth detection → AudioBox+SNR → ImageBind+AV-align → classification labeling) and caption generation (GenAU) are entirely automatic judgments made by models and thresholds; the paper mentions no manual annotation, manual spot-checking, or manual review step, nor does it explain how the various thresholds were calibrated (no manual verification, no ablation study mentioned). [Uncertain: threshold-calibration method]
【Inevitability of scale】100,000 hours amounts to roughly 45 million 8-second clips; even at 5 seconds of manual listening per clip, this would require about 60,000 person-hours, making full manual involvement economically infeasible. This is consistent with its positioning as a "scalable pipeline" — the very premise of scalability is the absence of a manual bottleneck.
【The only human involvement is on the evaluation side】The subjective-evaluation stage uses human listening tests, producing three subjective opinion scores — MOS-Q (audio quality), MOS-S (semantic alignment), MOS-T (temporal alignment) — reaching 4.14±0.68, 4.12±0.77, and 4.15±0.75 respectively (on a 5-point scale) on MovieGen-Audio-Bench. The paper does not disclose the number of evaluators, the recruitment method, the listening environment (whether monitoring headphones were used), or the scoring guidelines — all of which are fairly critical to the credibility of the MOS. [Uncertain]
【Resource-allocation orientation】As with UniVerse-1, this follows the allocation pattern of "fully automatic training data, semi-manual evaluation," with human effort concentrated on validating results rather than assuring data quality.

### [HunyuanVideo](../models/HunyuanVideo.md)

Both generations place human effort at the very end of the funnel (model pre-screening + manual curation) rather than in full-pipeline annotation:
【Original version】The SFT dataset of about 1 million samples was entirely selected through manual annotation, with annotators judging along two broad categories comprising seven dimensions: aesthetics side — color harmony, lighting quality, object emphasis, spatial layout; motion side — motion speed, action integrity, motion blur. The goal was to select clips that are "visually beautiful with rich motion detail."
【1.5】Human involvement is distributed across three places: (1) the SFT dataset is ultimately built through manual annotation; (2) for I2V RLHF, prompts and accompanying images are manually verified for text-image consistency; (3) for T2V DPO, preference pairs are manually labeled via GSB (Good/Same/Bad); (4) in the evaluation stage, "100+ professional evaluators" performed GSB human evaluation on 300 text prompts and 300 image samples. The size of the annotation team and the person-hour cost are not disclosed.

### [InstructAV2AV](../models/InstructAV2AV.md) ⚠️

The degree of human involvement is low and highly focused, concentrated in evaluation-set gatekeeping and the user study; the construction and acceptance of the 79K training data is fully automatic.

【Manual acceptance of the evaluation set (the only step where humans are involved in training/evaluation data construction)】The organizational method is disclosed in reasonable detail: 20 volunteers were organized into 10 judge pairs, and each candidate sample was assessed by 5 independent judge pairs, with each judge pair responsible for only one of the five criteria. A sample had to pass all five criteria to enter the 1K evaluation set.
  Two aspects of this design are worth recording: (1) the "one judge pair looks at only one dimension" per-dimension independent review avoids the halo effect and cross-dimension contamination that occurs when a single person scores across multiple dimensions, which is more rigorous than the common practice of "one person scoring all dimensions"; (2) using a "pair" rather than a single person as the review unit implicitly builds in a two-person internal-consistency check.
  [Uncertain] It is not stated how disagreements within a judge pair were resolved, inter-rater agreement / Cohen's kappa is not reported, the background and training of the volunteers is not stated, and how many candidates the final 1K was screened from is not reported.

【User study (evaluation stage, not data construction)】25 volunteers, with 20 samples randomly drawn from each dataset, made preference choices along three dimensions: audiovisual synchrony (AVS), text alignment (TA), and overall preference (OP). Results: on InsAVE-80K, AVS 49.00% / TA 45.40% / OP 46.60%; on AvED-Bench, AVS 46.80% / TA 41.80% / OP 43.60% — the highest preference rate in each four-way comparison (if the four methods were evenly split, each would get about 25%, so 40%+ represents a significant lead). [Uncertain] It is not stated how volunteers were recruited, whether they overlapped with the 20 people used for evaluation-set acceptance, the scoring guidelines, or consistency checks.

【Zero human involvement in training data】The 79K training pairs are entirely accepted automatically by Qwen3-Omni, with no manual review, no sample spot-checking, and no reported agreement rate between automatic acceptance and human judgment. This is the price of scalability, and a necessary consequence of the "scalable data synthesis pipeline" positioning — but the absence of a cross-validation experiment "comparing automatic and manual conclusions on a 1K manually-labeled set" leaves the reliability of automatic acceptance unquantified, a regrettable methodological gap.

【The role of humans in threshold calibration】[Uncertain] The basis for determining the CoTracker3 motion threshold, the LAION aesthetic threshold, the Audiobox threshold, and the MLLM scoring gate is not stated; it is presumed there is some element of empirical manual tuning, but the paper does not explicitly state, as Foley-Omni does, that these were "determined by manual inspection of a small validation subset."

### [Other Joint Audio-Video Generation Works, 2026](../models/JAVG_2026_misc.md) ⚠️

The degree of human involvement varies markedly across this batch of work; ALIVE is the only one with substantial manual annotation investment:
【ALIVE — two types of manual annotation + human evaluation (the heaviest investment in this batch)】
(1) Manually revised captions: the SFT training data for the caption model is "manually revised caption data," undergoing two rounds of SFT. This is a standard paradigm of "initial labeling by a large model + manual review and revision + distillation into a dedicated model" — a one-time human cost that scales in benefit.
(2) Manual annotation of sub-motion-unit timestamps: "decomposing sub-motion units to annotate the start and end timestamps of each complete small action" — actions are decomposed into sub-motion units, and humans annotate the start and end timestamps of each complete small action. This is the most expensive manual annotation item in this batch (the per-unit cost of fine-grained temporal annotation is far higher than that of attaching descriptive labels), indicating the team places great importance on temporal precision.
(3) The scale of both types of annotation (number of annotations, number of annotators, cost) is not quantified [uncertain].
(4) Human evaluation: blind side-by-side comparisons against Veo 3.1, Kling 2.6, Wan 2.6, Sora 2, and LTX-2; Alive-Bench 1.0's 264 general prompts + 90 reference-character prompts and 22 fine-grained metrics (six major categories) presumably include elements of manual design and judgment [judging-method details uncertain].
【StreamChar — human evaluation clearly quantified】"24 participants, each presented with 50 randomly sampled cases" — 24 participants, each evaluating 50 randomly sampled cases. This is the only work in this batch that gives both the number of human-evaluation participants and the sample count. No manual annotation on the data side [uncertain].
【OmniCustom】The data pipeline is fully automatic (SyncNet + aesthetic score + three duration rules + GLM-ASR), with no manual annotation step. There is human involvement in constructing the evaluation set: 100 carefully selected examples (30 real people outside the training set + 70 YouTube video clips), with a controlled 1:1 gender ratio — this 1:1 ratio must be the result of manual screening.
【NAVA】The data pipeline is highly automated (relying entirely on OCR / VideoCLIP / various scorers / VLM / LLM), with no description of manual annotation [uncertain]. The 160K high-quality SFT subset is automatically screened out by "multi-operator collaborative filtering" rather than manual selection. The evaluation includes user studies assessing video quality, audiovisual synchrony, audio fidelity, and timbre controllability across four dimensions; the scale of participants is not stated [uncertain].
【CCL / Baton】No description of human involvement [uncertain].
【ITS-JAVG】No training data, hence no annotation; its core methodology is precisely "replacing human judgment with model verifiers" — a combination of multiple verifiers + ARW adaptive weighting, essentially an automated approximation of human preference. But the paper also reveals the risk of this path (verifier hacking), indirectly demonstrating that human evaluation cannot be fully replaced.
【Pattern】The amount of human investment correlates positively with the level of detail in data disclosure: ALIVE discloses its data in the most detail and has the heaviest human investment; conversely, CCL/Baton disclose the least and describe no human involvement at all. This may reflect a consistency where "teams that value their data both invest in human effort and disclose it in detail."

### [Baseline Collection for Joint Audio-Video Generation](../models/JavisDiT_baselines.md) ⚠️

Human involvement is generally concentrated on the evaluation side rather than the data side, a common feature of this collection:
【MM-Diffusion (largest human investment on the evaluation side)】A large-scale Turing test was conducted, accumulating 10,000 human votes in total, with results showing that over 80% of generated audio-video clips fooled human evaluators. No manual annotation or quality-control step is described on the data side [uncertain], but the Landscape dataset's small scale of only 1,000 clips and its self-description as "high-fidelity" hint at possible manual screening.
【AV-DiT】No human involvement described [uncertain].
【JavisDiT / JavisDiT++ (clear human investment on both the data side and evaluation side — the most thorough in this collection)】
- Data side: when constructing JavisBench, all YouTube-sourced content underwent "rigorous manual legal and ethical review" — the only clearly stated manual review step on the data side in this collection.
- Metric-validation side: to validate the effectiveness of the JavisScore metric, a manually annotated evaluation dataset of 3,000 samples was built, using human judgments of synchrony to calibrate the metric. This approach of "first build the metric, then use manual annotation to validate the metric itself" is relatively rigorous in terms of evaluation methodology, but the number of annotators, recruitment method, and inter-annotator agreement are all undisclosed [uncertain].
- Model-evaluation side: JavisDiT++ uses 100 prompts from JavisBench, with 3 volunteers making blind win-tie-lose preference judgments.
【Harmony】Systematic human ablation studies were conducted to validate each component's contribution. On the data side's human involvement, its data sources include AudioCaps (about 128 hours) and Clotho (about 31 hours), which are manually annotated audio-caption datasets, while WavCaps (about 7,600 hours) is automatically annotated — the paper explicitly distinguishes the manual/automatic annotation attributes of these three, showing the team is deliberately conscious of stratifying annotation quality. The 4 million clips in their own dataset are annotated entirely by Gemini, with no description of manual review [uncertain].
【UniAVGen】No description of any human-involvement step [uncertain].
【Common observation】For all five works, the data pipeline is essentially fully automated, with human effort used mainly for evaluation and metric calibration rather than item-by-item data review — this contrasts with industrial-grade models (which often have dedicated data-annotation teams), and reflects the practical constraints of academic work.

### [Kling 3.0 / Kling Video 3.0 Omni](../models/Kling_30_Omni.md)

The basic form is "model-based automatic screening as the primary approach + human involvement at key junctures." Confirmed points of human involvement: in the post-training stage, DPO preference data has humans evaluate multiple video variants under the same MVL condition and give preference rankings (the Kling-Omni report explicitly states this is human-evaluated); on the data side, there is additionally "manually curated resources" supplementing the data sources. The same team's Kling-Foley Kling-Audio-Eval is entirely manually annotated/proofread: 20,935 samples, with annotators making corrections on top of model-pregenerated captions, and verifying whether labels belong to the predefined taxonomy and verifying audiovisual quality and alignment (valid-sample rules: foreground audio has no human voice, sound effects come from an object/action visible in the frame, video ≥5 seconds, sound effect ≥2 seconds, no copyrighted background music containing vocals). [Uncertain: whether the large-scale training data has a manual spot-check ratio, and the scale of manual annotation for the SFT curated set]

### [LTX-2](../models/LTX-2.md)

Human involvement is concentrated in building the quality-judging model, rather than in sample-by-sample annotation:
(1) Annotation of the aesthetic model: tens of thousands of image pairs were manually annotated for "which image is aesthetically superior," used to train a Siamese ranking network; upstream, a multi-label network first automatically labels millions of samples to constrain the range of pairwise sampling. This is a typical leveraged design of "manually label a small number of preference pairs → train an automatic scorer → fully automatic filtering at scale."
(2) Sample-by-sample captions: fully automatic, with the internal captioner re-captioning the entire training set; no manual review or spot-check process is mentioned.
(3) Evaluation stage: a human preference study is used to assess visual realism, audio fidelity, and temporal synchrony (lip sync, foley accuracy); at the LTX-Video stage, the human evaluation used 1,000 text-to-video prompts and 1,000 pairs of image-to-video samples.
Whether there is a manual fallback quality-control step in the data-cleaning stage is not disclosed.

### [LongCat-Video](../models/LongCat-Video.md) ⚠️

Human involvement on the training-data side is barely mentioned at all — the data pipeline is entirely automated model labeling + threshold filtering throughout, with no description of manual annotation or manual review steps.
Human involvement clearly exists at two downstream points:
(1) Evaluation: the internal T2V evaluation set has 500 prompts dedicated to human evaluation, using a dual-track approach of MOS (Mean Opinion Score) absolute scoring and GSB (Good-Same-Bad) relative comparison, with each video assessed by three independent annotators; the I2V evaluation set of 400 prompts likewise includes human evaluation.
(2) Reward-model training: both the Motion Quality and Text-Alignment reward models are "VideoAlign-based model[s] fine-tuned on internal annotated datasets/internally annotated data" — i.e., fine-tuned on internal manually annotated datasets, indicating an investment in manual preference/quality annotation, but the annotation volume, guidelines, annotator team size, and consistency metrics are all undisclosed.
This can be summarized as: humans do not participate in data cleaning, but human preferences are injected into training indirectly through "annotating the reward model." [Uncertain: the scale of the manually annotated data]

### [MOVA](../models/MOVA.md)

The degree of human involvement is very low; the annotation stage is fully automated, and humans appear only in "threshold calibration" and "final evaluation":
【Threshold calibration (the only manual involvement on the data side)】"we manually inspect the videos retained under different metric cutoffs and set reasonable thresholds for each dimension accordingly" — the researchers manually inspected the videos retained under different metric cutoffs, and set thresholds for the audio-quality, video-quality, and audiovisual-alignment dimensions accordingly. The appendix also reaffirms that these thresholds were "determined by empirical observation." This is a typical "human calibrates, machine executes at scale" pattern.
【Annotation stage】Zero human involvement. The three annotation tracks — visual, speech, and non-speech — and the final fusion are all completed by MiMo-VL / Qwen3-Omni ×2 / GPT-OSS-120B, with no description of manual review or spot-checking.
【Substantial human investment on the evaluation side】An Arena-style human-preference evaluation collected 5,000+ valid votes, over an evaluation set of 732 items (600 from Verse-Bench + 132 from a self-built benchmark), of which half of the originally English-only Verse-Bench speech data was manually translated into Chinese to construct a bilingual mixed set; evaluators had to make pairwise preference judgments along five dimensions — prompt adherence, audiovisual synchrony, lip-sync accuracy, video quality, and audio/speech fidelity — using ELO scoring (initial 1000, K=4, logistic scale 400, base 10, 1,000-time bootstrap).

### [Merged entry: ① Mochi 1](../models/Mochi_MAGI_Motif.md) ⚠️

① Mochi 1: no disclosure of any manual involvement on the data side. The evaluation side uses automated metrics — the official approach follows OpenAI DALL-E 3's prompt-adherence evaluation paradigm, using Gemini-1.5-Pro-002 as the judge model (i.e., replacing human evaluation with a large model). [Partially uncertain: whether there is manual quality control on the data side]
② MAGI-1: the data pipeline is fully automatic (PySceneDetect + 11-category Filter Actor + dual-model deduplication + MLLM filtering and labeling), with no mention of manual annotation or manual quality control. Human involvement is concentrated in the evaluation stage and is done quite thoroughly: the team built its own internal human-evaluation benchmark — designing a layered metric system (emphasizing completeness over conciseness, and requiring the metrics to be orthogonal to avoid redundancy), organized into four main dimensions — Overall / Motion Quality / Instruction Following / Visual Quality — each with its own sub-metrics; the benchmark dataset consists of 100 curated image–prompt pairs, deliberately diversified in source (real user inputs from existing video-generation platforms, FLUX-generated synthetic images, real photos from public image libraries, and professional film/TV footage); each sample is annotated with specific evaluation targets according to the metric framework; the evaluation uses strictly double-blind pairwise comparison (Win/Tie/Lose), with judges being "experts with well-trained aesthetic judgment." The conclusion is that the model is overall superior to the open-source Wan-2.1, clearly superior to Hailuo (i2v-01) and HunyuanVideo, and slightly inferior to the commercial model Kling1.6 (HD), with instruction following and motion quality being strengths while visual quality still lags.
③ Motif-Video 2B: the data pipeline is fully automatic, with no manual annotation/quality control. But there is one instance of "human in the loop" reflected in training scheduling rather than data annotation — the SFT corpus was assembled iteratively: run intermediate evaluations → have a human judge which subject categories are weakest → target additional data collection accordingly → re-evaluate. The paper also describes the entire training process as "a diagnostic loop rather than a single forward pass through a predefined schedule," and the mid-training insertion of the Shared Cross-Attention at Stage 9 was precisely because humans observed semantic-alignment regression at the 720p stage. The evaluation side relies mainly on the VBench automated metrics (Table 3, 16 dimensions), with no human evaluation reported.

### [Movie Gen](../models/Movie_Gen.md)

The pretraining stage is largely fully automatic (with humans used only to determine thresholds, e.g., the audio CAVTP threshold was "determined by manual inspection," and the aesthetics threshold was manually verified); the post-training stage, by contrast, involves heavy manual involvement following a "model pre-screening + manual review + manual re-labeling" paradigm:
· Of the four stages of video SFT, the latter two rely entirely on humans: in the third stage, humans judge cinematic quality (angled lighting/natural lighting or studio lighting, colors vivid but not oversaturated, uncluttered frame, non-trivial motion, no camera shake, no editing effects or overlaid text) — the paper explicitly states that "many aspects of high-quality fine-tuning data cannot be reliably captured by automated filters with high precision and recall"; in the same stage, annotators also manually trim videos to the target duration, actively picking out the most compelling, most engaging segment from the whole clip. In the fourth stage, humans correct erroneous details and fill in key information (camera control, character expressions, subject and background, detailed motion, lighting) on top of captions generated by LLaMa3-Video, and add annotations for 6 additional categories of shot types.
· Personalization SFT: starting from single-person videos selected from the T2V fine-tuning set, humans select videos with diverse actions to cover a variety of action behaviors, ultimately yielding O(1000) clips.
· Audio SFT: after automatic filtering by a cinematic-feel audio-video classifier + AED, the final selection is done via manual annotation.
· The evaluation side relies almost entirely on manual A/B testing (the paper argues automated metrics are unreliable); annotators underwent specialized training and were continuously audited; the same batch of 381 prompts was annotated four times to estimate the standard deviation of each evaluation dimension (overall quality 5.07%, frame consistency 4.08%, etc.), used to judge whether a win is statistically significant.

### [NVIDIA NeMo Curator](../models/NeMo_Curator.md) ⚠️

The framework is positioned as a fully automatic pipeline, with no built-in manual annotation/review interface or workflow; the official documentation describes no human-in-the-loop step (no annotation-platform integration, no spot-check-sampling stage, no manual-arbitration queue).
Human involvement actually occurs at two points outside the framework: (1) construction of discriminators — the text-overlay-detection MLP, video-type-taxonomy MLP, and motion-classification ViT within Cosmos WFM all require manually annotated training sets, but the annotation scale and process are undisclosed; (2) manual setting of thresholds — parameters such as the aesthetic threshold 3.5, the motion threshold 0.00098, the bottom 15% for DOVER, and k=10000 are set by engineers based on experience, with the framework providing only default values.
The framework makes an alternative design choice to reduce reliance on human effort through observability: filtered clips are moved into video.filtered_clips with updated statistics, performance metrics have been automatically logged since 26.02, resource and bottleneck monitoring panels are provided for each stage, and Pipeline.describe() supports development-time review — these let engineers audit the funnel after the fact rather than reviewing samples manually one by one. [Uncertain]

### [OmniHuman Dataset + OHBench](../models/OmniHuman.md) ⚠️

【Data side: the paper claims complete absence of human involvement】The pipeline is explicitly positioned as a "fully automated pipeline for high-quality data collection and multi-modal annotation," with full automation itself being one of the paper's claimed contributions. Four-level filtering, tracking and pose estimation, audio-visual attribution, hierarchical annotation, and consistency verification are all executed by models; the paper mentions no manual annotation, manual spot-checking, or manual review step.
【The quality fallback for automation relies on cross-validation rather than humans】Tracking results are used to verify the subject count from the MLLM, and ASR transcription is used to verify the caption's speech content (edit distance) — this is a design that substitutes "modal redundancy" for "manual review" (see model_as_data_judge for details). In principle this is more reliable than a fully automatic pipeline with no verification at all, but it still cannot cover dimensions that cannot be cross-validated, such as the accuracy of attribute descriptions, the correctness of interaction labels, or the consistency of emotion annotation.
【Human involvement in threshold calibration is unexplained】The paper says not a word about how the thresholds for the various filtering levels (CLIP aesthetics, DOVER, optical flow, SyncNet, 0.55 similarity, edit-distance boundary) were determined — whether they were calibrated via manual spot-checking (as MOVA explicitly describes, "manually inspecting the retained samples under different cutoffs") is unknown. Values like 0.55 and 5 frames, 3 frames look like empirical figures, but there is no recorded calibration process. [Uncertain]
【Evaluation side: subjective dimensions handed to Gemini-3 rather than humans】This is the most aggressive and most questionable choice regarding human involvement in this work. OHBench's core claim is that it "introduces metrics that are highly consistent with human perception," but the implementation uses Gemini-3 / Gemini-3-pro to give 1–10 scores, rather than organizing human evaluation.
【Key gap: insufficient evidence for human-consistency】In the main text, the paper's central claim of being "highly consistent with human perception" is only stated qualitatively (claiming that supplementary materials contain "evidence that our metric aligns better with human judgment"); the main text gives no user-study details at all — no number of raters, no rater background, no Spearman/Kendall correlation coefficients, no correlation figures compared against existing metrics like VBench, no inter-rater reliability (IRR). For an evaluation benchmark whose core selling point is "consistency with human perception," this is exactly the part that most needs to be quantified, yet is precisely what is missing — the biggest methodological weakness of this work.
【Point of comparison】MOVA built a 732-item bilingual (Chinese/English) Arena human-evaluation set; UniTalking conducted a blind preference study with 20 people × 50 prompts — both are limited in scale, but at least involve real human participation; OHBench presents no equivalent evidence in its main text.

### [Open-Sora series](../models/Open-Sora.md)

The degree of human involvement is very low, used mainly for **threshold calibration and sample verification** rather than large-scale annotation or review:
- 【Open-Sora Plan】The only clearly documented manual step is: to verify the reliability of the LPIPS motion-filtering threshold (0.001–0.3), **2,000 videos were manually inspected**, and only after confirming that the method had sufficient precision was it applied at full scale. This is a low-cost paradigm of using manual effort to "verify an automated rule."
- 【Open-Sora series】The documentation records no manual annotation or manual quality-control step; captions are all generated by a VLM with no manual review; the notes for tools/caption explicitly state that "manually annotating videos is expensive and time-consuming, so a powerful image/video description model is used to generate captions instead" — an explicit statement of deliberately forgoing manual annotation.
- Human involvement is more evident on the **evaluation side**: Open-Sora 2.0 used 100 prompts for human preference evaluation (comparing against HunyuanVideo, Runway Gen-3 Alpha, Step-Video-T2V, and Luma Ray2), scoring win rates along three dimensions — visual quality, prompt adherence, and motion quality.
- Both projects are essentially "fully automatic pipeline + minimal manual spot-check calibration," which is also the premise that lets them process tens of millions of videos at extremely low cost.

### [Ovi](../models/Ovi.md) ⚠️

[Uncertain: almost no description on the data side]. Confirmed human involvement is distributed at two ends:
(1) Data side: the acknowledgments mention Yi Cui, Manav Shah, and Diego De La Torre "for their contributions to data preparation," indicating dedicated staff handled data preparation, but whether their responsibilities were manual annotation, manual quality control, or engineering pipeline construction is not stated. The four steps of the data pipeline in the main text are all automated by models/rules, with no manual review step appearing; it can be judged that overall this is a "fully automatic pipeline, with no item-by-item manual review." The "extensive experiments" for the caption prompt represents human involvement at the template-design level.
(2) Evaluation side: human involvement is substantial and is the primary evaluation method — a blind pairwise preference study was organized with 50 participants, comparing Ovi against JavisDiT and UniVerse-1 across three dimensions — audio quality, video quality, and audiovisual synchrony — reporting Pairwise Win Rate (PWR).
No description of RLHF preference annotation, manual data-cleaning spot-check rates, etc. is found.

### [Script-a-Video](../models/Script-a-Video.md) ⚠️

The degree of human involvement is extremely low and unevenly distributed:
【Annotation stage: zero human involvement】The MTSS annotation for the 500K clips is entirely generated automatically by Gemini-2.5-Pro; the paper mentions no manual annotation, manual spot-checking, manual review, or annotation-guideline training step, and reports no annotation-consistency metric. For a structured schema that depends on cross-reference integrity, the lack of a manual or programmatic verification step is a clear gap. [Uncertain]
【Evaluation stage: fairly substantial manual investment】On the generation side, 20 professional raters were organized to manually score 225 generated videos (125 single-shot + 100 multi-shot) across five dimensions: Text Alignment, Visual Quality, Multi-Shot-Consistency, Identity Consistency, and Audio-Video Synchronization, all on a 1–3 scale.
【Methodological value of the human evaluation】The paper explicitly points out that human evaluation reveals the deceptiveness of automated metrics, a valuable observation in this work:
1) On the Intra-Shot SC metric, the baseline's 0.87 is higher than the full method's 0.59, but this is an illusion produced by the baseline generating near-static content;
2) On the A-V Sync (SyncNet) metric, the baseline's 6.86 "leads" the full method's 9.72, but the baseline's human-rated A-V score is only 1.18 — the paper explains this as an "information-sparsity artifact": it is trivially easy to synchronize flat environmental noise with the picture, whereas after generating complex dialogue the SyncNet score first rises (LTX-2-AV-MTSS reaches 13.86, with larger deviations being worse), and the full method pulls it back to 9.72 while the human score reaches 2.26. This analysis carries a direct warning for any team relying on SyncNet for data filtering: the SyncNet score has a systematic bias toward speech-free/flat-audio samples.
3) Reference ID Similarity drops to about 0.22 in multi-shot scenarios (due to drastic changes in viewpoint and lighting), but the human-rated Cons. score remains stable above 2.40 — automatic ID similarity is likewise unreliable in cross-shot scenarios.
【Annotation prompt】Not disclosed publicly, and it is not stated whether humans were involved in iterating on the prompt.

### [Seedance 2.0 and Seedance 1.5 pro](../models/Seedance_20_Seedance_15_pro.md) ⚠️

Human involvement runs through multiple stages and is explicitly mentioned: (1) for training the caption model, Seedance 1.0 collected data across diverse categories and performed "high-quality manual annotations"; (2) the SFT stage uses high-quality video-text pairs with "manually verified captions," collected in a targeted way across hundreds of categories, forming a Human-Curated Dataset; (3) the RLHF stage uses a multi-dimensional manual annotation protocol — selecting the best and worst videos under a specific dimension, while ensuring the best one is not inferior to the worst one on other dimensions; (4) the unified platform layer of the data-processing infrastructure explicitly supports "automating human-in-the-loop workflows," task management, data visualization, and pipeline monitoring; (5) Seedance 2.0 introduces expert reviewers from the advertising and game-production domains at the evaluation stage for subjective scoring and blind review, and also conducts an authenticity-discrimination study (reviewers distinguish generated videos from real footage), with results fed back into the aesthetic tuning process. The overall pattern is "model pre-screening + manual review/fine annotation." [Uncertain: the scale of manual annotation and the person-hours invested]

### [SkyReels series](../models/SkyReels.md)

The design of human involvement in the SkyReels series is relatively clear, adopting a leveraged strategy of "human-established criteria + low-ratio spot-checking":
【SkyReels-V2】
(1) Tiered spot-check rates (the most valuable quantitative disclosure in this entry): the pretraining stage has a manual spot-check rate of 0.01% (1 sample checked per 10,000), rising to 0.1% (1 per 1,000) in the post-training stage — quality-control intensity is configured differently by data stage;
(2) Human-in-the-loop validation covers every stage of the pipeline;
(3) Seed annotation for the labeling model: the motion-recognition captioner relies on 93,000 high-confidence manually annotated samples; the capability of SkyCaptioner-V1 is assessed via human evaluation (average accuracy 76.3%);
(4) Reinforcement-learning preference data: manual annotation produced "30,000 sample pairs" for Bradley-Terry reward modeling, mixed with automatically generated distorted samples;
(5) The concept-balanced taxonomy was manually defined.
【SkyReels-V4】
(1) The second stage of SFT uses "1 million manually curated high-quality videos" — human involvement is directly reflected in the construction of the final fine-tuning set;
(2) On the evaluation side, "50 professional evaluators with backgrounds in film production, audio engineering, and content creation" were organized to perform dual evaluation on 2,000+ prompts: 5-point absolute scoring and GSB pairwise comparison;
(3) The manual spot-check rate and annotation labor hours for the data-cleaning stage are not disclosed.

### [Sora 2](../models/Sora_2.md) ⚠️

Training-data annotation side: it is only known that data exists which "human trainers and researchers provide or generate," indicating human involvement in providing and generating data, but the scale, process, and whether there is manual quality control/review are undisclosed. Human involvement on the deployment side is explicitly described: collaborating with members of the external OpenAI Red Team Network to test (covering sexual content, nudity, extremism, self-harm, illegal behavior, graphic violence, political persuasion, as well as specialized policies on minor safety and likeness use); red-team feedback is used to adjust prompt filters, blocklists, and classifier thresholds; content moderation uses a combination of "automation + manual review" to identify abuse patterns, and in-app reporting and appeal channels are provided. [Uncertain]

### [SpeakerVid-5M](../models/SpeakerVid-5M.md) ⚠️

The degree of human involvement is low and concentrated at the very front end of the process, with all subsequent stages fully automated:
【Manual selection at the collection stage (the only clearly stated manual step)】The paper explicitly states this as "manually collected high-quality videos featuring two-person dialogue from YouTube" — i.e., high-quality two-person dialogue videos were manually selected from YouTube as source material (153K clips / 64,386 hours). This step determines the dataset's genre composition and overall quality baseline, and is the most critical human judgment, but the paper does not state the specific selection criteria, the number of people involved, or the pass rate of manual screening.
【Threshold setting】The thresholds for the five-dimensional quality filter (brightness 10/210, DOVER 0.25, minimum clarity 5%, blur score 0.1, ASR confidence −1.5, etc.) and the HQ subset threshold are all manually set empirical values, but the paper does not state, as MOVA does, whether they were calibrated via "manual spot-checking of the retained samples under different cutoffs." [Uncertain]
【Annotation stage】Fully automatic. Captions, topics, motion intensity, ASR, skeletons, and synchronization metrics are all produced by models, with no manual annotation, no manual review, and no two-stage "model pre-screening + manual acceptance" design.
【Quality assessment】The paper reports no manual accuracy assessment of the automatic annotation results (e.g., caption hallucination rate, diarization error rate, manual spot-check of speaking/listening judgment accuracy), leaving the annotation quality without independent verification. The multi-persona ensemble scoring can be viewed as a model-based substitute for human consensus, but is not equivalent to manual verification.
【Evaluation stage】VidChatBench uses fully automatic objective metrics (FID, FVD, PSNR, SSIM, ArcFace cosine distance, CLIP distance, SyncNet confidence, expression FID, SIM-o), with no human preference evaluation (human preference / Arena) design observed.

### [Step-Video-T2V](../models/Step-Video-T2V.md)

Human effort is concentrated at the very end of the funnel (pretraining fully automatic, post-training heavily manual), in three places:
1. Manual curation of SFT data: constructing the 30M high-quality video set is a two-stage process of "automation + manual" — first automatically filtering with various evaluation scores and heuristic rules, then removing outlier samples beyond a threshold distance from the cluster center by video category (cluster), and finally having human annotators review each item one by one, along the dimensions of clarity, aesthetics, appropriate motion, and smooth scene transitions; humans simultaneously refine/rewrite captions (caption refinement) — i.e., humans not only filter data but also fix the annotations.
2. DPO preference annotation: human annotators compose prompts following design guidelines; multiple videos generated from different seeds for the same prompt are then given preference rankings by humans; the entire process is supervised by quality control personnel to ensure accuracy and consistency of annotation.
3. Manual evaluation review: on Step-Video-T2V-Eval, manual evaluation is the primary assessment method, comparing multiple open-source and commercial engines.
The size of the annotation team, the person-hour cost, or annotation-consistency metrics (such as Kappa values) are not disclosed. The report's outlook section explicitly notes that the gains from DPO saturate "once the model can easily distinguish positive from negative samples," and proposes future use of a reward model to dynamically score newly generated samples instead of relying purely on manual annotation — i.e., the team has already recognized the scalability bottleneck of manual preference annotation.

### [UniTalking](../models/UniTalking.md) ⚠️

Human involvement is zero on the training-data side, while on the evaluation side it is the sole and central evaluation method — an extreme allocation of "fully automatic data, fully manual evaluation":
【Training-data side: completely no human involvement】The three-level filtering (video/audio/audiovisual) is entirely determined by automated models, all annotation is automatically generated by Qwen3-VL/Whisper-V3/Qwen3-Omni, and reference audio is automatically synthesized by IndexTTS2. The paper mentions no manual annotation, manual spot-checking, or manual review, nor does it state whether the various filtering thresholds were verified/calibrated manually (contrast with MOVA, which explicitly states that it "manually spot-checks retained videos under different cutoffs to set thresholds"). Given that no threshold values are disclosed anywhere in the paper, the calibration process is a complete black box.
【Evaluation side: humans are the primary evaluation method】The core evaluation of the T2AV joint-generation task uses a blind pairwise preference study:
- Number of participants: 20 participants per model comparison group;
- Test set: 50 speaker-generation test prompts;
- Three evaluation dimensions: video quality, audio quality, audiovisual synchrony;
- Scoring method: with OVI as the baseline (normalized to 100), the win rate is computed as rate = (win + tie) / (lose + tie);
- Results: relative to OVI, UniTalking scores 116% on audio quality and 107% on audiovisual synchrony, with video quality roughly even (the paper explains this as the two sharing the same Wan2.2 backbone, so the video side is inherently the same source).
【Methodological limitations of the human evaluation】The scale of 20 people × 50 prompts is relatively small; no confidence interval or statistical significance test is reported; the participants' background and selection criteria are not stated; whether sample randomization and order balancing were performed is not stated. Compared with MOVA's 732-item bilingual (Chinese/English) Arena evaluation set, both the scale and the rigor fall short. [Uncertain]
【Auxiliary role of objective metrics】Three objective metrics — Sync-C/Sync-D, Speaker Similarity, and WER — are used as supplements, but the paper questions the credibility of Sync-C (arguing that this metric favors exaggerated lip movement, so OVI's high score of 6.56 may not actually represent better true synchrony) — this in itself shows the team regards manual subjective evaluation as the more reliable criterion.

### [UniVerse-1](../models/UniVerse-1.md)

Human involvement on the training-data side is almost zero: the entire cleaning funnel (audio-track detection → resolution/bitrate/DOVER → PySceneDetect → audio-activity detection → Whisper speech detection → RetinaFace + SyncNet) is entirely automatic threshold-based judgment, and the annotation stage is generated entirely automatically online by QWen2.5-Omni and Whisper. The paper mentions no manual annotation, manual spot-checking, or manual review, nor does it state whether the various thresholds were verified/calibrated manually — a contrast with MOVA, which explicitly states that it "manually spot-checks retained videos under different cutoffs to set thresholds."
【The only human involvement is on the evaluation side】The construction process for Verse-Bench's Set2-V subset (295 items, from YouTube and Bilibili) is: LLM-generated captions + Whisper for ASR, followed by human verification. That is, human effort is concentrated on assuring the quality of the evaluation benchmark rather than the training data.
【Design implication】This is a resource-allocation orientation of "fully automatic training data, semi-manual evaluation data," consistent with its overall positioning of being constrained in compute/manpower (the Limitations section explicitly acknowledges being constrained by computational resources).

### [Unison](../models/Unison.md) ⚠️

Human involvement on the training-data side is zero: the entire pipeline (dataset aggregation → automated refinement → face detection + in-box SyncNet lip filtering → Mel-RoFormer sound-source disentanglement) is entirely automated, and the paper explicitly calls it an "automated processing pipeline." No manual annotation, manual spot-checking, or manual review is mentioned, and it is not stated whether the thresholds for SyncNet and other steps were manually calibrated.
【Indirectly inherited human effort】It should be noted that although Unison's own corpus involves no human effort, the five upstream open-source datasets each involved substantial human work during their own construction — CelebV-Text's text descriptions, VGGSound's sound-event category annotations, and VFHQ/HDTF's quality screening are all manual or semi-manual products. A hidden benefit of aggregation-based data construction is precisely "riding for free" on the human investment made upstream, which is also the premise that allows Unison to obtain usable corpus purely through an automated pipeline.
【Human involvement is entirely concentrated on the evaluation side】
1) The test set is "curated" — the 1,000 held-out samples are described as a "curated test set," implying some manual selection step, but the annotation itself was generated by Gemini, and the specific human responsibilities are unstated; [uncertain]
2) User study — 25 participants "from diverse backgrounds" ranked 40 groups of samples, producing 1,000 average ranking votes in total (40 × 25). The evaluation dimensions were three: Lip-sync, Speech-Sound Harmony, and Motion-Audio Align. (considering both speech and ambient sound). Participants had to rank multiple methods' videos presented in shuffled order (lower is better). Results: Unison ranked first in both Speech-Sound Harmony (1.55) and Motion-Audio Align. (1.92), while its lip sync (1.86) trailed LTX-2 (1.74) — this ranking distribution precisely confirms the paper's positioning of its contribution: its innovation lies in "acoustic-level balance" and "motion-audio alignment," not lip sync itself.
【Resource-allocation orientation】Consistent with UniVerse-1, both follow "fully automatic training data, semi-manual evaluation data"; but Unison's human-evaluation design is more complete (three-dimensional ranking + LLM-judge cross-validation), whereas UniVerse-1 has no subjective human evaluation at all.

### [Veo 3 / Veo 3.1](../models/Veo_3_Veo_31.md) ⚠️

Confirmed human involvement is concentrated on the evaluation and safety sides rather than the data-annotation side: (1) model-performance evaluation uses human raters for head-to-head preference comparisons (MovieGenBench, VBench-I2V); (2) red-teaming is performed by a mix of internal expert teams and externally recruited participants, throughout the model-development process; (3) assurance evaluations are developed and executed by a dedicated team independent of the model-development team, with the evaluation dataset strictly held out; (4) bias evaluation involves manual analysis — 16 videos are generated for each of 140 occupations, then classified by perceived skin tone (Monk Skin Tone Scale), perceived age, and perceived gender; (5) prior to release, manual approval is given by Google DeepMind's Responsibility and Safety Council (RSC). [Uncertain] The scale of manual annotation for the training data itself, the manual quality-control spot-check ratio, and whether a "model pre-screening + manual review" step exists are all undisclosed — from the phrasing, captioning and filtering appear to be essentially fully automatic and model-driven.

### [Vidu S1](../models/Vidu_S1.md) ⚠️

The data side is almost fully automatic: filtering and annotation are both done by expert models + an omni large model + heuristic rules (such as a speech-energy-ratio rule); the paper mentions no manual annotation or manual review step [degree of human involvement on the data side uncertain]. Human involvement is clearly present on the evaluation side: a human preference evaluation was organized on the self-built Vidu-StreamBench (500 samples), running paired A/B tests against three commercial systems — HeyGen, LemonSlice, and Kling Avatar 2.0 — with evaluation dimensions including Overall, Motion Dynamics, Identity Consistency, Audio-Video Sync, and Subject Controllability.

### [Wan 2.5 / 2.6 / 2.7](../models/Wan.md)

Human involvement follows a leveraged design of "small amounts of high-value manual annotation → train expert models → fully automatic at scale," appearing at six points:
1) Seed annotation for the visual-quality scorer: samples drawn from each cluster were given to humans to score 1–5 (1 worst, 5 best); all the annotated data was used to train an expert evaluation model, which then scores the entire dataset. This forms the cornerstone of level 2 of the whole funnel.
2) Seed annotation for camera angle and motion: a batch of videos was manually annotated, used both to directly train the caption model and to train an expert model for batch-scale label expansion.
3) Manual curation of post-training data: on the image side, besides the expert model's top 20%, there is additionally "manual collection" — humans collecting top-tier images from every category and source, and proactively filling in concepts missing from the dataset to enhance generalization.
4) Densely manually annotated caption training data: the report states that manually annotated dense captions for images and videos are "our highest-quality caption data," used specifically for the final stage of model training.
5) Manual data collection for Wan2.2-S2V: humans manually selected videos from publicly accessible sources containing deliberate and complex human activities (talking, singing, dancing), forming, together with automatic coarse filtering, the initial video pool.
6) Evaluation side: Wan-Bench trained a YOLOv3-like model to detect AI-generation artifacts, using 20,000 manually annotated AI-generated images as training data; the final composite score uses a "human feedback guided weighting strategy" to determine the weight of each dimension.
No description is found of manual review or spot-checking of individual captions; the scale of manual involvement for 2.5+ is unknown.

### [Collection of Audio-Video Generation Evaluation Benchmarks](../models/av_benchmarks.md) ⚠️

The five works differ markedly in the degree of human involvement, forming a full spectrum from "heavy manual" to "light manual":
【PhyAVBench has the heaviest human involvement】Human experts are involved throughout all five stages: removing infeasible/redundant physical principles generated by an LLM, reviewing the taxonomy to disambiguate and de-duplicate it, verifying and rewriting paired prompts item by item to ensure single-variable differences, personally organizing 184 participants to record 11,605 videos in a controlled environment, and, at the quality-control stage, reviewing physical consistency and tri-modal alignment and deleting/revising/re-collecting problematic samples. In the subjective-evaluation stage, PVR-MOS employed 74 raters to score, on a 1–5 scale, the correspondence between audio differences and physical changes.
【AV-SyncBench has the most rigorous cross-review mechanism】5 human annotators, with each video clip independently reviewed by at least 3 people, verifying the visibility of the primary sound-source in the frame and removing low-quality samples — an "N=5 annotator pool, ≥3-person cross-check per sample" is a directly reusable quality-control configuration. Coarse screening is done upfront by Gemini 3 Flash, forming a standard two-tier "model pre-screening + manual review" structure.
【Omni-Judge】6 PhD students with experience in audio-video generation research scored 600 videos on integer 1–5 scales across 4 dimensions (quality, semantic alignment, temporal alignment, aesthetics), serving as a human baseline for measuring the judging capability of the Omni-LLM judge.
【AVBench】4 domain experts performed pairwise comparisons (2AFC), selecting the better model per dimension and allowing ties, with the win-rate formula (W + 0.5T)/(W + T + L), then using the Pearson correlation coefficient to verify the consistency between automatic scoring and human preference.
【VABench】explicitly uses human workers together with an LLM to filter test samples and adjust the test-data distribution, with humans verifying category membership, element observability, physical common-sense constraints, plausibility of auditory inference, and question discriminability; but the number of annotators and consistency metrics are not disclosed [uncertain].

### [Video Captioning Model Ecosystem](../models/caption_models.md)

Human involvement in the captioner ecosystem is concentrated at three points, consistently following the pattern of "small amounts of high-value manual work + large-scale model inference":
【Point one · manual fine-annotation of seed data (highest cost, highest value)】
· SkyCaptioner-V1's camera-motion sub-expert: 93,000 high-confidence manually annotated samples + a 15,000-item manually annotated test set.
· ALIVE: the training data for the caption model follows the classic self-built path of "MLLM generation → manual revision (manually revised caption data) → two rounds of SFT," trading a one-time manual cost for large-scale low-cost inference; there is also manual annotation of the start/end timestamps of sub-motion units.
· Movie Gen: in the post-training stage, captions are manually refined on top of model output.
· Step-Video-T2V: in the SFT stage, humans "refine captions," serving as a fallback when not doing post-training specifically to suppress captioner hallucination.
【Point two · human research in labeler selection and evaluation】
· Panda-70M used a user study on 1,000 clips with greedy set cover to select 8 labelers, while also reporting that human inter-annotator agreement was only 44.9% — this figure defines the ceiling for the entire caption-quality evaluation, and is the most important human-evaluation finding in this ecosystem.
· Movie Gen's caption-scheme comparison relies entirely on manual A/B testing: video captions were preferred 67% of the time vs. 15% for the frame-by-frame rewriting scheme.
· AVSCapBench's 1,226 videos are manually annotated; UGC-VideoCap adopts a three-stage human-in-the-loop process (labeling audio-only / visual-only / joint AV respectively).
· The reasonableness of Open-Sora Plan's thresholds was validated through 2,000 manual spot-checks.
【Point three · the trend of humans being replaced by LLM-judges】From 2025–2026, item-by-item manual review of caption quality has been almost entirely replaced by GPT-4.1 / Gemini scoring (AVoCaDO's completeness score, AVSCap's automated verification, AuroraCap's VDCScore), with humans retained only for benchmark construction and the final A/B stage. The cost of this replacement has not yet been systematically assessed — given that human agreement is only 44.9%, the deviation between LLM-judge and human judgment may already be masked by the fact that "humans themselves are also inconsistent."

### [Geometric/Structured Annotation Dataset Collection](../models/geometric_datasets.md) ⚠️

Overall human involvement is very light, dominated by automation. SpatialVID has the most human involvement: at the initial-screening stage, humans reviewed 33,443 source videos, removing corrupted content, titles containing inappropriate words or the phrase "panoramic camera," and content incompatible with the MegaSaM reconstruction pipeline; all subsequent annotation is fully automatic. WildWorld: humans were used to construct the evaluation set — the 200 samples of WildBench were manually selected to cover multiple characters/weapons/monsters/difficulty levels/events, and a consistency check between human evaluation and VLM-judge was performed (85% agreement rate); data collection itself is done via automatic in-game recording. SceneScribe-1M: the paper describes no manual verification step; quality control is entirely automatic, done by Qwen2.5-VL-72B and TransNetV2 [uncertain]. Action100M: no explicit manual verification; three rounds of GPT-OSS-120B Self-Refine substitute for manual review.

### [Post-Training Data Strategies for Video Generation](../models/post_training_data.md) ⚠️

【Anchor paper】Human involvement appears only in two places: ① the "human preference annotation (y1,y2)" used for reward-model training, with the annotation volume, protocol, and annotator team size all undisclosed [uncertain]; ② the final evaluation uses the GSB (Good–Same–Bad) protocol, explicitly stating "we ask the human artist to give an overall comparison for the results" — a human artist gives the overall comparison. The paper's reason for choosing GSB over forced binary choice is clearly stated: GSB allows annotators to express "no difference" when the difference is subtle, reducing the noisy judgments forced at the margin — something especially important for video evaluation.
【The horizontal spectrum of human-involvement forms (post-training is the stage with the highest density of human involvement)】
· Pure manual preference annotation: Step-Video-T2V (annotators give preference scores for results generated from multiple seeds, with the whole process supervised by quality-control personnel for consistency), Kling 3.0 Omni (human evaluators compare multiple variants), HunyuanVideo 1.5 (GSB annotation; the I2V side's accompanying images also require manual verification of text-image consistency);
· Multi-dimensional structured annotation protocol (the most fine-grained): Seedance 1.0 — "select the best/worst under a specified dimension, while ensuring the best is not inferior to the worst on other dimensions." This constraint directly resolves the contradictory-pairing problem under multi-dimensional rewards, and is the same problem solved a different way by JavisDiT++'s "normalized modality-aware ranking" (whose explicit goal is "to ensure internal consistency within each modality, rather than pairing high-quality audio with low-quality video");
· Semi-automatic production line: SkyReels-V2 (30,000 manually annotated sample pairs + automatically generated controlled-distortion samples);
· Manual final review of the SFT set: HunyuanVideo's 1 million manually curated items (seven-dimensional rubric), SkyReels-V4's 1 million manually curated items, Movie Gen's manual selection for cinematic feel + manual caption rewriting, Step-Video-T2V's item-by-item manual review, Apollo Stage III's manually-curated high-quality set;
· Professional annotation of public preference sets: VideoReward hired "professional annotators" to record pairwise preferences (A wins/tie/B wins) for each triplet across three dimensions — VQ/MQ/TA — modeling them with a tie-aware Bradley-Terry model (BTT);
· Human effort used only for evaluation, not fed back into training (the most common form of waste in this topic): Movie Gen, CogVideoX, Open-Sora 2.0 (100-prompt manual win rate), Ovi (50-person blind test), Unison (40 samples × 25 people = 1,000 ranking votes), UniTalking (20 people × 50 prompts), InstructAV2AV (25 people × 20 samples × 3 dimensions), Foley-Omni (A/S/T-MOS), HunyuanVideo-Foley (MOS-Q/S/T), CineDance (10 evaluators per video, 5-point scale), UltraVideo (10-person comparison), LVD-2M (200 responses), Script-a-Video (20 professional raters) — all of these works have already organized human labor for subjective evaluation, and the marginal cost of extending the same batch of annotation into preference pairs would be minimal, yet none of them did so. This is a systemic gap in post-training on the open-source/academic side.

### [Survey of Mainstream Video Pretraining Dataset Collections: Panda-70M, InternVid, Koala…](../models/pretraining_datasets.md) ⚠️

**Human involvement ranges from "zero manual effort" to "200,000 items × 8 expert raters," an enormous spread, and the purposes fall into three highly differentiated categories**:
【Type A · manual annotation as model training data (two datasets)】
- **Koala-36M (200,000 items × 8 experts, the heaviest human investment in this survey)**: 200,000 videos were randomly sampled, with each rated 1–5 by **8 trained experts** for its "suitability as video-generation training data." Three dimensions: **dynamic quality** (the moving region must cover **more than 30%** of the frame; distinguishing amateur shakiness from professional camera work), **static quality** (rich subject detail, reasonable composition, aesthetically pleasing, clear subject, vivid color), and **video naturalness** (favoring natural, unprocessed content, penalizing special effects/transitions/subtitles/logos; excluding political, horror, violent, and gory content). Two bias corrections: **personal-preference bias** is corrected via per-expert z-score standardization restored using the global mean and variance; **annotation-fluctuation bias** is handled by averaging across the 8 raters. This annotation directly trains the TSA network.
- **Panda-70M (100,000 items, used to train the caption selector)**: 100,000 clips were randomly sampled, with annotators selecting the best of 8 candidate captions. The original instruction is interesting: "Imagine you are talking on the phone with your friend and you need to describe the video to him." An "All Bad" option was provided, and **12,064 items were discarded because all candidate captions were unacceptable**, with the remainder split into 86,131 training / 1,805 validation. A further 2 annotators re-labeled the validation set to measure human agreement (**only 44.9% R@1**).
【Type B · human involvement for rule/threshold calibration and source gatekeeping】Panda-70M: a teacher-selection study over 1,000 clips × 31 captions each (asking annotators to "select all the good captions," with the criterion being "contains no incorrect information, and covers the main action or all the main objects"), used as the basis for greedy set cover. Koala-36M: **manual transition annotation on 10,000 videos** (about half containing transitions) as an evaluation benchmark for the transition detector. **UltraVideo (the heaviest source-level manual gatekeeping)**: (a) starting from LLM-generated keywords, **manually searching** for the latest 4K/8K videos; (b) a **secondary manual re-inspection** of 5,000 raw videos to remove low-quality/blurry/watermarked/shaky ones; (c) **manual revision and confirmation** of the 7 LLM-derived themes and 108 subcategories. MiraData: the paper's limitations section mentions that "manual verification cannot guarantee complete accuracy," hinting at manual spot-checking, but **the scale is not stated**. [Uncertain]
【Type C · human effort used only for final evaluation, not fed back into the data】
- **UltraVideo**: **5 annotators each rated 1,000** randomly sampled videos (one batch each for UltraVideo and Koala-36M); a "bad video" was defined as hitting any of 15 defect types (subtitles, abnormal color blocks, green screen, blue screen, transition effects, watermark, sticker, border, split screen, screen recording, picture-in-picture, still video, blurry video, glitch/artifact, solid-color background), and raters were explicitly instructed to **ignore resolution** when judging. Results: **UltraVideo's bad-video rate was 2.3% vs. Koala-36M's 41.5%** (inter-annotator agreement not reported). Another 10 people participated in a preference comparison between UltraWan and the official Wan.
- **LVD-2M**: three sets of human evaluation — long-shot consistency (40 clips per dataset in a blind test), degree of dynamism (40 clips, 3-tier scoring), and caption quality (50 clips, A/B); the generation-experiment user study received **200 valid responses**.
- **Panda-70M**: a teacher-vs-student preference study over 200 videos with 5 participants.
【Type D · zero human involvement】**InternVid** only used manual verification when building the action-word vocabulary ("LLM extracted 5,001 actions, followed by manual verification"), with no human involvement elsewhere. **OpenVid-1M** shows no record of any manual annotation, quality control, or evaluation. [Uncertain]
**Two key conclusions**: (1) **None of the seven datasets performs manual verification or correction of caption content** — even Koala-36M, which invested 200,000 annotations, used humans only to assign a quality score, with captions generated entirely by a VLM and used as-is; UltraVideo explicitly states there is "no manual verification or correction of any caption content." (2) The mainstream use of manual effort is shifting from "annotating data" toward "calibrating automated rules + verifying results" — Koala-36M's 200,000 items × 8 raters is an exception, and the price paid for that effort was, ironically, an **unreproducible scorer** (the released version differs from the paper's version).
